# Chasing Rhinos With The Swami. Volume 3

## ...PRELUDE

From mid-1966 to mid-1970, in just four years’ time, Bhakti-vedanta Swami had built his International Society for Krishna Consciousness into a major spiritual force in North Ameri America and Europe. On August 2,1970, Srila Prabhupad from Los Angeles to Tokyo to commence his first round-the-world preaching tour, returning to L.A. in late June 1971, having planted the seeds for ISKCON in Japan, India, Russia, Malaysia, and Australia. For his second round-the-world tour, Prabhupada left Los Angeles on July 16, 1971, and now, in late May of 1972, after his heroic success in London, Nairobi, India, Australia, New Zealand, Hong Kong, and Japan, Srila Prabhupada is back in L.A., revving up for his third round-the-world adventure.

He’s met his with his North American ISKCON leaders, organized them, prepared them, and now he’s going to show them how to get things done, starting with Mexico. Armed with fresh cassette tapes, I jump in the front seat of Prabhupada’s car as we race to LAX for our flight to Mexico City—chasing another rhino with the Swami.

## One

On June 2, 1972, after a four-and-a-half-hour flight from Los Angeles, Prabhupada and his traveling team—Nanda Kumar, Pradyumna, and me-are lugging our gear around yet another crowded, chaotic, foreign airport. “\\Bienvenidos a Mexico!" says a sign in big letters. Mexico City is not exactly “foreign”—I’ve been here a couple of times and speak a little Spanish—but after we clear Inmigracion, come out through Llegadas, and there are no devotees to greet us, we might as well have landed on Mars. “Just sit down here, Srila Prabhupada,” I say. “Maybe they’re just late. I’ll try to phone them. Keep an eye on our stuff, Nanda Kumar.”

By now I’m in panic mode. I see a Cambio and change a few dollars into pesos, spot a bank of pay phones, and try to figure out how to load centavos into the thing—do you put in the coins before or after the dial tone? I slam in a few coins and dial a number for Chitsukhananda, the local temple president. A recorded voice says something in rapid Spanish. What?! Jeez, maybe I need a local prefix. I try again and again. No luck. Then I look across the noisy arrivals hall and see Prabhupada talking with a well-dressed Mexican couple. He catches my eye and waves me over. It seems these folks had just left the temple, and that everyone thinks our flight is arriving two hours from now.

Mrs. Adelita Diaz offers to take us to the temple in her little red car. Mr. Alfonso Araiza, a lawyer, will stay behind to inform the others when they arrive. Minutes later we’re on the road toward Mexico City.

Relaxed in the back seat, in bright saffron silk, Srila Prabhupada is looking good. After a month’s rest in Hawaii and Los Angeles, the fatigue from nearly three years of global travel has been erased from his face, and his keen wide eyes click back and forth, absorbing the sights and sounds of a place and culture he’s never seen.

The ISKCON temple is twelve kilometers due west across the city, says Mrs. Diaz, and as we drive past a vast open plaza on our right, she adds, “This is the Plaza de la Constitution, the Zocalo, the heart of Mexico City.” Stopped at a signal, we watch packs of tourists and gaily dressed locals gathered or strolling across the immense square, mariachi bands, pigeons swirling, vendors hawking snow cones and little Madonnas. “And that big church on your far right—that’s the Assumption of Mary, largest Catholic cathedral in the Americas, built in 1573. Before the Catholics came this area was called Tenochtitlan, the center of the ancient Aztec universe.”

It’s Friday, so traffic is heavy, and we crawl up Avenida Juarez, turn left on Paseo de la Reforma, and I explain to Prabhupada what little I know about the Aztecs, who built pyramids to their gods. The Spanish came along, I tell him, and with less than four hundred men and with their horses, armor, guns, and disease they quickly conquered the Aztec empire. They looted most of South America, sending their booty to Spain and in just a few decades multiplied the old-world gold supply several times over. This vast amount of precious metal capitalized the Renaissance expansion of Europe, led to a number of international wars, and launched the centuries-long history of slaughter and greed for gold that grips the West to this day.

Prabhupada takes it all in, not saying much. When Mrs. Diaz says that 85 percent of Mexicans are strict Catholics, Prabhupada says, “That is their saving grace.” We swing south on Avenida

Constituyentes—“That’s Chapultepec Park on the right. We are very near now.” We drive down a wide boulevard through a neighborhood of large, elegant homes, and pull up to the curb at #45 Gobernador Tiburcio Montiel. Nanda Kumar, Pradyumna, and I grab the luggage and follow Mrs. Diaz and Prabhupada through an ornate iron gate set in a high stucco wall.

“Wow! Check this place out, Pradyumna!” Before us stands a two-story Spanish-style mansion, with arched windows, tree-shaded porticos, round cupolas at corners of the building like you’d see on a fortress. Nice, real nice. We enter through the kitchen door and pass into the main room, where startled devotees shout “Prabhupada!” and fall to the floor in obeisances or kneel, hands folded, in the Catholic manner. Chandeliers glow from the high ceiling, and showers of rose petals rain down on us from the balcony that surrounds the richly-appointed room. Prabhupada grins with delight and says, “It is just like India.”

We climb wide interior stairs to Prabhupada’s large, rectangular room on the second floor, with its balcony looking out on a swimming pool, lawn, and gardens. Nanda Kumar, Pradyumna, and I are assigned an adjoining circular cupola room. From the window we see Chitsukhananda arrive in a big American car festooned with flowers. He races upstairs and into Prabhupada’s room, bowing and crying, apologizing profusely. Prabhupada quickly calms him with praises for all he has obviously done here in less than a year.

Other than through letters and phone conversations I’d never met Chitsukhananda, but I knew he’d been president of the San Francisco temple and had founded the San Jose temple before pioneering in Mexico. His obvious sincerity, energy, and intelligence had endeared him to Prabhupada immediately. Two years earlier, Prabhupada had arranged Chitsukhananda’s marriage to Chandrabali dasi (Claudia, Malati’s old friend from our preSwami days), and the stormy relationship between these two hot-blooded Latins was notorious on the ISKCON gossip circuit. This is the story we’d heard: Once, Chitsukhananda was giving a

Srimad-Bhagavatam class at the San Francisco temple, pitching his remarks especially to a good-looking young brahmacharini, Chandrabali came in, loudly accused her husband of lusty thoughts, ripped off his dhoti in front of the startled devotees, and stomped from the room. Chitsukhananda chased after her in his underwear, and grabbed and threw her through a wall. She picked herself up, covered in white plaster dust, and ran into the kitchen, where she snatched a butcher knife. He grabbed a broom. Their swordfight down the hall became the stuff of legend. Eventually, they had four children together.

“How did you get this fantastic place, Chitsukhananda?”

“This was the former Ethiopian embassy. I told the owner that we were a huge, worldwide organization with plenty of money, and she believed me. Actually, we do have some wealthy followers. One guy especially, Danny Partida—he’s a famous Mexican race-car driver. He paid for all your airline tickets to come here.”

Prabhupada mounts the opulent vyasasan and delivers his arrival speech to maybe three hundred devotees and guests crammed into the temple room, pausing again and again while Chit translates into Spanish. The mood of Catholic awe and reverence is palpable. Incense smoke is cast about the room in thuribles, and the altar servant slings holy water on the congregation with a bona fide Catholic aspergillum. What angelic people these are, ready-made devotees, passionate believers. They’re well dressed, many of the women wearing saris, and they gaze on Prabhupada like he’s the Pope. Prabhupada is surprised and greatly pleased to return their outpouring of devotion. And Hanuman Swami is here, shouting in Spanish with his French accent! We last saw him in Los Angeles—he’s stopping over here on his way to open temples in South America.

That evening we go to the Freemasons Lodge in Colonia San Rafael, the Muy Respetable Gran Logia Valle de Mexico. Chit tells us these guys wield most of the political power in Mexico, and that belief in God is one of the prerequisites for initiation into the ancient Freemason order. Prabhupada delivers his lecture, with its Spanish translations, telling a hundred stoic mafia guys in black suits and sunglasses that Krishna consciousness is the ultimate fraternal order. The audience looks like a junta in the making. When we get back to the temple, Chitsukhananda mentions to Prabhupada that he had recently appeared on Mexico’s most-watched TV show, 24 Horas, and that the host really wants Prabhupada to be on the show while he’s in town, “So which night would be most suitable for Your Divine Grace?”

Prabhupada: How many people watch the show?

Chit: Twenty to thirty million.

Prabhupada: Then let us go tonight. Why wait?

Chit: But Prabhupada, it’s so late...

Prabhupada: Never mind. Tonight we work. Tomorrow we will sleep.

I laugh to see that Prabhupada is not affected by the manana syndrome, putting off until tomorrow what can be done today. Chit runs off to make some phone calls, then comes back and says, “We’re on tonight’s show, Srila Prabhupada!” After a half-hour drive we arrive at the Televisa studios just before midnight. The host, Jacobo Zabludovsky, is from a pious Jewish background; his reverence for Prabhupada is obvious, and he foregoes his usual sarcastic, tumultuous TV demeanor to conduct a penetrating look into spirituality. Prabhupada responds in a humble manner, Mr. Charm himself. Jacobo is so visibly shaken by Prabhupada’s presence that near the end of the show, when he asks the Swami, “Who is God?” and Prabhupada answers “He is the Supreme Personality. He is very beautiful, He is all-attractive, and He likes to be called Krishna,” Jacobo puts his head in his hands, elbows drop to his knees, and he cannot respond—fade to commercial. Srila Prabhupada conquers Mexico in one night.

On the morning of June 3, a Saturday, we walk through Chapultepec Park, just a couple of blocks from the temple, and

Prabhupada very much enjoys the vast green lawns, the profusion of tropical flowers and trees, remarking that it is more beautiful than any park in India. He’s impressed that “Los Pinos,” the palatial residence of the president of Mexico, is just three blocks from the temple.

Chitsukhananda has arranged two programs for today in Cuernavaca, the capital city of Morelos state, about fifty miles south of Mexico City. At 10 a.m. we pile into a luxurious Mercedes piloted by none other than Formula-1 driver Danny Partida. As we head south on the Avenida de las Insurgentes, Chit points to a huge stadium on our right and informs Prabhupada that this is the Plaza de Toros, la corrida, where fifty thousand people gather to watch men kill bulls with swords.

For the next many miles, Danny shows his driving skills on the wide, winding mountain highway south to Cuernavaca, the symmetric snow-capped eighteen-thousand-foot Popocatepetl volcano rising before us—is that smoke coming out? Prabhupada speaks of bulls and killing, saying that the bull is the symbol of dharma, religious principles, and that it stands on four legs: austerity, cleanliness, mercy, and truthfulness. Dharmah pada ekena charan, he says. In this Age of Kali three legs of dharma have been cut off, leaving only truthfulness to stabilize this precarious condition. “Just see. Now the bulls are meant for being slaughtered and eaten up as foodstuff by a class of men who do not know what is brahminical culture. When brahminical culture deteriorates the picture is televisioned by the acrobatic killing of bulls. These Christians. Big demonstration of cowfighting, bullfighting, and kill them. Such a Christian is trying to convert others as Christians! Just see the fun. Christ said, ‘Thou shall not kill,’ and they are sporting with the life of bull, and they are Christian.”

Miles pass as we meditate on this dark topic, then Prabhupada sheds further light on the matter: “One of our students in Montreal—he was saying that his father took pleasure in bullfighting in Spain. When the bull is killed by fighting, he was taking pleasure. So different kinds of men. One person is seeing, ‘It is horrible,’ another person is enjoying, ‘Oh, it is very nice.’ You see? So Krishna can accommodate. If you want to love horrible things, Krishna can present Himself as a lion, Nrisinghadeva, ‘Ah, now I will kill you!’ \[laughter\] Yes. And if you want to see Krishna as very loving friend, He is Vamshi-dhari—like Arjuna. If you want Krishna as loving child, then He is Gopal.”

And then a few miles later, the perfect last word on the subject: “You’ll see always Krishna is joyful. When He’s killing an animal, He’s laughing. He’s joyful. You have seen Krishna’s picture? He is fighting with that bull, Arishtasura demon, attacking with his horns. Krishna is just like playing with a toy. That is Krishna.”

Soon we arrive in Cuernavaca and are taken to a devotee’s parents’ home for a siesta before the main event. Prabhupada is shown into a small, clean bedroom, and I close the door behind him. A few minutes later I hear “Shyamasundar!” spoken gravely behind the door. Prabhupada is sitting at the end of the bed—he seems so small and sad, tears leaking down his cheeks. He points to a small crucifix with Christ hanging in agony high on the wall before him and says, “Please remove that... They have killed their spiritual master so they can go on sinning.” I remove the potent Catholic icon, but Prabhupada has lost his appetite, refuses lunch, and says, “Let us go out from here.”

Chitsukhananda has booked the Glorieta de la Luna, a vast circular plaza surrounded by eight-lane Plan de Ayala, Cuernavaca’s main thoroughfare. Devotees have arrived in a Ford Falcon station wagon and set up a stage and microphone. Very few people have gathered, but after a resounding kirtan, Prabhupada begins to speak as if to millions, and gradually a hundred curious citizens fill the plaza, clapping above traffic noise when he’s finished. As Chit runs off to get the car, Prabhupada walks toward the street, accompanied by half a dozen of his stalwart men. Suddenly, we glance up and see a drunken man in a big sombrero stumbling through our midst mumbling something about Buddha and—just before he collides with our master—Prabhupada slips sideways and walks forward as if nothing has happened. While devotees behind us help the guy to his feet, we hurry to catch up with Prabhupada, awestruck by our fearless hero’s strength and perfect timing.

Danny drove us two miles north up Plan de Ayala to the Casino de la Selva, a grand hotel built in the 1930s with soaring avant-garde, hyperbolic architecture covered in huge paintings by noted Mexican muralists. Here, the country’s upper class could revisit their ancient past and enjoy themselves in twenty acres of lush (but cultivated) jungle. After Prabhupada spoke, Haihaya das, a wonderful Mexican devotee, set up a small table to sell, fresh off the press, Prabhupada’s first book in Spanish translation: La Conciencia de Krishna, El Mas Elevado Sistema de Yoga (Krishna Consciousness, the Topmost Yoga System). All fifty copies were sold to members of the erudite and well-mannered audience as they patiently stood in line so Prabhupada could autograph each copy.

By now it was 9 p.m., and Prabhupada hadn’t slept for about two days and hadn’t eaten anything since breakfast. He politely declined an offer to overnight in Cuernavaca and insisted we drive back to Mexico City. By the time we returned to the temple, it was well after midnight.

Chitsukhananda: Now Srila Prabhupada was in his room, and he was in great ecstasy. His eyes were shining and his mouth was very bright. All he wanted was a cup of hot milk with about eight puris and a cup of sugar. He crushed the puris into the sugar and drank the milk. And with great joy and happiness he said, “This is our life. To work all day for Krishna and take a little prasad at night.” He said, “This is happiness. This is life.”

**\* \* \***

The next morning, a Sunday, Prabhupada again walks through Chapultepec Park, returning to the temple for his morning lecture. Afterward, climbing the stairs to his room, he looks down and sees an older, gray-haired woman trembling, crying, and laughing at the same time—traditional symptoms of spiritual ecstasy. Turning to Chitsukhananda, he says: “She must be initiated.”

Chitsukhananda: I was thinking, “This lady has only come here once or twice, she is not qualified” But she became Apsara devi dasi. Apsaras are beautiful, supernatural female beings, celestial maidens. That day she became a little holy lady, and we loved her very much.

Later that morning, Swami Jyoti and Swami Tilak, two Indian holy men who were traveling around Mexico, came to visit Srila Prabhupada. The three swamis had a great time conversing, mostly in Hindi and with much laughter, and we were enlivened to see these sadhus treating Prabhupada like their own guru.

That afternoon Prabhupada went down to the temple room and initiated about twenty new devotees, including Apsara devi. In his room after the ceremony we could hear about five hundred people downstairs chanting “Jaya Prabhupada! Jaya Prabhupada!” again and again, the way the faithful call the Pope from St. Peters Square at the Vatican. Chitsukhananda said, “They want to see you, Prabhupada.”

“Well, let them come!”

And what followed was one of the most amazing things I’ve ever seen.

There were two entry doors to Prabhupada’s room. Hundreds of people lined up in the hall before one of the doors, spilling down the stairs and out into the yard, circling twice around the house. Chitsukhananda let one person at a time into Prabhupada’s room, and each bowed before him, spoke a few words in Spanish, left a gift, and received a “Hare Krishna!” blessing before rising, red-faced and ecstatic, to leave by the other door. This went on for about two hours, one by one. As each person left the room, he or she felt like the most important person in the universe, specially blessed by the Holy Father, Rock and Pillar of the Church of Krishna.

That evening, a Mexican fish biologist named Martin came to visit, and Prabhupada’s recent fiery Los Angeles debates about Charles Darwin and evolutionary science flared anew as a huge thunderstorm crackled in the twilight air:

Prabhupada: How many forms of fishes are there within the ocean?

Martin: I studied one.

Prabhupada: Huh?

Martin: I concentrated on one fish. But I learned about \[loud crack of thunder in background\] maybe a thousand.

Prabhupada: Jalaja nava-laksani—there are nine hundred thousand aquatics. Exact number. So what good is your knowledge? This is our scientific information from the Vedas, We haven’t got to make research. Everything is there. Similarly, this Darwin’s theory is there in two lines from Padma Purana: asitim caturas caiva laksams tan jiva-jatisu. Asati means “eighty.” Catura means “four.” Eighty-four lakhs, or 8,400,000, Jiva means “living entity.” Jati means “species.” The living entity is transmigrating from aquatics to trees, plants, then insect, then birds, then beasts. In this way at last he reaches the human form of life, by the evolution of birth. Now this form is for understanding Govinda, God. The other forms, they cannot. Darwin’s grandfather is monkey \[laughs\], so he cannot understand.

The next day, June 5, there was a rather subdued noon lecture downtown at the Theosophical Society on Calle Iturbio Mariscal. Prabhupada was spared questions about Atlantis and Lemuria. Then later that afternoon Prabhupada lectured in a big hall at the University of Mexico. I was fascinated to see the famous mosaic murals covering all sides of the huge, cube-shaped biblioteca, depicting Aztec calendars and epics from Mexico’s bloody past, the Diego Rivera murals I’d seen in my mother’s Sunset magazines as a child. Maybe a thousand students showed up, and Prabhupada knocked them out with his charm and erudite knowledge. At the end of the lecture a student asked, “Do you see God?” Prabhupada replied, “What do you think? Do I see God?” Long pause. Then the student answered, “Yes, I think you see God.”

That night Prabhupada received a letter from Visakha dasi, along with the copy of a piece she has written for a Bombay publication entitled “Prabhupada: India’s National Hero”:

Hero refers to a man of superhuman qualities who is favored by the Gods and demigods; one who has fought bravely for his country; and a man admired for achievements and noble qualities. No one in India fulfills this description better than Srila Prabhupada.... It is only by the mercy of Krishna that a pure devotee walks among us and drags us out of the prison house of this material world, back to freedom. Srila Prabhupada is actually an interplanetary hero.

Prabhupada replied to Visakha: “I am very grateful to you for your kind words about me, but I do not think that I have done anything, but I am only delivering the best message as it is. Actually, anyone who is a sincere devotee of Krishna and who is rendering service by preaching His message is to be considered as hero. So you are all heroes of your country and your humanity. Hero means someone who others want to follow as example of the best type of person. So you all become like that, perfect examples of Krishna Consciousness heroes and heroines, and preach the message exactly as I have taught it to you very seriously and being fully convinced, and others will automatically come forward and join us. We shall all be like one great army of heroes for Lord Caitanya Mahaprabhu.”

**\* \* \***

In a surprise turn of events, Prabhupada decides to fly to Puerto Vallarta on the morning of June 6. Danny Partida has been telling

Prabhupada about his ashram near Puerto Vallarta, on Mexico’s west coast—some land and a nice house on a hill overlooking the Pacific Ocean. He’s offered to donate the place if Prabhupada likes it. I’d seen Night of the Iguana, with Richard Burton and Liz Taylor—sultry nights in Puerto Vallarta—so I told Prabhupada what a beautiful place it is. He decides to take a look. We’re scheduled to return to L.A. from Mexico City on June 7, then fly to Portland, Oregon, on the 8th, so Danny reroutes our tickets via Puerto Vallarta, a ninety-minute flight almost due west from Mexico City.

As we drive south into the highlands of Cochas Chinas from the Puerto Vallarta airport—Prabhupada, Danny, and I in one taxi, Nanda Kumar, Pradyumna, and Chitsukhananda in another—we’re struck by the magnificent location of Danny’s ashram, El Palomar, “the place of doves,” high atop open meadows with a view of the vast Pacific. The adobe, tile-roofed hacienda is open to warm sea breezes and decorated with flame-red bougainvillea and flowering cacti. It’s quiet and peaceful, and Prabhupada says he will take a little siesta.

From the back of the house you look far down into a lush jungle valley, a small river meandering in its depths. I ask Danny if it’s possible to swim there, and he says, “Yes, just follow the path down the hill.” Prompted by a possible jungle adventure, I ask Prabhupada if it’s OK if we take a swim while he rests, and he says, “Why not?”

The Rio Cuale is swollen and chocolate-brown from recent rains, but slow and warm, and as we cautiously dip into a pool, I yell to Pradyumna, “Hey, look out for that croc!” At 6’2” and skinny as a rail, blind as a bat without his glasses, Panditji’s burlesque floundering dash to shore is so hilarious I almost drown. We laugh and splash around for a while, then hike back up the hill full of good cheer to find Prabhupada bored and restless. “What shall I do here?” he asks. “No one is here. Let us go back to Los Angeles.” So Danny runs to town, changes tickets to the first flight out, and early the next morning, June 7, we fly back to the City of Angels.

At the Puerto Vallarta airport I bought the latest issue of Time magazine, and aboard the plane I read to Prabhupada an article called “Baptists Besieged,” describing President Nixon’s visit, after summit talks, to a Baptist church in Moscow. Prabhupada immediately dictated a long letter to the Russian Minister of Education and Culture:

With reference to an article entitled “Baptists Besieged” published in the international edition of Time magazine of June 12, 1972, page 66, it is understood that your people are now protesting against atheistic government. So far we know your government is pledged not to believe in God. But this kind of suppression will not be tolerated by the general mass of people. It is understood also that your Constitution allows freedom of religious expression. But it appears from the facts that you do not wish to indulge in religious principles which are simply sentiments. We agree wholeheartedly that without philosophy, religion is reduced to sentiment or “the opium of the masses.” We are likewise in full agreement with your national leaders that the state and the citizens in general must be organized upon philosophical principles.

Prabhupada goes on to suggest that the Bhagavad-gita should be the guide for a successful Soviet state, and offers to publish a Russian-language version: “Why not give them opportunity to read a book which is full of philosophy and science in the matter of developing their firm understanding of the truth or God consciousness, and thus very easily unify and satisfy all the citizens? Thanking you in anticipation of an early reply.”

The minister never replied.

We overnight in Los Angeles, and next morning, Thursday, June 8, we hop a flight up to Portland, Oregon, my old stomping grounds. Danavir is the president of ISKCON Portland. He’s arranged four days of programs in the City of Roses. To meet their master, about fifty additional devotees from Seattle, Vancouver, and San Francisco have converged on the Portland temple, a 1920s four-bedroom wooden house at 2325 East Burnside Street. They pack the full-length covered porch in front of the two-story building, spilling onto the sidewalk and street, whooping and chanting as Prabhupada arrives. En route from the airport, Prabhupada had remarked on the forests of tall fir trees lining streets and blanketing Portland’s hills, referring to them in his arrival address in the crowded temple room:

Now the modern advancement is to be naked. So these senseless, shameless persons who are advancing in their knowledge by becoming naked, they are going to be trees next life. The naked tree is standing naked for many thousands of years. Or animals—they have no shame. In the universities they say that “What is the wrong if I become an animal?” I asked them, “Do you like to be animal?” “Yes.” “Then all right, next time you become animal.” \[laughter\] Krishna is so kind: “You want to be naked? All right, you become naked for ten thousand years and stand up as tree.”

My youngest sister, Jane, lives in Portland, and I show her into Prabhupada’s room to meet the Swami. She recently graduated from Reed College and is singing opera in local productions.

Prabhupada: Oh, your sister, she is very beautiful! Shyamasundar: Yes, and she is also an opera singer.

Prabhupada: Do you know this, our Hare Krishna mantra? You can sing?

Jane: Well, I can try...

She closes her eyes and without hesitation belts out the maha-mantra) her loud, operatic soprano penetrating the house and bringing all activity to a standstill. In the long silence that follows, Prabhupada’s eyes are as wide as saucers and he is visibly moved by Jane’s euphonious rendition.

Later this afternoon Danavir has Prabhupada scheduled to speak at Portland State College. PSC educates 10,000 students a year on its urban campus, which spreads through much of downtown Portland. Seven years ago Yamuna and I lived in the “Beanstalk,” an old gothic house in Goose Hollow a few blocks from Portland State, so it’s like going home as I motor with Prabhupada west on Burnside, across Burnside Bridge over the Willamette River, and head south on SW Park Avenue. We’re in the temple van, a battered Ford Econoline with only two seats—very uncomfortable. Prabhupada’s seat is tied down with ropes so it won’t swivel, but he looks like an eager teenager up there, bouncing in his seat and checking out the action. Twenty devotees are crammed into the back.

We arrive a little early, so while devotees set up the stage in Lincoln Hall, Prabhupada clears me to look for an old friend who attends classes here and invite him to the show. I run to the Registrar’s office and am informed that Stewart L. is this minute in an anatomy class in a nearby building. Stewart and I were close friends at Reed, had tripped on LSD together, and had spent a hundred hours discussing philosophy—he was a spiritual soulmate. I walked into the classroom with a shaved head, wearing a dhoti— and anatomical instruction ground to a halt as every face in the room turned. Stewart saw me and hustled me out into the hall. I was happy to see him, and rushed to tell him about Prabhupada and Krishna, but he was flustered, embarrassed, and quickly told me he was no longer interested in matters of the spirit—he was going to become a doctor and help the suffering. “But can you come down to the Swami’s lecture after class?” “No, I’ll be too busy. Sorry.”

Prabhupada gave a dynamite lecture to about a hundred students, who were seated in lotus position on the floor of the hall, and afterward he asked me, “What about your friend?” “Prabhupada, he was my friend in another life.”

I phone my folks in Salem, Oregon, about fifty miles south of Portland, and ask them if I can borrow one of their cars while Prabhupada is in town. That evening they drive up in two cars to greet the Swami and their wayward son. Prabhupada had met my mom in 1967 at my wedding in Haight-Ashbury, but he’s meeting my dad for the first time. Both my parents had also come to the London temple in Bury Place in 1970 during a stopover to see the pyramids in Egypt, and they’d spent a day at George’s Friar Park estate in Oxfordshire. We corresponded every few months. About a year ago my mother had mailed me a clipping from the “Letters to the Editor” section of the Oregonian, the state’s largest newspaper, dated November 12,1970:

KIND “KOOKS”

To the Editor: Concerning the letter of Lucille Blowers denouncing the “kooks” in yellow robes on the street corner, may I assure her that these people are harmless and that there is no need for her to be afraid. They are simply missionaries of the Hare Krishna Movement, an East Indian religion that began in the United States to help the young people. If their chanting and dancing disturbs her, please ask her to try and be tolerant—they are doing a great deal of good in every city in which they are located.

In order to be a follower of this movement, one must adhere to 26 strict rules. Among them are: To be kind to everyone, to be charitable, mild, clean, peaceful, to have no interest in material gain, etc. The devotees do not practice sex outside of marriage, and they use no drugs, coffee, tea, meat, tobacco or alcohol.

I do not know where their Portland “temple” is located— on E. Burnside, I believe. But I do know that at any time Mrs. Blowers would care to call on them, they would welcome her and offer her free food as is done daily to everyone. I have visited their places in San Francisco and in London and have found packed houses. The young people were praying on their knees or were dancing and chanting, accompanied by various instruments.

Perhaps their way of attracting attention on street corners is different than that of the Salvation Army, but has this upset lady watched a Chinese, a Buddhist, or even some of our Christian ceremonies recently?

-PAULINE SPEERSTRA

Bless her heart, my mom was slowly getting it.

I find my mom and dad chairs, and they spend a tension-filled but cordial hour with the Swami, trying to understand just what it is their son is doing. Prabhupada treats them like exalted personalities, parents of a Vaishnava, and I observe their growing conviction that, yes, our son has found a truly wise and special teacher. They depart back to Salem, and my dad has left us his brand new 1972 V-8 Chevrolet Impala. Now we have some class wheels.

Prabhupada: Your father is very respectable gentleman, mother is nice, and sister is very beautiful, artist.

Shyamasundar: They are trying to understand this whole Krishna thing—what I am doing.

Prabhupada: \[laughs\] Yes. They are not very happy that you are in this movement, not in the family, naturally. Your father is a rich man, lawyer, he has got good estate, and you are only son. But son does not like to stay with father. They think, “Our lost child.” \[laughter\] They cannot appreciate that “My child is lost for better purposes.” That is the case.

Early the next morning, Friday, June 9, we walk in twenty-seven-acre Laurelhurst Park, once voted “most beautiful park on the West Coast,” just half a mile east on Burnside from the temple. I say, “That tree is a western red cedar, Prabhupada. The Indians made fine clothes from the bark. Those are Douglas firs, and these are western hemlocks. And this is a white fir, also called grand fir.”

As we stroll through the park, Prabhupada is surprised I know the names of all the trees. “This is a ponderosa pine. It grows better in dry climates like eastern Oregon. These are azaleas, wild flowers native to the coast.” He stops to gaze at Rhododendron Hill, acres of big pink flowers in full bloom, and says, “Yes, I have seen these blossoms in Kashmir. The entire mountains are blooming.”

“Oh, you have been to Kashmir?”

“Yes, I went there for business in 1925.”

That Prabhupada—always full of surprises. I live to surprise him and be surprised by him.

Danavir: As we circled the lake I asked him if this is tvhat Vrindaban looks like, and Srila Prabhupada says, “No, the lakes in Vrindaban are not so dirty like this.”

After the walk and some breakfast, we leave for Salem in my dad’s car—west on Burnside, hang a left onto the 1-5. Danavir has arranged for Prabhupada to be interviewed on a popular call-in program at KSLM radio station at eleven. I grew up listening to KSLM; in the ’40s and ’50s it was Salem’s only radio station. We’d tune to 1390 on the dial for Salem Senators minor league baseball games, The Lone Ranger, Superman, and (my favorite) Lamont Cranston as The Shadow: “Who knows what evil lurks in the hearts of men? The Shadow knows!”—always followed by a maniacal laugh. KSLM had a small studio atop Kingswood Heights in West Salem. My parents came over—dad taking time from his busy law practice—to stand outside the glass and peer in at their son, his friends, and the Swami. They probably thought this the height of local fame—to broadcast live on KSLM radio. Prabhupada delivers a concise description of Krishna consciousness, and then it was time for the phone calls. Most listeners call in with questions about Christianity, like, How does Jesus fit with Krishna? Prabhupada replies:

Jesus is a favorite son of Krishna—you may call Him also Jehovah—just like we are all His sons and daughters. So far I know, there is not much information about God the Father in this, your Bible. But we are having full information, who is God, what are His bodily features, what is His address, what is His mother’s name, His activities, how you can associate with Him, how to go there. Like you, we are interested only in love of God, or Krishna.

Prabhupada often reviewed his public performances after an event with his devotees gathered around him, like a prize fighter and his coaches after a championship bout. On the one-hour drive back to Portland, Danavir said, “I liked the part where you said, ‘Christ died so you may not sin again, and still you are sinning’—and the guy was silent, he couldn’t respond.”

“Oh, you liked that part?”

Then from Pushta Krishna in the back seat: “And what about when you told that lady, ‘You say you love Jesus, then why you are disobeying? Jesus says thou shalt not kill. And you are killing and eating every day.’” In the silences, I ponder the miraculous appearance of my spiritual master in this very small town of my birth, where I dreamed from childhood to find and follow such a hero—and here he is, twenty years later, speaking to hundreds of my neighbors, then hurtling up the freeway at my side.

After a late lunch and a short rest, we hit the road again, back down the 1-5 for 120 miles, past Salem to Eugene, a town largely occupied by University of Oregon students. Danavir has arranged an evening program at the Unitarian Church on 13th Avenue, just a few blocks south of the 1-5. He has also befriended followers of the Ananda Marg, a popular tantric yoga system devised by Prabhat Ranjan Sarkar, who have agreed to vacate their Eugene premises so Prabhupada and his entourage can overnight there. We pull into the Ananda Marg place for a bit of rest before the church event at 7, and Prabhupada is visibly upset by the images of Sarkar, OM signs, and the impersonal, abstract feel of the ashram. He says, “Let us go out from here.”

More than three hundred students and hippies jam into the Unitarian church, and Prabhupada gives a lecture perfectly pitched to young seekers. Everyone joins in the kirtan with wild abandon. The question-and-answer period lasts more than an hour:

Hippie: During the lecture there was a baby crying and you asked that the baby be taken out. Why did you do that? Was it because the baby would disturb you?

Prabhupada: No, because it would disturb you.

Christian: You mean Christ could be right and you could be right at the same time?

Prabhupada: Why not?

By the time prasad was served and we could get out of there, it was nearly midnight. Prabhupada didn’t want to go back to the Ananda Marg ashram—“Let us return to Portland.” It was late, and after a while Prabhupada seemed to doze off in the passenger seat, so I punched that V-8 on the empty freeway running straight as an arrow through the Willamette Valley, making the normal two-hour trip in little more than an hour.

Next morning, Saturday, June 10, Prabhupada called me into his room. We discussed last night’s program, and then he said, “But one thing, next time we should not go so fast. I was watching. You were going 110.” Busted. (Later, I thought probably this is the fastest Prabhupada has ever traveled in a car, and I knew he kind of liked it...)

Danavir came in and told us that Prabhupada was scheduled for two TV interview shows today, one in the afternoon, the other this evening.

Danavir: Now it is time to go to one afternoon TV show; however, Srila Prabhupada feels fatigued due to taking part in so many preaching programs. “A man must have some rest” he states, “Shyamasundar should not have made so many engagements”

I’m usually the bad guy in these situations, and was much relieved when Prabhupada sent Pushta Krishna in his stead. At first

I thought I’d finally found the limit to Prabhupada’s transcendental endurance, but I soon realized he was more interested in training his troops than appearing himself. The afternoon free, I asked Prabhupada if I could visit another old friend from Reed, whose current address I had gotten from Stewart at Portland State College.

I drove around the southwest outskirts of Portland in my dad’s car, got lost several times, and finally pulled into a muddy yard filled with junked cars surrounding a sagging old house, honking my horn and yelling for Dave to call off his mangy barking dog. I heard indistinct grunts, and looked out the window to see Dave’s unmistakable motorcycle boots and black jeans emerging below a blocked-up, broken down pickup truck. The reunion was awkward. Dave seemed nervous, like, withdrawal nervous, didn’t want to hear anything about any Swami, said his wheels broke down, he’s coming down, and could I rush him to Owl Drugs so he can get some codeine cough syrup to take the edge off?

Today was the Rose Festival, Portland’s biggest annual municipal event, held on the second Saturday of every June. Danavir and dozens of devotees went downtown to do sankirtan among the thousands of celebrants. The police arrested all of the devotees, loaded them into vans and hauled them to jail. Bail was set at $50 each. I was out visiting Dave while this was happening, but I heard the story when I returned to the temple—how Prabhupada had dug into his little white bag and sent $50 cash downtown for Danavir’s bail. At least his local leader was free.

That evening we drove with Prabhupada and a humbled Danavir to KOIN-TV’s skyscraper studios on SW Columbia Street, downtown. The show’s liaison lady told us, “No, we don’t see any Bhaktivedanta Swami on the list for tonight’s program. Sorry.” Sitting in the lounge we were a downcast group, having dragged Prabhupada all this way only to be rebuffed. Then a young man walked up to us and said he was the disc jockey for a live radio show coming up soon. Can he interview the Swami? Prabhupada agreed and climbed some stairs to a tiny studio.

DJ: Why have you come to Portland?

Prabhupada: To teach you to love God.

DJ: Does that mean you are the Messiah?

Prabhupada: Yes.

Sunday, June 11 is Prabhupada s last day in Portland, and the temple house is packed to the rafters for a fire ceremony to initiate about twenty new devotees, followed by the weekly Love Feast. \[Three months later, a boy named Steve Jobs would enter Reed College, and for the next two years he would come to the Burnside temple for the free Sunday food. Jobs declared in his famous 2005 Stanford commencement address: “And I would walk the seven miles across town every Sunday night to get one good meal a week at the Hare Krishna temple. I loved it. And much of what I stumbled into by following my curiosity and intuition turned out to be priceless later on.” I’d like to think that Srila Prabhupada had something to do with Steve Jobs’ seven-month journey to India in 1974 to look for miracles and inspiration, returning to America to start Apple and revolutionize the world.\]

On the plane back to Los Angeles that night, I meditate once again on the intensity of living so close to Srila Prabhupada: his personal servants have to be hyperalert, ready for anything. Prabhupada never sleeps, and the constant pressure to perform under fluid conditions is enough to break most men. Offsetting this is the tangible presence of magic, the sanctity of action in Prabhupada’s service: whatever I do, nothing can go wrong. Every little task performed for him turns out perfect somehow—just focus on the job at hand, move this pen to that paper, this idea to that receiver, and let it flow. Let Krishna do the rest. Just see how easy it all is.

Over the previous ten days, our seventy-six-year-old Srila Prabhupada has traveled to seven cities in two countries and given twenty-one lectures. He’s appeared on TV and radio programs, initiated dozens of new devotees, touched the hearts of thousands in very deep ways. How fast all this is going. How intense and wonderful it all is: chasing rhinos with the Swami!

Prabhupada is glad to be back in the L.A. routine in his comfy, sunlit rooms, his garden, and with the gorgeous Deities and constant sounds of Their worship downstairs as his generals pop in and out of his room with reports. I check with the typists. They’re almost caught up with the philosophy book. Prabhupada wants to finish it quickly. So first morning back, Karandhar, Swarup Damodar, and others, taking up where we left off, gather in Prabhupada’s room to discuss Sigmund Freud (1856-1934).

Shyamasundar: Freud’s philosophy is that people have neuroses or disorders of their total personality, that there’s conflict, anxiety, frustration, and that all of these have a sexual origin. The sex drive is the basis of our existence.

Prabhupada: That we also say. As soon as there is an embodied living being, he must have sex impulse. But Freud is encouraging sex, and our process is to stop. That is the difference. Therefore our solution is to control sex appetite from brahmachari stage, and if sex drive remains, then you get married. Animal impulses are there—what is the use of so much philosophizing?

Shyamasundar: So what is the purpose of discussing Freud?

Prabhupada: Just to prove that he is a big rascal. He may be a very big man amongst the other smaller rascals. What improvement has he made with his philosophy in the Western countries? He has degraded them more, putting the attention on sex. What is his solution?

Shyamasundar: Freud’s idea is that many of our unconscious conflicts have their origin in childhood traumatic experiences. He says the cure for many of our present conflicts is to recall these painful experiences, to analyze and try to correct them. This is called psychoanalysis. By recognizing these emotions, then the original shock can be released through remembering, confessing.

Prabhupada: What is the guarantee that he will not get another shock? He is getting shock after shock. So our program is total cure: no more shock of any kind. His treatment is useless because as soon as you solve that problem, another problem is there immediately. So long as you are under condition by the material nature, shock after shock will come. If one is Krishna conscious, no more shock.... In this sexual pleasure society, there is some pleasure, but what is the value of that pleasure? That is compared with one drop of water in the desert. You are seeking after unlimited pleasure. What this will pacify you? Therefore nobody is satisfied. He is having sex in different ways. Now these young girls are almost naked. And the man will take advantage, that “Milk is available on the market. What is the use of keeping a cow?” So they will decline to keep a cow because milk is so cheap. So this is social desertion. They are becoming hippies. What benefit is there? He has degraded the whole nation.

For two days the Freud session rages on as morning sun lights his room. Devotees slip in and out, seating themselves in a semicircle on the floor before him as Prabhupada systematically dismantles Freud’s ideas, one by one.

Shyamasundar: Another area of Freud’s investigation was the problem of anxiety. He says that the source of anxiety is the id, or the primitive instincts, which are always forcing us to do this and do that. In other words, deep desires. These impulses threaten to overpower the rational or moral self. So there is always a tension or anxiety produced.

Prabhupada: You cannot be free from anxiety in your conditioned life. Frustration must be there because you desire something which is not permanent. Suppose that I wish to live forever, but if I have accepted this material body, there is no question of living forever. So I am always anxious when death should come. So therefore the conclusion is that anxiety is due to our acceptance of something which does not exist. This is the right definition of anxiety.

Shyamasundar: Freud says that the ego develops strategies of defense against this anxiety, and one of the strategies it develops is repression. Whenever there is some strong animalistic desire, the ego represses that desire in order to preserve itself.

Prabhupada: Just like if you are in a diseased condition and you desire to eat something which is forbidden by the physician. So consciously you have to repress in order to cure, repressing something which is going against my welfare. This is called tapasya, voluntary repression.

Shyamasundar: I heard you say once that we cannot really repress desire but we have to channel it into other objects, control it.

Prabhupada: That is our process. If you have got better engagement, you give up inferior engagement. When you are captivated by seeing the beautiful form of Krishna, naturally you have no more desire to see the beautiful form of a young woman. Sometimes, just like wild tiger, you have got to control by repression. But when tiger is under control, there is no question of repression. You can play with the tiger; he becomes your friend. So repression is not always bad.

There is a huge backlog of letters that need replies. In these letters, devotees increasingly lay their managerial and personal problems onto Prabhupada, and as the number of devotees grows, the sheer volume of problems and complaints grows too. The days are gone when the Swami could personally solve each and every individual’s problems. Now it’s time for his leaders to take up the slack. One devotee complains that his mind is agitated and wonders if things will improve if he changes his occupation. Prabhupada replies: “This restlessness or agitation of the mind cannot be rectified by altering the material circumstances. If we are Krishna Conscious, any type of occupation will suit me and we shall always be satisfied.... Now I am feeling more and more inclined for philosophy, so I want to sit down here in Los Angeles and translate my Srimad-Bhagavatam without much interruption. So I am requesting my good disciples as much as possible to consult the senior disciples in matters of management, philosophy, and personal problems.... You, senior members, can manage everything there very nicely and relieve me of such questions.”

Problems, problems, problems.

Shyamasundar: Do you remember the Sarna family in London, Srila Prabhupada? Mr. Sarna just phoned me. He wanted his two daughters to go to their relatives in Nairobi, but they didn’t want to go, so they ran away to Bombay. They are now staying at our Juhu temple.

Prabhupada: I did not know all these things.

Shyamasundar: It’s not very important. He can send a letter, or... There are so many things like this going on all over the world, Prabhupada, and if you try to deal with all of them...

Prabhupada: So why they have gone to Bombay?

Shyamasundar: They just don’t... They want to remain devotees. They say their father eats meat, he violates all the principles, and forces them to stay away from the temple.

Prabhupada: But how we can advise them not to disobey their father?

Shyamasundar: No. Dhananjaya called me to ask advice, and I told him that if their father wants, then they should go to Nairobi. But he said, “But they don’t want to go. What can I do? I can’t force them to go.” So they ran away. I just found out maybe two days ago that they’re already in Bombay.

Prabhupada: But the father is our life member. If he wants that his daughters should come back, then you should help.

Shyamasundar: So I asked Mr. Sarna to please talk with me. I will relay the message. He said, “No, no, I want to talk to Prabhupada.” I said, “Why can’t you send a wire or letter?” There was a long pause, and the line went “click.” He hung up. Another thing: some devotees want to start a temple at Danny’s ashram in Mexico. But if so many problems are happening as a result of too much expansion, why should we take more risk? Maybe not open any more centers for a while?... There are devotees waiting outside. You want to have a philosophy class?

Prabhupada: Mm-hmm.

Shyamasundar: Oh, OK then. I’ll tell them to wait a few minutes while I try to phone Mr. Sarna in London.

On June 14 and 15 we discuss philosopher and psychologist Karl Gustav Jung (1875-1961).

Shyamasundar: Karl Jung sees that the mind is composed of a balance of conscious and unconscious, like light and dark, and the function of the personality is to integrate the conscious and unconscious functions—something he calls individuation. For instance, if one has a strong sex desire, he may be able to cultivate or channel that into a creative art or a creative value.

Prabhupada: That is our process. Just like sex impulse is natural for everyone in the material world. Hrid-rogam kamam asv apahinoti. To be lusty is a heart disease. But if anyone hears about the pastimes of Krishna and the gopis—through right source—then this hrid-rogam, this lusty desire in the heart, is suppressed, and he will develop devotional service. Krishna is the only purusha, enjoyer. We are prakriti, the enjoyed. When we are enjoyed by Krishna, that is also our enjoyment. Predominator enjoyer and predominated enjoyed—reciprocation of enjoyment. Both predominated and predominator are conscious. Without consciousness there is no life.

Shyamasundar: Jung says that there are two kinds of subconscious state. The first one is the personal unconscious, or those personal items from one’s infantile history, and the collective unconscious, which are archetypal tendencies shared by all humans from the remote past, the ingredients of our ancestors. For example, a circle would represent something whole or complete to all humans.

Prabhupada: Yes. Dharmena hina pashubhih samanah. That is religion. As soon as you come to the platform of human being, there is one similarity: religion. It may be under different name. Even in the aborigines, there is religion. Just like eating principle is there, sleeping principle is there, so similarly, religious principle is there.

Shyamasundar: Actually, this Jung has had a great impact on modern thinkers because he took psychology out of the laboratories and made it more a human science, a personal science that involves unconscious states, mystic states, religious states, not just something analytical and cold. And especially nowadays younger people are very much fond of Karl Jung.

Karandhar: This Karl Jung once drew a picture of what he thought the face of a realized soul might look like ... a person in perfect knowledge. And that picture was printed, and it looks like you, Srila Prabhupada! \[laughter\]

Shyamasundar: Jung says that the purpose of psychology is to come to grips with our unconscious or our shadow personality, to integrate ourselves, and thus to know who I am completely.

Prabhupada: Yes. That is real knowledge. But it requires the assistance of guru to understand our real identity. That can be explained in this way. Just like a soul who is now in sleeping state, he can be integrated by awaking into original consciousness, Krishna consciousness. Just like Chaitanya Mahaprabhu says, jiv jago jiv jago gaurachanda bole / kota nidra jao maya-pishachira kole: “You are living entity, just get up, get up! How long you shall sleep in the lap of illusion?” So this is the process, hari-nama, guru gradually bringing the sleeping man to the real consciousness of life. And that is education.

**\* \* \***

If you were a new, young Krishna devotee in June of 1972 at the New Dwarka temple in Los Angeles—perhaps you’re from Oxnard, San Bernardino, or fresh on the West Coast from Akron, Ohio—seeing Bhaktivedanta Swami enter the temple room at 7 a.m. for the morning program, you’re struck with awe and reverence. Your saintly master lives upstairs in his sanctuary, tended by his chosen leaders and advisors, unapproachable by us newbies. Hours earlier, at 4 a.m. you have risen and fought for a quick shower in your crowded quarters, then you greeted the Deities and sang with the others at mangala-arati. Then you sat in the temple and chanted japa on beads, fighting to stay awake. At 6, you stood outside cheering the Swami as he slid into a car for his morning walk on Venice Beach.

These new devotees—most of them far more qualified for spiritual life than I—could go on for hours alpout a smile they saw on Prabhupada’s face or a single look, one piece of prasad received from his hand, a garland they gave him—even a single blossom they retrieved after he’d stepped on it on his way to the car. And here I am, getting it all! I meditate on this often—how unqualified yet how lucky I am.

Near 7 a.m., if you’re that newbie, you find a place in the temple room, squeezed between a hundred devotees, boys on one side, girls on the other, to await the appearance of your glorious master—and there he is\\ The crowd goes wild. You’re red-faced and nervous, more excited than you’ve ever been as—lucky you!—you slip a garland around his neck as he sits before you on the vyasasan, your Srila Prabahupada, looking deep into your eyes. Yamuna’s heavenly voice fills the room, and the harps and cellos of the London Philharmonic—govindam adi-purusham tam aham bhajami— over and over as devotees, one by one, approach Prabhupada and place rose petals at his feet. Prabhupada, seated before his assembled children, clears his throat and speaks.

Through May and June 1972 Prabhupada spoke from Srimad-Bhagavatam, Second Canto (“The Cosmic Manifestation”). He focused on the third and fourth chapters (“Pure Devotional Service: The Change in Heart” and “The Process of Creation”). With so many new devotees present, Prabhupada kept it simple, developing a powerful consistent theme over the days. If you had to give titles to these lectures you might call them something like:

If You Want to Live Forever

Hogs, Dogs, Camels, and Asses

Doghouse or Igloo—Home Sweet Home

A Thorn-Chewing, Blood-Sucking Civilization

Ear Holes Like Snake Holes

Criminal Enjoyment

Lifting the Burden from Society

Peacock-Plume Eyes

Only a Devotee of a Devotee Can Be a Devotee of Krishna

Steel-Framed Hearts

Spiritual Realization Through Sound Vibration

Searching After Krishna

Simply Negating Is No Good

Fight for Freedom

Make Krishna Your Beloved

And most mornings and nights throughout June the hot debates about Darwin and evolutionary science continued in Prabhupada’s room.

Prabhupada: What is that scientific progress? They cannot create even an ant.

Shyamasundar: Well, by changing the chromosomes and gene structures they’ve mutated bodies. Just like, a few years back there was a drug called Thalidomide, which was given widely to pregnant women. It was supposed to help them, but it changed the cell structure in the womb, and many babies were born without arms and legs.

Prabhupada: That much harm they can do. That’s all. Without arms and legs?

Shyamasundar: Srila Prabhupada, what is “curiosity”? What is that feeling of curiosity?

Prabhupada: Curiosity means inquisitiveness: “What is that? What is that?”

Shyamasundar: Is that present in the spiritual world also?

Prabhupada: Yes, curiosity is there also, even though the answer is already given. But in material world, curiosity may lead into trouble. Just like the snake’s body is very cold. So if your body is very heated and you take the snake to become cold and the snake bites—finished. Material world is like that: taking cooling effect from the body of the snake.

Shyamasundar: There seems to be a natural propensity for man to enjoy putting two things together and watching them, creating something. A satisfaction.

Prabhupada: That is the creative force. So God is creating. He has got huge creative force, multienergies. But we are only imitating. Just like a small child, a baby, he also takes another baby, doll baby. It is like that. I mean it has no value, \[laughter\] It is like that—playing with dolls and taking it seriously. Sometimes small children, they cook. They have got small utensils, \[laughter\]. My sister’s son, when he was young, they’re cooking with a lamp \[laughs\] and a small piece of dough, and I was the guest, \[much laughter\] It is not cooked; simply dipped in butter. And I was to eat. Eh? Hot puri! It is like that. “Mother is cooking, so we should cook.” It is like that. God is creating. Lamp. “Ah, we shall create.” Childish playing. And foolish man like me is eating those puris. \[laughter\] You see? So, these things are enjoyed by rascals and fools. It has no value.

Shyamasundar: But why is there pleasure in creating something? In making...

Prabhupada: Pleasure to you, not to others. This creation will not please anyone else except that rascal. Because you are living being you are active. You get pleasure from your sense activity. So your senses are satisfied that you have created something. By that sense satisfaction you are thinking, “I am creating this.” But it is only a childish shadow-play.

Shyamasundar: Ohhh.

Swarup Damodar: What is the duty of a modern scientist?

Prabhupada: Just like Krishna says, raso ’ham apsu kaunteya: “I am the taste of water.” This is chemistry, is it not? So now this water is composition of hydrogen and oxygen. Now find out hydrogen, oxygen, how the taste can be Krishna. Make this research. And Krishna says that “I am the shining of the moon and the sun.” Now you can find out by physical experiment. This shining, there is a source. Now, the sun is the source. Now try to find out wherefrom the sun came, who created the sun. Similarly, the electric energy is coming from the powerhouse. So you have to find out wherefrom these energies are coming. I have got some stock of energy by which I can lift this object, is it not? So find out wherefrom this energy is coming, the lifting and moving of everything. That is real research. Not to propose some nonsense things: “In future we shall do this and that.”

Swarup Damodar: Scientists believe that they are improving the world, that their discoveries will make better houses, better living conditions, nicer things. Is it possible to be a scientist and also be in Krishna consciousness?

Prabhupada: Yes. If the scientists turn to Krishna consciousness then the face of the world will be changed. Science means to know everything about something. If we say something by hearing from you, that is secondary knowledge. But if you say with sound footing in scientific language that the original source of everything is Krishna: aham sarvasya prabhavo mattah sarvam pravartate, “Everything emanates from Me,” that will be nicely followed by others. Yad yad acharati shresthas. The leading personalities, if they do something, others will follow. That we want. You become a big scientist, at the same time devotee. Then people will follow.

Thoudam Damodar Singh, now Swarup Damodar das, is from Manipur, India. He is a PhD candidate in chemistry at UC Irvine. Mild-mannered almost to a fault, he gradually asserts his voice into these discussions, and Prabhupada asks him to come on the daily morning walks, urging him to contest the theories of his fellow scientists. \[One year from now, Swarup Damodar will become Bhaktiswarup Damodar Swami and found The Bhaktivedanta Institute. He will dedicate the rest of his life to proving that life comes from life in science forums throughout the world.\]

Typed manuscripts for the philosophy book were piling up, and I spent my days editing them. I was also busy making travel arrangements for the upcoming months and typing Prabhupada’s many letters, so I didn’t go on most of the morning walks, giving others a chance to go. Besides, as Prabhupada often said, familiarity breeds contempt, and love is stronger in separation. Our energies seemed to synch better with a little space or a time out now and then: a walk to the drugstore for aspirin or to the 7-Eleven to buy a Hershey bar with almonds. When I’d get back, even just an hour later, I’d be so happy to see him again. I would learn he had asked for me—“Where is Shyamasundar?”—and he’d be happy to see me too.

В. F. Skinner was a contemporary philosopher-psychologist from the rats-in-a-maze behaviorist school. He was to be the very last thinker we’d discuss for the philosophy book. This one should be fun.

Shyamasundar: Skinner has one main idea—to be able to control human behavior. He says you can train a man to accept certain values by reinforcing—rewarding them when they are right and punishing them when they are wrong. His idea is taken from his work with rats and pigeons.

Prabhupada: His authority is rats and pigeons. Our authority is Vedavyasa. \[laughter\] That is the difference between the West and East.

Shyamasundar: Skinner himself believes in Judeo-Christian ethics combined with a scientific tradition. He says we can program society to be good to your neighbor, to love one another, to be honest, upright, like that.

Prabhupada: If basically there is nothing but dogs, hogs, camels, and asses, then suppose dog has given some program and the camel says, “No. This program is better than this one.” And the ass comes, he introduces another program, “This program is better than this program.” So either of these programs, because they are made by dogs, hogs, asses, and camels—they cannot be perfect. Take a program from a real human being. Then it is perfect.

Shyamasundar: He says that we can design a culture that will survive due to its being moral, upright, honest, hard-working—all typical American behavior.

Karandhar: What about the standard? Someone has to be God in order to set the standard.

Shyamasundar: He says there is a curious similarity between himself and God, adding, however, that “Perhaps I must yield to God in point of seniority.” He wants to play God, design the culture.

Prabhupada: What is his conception of God?

Shyamasundar: Senior qualities.

Prabhupada: That’s right. \[laughter\] We accept that. Nityo nityanam. \[laughter\] We accept that.

Karandhar: No, no. I’ll tell you what he says about God. He says that the belief in God arose due to man’s inability to understand his world, but that man no longer needs such a fiction. We have the capacity to take matters into our own hands. We don’t have to believe in some controller far away whom we have no control over.

Prabhupada: Then one has to believe him? But that you cannot do. You cannot take the question of birth, death, old age in your hands. If God can give you better life, where there is no death, there is no old age, there is no disease, why not accept it?

Shyamasundar: This is their dilemma now—that they cannot find any standard of behavior. Formerly, people’s behavior was motivated by deprivation. They wanted more economic gain because there was hunger. But now we have everything, so no one wants to work anymore. So now there is nothing that satisfies people enough to make them behave.

Prabhupada: Therefore the Vedanta gives for him: athato brahma-jijnasa. Now we have got enough to eat, enough to enjoy. Now we must inquire about Brahman, or the Supreme. We are giving the ultimate benefit.

Shyamasundar: So we have to condition people that every time they press our Hare Krishna button, they get some pleasure?

Prabhupada: Yes.

Shyamasundar: Skinner says that he can train anyone to become any type of person he wants, regardless of his talents or nature, his tendencies or abilities, by a system of rewards and punishments.

Prabhupada: Yes, anything. Just like there is a story, the Tarzan. I have seen in the cinema. He was living in the society of monkeys, and he learned how to jump from one tree to another, \[laughter\]

Shyamasundar: What do you think of this statement from Skinner: “Sun streams in my room. My hi-fi is midway through the first act of Tristan and Isolde. A very pleasant environment. A man would be a fool not to enjoy himself in it. In a moment I will work on a manuscript which may help mankind. So my life is not only pleasant; it is earned or deserved. And yet, yet, I am unhappy”?

Prabhupada: In that sense he is a truthful man.

Shyamasundar: He wants to ... He is trying to understand.

Prabhupada: He cannot. That is not the way of understanding. The Vedic way is that you first approach a guru. He cannot personally search for the truth. That is not possible.

Shyamasundar: That about wraps up the philosophy book, Srila Prabhupada. We have discussed thirty-nine Western philosophers, from Plato to B. F. Skinner, and you have defeated them all.

In a way, I’m a little sad to be ending these philosophy discussions, but also relieved that ten months of such intense mental workouts have come to an end.

On the morning of June 29 we fly south from LAX to San Diego for $17 each on Pacific Southwest Airlines, for a lightning-quick visit—three days—to the ISKCON temple at 3300 Third Avenue, half a mile north of downtown, in Southern California’s most beautiful city. What a paradise! Bhakta das drives us around San Diego and La Jolla to show Prabhupada where the sankirtan parties are selling books. He points out the huge harbor, home to the Pacific Fleet; he points to parks, flowers everywhere, and people in casual mode on their way to the beach. Everybody is havin’ fun in the warm California sun.

The temple is a three-story 1920s vintage wooden house, like the Portland house, with mature palms in the yard instead of firs. The building is brightly painted in yellow, orange, and vivid purple. As we start up the front steps, Prabhupada notices the maha-mantra painted on the risers, and stops. He won’t tread on the holy names, and tells Bhakta das to remove them. He uses the side entrance.

Just three blocks east of the temple is Balboa Park, a twelvehundred-acre urban Shangri-La with its vast lawns and gardens, palm trees, eucalyptus and oak groves, bridle trails, museums, libraries, a zoo, a golf course, and the US Naval hospital. Prabhupada takes long morning walks there, returning to the temple to enjoy regional fruits, like peaches, kiwis, and avocados.

On Saturday, July 1, Prabhupada speaks at a seminar on Hinduism at the beautiful campus of the University of San Diego, a Catholic school of higher learning. He blows our minds when he says to all these big scholars and priests, “You are what you eat. In this age, many people are eating hogs.” On Sunday afternoon, Prabhupada lectures in the Starlight Bowl in Balboa Park. It’s hard to compete with the beach on the July 4 weekend, so only a couple of hundred people show up for the “Hare Krishna Festival” in this huge outdoor amphitheater—leaving nearly four thousand empty seats—but as usual, Prabhupada speaks as if to millions.

That evening, we return to L.A. for a day to pack and get ready for our trip to Europe. Prabhupada will enjoy London Ratha-yatra for the first time. After that he will probably travel to temples around the UK and then go on to Paris. We leave L.A. for New York on the evening of July 3 in order to spend a day at the New York temple before flying to London on the night of the 4th.

July 4, 1972, was an action-packed day for Srila Prabhupada at the Henry Street temple in Brooklyn. First, he gave a morning Srimad-Bhagavatam class and introduced to his East-Coast disciples the interactive chanting of the Sanskrit verses. “I will say first, then you repeat.” Prabhupada had encouraged group practice of Sanskrit pronunciation in every ISKCON temple since his visit to Australia in April. “If you go to sell these books and if some customer says, ‘You pronounce it,’ then what you will do? So therefore it is necessary now, you have got nice books, each and every shloka, verse, should be pronounced.”

Prabhupada then initiated twenty-seven new devotees, gave second initiation to twenty-five more, and afterward sat in his room talking with children and devotees all day as fireworks popped outside in the patriotic borough of Brooklyn. Devotees desperate for Prabhupada’s brief association crammed into his room, asking questions on everything from “How do we teach our children?” to “What would you like for lunch today?” Prabhupada guided them on a hundred topics, from how to conduct media interviews to the recipe for handmade soap. Bob Cohen was there, asking more perfect questions and receiving more perfect answers. My friend Ritchie Slavin was there too-last seen in Vrindaban - and I was glad he’d returned safely to the States from his austere years in India. Some old veterans were also there— Kirtanananda, Bali Mardan, Vishnujan Swami. Prabhupada gave painting instructions to Jadurani, advised his BTG editors to present articles in a way that “people will understand it is reality, not sentiment or fiction.” A theology professor from Columbia arrived at 5 to discuss philosophy. Prabhupada talked and talked, hesitant to leave, talking over his shoulder even as he got into the car for Kennedy airport to catch the red-eye to London.

**\* \* \***

On the morning of July 5, 1972, we fly into Heathrow, then drive through London. As we turn right off Great Russell Street onto Bury Place, the short street is jammed with a hundred wildly singing devotees. The Hare Krishna mantra booms off the black brick buildings of Bloomsbury. “Jaya Gurudeva!” is spelled out in flowers across the temple facade, and rose petals shower from windows above as Prabhupada, weighted by garlands of French gardenias, enters the building to a tumultuous reception. Devotees and guests fill the place, packing rooms, halls and stairs, shouting “Prabhupada!” so loud it hurts your ears. Prabhupada is lit up like a light bulb as he enters the packed temple room and bows before his favorite Deities, Radha-Londonishwara. Many old friends are here: Dhananjaya, Tribhuvanath, Jai Hari, Ranchor, Kulashekar, Devadatta, Draupadi—ahh, and there’s Rebatinandan! I haven’t seen him since India—and Ksirodaksayee, also back from India. And there’s Hamsaduta and Himavati from Germany, and the crew from France—Mondakini, Suryadas, Yogeswara—and Bhagavan, from Detroit—and lots of new bright, apple-cheeked faces.

Rama dasi: When he stepped out of the car, he was glowing in a halo of bright light, and the devotees just took off, they just left the ground, and there was such incredible joy on everyone’s faces and such a feeling of safety and security being around Prabhupada.

Prabhupada is elated to sit on his vyasasan and greet his London family on this, his third visit to the UK. From its earliest roots in 1968, Prabhupada has invested great attention to the founding and development of the London mission. Now, four years later, he’s relishing the mature fruit of his endeavors. Senior American disciples like Mukunda and Dayananda are gone; now, young British devotees are in charge. After a short welcoming address, Prabhupada retires to his familiar room upstairs and is almost immediately joined by Sumati Morarjee, the Bombay owner of Scindia Steamship Lines who sponsored the Swami’s 1965 journey to America. She happens to be in London on business. Her huge residence in India is almost adjacent to our new property at Juhu, so Prabhupada solicits her help in settling our tangled Bombay affairs. After some small talk on how to prepare milk with ginger and saffron, and after Swamiji brags a bit about how big his movement has become, they get right down to business.

Sumati Morarjee: Yes, just now I have become life member.

Prabhupada: She is with me, life after life, \[laughter\] She is lifeafter-life member.

Sumati Morarjee: No, no, but, I’ll talk to this, ah, Giriraj, in Bombay, about becoming the patron myself.

Prabhupada: Let us form a trust, and you become president of the trust, \[laughter\] You become the president of the trust and do the needful. So, whether you agree to this or not?

Sumati Morarjee: Yes, yes.

Prabhupada: Yes. Immediately, make her the president of the trust, or Giriraj. Giriraj is also very nice boy. You liked him?

Sumati Morarjee: He’s a good man. The first time I called him, he came. I said, no, you I don’t want. Where is that Shyamasundar? \[laughter\]

Prabhupada: Shyamasundar, immediately, you write one letter to Giriraj that, uh, I suggest some names for becoming trustee, or Mataji, you can suggest some of the life members you know. In Bombay we can always go to you: “Mataji, we have no more money, please give me some.” \[laughter\]

They are old friends; the conversation is intimate, almost flirtatious, as she agrees to use Scindia ships to move ISKCON goods around the world: cars from the US to India, incense punks from Hong Kong to Los Angeles. Prabhupada gives her his garland, his one and only copy of the new BTG in Gujarati, and several to-go bags of prasad.

Sumati Morarjee: I am going now because you are tired and oldly. But I’m going to preserve this garland, give it to my daughter. She is very fond of roses.

Prabhupada: How old is she now?

Sumati Morarjee: She’s ten. Today she gave a Manipuri dance recital in our office and became Radha. The part when Radha is getting angry with Krishna.

After Sumati Morarjee left, Dhananjaya asked Prabhupada if he knew how old she was.

Prabhupada: Maybe over sixty.

Dhananjaya: She’s very much alive, so much alive.

Prabhupada: Her father was one of the richest men in Bombay, and her husband, her father-in-law also, one of the richest men. And she’s so intelligent. She is boss of that Scindia Steam Navigation Company, very big company. It is a semi-government company, and she’s the managing director. All the big, big officers are respecting her as mother, carrying out her order. And she’s a great devotee of Krishna. But she does not live with her husband \[laughter\], They live in separate buildings. That means she looks after one department of business, and he looks after another department. That child is adopted. She has no child of her own.

First chance I get, I go upstairs to the office and dial Henley 6330. I can almost hear the phone jangling through the cavernous rooms at Friar Park. Pattie answers on about the twelfth ring. She has George call me back, and he’s happy to hear we’re in town and asks, “Can you come out tomorrow, noonish?”

That night, devotees gathered in Prabhupada’s room, and the hours-long jolly conversation ranged over many topics, starting with milk again.

Rebatinandan: Ginger powder and a little bit of saffron and sugar. Heat it up for a while, and then after a little while mix thoroughly and serve like that. It’s good for digestion.

Prabhupada: You just give recipe to Nanda Kumar, and he will make it like this for me. Where is Nanda Kumar? Sleeping?

Shyamasundar: He’s very tired. Last night, on the plane from New York, that Charlie Chaplin film kept us up all night, \[laughter\] Prabhupada: Hm. That was nice, \[laughter\] He is really funny man. \[laughs\] All his comic play has got some originality, that is the beauty. How he invented! \[laughs\] As sober man, he will not pay. But as drunkard, “I will pay. You are my lifelong friend. Whatever you want, you take.” \[laughs\] So these characters he’s painting, it’s very good intelligence. And he saved a man when he was going to commit suicide. He stopped him. Accidentally he stopped, \[laughs\] Not willingly.

Shyamasundar: Yes. He tried to pull him up from the water, but he fell in himself.

Prabhupada: It is very nice, funny man with intelligence. And before him there was another, Mr. Max Linder. He was also very funny Englishman. In our childhood we used to enjoy their play, Max Linder and Charlie Chaplin ... Max Linder, I remember, he was sitting in a park in that English dress coat, with coattails. So the tail was hanging, so some naughty boys, they nailed his coat to the park bench, you see? \[laughs\] So when he got up, the whole tail was gone, you see? But he could not understand. He went to the ball dance. So he’s dancing, so everyone’s seeing his coat, no tails, laughing at him. So he thought, “Oh, what is the matter?” He went to the mirror and he saw, “Oh, my tail is lost.” \[laughs\] Then he came again in the ball dance and he was pushing everyone, just to show. And everyone was asking, “What is this?” “Oh, you do not know? This is latest fashion.” Then all of them cut the tails. I think he had taken the idea from a Sanskrit story.
