One monkey, he lost his tail, and he began to advertise, “This is the latest fashion.” So that ball dancing without tail, that was, I remember, it is very enjoyable.

When Prabhupada laughed like that, he got on an unstoppable roll; when his eyes shut tight with hilarity and mouth pulled so wide you thought his face would split, he appeared like a ten-year-old child surrendered to happiness.

Rebatinandan Swami has been leading a sankirtan party around many cities in the UK, selling books on the streets, and Prabhupada asks Dhananjaya, now London temple president, how much they’ve been collecting.

Dhananjaya: They’re averaging sixty to seventy pounds a day.

Prabhupada: And how much you are collecting in London?

Dhananjaya: On Oxford Street we’re collecting about forty, fifty pounds a day, and on traveling sankirtan, sixty, seventy pounds, because they’re doing it for more hours. So altogether we’re collecting over a hundred pounds a day. That’s about 250 dollars.

To Bhagavan das, his GBC appointee freshly arrived from Detroit, Prabhupada says:

“So, at least two first-class cars must go from USA to India.” Bhagavan: Cadillacs?

Prabhupada: Any car. \[laughter\] If the American guru has no car, he’s walking on the street—that is disgrace for you, is it not? \[laughs\] And they criticize, “Oh, what kind of guru you are, you are walking on the street? You are American guru?” No. You write on the car, “The Spiritual Master of USA.” \[laughter\] And people will see, “Oh, here is the spiritual...” \[laughs\]

At about 10:30 p.m. the devotees left the room to sleep, but I remained behind and we talked alone for another hour while Prabhupada slowly sipped his milk, weighing the merits of milk from America, England, and New Zealand. Prabhupada composed a letter to Giriraj regarding Sumati Morarjee. We talked about Indian building materials, British history in India, and the differences between America and Britain. Prabhupada said, “I have studied Englishmen. They consider America is still uncivilized. They say like that. In America, general life is becoming wretched.” We discussed whether cars or vans were more suitable for India. Prabhupada suggested we use cars to pull trailers. At last, the evening drew to an end. I said, “I talked with George today, and he wants me to come out to his place tomorrow. Is that all right?”

“You are George’s best friend. Just work sincerely and everything will be supplied by Krishna. Now take rest.”

Shortly after noon on July 6,I pull the borrowed Vauxhall to a halt before Friar Park’s ornate iron gates, and get out and ring the bell over and over. Finally, a figure emerges from the coach house and lumbers toward the gate. “Hare Krishna! Harry, it’s me!”

“Shyamasuda? Come to see George then, have ya?”

Harold is George’s elder brother from Liverpool—and with his bushy black eyebrows and big teeth he looks a lot like their dad, Harold senior. He opens the gate with a flourish and I drive in. “Life been good to you, Harry?”

“Yeah, wull, here I am, guardin’ th’ gates o’ paradise. Can’t complain.”

When I’d left George at the end of September 1970 to join Srila Prabhupada in India, I was deeply worried. By leaving I felt I was letting George down. He would be on his own, without his close spiritual friend around. Prabhupada had told me, “Regarding George Harrison, I think you have sufficiently helped him,” but my allegiance was torn. OK, Mukunda is there when George needs a Krishna friend, so I guess it’s time to cut him loose. Let’s see how this works in separation. My thoughts dwelled on George constantly, and I prayed to Krishna that He would sustain and advance him in Krishna consciousness while I was away. I wrote George letters almost every week from India or wherever we were, and he assured me when we’d met last summer in New York City that those letters had really helped him remember Prabhupada and Krishna.

Meanwhile, George’s career had flourished. His All Things Must Pass album and the single, “My Sweet Lord,” were #1 hits for several months in 1971. George’s songs had alerted the entire world to Krishna. The Bangla Desh concert was a huge success, and the triple album that came from it—including “My Sweet Lord”—had been a chart-buster in early 1972. George’s personal ambitions had now been realized, and his spiritual urges were recognized by the world. He was a superstar in his own right. He’d proven himself, he had Krishna, he had deep beliefs, a discipline, a spiritual practice— and he had Friar Park, his castle retreat. What kind of George would I find here today?

I coax the little car up the long drive to the main house, heart in my throat—I always have jitters just before seeing George—and am glad to see the OM flag flying. I stop next to a snazzy BMW coupe parked by the front door. A light summer drizzle wets the pavement.

Here comes this silent golf cart tooling across the lawn from the rhododendron dell... it’s George! Hair shorter, mustache but no beard, still skinny as a rail, handsome and lit with energy—the same magnetic toothy grin, that special steady gaze and mischief in his eyes. The bear hug, the how-ya doin’-awkward moments—and yes, he’s calmer now and more relaxed, happy to see me, and my trepidations melt away.

“What’s it been, a year now since we met in New York?” Small talk, a guffaw at some joke he’s made, standing there in mud-spattered boots and old jeans.

“Quite the country squire these days, huh, George?”

“But I ’ve still got me neck beads ...”

I point to the golf cart: “What’s this about a car accident? I saw something in the newspaper in India.”

“Yeah, last February on the M4, in the Mercedes. Lucky it was a big car. Banged Pattie and I up a bit—blood all over the place.”

“What?!”

“Krishna’s wake-up call. I got fined yesterday at Maidenhead court, £20.1 don’t go to London much anymore. Hey, it’s stopped raining, shall we have a look round the garden? Show you what we’ve done with the place. There are some wellies in the cart-should fit you.”

George tells me to buckle up, put away tray tables and bring my seat to an upright position—and off we race at about 1 mph through the acres of gardens, places we played when it was an unkempt jungle two years ago. The rhodies are in full bloom.

“Remember ’em before, totally covered with ivy and brambles?” George asks.

“Yeah, and we zapped ’em with those army flame-throwers you found somewhere—‘Take that, you commie pinko bastards!”’

Past the lakes, over fairy-tale bridges, waving at a gardener who pokes his head above a hedge—statues, gazebos, Japanese gardens, sundials, and swans; fresh-trimmed topiary, burbling brooks, and then up to the Matterhorn. George opens a door in the side of the mountain, lights a candle, and leads me down a set of rock stairs, along an underground creek, saying, “Careful there, the water’s cold.” We walk, crouched, to a stalactite-lined grotto deep beneath Friar Park, where he lights four or five candles in little niches. There’s a nice picture of Krishna playing His flute, and on a rock shelf, a three-foot figure of Jesus opening his robe and tunic to reveal his bleeding heart. “I found that statue in the house when I bought the place,” George says. Ethereal mood. We don’t speak for a long time. “I come here sometimes to chant a few rounds, get me mind off things.” A flash in those intense eyes reveals his troubled side, but I don’t pry. He’ll tell me in good time.

We return to the light of day, raining again and, tour over, head to the house. We lounge around the kitchen, much improved since the days I scraped off the Victorian-era paint, and snack on chips and apple juice. Seems Pattie is out for the day. We move to the main room to admire George’s latest addition: a genuine lit-up Wurlitzer jukebox. “Want to spin a few platters? Remember this one?” He punches some buttons, we watch the gizmo slide down the track, pull down a 45-rpm record, lay it on the turntable, the needle drops, su-shuss su-shuss—and Hare Krishna Mantra echoes out through the huge oak-timbered hall. We listen to the record five times over, relishing different bits like the speed-up and the gong at the end. Next, George spins a couple of Slim Whitman songs, saying, “These were my dad’s favorites—got me liking music, you might say.” We yodel the chorus together, “Whoo-ooo-yodelde a hoo, singin’ my cattle call,” and fall down laughing. I yodel a couple of Swiss tunes I’d learned—yodeling can only be done in loud, excited voice—and even the full-armored knight stationed by the stairs seemed to be laughing. George keeps the high mood going, switches to his LP hi-fi and plays a couple of Lenny Bruce monologues. “Oh, you ever heard this one?” and we listen to Bob Dylan’s long rap “Last Thoughts on Woody Guthrie”: When yer head gets twisted and yer mind grows numb...

In the silence after, I ask George what songs he’s working on these days, and he grabs an acoustic guitar and sings and hums a few lines of a song—“pretty much finished,” he says:

Give me love

Give me love

Give me peace on earth

Give me light

Give me life

Keep me free from birth

Give me hope

Help me cope, with this heavy load

Trying to, touch and reach You with,

Heart and soul

OMMMMMMMMMMMMMM

МММ My Lord...

PLEASE take hold of my hand, that

I might understand You

Flushed, feeling it, he wears a silly grin by the end, like, Whaddya think? “I love it, George! Perfect! Wow, you’re making such spiritual advancement!”

“Yeah, maybe, but a lot of problems too ...”

On our way to the library George picks up a mandolin lying on the couch and as we walk he plucks out another new tune. “Here’s another one I’m working on, maybe for Cilla Black. Not sure what to call it yet. Maybe ‘Light of the World’ or somethink.”

I’m grateful to anyone, that is happy or “free” for giving me hope while I’m looking to see ... The light that has lighted the world.

We wind up in the small library, now converted to a dayroom. It was like a den where you could sit and chat with guests, have tea, and look out on the fountain, lawns, and lakes. The room is stocked with symbols of George’s interests: the psychedelic sitdown harmonium, guitars scattered around the room, pictures of Krishna and Ganesh, Prabhupada and Yukteswar, gold records on the walls. Tiffany lamps, candles in holders, flowers in vases, picture books, a little marble Shiva statue.

We talk about how special the Bangla Desh concert was. George says, “Yeah, but you wouldn’t believe the hassle afterwards!” and tells me about the complications that followed the performance for Bangladesh. “It was & charity, for God’s sake. The government wanted to tax everything, and then the record companies withheld the money. Lawyers messed up the whole thing. I’m not sure if any of the money even got to those poor blokes in Bangladesh.” George is deeply disturbed. It seems like everything he tries to do is troubled by lawyers and litigation, bumbling bureaucracy. He grabs a banjo, plucks a square-dance riff, and sings a few lines from “Sue Me, Sue You Blues”:

Bring your lawyer and I’ll bring mine,

Get together, and we could have a bad time...

“You know, after all that, I just withdrew here to Friar Park to garden, chant, hang out with Ravi.”

Ah, I can see it now—George is in a monkish period, practicing his sadhana, holding on to what he’s learned from Srila Prabhupada.

“I don’t go to Bury Place much, not since Mukunda left a year or so ago,” George says.

“Yeah, it’s getting really crowded over there. Since you’ve told the whole world about Krishna, devotees are joining right and left— there’s no room for all of them!”

“I’m thinking about forming a charitable trust, without paying so many taxes and getting ripped off by lawyers. Then I can help people like you, like buy you a temple or, what I like, an ashram like this place, in the country, all this fresh air.”

The full-length French doors are open, and perfume from fresh-mown lawns mingles with some special agarbatti George has found and wants me to try. For a long time we don’t speak, just gaze out at gentle summer rain falling on layers and layers of green—the yellowgreen of willows, the blue-green of spruce trees, and every shade of emerald. As dusk falls, I describe the past two years traveling with Prabhupada, how amazing he is, what a special blessing it is to be Prabhupada’s secretary. In the semi-dark George’s eyes gleam with understanding as he hears the story of Prabhupada’s beauty and love and superhuman prowess. George discloses his heart.

“I get it. I practice. I feel the connection. But sometimes I fall, do bad things—what to do?”

“Well, yours is a very rare path, George. Finding your way through the most dazzling stuff maya can throw at a person. But just like an electric fan, when it’s unplugged the blades will turn for a while, then they stop, slowly stop. The whole world knows about Krishna now because of you. Krishna is so happy with you!”

“Hmm, you know, I’m ready to go: ‘Any time, Krishna, just take me away!’ I’ll gladly go.”

“No more material fantasies, George?”

“Well, maybe a few ... But I gotta lot of work to do, trying to get a message through.”

Not many more words are said. We’d sort of get started, and the other would finish the thought, understand the rest. We’d argued through the philosophy years ago. Now we were two ships in a great sea and, despite all weathers, still on the same course. George and I are like brothers, alike in so many ways. Perhaps I’m the side of George that gave up the clamor to become a monk—and he’s the monk that has to live with the clamor.

“Prabhupada really wants to see you. When are you going to come into London to see him?”

“Maybe you can bring him out here? I’d love to show him all this.”

“OK, I’ll work on it. But it’s getting late, George, I gotta get back to Srila Prabhupada.”

I give him a few perforated sheets of the new Lord Chaitanya postage stamps, and we say good night. On the long drive back to Bury Place I ponder this strange fact: aside from Srila Prabhupada, I’ve never had a friend like this who so completely knows my mind— and maybe vice versa.

With Ratha-yatra little more than a week away, devotees were busy preparing for the biggest annual event on the ISKCON calendar. Day and night people raced in and out of 7 Bury Place, workers and warriors swarming around our King Bee at the center of the hive. Prabhupada lectured every day in the temple from the first two chapters of Srimad-Bhagavatam, returning to basics for the new young devotees.

The Swami got into his usual routine, walking in the mornings in Russell Square, St. James Park, or Regents Park. He often told us that there are only two places in the world where a gentleman can live: England and India.

Prabhupada spoke at the Commonwealth Club on Kensington High Street, and at the Hindu Centre on Grafton Terrace in Hampstead. Ksirodaksayee—so instrumental in obtaining the Saraf property in Vrindaban—was back from India, and he asked Prabhupada to install Deities in his family’s living quarters above Friendly Store, his small Indian grocery at 85 Dudden Hill Lane in the Dollis Hill suburb of NW London.

One morning we had a surprise visitor. Nanda Kumar had met a nun, Sister Mary, while walking down Bury Place, and had invited her upstairs to meet his Indian guru, a real swami from India.

Prabhupada is pleasantly surprised to see this female Christian ascetic in a full gray habit with white scapular, cowl, and black hood. A dozen devotees filter into his room to hear what transpires, and I click on the tape recorder as Prabhupada launches into fullcharm mode.

Prabhupada: We are teaching how to love God. That’s all.

Sister Mary: Yes. So we're doing the same thing.

Prabhupada: Yes. But the test is there, whether he has become a lover of God or lover of dog.

Rebatinandan: Jesus used the expression loving God or loving mammon.

Prabhupada: So one test is bhaktih pareshanubhava-viraktir anyatra syat. If one has become a lover of God, naturally he will be detached from material enjoyment. Just like Lord Jesus Christ. He never advised for economic development. He sacrificed everything for God. That is love of God. So the ideal is Lord Jesus Christ, and the follower must be, at least to some extent, to that point. That is test. You may follow any religious path. That doesn’t matter. We want to see whether you are lover of God. That is our propaganda. And our method is simple: chant the holy name of God. If you have got any name, you chant.

Rebatinandan: Sister Mary was showing me—they have got a rosary, almost like our japa beads. Show him.

Sister Mary: You see, here. We chant in congregation in the Orthodox Church. And we take turns doing two hundred times: “Lord Jesus Christ, son of God, have mercy on us.”

Prabhupada: That’s very nice. Therefore let us cooperate fully. Don’t think that we are against Christianism. Jointly, let us preach all over the world “Chant the holy name of God.” Let us join together. That should be the real purpose of devotees of God. We are preaching this cult, that chant the holy name of God. That’s all. So what objection there may be from other sects? But they are envious. We wanted to purchase one church, and the priest in charge said that “I would rather burn this church then give to them.” Why? What we have done wrong?

Dhananjaya tells us he’s arranged a Thursday afternoon program in Reading, a mid-sized town in Berkshire, about forty-five miles west of London. Mr. Prajapati, an influential Indian man, has hired a small church hall for the occasion. Reading is near Friar Park in Henley-on-Thames, so I coordinate with Prabhupada, George, and Mr. Prajapati that we would have kirtan and a lecture at the Reading hall, and then I would drive Prabhupada and Dhananjaya over to George’s place in the afternoon, returning to the Prajapati home in Reading that evening. My strategy was this: I thought George would buy us a temple or ashram in the future, but these things take time and, since I’m traveling with Prabhupada I won’t be around to encourage it. Better George get to know Dhananjaya, the London president, so he can liaise with him in these affairs. Sure, Dhananjaya is a proper Scotsman, not exactly Mr. Personality, but he’s a staunch devotee, honest and strong, clear-headed, and pretty good at handling business and money—dhananjaya means ‘conqueror of wealth”—and he’s the only one I trust from these young Brit devotees to get the job done in my absence.

So on the afternoon of July 13, 1972, I guided the temple’s tank-like Ford Transit panel van, with Prabhupada buckled into the passenger seat, away from Reading and eight miles down Henley Road to Friar Park. It was a gorgeous, lazy summer afternoon, and we saw people with parasols rowing on the river Thames.

Pattie greets us at the heavy castle door, shy and beautiful as ever, in a canary yellow blouse and culottes under a long, open, creamcolored gauzy kameez. She tells us George will be along in a minute. She leads us through the vestibule into the main room, and we sit on couches, a little awkward, Prabhupada sitting forward and resting his head on his cane as his eyes take in the splendor of this vast English hall. Suddenly, George bursts into the room, his arms filled with freshcut flowers from his garden. He dumps them on a table and falls flat on his face before Srila Prabhupada, who is beaming with pride as he pats, then rubs his hand through George’s hair. George is in his usual country attire: bell-bottom jeans, no belt, and a light-blue work shirt, his face suffused with a rare happiness. They catch up.

“Shyamsundar has been telling me—sounds like you’ve set India on fire!”

“Yes, we have made some impression.”

“And how are things at Bury Place?”

“They have become so crowded they are sleeping on the stairs.” \[laughs\]

“You need a place like this ...”

And we stand to make a tour of the place. George leads us up the wide staircase through a long hallway, past the huge master bedroom with its canopied bed, to his temple room. Along one wall of the room is a twelve-foot-long marble slab supported and surrounded by massive carved mahogany columns, balustrades, and intaglio backdrops. It’s breathtaking! At the center of George’s altar is the three-foot-high Orissa-style pagoda I’d built a couple of years ago, painted bright gold, with a color photo of Radha-Londonishwara inside. Candles stand in front of Them, and on the right, a big picture of Krishna seated, wearing a jeweled crown and playing His flute. On the left, Krishna dances on the hoods of the Kaliya demon.

“Wow, George, this is magnificent!” I say, but George is modest, and says, “I chant rounds here every morning, and meditate, light some agarbatti—you won’t see me around the house most days till after noon.”

Then we walk around outside for a while, chattering like chipmunks. Prabhupada has the outsized head and feet and gangly walk of a teenager, loose at the waist in full stride, neck cocked back, knifing the wind. He’s on a leisurely summer afternoon stroll in Oxfordshire with gentlemen and a lady to see the estate and examine world affairs. We walk through alpine gardens at the base of the Matterhorn, past the topiary, the herb garden, and over to the maze. George explains to Prabhupada, “You go in that opening in the hedge and try to find your way out to this opening, over here.”

“Yes, it is just like the material world—you are lost inside, you cannot get out, but if you find a spiritual master, he can tell you, this way, that way, and you will come out successful.” \[laughter\]

We pause on the way back to the house when I ask if I can take a photo of everyone. I can see George is a little uptight with that—probably thinking by tomorrow it’ll be in the Daily Mirror: “Beatles New Guru!”—but he agrees, reluctantly. I know I’ve only got one shot at this—there will be no “now you take the camera and take a picture of us”—so I shoot the now famous photo of the four of them: Prabhupada, on the far left in flower-garden garland, hand on his cane, upright and smiling, proud of his children; Pattie, close on his left, grinning and shy; George, a bit distant from her, hip-cocked and formal, almost grim; and two feet away to his left, shaven-headed Dhananjaya in his yellow robes, maybe thinking, “Hey, I’m just the new guy here.”

As we continue our stroll around the property I sense a new dynamic here. George is short with Pattie, even a little cross. What’s going on? They seem distant from each other today. Is the honeymoon over? (Years later, Pattie wrote in her memoir, Wonderful Tonight, “I suppose I didn’t really think of it as my house. I felt very undermined and unloved and George was so terribly difficult to talk to. He would either be counting his beads and muttering under his breath all the time all day long, so if you talked to him you didn’t know whether you would get an answer in the middle of his chanting or whether he would bite your head off.”)

Making our way slowly back to the house, we pass a lake, weeping willows and tall Scots pines cast across the water in tranquil reflection, and Prabhupada says: “We can see that the trees on the bank reflect upon the water with their branches down and roots up. Urdhva-mulam adhah-sakham. In other words, the tree of this material world is upside-down, only a reflection of the real tree of the spiritual world. This tree, this material tree, is an exact replica. Everything is there, original, in the spiritual world.... The material world is the perverted reflection, so the spiritual world must have the same variegatedness, but in reality. A reflection is temporary, for it is sometimes seen and sometimes not seen. But the origin from whence the reflection is reflected is eternal. The material reflection of the real tree has to be cut off.”

Back inside the manor, we move to the small, south-facing dayroom in the alpenglow of a long midsummer evening. Prabhupada turns to George and says, “Now you have given Krishna to the whole world, and He has given you, reciprocal. You have very nice arrangement, practically you have everything, wealth, fame, beauty... But all of this is gone at the moment of death.”

Prabhupada sits in an armchair in the half light, and there is, yes, a bluish aura around him—around us. Time is suspended in the gloaming, as Prabhupada gives us transcendental vision and takes us with him into the spiritual world. “In the spiritual sky, there is nothing but freshness. Even the flowers picked from the trees and plants remain fresh, for everything in the spiritual sky retains its originality and does not fade. The fragrance of the flowers picked from the trees and made into garlands does not fade, for both the trees and the flowers are spiritual: paritam vana-malaya. The bees are equally attracted to the flowers whether they are on the garland or on the trees. The significance of spirituality is that everything is eternal and inexhaustible. Everything taken from everything remains everything. The bees hum around the fresh flowers, and their sweet sound is enjoyed by the Lord.”

A phone jangles somewhere deep in the house, and Pattie rushes off to answer it. Shadows deepen across Prabhupada’s face and a patch of late sun, reflected from a mirror, slowly moves across the floor by his feet as he continues. “The women there are hundreds and thousands of times more beautiful than the women here in this material world, and the spiritual atmosphere is also many times better. Yet despite the pleasant atmosphere, the minds of the denizens do not become agitated because in the spiritual world, the Vaikuntha planets, the spiritualistic minds of the inhabitants are so much absorbed in the spiritual vibration of chanting the glories of the Lord that such enjoyment could not be surpassed by any other enjoyment, even sex, which is the culmination of all pleasure in the material world. Sex life is insignificant.”

Pattie returns with a tray of different juices, asks each what he would like, and passes them around. Prabhupada chooses mango juice, takes a small sip, and has our full attention.

“Chintamani-prakara-sadmasu kalpa-vriksha-lakshavriteshu. In the transcendental world—as we have got experience here, the houses are made of bricks—there, the houses are made of chintamani stone. Of course, there is no exact translation, but it is understood it is something like touchstone. Touchstone means the stone which if it touches iron, it transforms into gold. So the abode of Krishna is described that there are houses, chintamani-prakara-sadmasu. Prakara means house. And kalpa-vriksha, that the trees are desire trees. What is that desire tree? Just like here in this material world, a tree is meant for delivering a particular fruit or flower, but desire tree means whatever you desire you get immediately delivered from the tree. Kalpa means whatever you like, whatever you will, the tree will supply immediately. Everything is complete. Such a place and such abode and such activities are described about Krishna.

“And in the Vaikuntha world, the spiritual sky, only the mode of goodness in its pure form exists. The Lord and His devotees reside in the Vaikuntha planets, and they are of the same transcendental quality, namely, suddha-sattva, the mode of pure goodness. The Vaikuntha planets are very dear to the Vaishnavas, and for the progressive march of the Vaishnavas toward the kingdom of God, the Lord Himself helps His devotees. Bhagavan shabda-gocharah”

And this was the kicker: “But even within the material world one can spiritualize one’s existence by seeing the Supreme Lord within everything and worshiping Him by constant meditation.”

We sit silently for many minutes as the sun winks out—a quick glance at my watch says 9:15—and full dark descends on the room. Prabhupada holds us in a magic spell, and I sense this is a historic moment. Prabhupada has zapped us, convincing George beyond all doubt now. When he finally speaks, George clears his throat then says in husky voice, “I want to buy you an ashram, like this place. I will get my estate agents to start looking tomorrow.”

We start for the door to leave, inviting George to the Ratha-yatra festival three days from now. He says he probably won’t attend, but will watch it on the telly. What he did in the next few days was write a song called “The Lord Loves the One (That Loves the Lord)”:

We all move around,

with objectives in mind,

to become rich or famous,

With our reputations signed,

But the few that can reach,

to this coveted slot,

don’t escape old age creeping,

through their bodies, like a rot.

While the Lord loves the one that loves the Lord.

And the law says if you don’t give,

then you don’t get loving.

Now the Lord helps those that help themselves,

And the law says whatever you do

is going to come right back on you.

\[Years later, George wrote in his autobiography I, Me, Mine: “This song I wrote after Swami A. C. Bhaktivedanta came to my house one afternoon. His life is an example for us all.”\]

We drive the eight miles back to Reading to find a fifteen-course gourmet Indian feast awaiting us at Mr. Prajapati’s house. By now it’s nearly midnight, but Prabhupada is in ebullient mood, he is hungry, and he urges us to eat everything. We’re so tired and so full that some of us fall asleep where we sit. Prabhupada is given a bed in a side room. At about 3 a.m. he wakes us all up. It’s time to head to London now, for mangala-arati at 4:30. When we return to Bury Place, all the lights in the building are on and devotees are scrambling to and fro in feverish preparation for Ratha-yatra. The clock is ticking.

**\* \* \***

If you had been a disinterested Englishman out for a warm, Sunday afternoon stroll in Hyde Park, you might have heard the commotion near Speakers’ Corner and, curious fellow, let’s go have a look. What’s this gigantic contraption? Must be fifty feet high, covered in red cloth, on four huge, yes, those are wooden wheels. And the noise! Who are all these people? Thousands of them! “Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna...” Ah, it’s the Krishna people, heard about them, friends of the Beatles, some sort of Indian thing—but what are they doing? Who’s the gentleman sitting up at the front of this machine beneath so many garlands? They’re fanning him. Must be their leader. He’s old, but looks very fresh. Maybe he’s a yogi or something. They’re moving now, turning down Park Lane. Maybe I’ll follow a while, see what happens. “Hold on! You’re selling those magazines? Fifty pence? Yes, I’ll have one, please.”

Or perhaps you were a Gujarati refugee, freshly settled in London from trouble-plagued East Africa, invited down here by your friend from the Hindu Centre to see—you won’t believe it!—a real Ratha-yatra. But those are Britishers up on the rath car, and so many English people chanting and dancing ahead and behind. They’ve taken your tradition and are genuinely practicing it better than you are yourself! And who is this Swami, riding in front? No Swami in history has taken your Hindu belief out into the world like this—“Come Sanjay, let’s get in front, help them pull on the ropes.”

But if you were a Krishna devotee on that July 16, 1972, afternoon, you’d been awake for three days straight, pounding nails or stringing flowers or cooking prasad for the biggest day of your life—Prabhupada’s in town, his first Ratha-yatra with us!—feeling the furious magic of Krishna making the impossible happen, months of work for one afternoon’s glorification of God. But what’s this? The cart’s stopping in the middle of Piccadilly Road, and Prabhupada’s getting off? He’s going into the Ritz Hotel? Hah! All of London has to pause while Prabhupada takes a bathroom break. He’s back, we’re halfway there, through Piccadilly Circus, down Haymarket, and there they are, our circus tents in Trafalgar Square, our stage, the deity thrones set around the base of Nelson’s Column. “Hare Krishna!”

If you were a disinterested Englishman descending the steps from the National Gallery after that Sunday afternoon’s viewing of Van Gogh’s Sunflowers and the Turner collection, you looked out into a strange sea of commotion at Trafalgar Square; if you were in an Indian’s body, you’d be sweating from the three-hour march and feeling a new hope rising in this cold land; and if you’re a Krishna devotee you’re now hoarse and thirsty and you raise your voice to an out-of-body pitch as Prabhupada descends from the rath car and takes his place onstage.

Ranchor das: The scene at the base of the column created a striking tableau. In the center, beneath the great bronze relief depicting the death of Nelson, the Deities of Jagannath, Subhadra, and Balaram stood in a row inside their sculpted thrones. On their right Prabhupada settled into his seat under the shade of his ornate parasol. All around these central figures rose a tide of devotees thronging the plinth, jostling for a view of their leader, their eyes fixed on him.

Prabhupada begins:

Ladies and gentlemen, I thank you very much for coming here and participating in this great movement. The purpose of this movement is to cleanse the heart. Cheto-darpana-marjanam bhava-maha-davagni-nirvapanam. We have created so many problems in our life simply on the platform of misunderstanding. What is that misunderstanding? The misunderstanding is that we are accepting this material body as the self.... But there is another nature, which is called spiritual nature. So that spiritual nature, or spiritual sky, is described in the Vedic literature that there is no need of sunshine, there is no need of moonshine, there is no need of electricity. So our only business is to transfer ourselves from this sky to that sky, that illuminating sky. That is the Vedic injunction. Tamasi ma jyotir gamaya: “Don’t remain in this world of darkness. Come to the world of light.” So this movement is very important movement. We are trying to educate people how to transfer from this world of darkness to the world of light. So I am very much thankful to you that you have given me your time. So we have got our books; our devotees are preaching. Take advantage of this opportunity and make your life successful.

Despite hard looks from the bobbies on patrol, a full Vedic fire ceremony followed the lecture, and under a blue pall of sacrificial smoke Prabhupada initiated twenty-two new devotees in front of “25,000 persons, mostly Englishmen. All in all, it was the best Ratha-yatra we have seen to date,” Prabhupada wrote in a letter later that day. What struck me most throughout that day was Prabhupada’s regal bearing. He looked and acted like royalty, the true monarch of the British Isles.

As usual, there were constant changes to Prabhupada’s itinerary. He enjoyed being in the UK, and would soon travel to Edinburgh and Glasgow. He also accepted invitations for short visits to Paris and Amsterdam. But London will be his home base at least until the end of August, and then he’ll return to Los Angeles to help Karandhar finalize the purchase of property adjacent to the Watseka Avenue temple. He also promised Brahmananda to come to Nairobi in August. I’m constantly writing letters to make arrangements, then writing new ones to change those arrangements, then running to travel agents to rebook and change tickets, but I love the way Krishna weaves His plan, the inscrutable plan of the spiritual master.

**\* \* \***

On July 20 we fly to Paris. Fifty devotees have gathered from France, Germany, and Holland for a gala reception at Orly airport, where Prabhupada speaks to French reporters looking for scandal, Guru Gauranga translating.

Reporter: How old are you?

Prabhupada: I am now seventy-seven.

Reporter: Where is your wife?

Prabhupada: This is not an important question.

Reporter: How many children do you have?

Prabhupada: Thousands—and all of them I have had without my wife, \[much laughter\]

Prabhupada and entourage were whisked away to the temple in suburban Fontenay-aux-Roses, six miles from downtown Paris. We’d visited this temple for a couple of days en route from Moscow—was it only a year ago?—and Prabhupada liked the quiet neighborhood. Bhagavan and Harivilas were overseeing frantic temple renovations, and the place smelled like fresh paint. Prabhupada spoke for two nights in a hall at L’Ecole Speciale d’Architecture, a grand edifice on Boulevard Raspail, Montparnasse, in the very heart of Paris. The devotees had also planned an outdoor Hare Krishna festival between July 23-26 in Luxembourg Gardens, the magnificent, fifty-seven-acre park surrounding the palace of King Henry IV. The Sorbonne, France’s largest university, was just a few blocks away. It was just four years since the Night of the Barricades, when twenty thousand Sorbonne students had rioted, a million Frenchmen went on strike, and you almost had another French revolution. This was Camus and Sartre country.

Many student radicals in dark clothes and goatees attended the festival on that first day, intent on contesting Prabhupada’s words as he spoke under threatening gray skies. In his infinite wisdom, Prabhupada defused the situation, praising the French penchant for revolution and their call to “liberty, equality, fraternity.” He described Krishna consciousness as the ultimate “revolutionary movement”—to the students’ loud cheers. A Vedic fire ceremony followed, and then it started to rain, then rain heavily. Prabhupada glanced at me, water dripping from his nose. I hustled the car and driver, and we disappeared mid-ceremony to finish initiating twenty new disciples back at the temple. It rained for the next three days, so Prabhupada remained in Fontenay, lecturing at the temple each day.

The Paris leaders wanted to install Radha-Krishna Deities and were making high-energy preparations for that, but after a few days assessing the situation, Prabhupada was doubtful that the French devotees could provide the necessary standard of worship. He said, “These Deities need nine brahmins to worship Them. Do we have these brahmins?” When the leaders said they did not, Prabhupada suggested that it was better not to install Them just now but to wait. Many little hints led to Prabhupada’s decision, such as the halffinished Mahavishnu logo on the side of one devotees’ van.

Vasudeva das: When Prabhupada looked at the painting of Mahavishnu more closely, he became angry and chastised me. I didn't have time to finish the painting, and two of Mahavishnu's four arms were only drafted. He didn't approve of my decision to leave them like that and show something halffinished in public.

Prabhupada’s feeling that his European disciples were still immature and struggling and not yet ready for serious Deity worship, carried over to Amsterdam. We flew there from Paris on Friday, July 28, straight into pandemonium.

Dhananjaya and his wife, Bala Gopala, had gone from London to Amsterdam in 1971 to plant the ISKCON flag. After great effort the devotees opened a temple at 39 Bethanienstraat, just near the Nieuwmarkt, in the heart of old Amsterdam. By the summer of 1972, Amsterdam was hippie heaven. Lax drug laws, squatter flats, open parks, and the Dutch liberal tradition made Amsterdam a mecca for penniless youth from around the world looking for drugs, sex, and adventure. It was Haight-Ashbury all over again, but a more hedonistic, degenerated version of it.

Amsterdam was three days of chaos. It started in the sweltering VIP room at Schiphol airport, where one devotee fanned sweat-drenched Srila Prabhupada with a magazine. Prabhupada had agreed to install Jagannath deities the day he arrived—the Dutch devotees had even phoned Nanda Kumar in Paris to get instructions on how to do it. But when we entered the temple on Bethanienstraat, disorder and confusion reigned. Hundreds of people filled the temple room and spilled out onto the street and sidewalk, including the press and a TV crew. Prabhupada was beaming—until he saw the half-finished state of affairs: “What is this? No fruits? No flowers? What are these pots that you want to bathe the deities in?” The anger in his voice froze everyone. “I cannot bring the Lord of the universe here. This is a farce! How can you think that with so little preparation Lord Jagannath will live in this place? Who is responsible for this?”

But Prabhupada relented and allowed the show to go on-shouting instructions while the TV cameras rolled. Nanda Kumar jumped in to help. The Deities were dressed and propped up with stacks of books, and when the curtain opened, Prabhupada’s mood lifted and he led a powerful kirtan.

Rebatinandan Swami performed the fire ceremony. Prabhupada interrupted him to correct his Sanskrit, and coach him from time to time—“No, put ladle in right hand.” After a long afternoon of welcoming Lord Jagannath to Holland and initiating new devotees, finally, there is a smile on Prabhupada’s lotus face—and we breathed a sigh of relief. The world was right again.

After the beleaguered installation ceremony, Prabhupada and his crew were driven to their quarters about a mile from the temple.

The driver slammed the brakes suddenly, and Prabhupada’s prasad plate hit the floor, mashing the food into a homogeneous mess. Jeez, I’m thinking, when things go wrong, they go wrong in spades. We settled into the cramped apartment facing the Vondelpark, and Prabhupada pulled a face but ate all of his prasad anyway, showing us that this special form of the Lord’s mercy is always desirable in any condition. I climbed ladderlike stairs to a tiny garret under the gables, banging my head and thinking people must have been smaller in the old days. I set up my typewriter and took a deep breath, pondering Prabhupada’s anger. At one point that afternoon in the temple, when I’d timidly asked if we should fetch some fruits, he had yelled at me, “Don’t tell me what to do!” and it still stung. But when I stole back down the stairs and chanced a glance in his direction, Prabhupada was calmly sitting with a few devotees as if nothing had happened. When he saw the troubled look on our faces he said, “It is for your education, not for my sake. A devotee does not become angry when personally offended, but when he sees that the Lord or another devotee is offended, he shows anger.” I realized, yet again, that the master’s anger is not really anger but is a seldom-used, effective tool to help us improve in spiritual life.

For Saturday, July 29, the devotees had rented a hall at the Krasnapolsky Hotel, near the Dam, the central square in Amsterdam, for a Hare Krishna festival. Many hippies in Amsterdam hung out in the Dam all day, sleeping, smoking dope, and pestering tourists and residents for spare change. To attract people to Prabhupada’s lecture, Hamsaduta and Rebatinandan led a sankirtan party around Dam Square, and an immense crowd of hippies followed them back to the hall. Most of them were stoned and acting stupid, heckling, mocking the devotees’ dancing. They jeered when asked to put out their cigarettes. Prabhupada came onstage, sang the Jaya Radha-Madhava prayer, then beckoned for Hamsaduta and told him that these people were all intoxicated, so it was useless to discuss philosophy with them. We should just chant and serve prasad, that’s all. Then he got up and we left the place.

The next morning, Sunday July 30, we took a walk across the street from Prabhupada’s apartment in the Vondelpark, a 120-acre open space, rare in this city where, like Hong Kong, people lived in tight quarters. Vondelpark is aptly named “Wander Park,” and was now home to thousands of homeless wanderers, modern-day gypsies scattered on the lawns in sleeping bags or—we couldn’t help but see them—fornicating under the trees. “Just see,” Prabhupada remarked, “in the material world it doesn’t matter whether one is rich or poor, there will always be some facility for sex life.”

The Dutch devotees had built a pandal in Vondelpark, where they conducted an all-day program of kirtan, lectures, and prasad distribution. Prabhupada could hear their amplified words from his balcony outside the apartment and expressed satisfaction to hear his disciples preaching to the restless and ill-mannered youth. When it came time for Prabhupada to walk over and mount the stage, there must have been two thousand hippies out there, and trouble was brewing. Prabhupada spoke calmly on the basics of Krishna consciousness, interrupted now and then by hoots and jeers from the unruly crowd. When he was finished, a half-naked hippie in front started yelling obscenities, and Chakravarti das, a German devotee, jumped from the stage and started pounding the guy. Rebatinandan, robes flying, sailed past me and into the melee, fists and elbows pounding. A thousand voices rose and a full-scale riot erupted! Prabhupada grabbed his kartals and started to chant the Hare Krishna mantra into the microphone—and gradually the crowd calmed down. After a few minutes he turned to me and raised his eyebrows. I pointed to the back, and we softly made our exit, Prabhupada shaking his head in disbelief.

Chakravarti: After the program, Prabhupada reprimanded us for our foolish action. He was disturbed and said that only the chanting of the holy name had prevented a disaster: two thousand hippies fighting with fifty of us. He said that it was one of our Western diseases to become easily angered and start a fight over a trifle. The next day, this guy I had attacked came to the temple and apologized. To my amazement, he admitted being impressed by the decisive action taken against him. And he actually stayed and became a devotee.

During our July 31 early-morning flight back to London, Prabhupada dictated a letter to Hridayananda Goswami, who was lecturing at American university campuses: “We are observing here in Europe that many hippies have become disgusted with material life, but they are also now so much degraded that they will not hear our philosophy, simply mocking.... So far preaching to the hippie class, it is better not to preach very much philosophy. Just somehow or other get them to chant Hare Krishna mantra.”

We transited at London Heathrow and caught a noon flight to Edinburgh. Scotland would be a highlight, a journey of discovery for Srila Prabhupada. He had studied for four years at Scottish Churches College in Calcutta under professors with names like W. S. Urquhart and J. C. Scrimgeour, and their difficult burrs could still be heard in Prabhupada’s English pronunciation if you listened for it. He often referred to these men as honest, upright, exemplary gentlemen, and he was curious to see their homeland.

Tribhuvanath and Kishor had come up to Edinburgh about a year ago, and despite extreme poverty had managed to lease the upper floors of Oddfellows Hall at 14 Forrest Road. ISKCON Edinburgh is close to the National Museum of Scotland on one side, the University of Edinburgh on the other, in the Greyfriars district at the very center of town.

Ranchor: Its gothic facade was encrusted with heraldic beasts and gargoyles. Inside was a flea market on the ground floor, and upstairs, spacious rooms with a large meeting hall.

Prabhupada appreciated this high-ceilinged, drafty place, warmed as it was by the same spirit that prevailed at Bury Place in London: sincere, enthusiastic, impoverished devotees doing their best to glorify Krishna and Prabhupada. The wooden floor was painted a deep blue with a pink lotus design in the center. The walls were yellow and pink and hung with large paintings. The devotees had brought a huge red vyasasan with pink satin bolsters that Naranarayan had built in London, and had festooned the temple with roses they had nicked the night before, climbing over the high iron fence around Princes Street Gardens.

Prabhupada spoke in the temple room to a modest crowd of devotees and a few inquisitive guests. “All these big, big buildings were constructed in your country—Edinburgh. The idea was that ‘We shall show something opulent that we are a very great nation. We have got nice buildings, nice churches, nice roads.’ Puffed up. Everyone wants to be puffed up before his friends and relatives. So this is materialism. But the same thing done for the satisfaction of Krishna, that ‘We shall construct this temple for Krishna’s inhabitation, for providing the devotees of Krishna so that they may be able to chant Hare Krishna maha-mantra without any disturbance,’ that is spiritualism.”

Prabhupada moved to his bedroom and chatted into the evening with anyone who dropped by, including several rather skeptical reporters and, later, Prabhavishnu’s traveling sankirtan team. The floorboards and walls in the bedroom were painted white, but the room was otherwise unadorned. The bed was a simple canvas camp cot. There was only one bathroom in the place, shared by all—with no hot water—but Prabhupada appreciated the Scots devotees’ spirit of renunciation. He was content with these monastic conditions, and he charmed everyone with his jolly mood, as if yesterday’s anger in Amsterdam had never happened.

The next morning we walked in a nearby public park called The Meadows through a cold Scottish drizzle. At noon we taxied to Waverley station and took the fifty-minute train ride to Glasgow Queen Street station. From there we taxied to Woodside Hall, on Glenfarg Street near St. George’s Cross, in central Glasgow. The hall was in a tough neighborhood, but very close to the University of Glasgow. Tribhuvanath’s vigorous campaign to mount posters all over town—dodging the bobbies at night—attracted nearly three hundred Scottish citizens, mostly students, to Prabhupada’s lecture. And Prabhupada was in top form, lecturing to the greatgrandchildren of the Urquharts and Scrimgeours.

His topic was Bhagavad-gita 2.1—Krishna’s famous declaration: “Those who are wise lament neither for the living nor the dead.” “You have to understand only three things,” Prabhupada said, “and then you become peaceful. What is that? The first thing is that ‘God is enjoyer, I am not enjoyer.’ Everyone is thinking, ‘I am enjoyer.’ But actually, we are not enjoyer. For example, just like my hand is part and parcel of my body. Suppose the hand catches one nice palatable fruit cake. The hand cannot enjoy it. The hand picks it up and puts it in the mouth. When the energy is created by eating that food, that is enjoyed by the hand. Second thing: ‘God is the proprietor, I am not the proprietor.’Just like in this Woodside Hall. Before we entered this hall, the hall was existing, and when we leave this hall, the hall will exist. If we falsely claim that by sitting here for one hour we have become proprietor, that is false impression. And third thing: ‘God is the best friend of everyone.’ God is actually perfect friend of all living entities. If we simply understand these three things, then we become peaceful immediately.”

Prabhupada was the graduate maxima cum laude delivering his treatise on the summum bonum of philosophy, religion, and literature. After his long, perfectly crafted lecture, the audience stood and clapped for five minutes. Scotland was a glorious victory, another rhino for Srila Prabhupada, fifty years in the making.

That night we stayed in a hotel at Glasgow’s Abbotsinch airport, flying back to London on the morning of August 3.

**\* \* \***

That afternoon, George shows up at Bury Place. In this, his fifth face-to-face meeting with Srila Prabhupada, George appears different, changed. It’s been three years since their first visit at John Lennon’s place in Ascot in September 1969. George falls flat on the floor, across from the desk, but Prabhupada motions him closer, “Sit here beside me,” and embraces him, musses his hair like a favorite son. By now, George accepts this unassuming but powerful swami as his true master, as perfect a living teacher as he’ll ever find, and himself as the master’s humble servant, his child and dear friend.

George explains that Ravi Shankar’s in town, so George will be coming to London most days for a week or so to arrange the public viewing of a film called Raga that he and Ravi produced. They yak for a while, Prabhupada showing him the finished Krsna book and telling stories from it. George says he’s been to his estate agents that morning, engaging them to look for likely ashram properties on the outskirts of London. I mention that I’d seen billboards around London advertising the him Bangla Desh. George explains why it’s taken a year for the him to be shown, and expresses his disgust for lawyers and politicians in general. He reveals his plan for a charitable trust so he can buy an ashram and “spread the news about Krishna.” “So I’ll be off now—try to beat the traffic. Can I drop round tomorrow?” Prabhupada replies, “You shall come every day.”

Next day George reappeared at Bury Place in the early afternoon, the normal time for Prabhupada’s siesta. I was at the travel agent changing plane tickets yet again. Nanda Kumar insisted Prabhupada would be happy to see George, and while they paused in front of Prabhupada’s door, George was visibly shaking and said, “I get so nervous when I’m around His Divine Grace—I just got my hair cut and shaved me mustache to see Prabhupada.” This from a guy who meets the Queen and hangs out with Elvis and Bobby Dylan.

Prabhupada beckons George to sit next to him again, and by the time I arrive they’re deep into a discussion about George’s personal life. What should he do? Should he shave his head and don the robes? Wear tilak, give up his music?

“No, no,” Prabhupada says. “You remain as you are. There is no need to change the outer dress. The world knows you like this, you stay like this, make your music. Just like the example is given, when a man decorates his face, he does not feel much enjoyment because he cannot see his own face. But he enjoys his beautiful face when it is reflected on the mirror. In other words, when a living entity renders service to the Lord, the Lord enjoys the situation, and the devotee sees the Lord’s pleasure in the mirror of his heart, and he also feels happy.”

“I can give you some ideas,” Prabhupada says, and he asks Nanda Kumar to bring over his harmonium. Tinkering with the keyboard a moment, Prabhupada pumps some minor chords, settles the mood—and our minds fly away in complex melody as he closes his eyes and in a high, full voice croons this Bengali verse:

hari hari! bifale janama gonainu

manushyajanama paiya, radha-krishna na bhajiya,

janiya shuniya bisa khainu

Prabhupada sings a couple of verses, then stops. There’s a long silence. We don’t understand the words, but Prabhupada has tears in his eyes—there is obvious deep meaning to what he’s sung, and he explains: “‘My dear Lord, I have simply wasted my privileges of human form of life. This life was meant for understanding Krishna consciousness and the pastimes of Lord Krishna with Radharani, but I did not take care of this important business of life. Therefore I have not only wasted my valuable time, but also I have willingly drunk poison for committing suicide. My heart is always in blazing fire because of my association with material sense enjoyment, and I did not fix up my mind in the Krishna consciousness movement, which is imported directly from the kingdom of God. This Krishna consciousness movement is inaugurated by Lord Chaitanya and Lord Nityananda, who are Krishna and Balarama respectively. They have descended very kindly to reclaim all fallen souls of this age. Now, forgetting all mistakes that I have committed in my past life, I humbly surrender unto You, my Lord Krishna, the son of Nanda Maharaja, and also to Srimati Radharani, the daughter of

King Vrishabhanu. So both of You are present together, and I fully surrender unto You. Please do not reject me, as I have no other shelter except Yourselves.’

“So this song is sung by Narottama das Thakur—he is student of Jiva Goswami in Vrindaban. So this is authorized song, meaning songs which were sung or composed by self-realized acharyas”

Prabhupada then stares intently into George’s eyes and says, “I am so glad to learn that you have given up composing nonsense songs. It is a great advancement of your spiritual life. You have got this aptitude for writing songs, so go on composing. I shall be very glad to give you many such themes of songs on which you can write in your own language and expression.”

I slide the harmonium over to George, and he asks Prabhupada, “What about this song?” Then he sings:

Om Hare Om Radha Krishna

Om Hare Om Rama Radha

Om Hare Om Bala Shiva

Om Hare Om Rama Sita

Om Hare Om Gopala Krishna

Govinda Jai Gopala Jai Jaya...

Prabhupada says, “That is nice—you have included Krishna. The transcendental vibration om is also authorized but is specifically sung by the impersonalists. Although there is no incongruity, still, because we are preaching the personal feature of God, we shall not chant Hari Om without saying also Krishna.”

George has to leave, but he says that his agents have found a property to view, and would Prabhupada be free tomorrow about noon to meet him there and see the place? It’s near Runnymede in Sussex, about an hour’s drive from Bury Place—out near John Lennon’s place. Yes, Prabhupada says, “And if we get this place, I shall make London my permanent world headquarters. Just make arrangements with Shyamasundar.”

In the hall outside we quickly coordinate the plan: Dhananjaya will call the agent, get the exact address, and we’ll meet George there at 1 p.m. tomorrow. Bhakti Pramode will drive us in his car.

On the afternoon of August 5 we meet George at the entry gate to Runnymede Farm, an old, seventy-five-acre estate on the river Thames, very near the spot where, in 1215, England’s King John signed the Magna Carta, the first great document defining civil rights and freedom under the law. We stop the cars halfway up the long drive into the place, surrounded by meadows and trees. George tells us there’s a lake over to the left, and we set out on foot through the tall grass to find it. George wears a collared Peruvian peasant sweater over his plain plaid shirt, blue jeans, and white tennies. Prabhupada admires the huge oaks, but notes the neglected appearance of the grounds. He turns to Bhakti Pramode, a manager at Midland Bank and dressed today in a dark-blue three-piece suit, “This property has been on the market for a long time. Just offer them half of what they are asking.”

Then the brush thickens to an ill-defined track, and Prabhupada takes the lead, deftly whacking branches from the path with his bamboo cane. Bhakti Pramode follows, then Dhananjaya and Ksirodaksayee, everyone in a holiday mood. George and I are in the rear, laughing to see Prabhupada as point man boldly leading this expedition toward some long-lost El Dorado. We reach the lake, but it’s a bit of a disappointment. It’s nice with the lily pads and all, but green and stagnant, foul-smelling. Later, when we see the main house, it’s huge beyond imagination but crumbling, the stone facings covered with lichen and moss, windows broken, rooflines sagging. We all reluctantly agree that it’s well beyond our means and desire to restore. As we head back to our cars George quips, “They should have called this place Runnydown Farm!”

Next day, George brings Ravi Shankar to Bury Place, and though Prabhupada and Ravi have never met, they seem like old friends, laughing and yakking in Bengali. George, proud student of both these masters, flushes with pleasure at their meeting. It’s like a circle completed.

I’ve always been a little worried that Ravi considers Krishna to be just a demigod, like his own goddess of music, Saraswati, and that he might influence George in this way. I’m inwardly hoping Prabhupada will not stir up a big argument about who’s who in the chain of divine beings. Sensing this, perhaps, Prabhupada treads lightly with the philosophy, but he makes it clear that Krishna is the Supreme Personality of Godhead, and says to Ravi, “Devim saras-watim vyasam tato jayam udirayet. This is from the beginning of Srimad-Bhagavatam, that the Vaishnavas always offer all respects to Saraswati Devi.”

In early August heavy rains fall on London with high winds and below-normal temperatures, and for several mornings Prabhupada walks in the parks carrying an umbrella in the downpour. One morning, in Regents Park, he asks Nanda Kumar to break off some twigs from a willow tree for his use as toothbrushes. Nanda Kumar returns, pursued by a park attendant threatening legal action for defacing park flora. Prabhupada defuses the situation, and afterward he says, “He has come to India for two hundred years and exploited and stolen so many things from our country, and he won’t let us take a twig from his park.”

Ksirodaksayee arranged a lecture series for August 8-15 at St. Michael’s Hall in the mostly Indian ghetto of Wembley, NW London. Admission is free, and Prabhupada will speak every night from Srimad-Bhagavatam—a traditional seven-day discourse called Bhagavata-saptha.

Days earlier, in a morning lecture at the temple, Prabhupada had said, “In India there is a class, they are professional Bhagavata reciters. They make a contract that he shall recite Srimad-Bhagavatam, finish within a week, and he should be rewarded, paid something. So actually, this Bhagavata-saptha is going on in India from village to village, but we see the effect is that they are not becoming Krishna conscious ... because they take it as a matter of some entertainment, refreshment. No. It should be taken very seriously and should be heard from the right source. Then you will get the result.”

That first night in Wembley Prabhupada initiated sixteen devotees onstage, Nanda Kumar conducting the fire ceremony. Perhaps because the hall is small and dirty and the audience somewhat unreceptive, or maybe because he was coming down with a cold— Prabhupada delegates Rebatinandan Swami to carry on without him for the next six nights.

Prabhupada’s health is suffering from the dreary weather, and he sends Jai Hari to the tobacconist to buy him a tin of Hedges’s mentholated snuff to clear his congestion. When I enter his room in the morning he looks like a sad spaniel—no sleep the night before, snail trails running from his lotus-like nose. I say, “Prabhupada, your upcoming itinerary looks very heavy. You have canceled Nairobi, but you must be at New Vrindaban for two weeks by the end of August. You promised to visit Dallas, Pittsburg, and other US centers next month, and you’ve planned a big program in India for October. You’ve accomplished so much here in London. What more can you do? You should go somewhere warm, take a little rest, and besides, affairs in Los Angeles need your personal attention.”

So Prabhupada decides to return to Los Angeles earlier than planned, and I book tickets for August 14, 1972.

Srila Prabhupada has been in Britain nearly five weeks this summer. He loves the English high manners, the intelligence and open-hearted sincerity of these young boys and girls who’ve pushed themselves to the limit to serve Krishna. Prabhupada has made an intimate connection with his London disciples as they passed these weeks together packed under the same roof. A lot has been achieved: The UK centers are expanding under strong leadership; the Indian community is supportive; Prabhupada was the king of London for the best Ratha-yatra ever; and, though finding a large property for an ashram will take more time, the search is now in the competent hands of George and Dhananjaya. And one of Prabhupada’s unseen but foremost achievements on this UK trip: he has planted the seeds of half a dozen songs about Krishna in George Harrison’s creative mind.

Maybe seventy devotees travel to Heathrow to say goodbye to Srila Prabhupada, surrounding him and chanting at the top of their lungs right up to the door of the walled-off Passport and Immigration area. The devotees fall silent as Prabhupada turns and, with a loving but sad expression, raises his arms, bead bag in hand, and then disappears behind the partition.

Ranchor das: When Prabhupada is around for a few weeks, you start to take him for granted a bit. I was thinking, “This is Krishna’s punishment. He’s taking Prabhupada away because we’re not appreciating him enough.” We went with him right up to the passport barrier, but we couldn’t go with him any further.

## Two

On the long flight from London to Los Angeles, seated in the row behind Srila Prabhupada, I had time to examine my life and ponder this jet-age adventure with my amazing spiritual master. How could all this be happening to me? I’m an insignificant nobody, yet I rub shoulders and have the ear of the greatest man on earth. What great fortune! I couldn’t have earned it—jeez, look at me, I’m still looking down the cleavage of the stewardess when she leans over to hand me a cup of water.

Prabhupada. No one on this planet can do what he does. Maybe no one in history. Who can imagine what he has done—what he is doing? Prabhupada has fanned a tiny spark of the Absolute Truth, the ancient knowledge of God and the self that had almost died—Krishna calls it “the most secret of all secrets”—and singlehandedly, in a few short years, has torched the bone-dry tinder of material complacency and created a worldwide conflagration. I’m so inside it I can’t really see it, but surely his appearance among us will have long-lasting consequences —maybe even save the world.

Once in a while I peek over to see the wedge-shaped top of his head, large in back and tapering forward like the prow of this 747 knifing through space—his short gray sikha in a little curl-looking to see if he might need anything, and my heart goes weak with affection. I’m not exactly his pal, but a close companion in a way, and about as unlikely a match as can be conceived of. Prabhupada often calls to me on his walks, in conversations, even in the middle of lectures—“What is that, Shyamasundar?”— looking for the meaning of a word, a fact, or some detail about a recent occurrence on our tour. I love it. I rise to the occasion, burning energy day and night to keep up with this blazing comet of a man. I prompt him, laugh or fret as he speaks, and I can see that he sometimes cues his remarks off my reactions. I was often smiling, laughing, or trying to suppress a laugh while Prabhupada spoke in a most somber way, as if I could see the humor in truth being forced by a father down the throats of recalcitrant children. I’m happy beyond description.

Prabhupada looks out for my wife and child while I travel with him. Malati and Saraswati have gone to Calcutta from London, and Prabhupada writes to Bhavananda in Calcutta, “Why not these girls can assist your wife in worshiping the Deities?” So Malati is in India with her buddies Palika, Kaushalya, and Srimati dasi, and I don’t have to worry; and as for little Saraswati, Prabhupada again writes to Bhavananda: “You may engage one Sanskrit teacher for Saraswati so she shall become a very great scholar.”

My parents are OK, not super happy, but content, with their only son’s life. At least I’m no longer causing them pain. I don’t have any money, wouldn’t know what to do to earn some, but I don’t need money, I have the best job in the world working twenty-four hours a day for room, board, plane tickets—and liberation.

Sure, there’s always a lot of trouble and confusion around us, especially in India, but being with Srila Prabhupada is like being in the eye of the hurricane: always calm, quiet, and cool. It’s like having a ticket to the Magic Theater, watching the action on a stage and unconcerned with the players’ impermanent emotions and stress, knowing that all will turn out well in the end. I’m watching the Knower of the Field live his life, gracefully conducting the ever-expanding symphony of ISKCON. He speaks in anger one moment, the next with gentility, even submission and charm; then he’s the lawyer, grave and demanding, wielding the sword of logic-whack! Take that! Then softly in a letter to a young devotee he says, “You may name your new baby Nandini devi dasi.” Prabhupada is always thinking ahead. He’ll get an idea in the middle of a program, pause, turn to me and whisper: “Find out where is So-and-so just now,” or “That man in second row-take his address.” And no matter how hectic the surroundings, Prabhupada maintains his fixed routine. Even sitting in an airport four hours due to a flight delay he’d say, “Nanda Kumar, bring my lota. I shall bathe now, then chant my rounds.”

As secretary, my main job is to transmit his words through letters to the world around him. Prabhupada is matter-of-fact in most of his letters, sticking to the points that are raised in letters to him, advising on mundane and personal problems, praising or critiquing devotees’ achievements, moving manpower from center to center, transferring money. But in each letter, whether in a few sentences or in three or four pages, there will also be spiritual advice and instruction to propel the person forward. Whatever topics come up in a letter to Prabhupada, I sit with him and try to tease out more details in his replies, knowing how much the recipient depends on his every word. Despite the urgent, worried sound of many letters, Srila Prabhupada remains calm, unrattled by events being described therein. He compartmentalizes—when it’s time for business, it is solid business with every stroke of tough thinking; as soon as that is over, he’s back to the simple bliss of whatever is going on around him in the moment.

“Shyamasundar,” he says, his eyes peeking around the seat in front.

“Yes, Prabhupada?”

“Do you not remark the clouds below? We are flying in Krishna’s sunlight, while below they are seeing only darkness.”

I can’t imagine doing anything else but this, sailing along in my master’s wake, not yet seeing how my life will change so radically in a few months’ time.

After a few days in the warm Los Angeles sun, Prabhupada’s health was back and he was feeling chipper in New Dwaraka, his favorite place of respite. Here, senior devotees managed everything well, there was good cash flow, and no big problems. Prabhupada and Karandhar shifted some money around and bought an apartment house at 9701 Venice Boulevard, on the corner of Watseka, to expand the L.A. operations. Prabhupada spoke mornings in the temple room from Srimad-Bhagavatam, walked on the beach, sat in his garden, and translated all night, laid-back in lotus land. I spent my days editing the philosophy book, typing long letters, and sending Prabhupada’s telegrams to his Indian leaders, advising them on affairs in Juhu, Vrindaban, and Mayapur. We passed two quiet weeks in Los Angeles, and on August 31 we flew to Pittsburg. From there, we motored one and a half hours in a black Lincoln Continental into the West Virginia hills, from freeways onto increasingly smaller back-country roads, sixty-eight miles to New Vrindaban.

Kirtanananda and Hayagriva had purchased New Vrindaban’s original 114 acres in 1968, to establish ISKCON’s first Krishna conscious farm community. Since then, they had expanded the property to 365 acres of hilly grasslands and deciduous forests, and had built a temple and several other buildings on the land. Prabhupada had stayed here in an old wooden farmhouse for a month back in May and June 1969, with about twenty devotees in residence. Now, only three years later, as we drove up the dirt road and pulled to a stop, Prabhupada beamed from ear to ear to see about four hundred devotees in dhotis, saris, and rough work clothes rush to greet him. We had seen lots of big devotee crowds in India, but this was different: for the first time we’re seeing hundreds of red-blooded American devotees. For me it was like walking into Golden Gate Park for the Human Be-In in 1967: “Wow, there are lots of us now!”

Children running around, women, some old friends, sann-yasis wielding their staffs of authority—there’s a guy on a horse! Kirtanananda and Hayagriva are here, of course, and there’s Rupanuga in granny glasses, Bali Mardan in his granny glasses, yes, Vishnujan—and all the swamis: Hridayananda, Pushta Krishna, Satsvarupa, Sudama in from Tokyo. There’s Jayatirtha from L.A. and Jayadwaita from New York. Most of these devotees are strangers to me—I’ll never learn all the names—but their childlike glee and spontaneous love for Prabhupada make them my familiar friends. I see Ritchie, the American guy we’d met in India, his shaggy hair tied back, scrubbing a huge fire-blackened pot near the cooking area, and I’m glad to see he’s engaged at last in humble service.

Prabhupada will speak his “Bhagavat Dharma Discourses” here each day for a week. But this occasion will be different from the Ratha-yatras or the festivals in India, where we were a few devotees in a sea of others. Krishna devotees have traveled from all parts of the US and Canada to be here exclusively with Prabhupada, to be in his intimate company without distraction from outsiders, to party together in sacred celebration. After so many cities, freeways, and timetables, this week at New Vrindaban is a welcome country interlude for Srila Prabhupada, like a down-home, front-porch gospel kirtan hoe-down, with cottonwood tufts blowing across green swaying grass, cicadas buzzing, the lazy end of summer, stands of oak, ash, and hickory marking distant fence lines.

By 1972, the late-’60s hippie revolution occurring in cities like San Francisco and New York had spread out into the countryside. Hippies wanted a simpler lifestyle, free of drugs and money, and self-sustaining utopian communes were taking root around the US. Mother Earth News magazine covered the latest trends in this back-to-nature movement, and the July 1972 issue featured— among articles like “How to Do a Recycling Center,” “Herdsman’s Handbook for the Modern Homesteader,” and “Macrame a Hammock”—an article by Howard Wheeler (Hayagriva das) called “New Vrindaban: The Making of a Village.” Hayagriva wrote:

There are seemingly endless problems of finance and manpower. There has to be money to buy property and building materials. Then the construction has to be carried out by people who know more than just how to drive a nail. Wells have to be dug, sewage disposed, roads built, land plowed, gardens tended, supplies brought in, houses winterized, rooms heated, hay cut and baled, cows and horses fed ... if you don’t believe in Krishna when you start, you’re sure to believe in Him by the time you get through.

New Vrindaban is divided into sectors named after the sacred forests of Vrindaban. Prabhupada and his servants are quartered in a farmhouse in Madhuban, about a mile from the newly finished temple in Bahulaban. The temple is home to spectacular Deities, the proprietors of New Vrinadaban, Their Lordships Sri Sri Radha-Vrindavan Chandra. Kirtanananda has erected a pandal-like pavilion with an aluminum-sheet roof on top of Govardhan Hill, the highest summit in the area. You can see the gleaming structure about a quarter mile up the hill from the temple—across the pasture, past those cows, just follow that line of electric poles. Up there, in a couple of days, on September 2, Prabhupada will officially open his discourses.

The weather has been bad. In August, the aftermath of Hurricane Agnes—at that time, the costliest storm to hit the US in recorded history-pounded West Virginia with eight inches of rain, but the sun is out today for Prabhupada’s arrival and, despite mud and scant facility, the devotees are ecstatic, camped in vans and tents around the property, fanning out on the grass in front of the temple to hear their spiritual master’s inaugural address:

This bhagavata-dharma was spoken by Bhagavan Himself. Bhagavan is Lord Sri Krishna, bhaga-van. It is a Sanskrit word. Bhaga means “fortunes,” and van means “one who possesses.” These two words combined together makes the word Bhagavan, or “the supreme fortunate.” The Lord is Bhagavan and the devotee is bhagavata. So bhagavata-dharma means the relationship between the devotees and the Lord. So our human being has got a particular type of business. That business is to study or to discuss on the bhagavata life. We should try to understand Bhagavan, our relationship: bhagavata-dharma. So it is very easy. How? Now simply you have to hear about Krishna. If we simply hear about Him, then we remember. It is a simple process.

The next day, Friday, September 1, we celebrated Janmasthami, Krishna’s birthday, in the temple room, and Prabhupada initiated about twenty new devotees. The following day was Vyasa-puja, Prabhupada’s birthday, and we trudged up the slippery grass-covered slopes to the hilltop pavilion. Prabhupada arrived by the back road in a VW Beetle. It’s Labor Day weekend, so neighbors and visitors from Moundsville and Pittsburg swelled the audience to maybe eight hundred people, and we could all feel that this was a landmark event—the great sage speaking eternal wisdom to inquiring minds gathered on a mountaintop.

Bhaktivedanta Swami humbly explains to the crowd that today’s glorification of the guru is not what it seems:

An outsider may see it that “Why a person is being worshiped like God?” There may be some doubt. So this is the etiquette. This ceremony is called Vyasa-puja. Vyasa was the original author of Vedic literature. He received the knowledge from Narada. Narada received the knowledge from Brahma. Brahma received the knowledge from Krishna. So in this way, by disciplic succession, we get transcendental knowledge handed down... So this Vyasa-puja means one day in a year, on the birthday of the spiritual master, because he is representative of Vyasa, he is delivering the same knowledge which has come down by disciplic succession without any change, he is offered the respect. And the spiritual master receives all honor, all contribution, on behalf of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, not for his person ... Don’t misunderstand that “This person is sitting very comfortably and taking all honors and contribution.” It is needed just to teach them how to respect the representative of God.

Baradraj struck a chord on the harmonium and slowly started a kirtan; mridangas thumped in, kartals, the pace picked up—and Vishnujan took over the lead, his rich voice rising to incredible emotion as Prabhupada sat stunned, eyes shut, moving his head back and forth, then himself took over the lead.

Satyanarayan das: At that moment, everyone went absolutely mad. There was a rush toward the stage, we were crushed into the stage, and it looked like a thousand arms were outstretched to Srila Prabhupada. Devotees were all crying. Prabhupada’s chanting was so beautiful, it’s almost like the sky opened up, love of God came down and poured over all of us.

One by one, sannyasis first, the devotees come before Prabhupada, lay down some flowers or a wrapped gift, some cloth—it's a birthday party after all—then fall flat before him in full surrender. With so many devotees, this takes an hour. Then we hear shouting—“Make way!”—and eight men stagger into the tent bearing a giant cake. For days, half a dozen cooks had baked in wood-fired ovens made from oil-barrels, stacking cakes a layer at a time, until they had a cake six feet square and about six feet high. It was set before an amazed Prabhupada, all eyes glued on him while he tastes a small piece. Then he gives the signal, and devotees rush the cake and devour every crumb within minutes.

The September 4 issue of The New York Times featured an article by Jon Nordheimer. Below the banner “Young Ascetics Honor Lord Krishna”—and two large photos—Nordheimer wrote:
