On the crown of a lovely green hill in the West Virginia countryside, the faithful gathered this week to chant the name of Lord Krishna and kneel at the feet of their spiritual master, A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada... The members exhibit the enthusiasm of Jesus Freaks, the abstinence of monks, and the persistence of a sidewalk Salvation Army drum-beater ... And all display the beatific look of rapture as the chanting rises in volume and intensity ... They sojourned here in buses and vans, some hitchhiked and some came by commercial transport for thousands of miles to praise Krishna at his festival of birth and listen to Prabhupada spread the wisdom of 5,000 years of succession through disciples.

The whole scene was primitive, a harkening back to basics, and in that mood the devotees fashioned a palanquin mounted on two poles and, for the next six days, four stout, bare-chested men hauled Prabhupada up and down Govardhan Hill—straight out of a movie like Ben-Hur or Quo Vadis. Pandemonium, the unruly mob, flashlights and torches snaking down the hill in the dark, smoke, dust, mud, lightning—the kids had sparklers—Krishna’s legions with standards raised, cohorts cheering and ululations of victory over pagan armies. Srila Prabhupada, the hero and emperor—now grinning, now serious—hoisted by centurions above the roar and chanting of the troops, horns, pipes, and clashing cymbals.

From his vyasasan, Prabhupada informed the gathered masses:

So there was a great meeting. Just like we are holding here meeting for a few days, a similar meeting was held thousands of years ago, at least four thousand years ago, in a place which is called Naimisharanya. Naimisharanya is in India. It is near Lucknow. The station is called now Nimshar. It is in Hardoi district. So still the place is there, and if you sometimes go to India you can visit this place. It is a very nice place for spiritual atmosphere. So formerly all the great sages used to assemble in that Naimisharanya forest. It is said that all the demigods used to visit that place. So in that great meeting, Bhagavata was discussed.

For six days Prabhupada thrilled us, building a palpable connection to our own ancient history. His voice rose from deep silence on that hilltop, above the chirping of crickets and the lowing of cows somewhere in the dusk, and transported us back into time immemorial when the Absolute Truth was revealed to submissive ears. He spoke with a humble yet authoritative voice, clearing his throat between sentences, smacking his lips and tongue as he talked—a liquid sound—as if he was tasting something delicious. Deep-throated, slow, rising with emphasis, speeding up to a countertenor, stopping, looking around, questioning, like, See what I mean? or, Can you believe it? Stabbing a gesture, then withdrawing it—reforming it sideways, and slipping it home. Sometimes an “uh” between thoughts to give us a chance to catch up. His mobile eyebrows bushy and strangely black. “Krish” like he’s crushing a succulent fruit—“na!” like a thunderclap over the hills, “Krish-na!” like an avalanche in a far ravine.

Hallelujah days in Appalachia.

One morning near the end of our stay at New Vrindaban, Nanda Kumar told me he had met a girl and that he might get married and leave Prabhupada’s service. What?! I am truly shocked—can’t believe it. How could ... ? “Yes,” he said, “sex desire has disturbed me so much I cannot in good conscience remain as Prabhupada’s personal servant.” Later, others said they saw it coming; I had no clue. OK, Nanda Kumar doesn’t show up some nights, and when we were in Los Angeles he was always “in the kitchen” when Prabhupada called for him. It seems he’d met this pretty young brahmacharini in L.A. last June and they’d been furtively spending time together ever since.

“OK”, I say, “but you better be straight with Prabhupada and tell him what’s going on right now.” He’s undecided, he says, as he reluctantly disappears into Prabhupada’s room, but I can see my dear friend Nanda Kumar is slipping away.

Nanda Kumar’s stout physical presence, his equanimity, fortitude under harsh conditions, and his easy sense of humor-how can I ever forget him as the pujari waving incense in circles before Radha and Krishna, bare-chested in that freezing tent at Allahabad, perfect rapture in his eyes? How can I count the times with deep love he brought Prabhupada his lunch, or medicine, or soap and a towel? And Prabhupada’s eyes would always light up to see him.

Kirtanananda has found a replacement for Nanda Kumar, Srutakirti das, a twenty-year-old Italian lad (Vinnie Fiorentino) from Philadelphia. He’s easy-going, tall, and handsome in the American way. Srutakirti has a big white-toothed smile, and a simple, unaffected demeanor. On September 7 he begins his training, with Nanda Kumar showing him the ropes. Prabhupada breaks him in gently.

Srutakirti: Srila Prabhupada picked up a lota on the floor by his desk and said in a deep voice, “OK, come with me” We walked outside. “All right, wait here,” he instructed. Prabhupada walked about another fifty feet into a wooded area. After a few minutes he returned to the edge of the pavement where I stood. As he walked by me he handed me the lota and said, “Wash this with some dirt and water.” He then walked back into the farmhouse.

On September 8 we drove to the Pittsburgh temple, where Srila Prabhupada would spend one night. He lectured at the famous 3700-seat Syria Mosque, followed by dramas staged by Vishnujan and his Vaikuntha Players, which Prabhupada very much enjoyed. The next day, September 9, we flew to Dallas, Texas.

In late 1971 a group of devotees led by Satsvarupa Maharaja purchased the Mt. Auburn Christian Church at the corner of Gurley Avenue and Graham Street in a residential section of East Dallas, in order to develop a Krishna conscious school (gurukula) for children.

By September 1972 there were about seventy devotees—including thirty children—on the premises, and Satsvarupa was eager for Prabhupada’s personal instructions on how the school should be run. It was a heart-rending moment when we pulled up to the curb and saw these children, from four to maybe ten years old, their bright faces adorned with tilak, the boys with shiny shaved heads and yellow dhotis and the girls in pigtails and little dresses, lined up on the sidewalk to greet Srila Prabhupada and so sweetly singing the Hare Krishna mantra.

The weather was very hot over the next few days as Prabhupada attended classes and gave the kids personal instruction in reading, writing, finger-painting, and arithmetic. He listened to them chant the Bhagavad-gita in unison, their tiny voices forcing tears from his eyes. The children were shy at first, deeply affected by the godlike grandfather figure in their midst. But as Prabhupada gave them tickles and showed them his childish side, the kids gradually shed their awe and began playing tricks on him, like hide-and-seek; but when he called for attention and held up a stick, they listened carefully and mimicked how he held his pencil and formed akara, the Sanskrit letter A.

Prabhupada was training the teachers, too, how to transmit practical knowledge in a Krishna conscious manner. Despite hundreddegree temperatures, we trooped around the school and grounds, and Prabhupada critiqued all aspects of the teachers’ behavior, saying over and over that they must be living examples for the children.

The Dallas temple had acquired a four-hundred-year-old set of four-foot high Radha-Krishna Deities from India, and Prabhupada agreed to install Them on September 12. When he examined Them the day before the installation, he was shocked to see that someone had removed the paint on Their faces! Prabhupada exploded: “Why you have done this?!” When Satsvarupa explained they would repaint Them that night with fast-drying paint, Prabhupada was not convinced. Maybe it was the heat, maybe it was increasing symptoms all over the world that his disciples were becoming overambitious, or that his movement was growing too fast to maintain high standards—but just like in Amsterdam a few weeks earlier, Prabhupada’s anger ripped at our hearts. Walking past the freshly cut stump of a once giant oak, Prabhupada asked, “Who has cut this tree?” When Satsvarupa timidly said, “Er, Prabhupada, we had to cut it. There was danger it would fall on the school,” Prabhupada erupted again. “This tree, it has taken a thousand years to grow, and you decide and you kill it in minutes?” He was furious, disgusted. I felt sorry for Satsvarupa, but knew Prabhupada’s anger was directed at me also—at all of us—for our blunders and shortcomings in serving Krishna.

Five minutes later, Prabhupada would look at flowers blooming in a planter by the path and say, “Oh, very nice. You are making everything beautiful for Krishna,” and praise us in that way. We were his beloved children too, not much different from the six-year-olds inside their classrooms, who had to be shown the stick from time to time to help us advance.

Over the past couple of years, Prabhupada had told me several times that when Saraswati was old enough, four or five, I should enroll her here, in the Dallas gurukula. I was very attached to my daughter—seeing these kids made me miss her more—and I was vaguely averse to the idea of sending her to boarding school at such young age, but as we roamed around I thought, Well, it’s not so bad here. She needs to go to school soon, I’m not settled anywhere, and the children look so pure and happy. Maybe it’s not such a bad idea.

The Deities, Sri Sri Radha-Kalachandaji, were installed without incident (the paint was dry), and on September 13 we jetted back to Los Angeles.

**\* \* \***

Prabhupada will spend a month in L.A., resting, reorganizing, taking stock of KC expansion around the globe, addressing hotspots and the problems cropping up with the so-much-so-fast pace of his movement’s growth. He will fire off more than fifty letters. We’re also preparing for another round-the-world trip. So far, the plan looks like this: we’ll spend the winter months in India, first in Vrindaban, then maybe doing pandal programs in New Delhi, Hyderabad, Bombay, and Calcutta. After that, we’ll see.

The days here in L.A. begin as usual, with Prabhupada’s morning walks on Venice Beach, discussing science with Swarup Damodar and a few straggling devotees straining to hear every word above the sounds of surf, wind, and the screeching of gulls. Walking along the beach on September 19 Prabhupada surprises me with a question that enters deep into my heart:

Prabhupada: Nunam pramattah kurute vikarma. Everyone is mad after sense gratification. They are doing all nonsense. Vikarma means prohibited action. If you constantly break the laws of the state, you will be liable for punishment. But if an outlaw does not know this, and if he wants to be happy, how he can be happy? What is the reply, Shyamasundar?

Shyamasundar: Er—

Prabhupada: He does not know. Kleshada asha dehah. You have tried many things in your past life to become happy, but because it was simply breaking the law, you could not.

Shyamasundar: You can’t beat the law. There’s no way you can escape the law. No criminal ever escapes free. He gets punished in the end.

Prabhupada: Even he can escape the man-made laws without any fail, laws of karma will punish. He is thinking, “Now I am first-class thief.”

Shyamasundar: Most criminals, when they begin to break the law, they get puffed up, proud, because they’re getting away with something. As soon as they get puffed up like that, automatically they give themselves away. Then they can be detected. They get careless.

Prabhupada: These rascals do not know that for breaking ordinary laws there are so many arrangements by the state: FBI department, this department, that department. And God has no department? They have taken God as fool number one—that they can do anything and everything, and God will not be able to check. He does not know that God is residing within your heart. He is looking every second, every moment, to your activities, and it is being noted down. Anumanta upadrshta. Always seeing what you are doing, what you want: “All right, take it.” They do not know; that is a fact. But when you give them knowledge, they will not accept, because they are obstinate. This is foolishness. A man is ignorant, that is not fault. But when he is given the knowledge, and he does not accept, then he is rascal number one.

Prabhupada’s words hit me like a thunderclap. I go quiet, thoughts racing through my head: “Outlaw—that’s me, pretending, doing one thing and thinking another, duplicitous, getting away with it, but guilty to the core. How long can I pretend? Where is this going? I’m not surrendered, really surrendered. I’m just pretending, cheating to stay ahead of the game, that’s me he’s talking about, the first-class thief.” Conversation dims to murmurs, minutes pass as I struggle with these destructive thoughts, remembering some words from an old Platters’ song, “I seem to be what I’m not, you see ... ”, rescued finally by Prabhupada’s comment on the path we’re treading:

Prabhupada: This path is made very recently. It is cracked and broken. Is it new?

Shyamasundar: They tried to pour concrete directly onto the sand, without any foundation.

Prabhupada: Just see.

Every morning for nearly a month in Los Angeles Prabhupada speaks from the First Canto of Srimad-Bhagavatam, describing the many incarnations of Krishna through millions of years and guiding us to this realization:

So this sankirtan movement, Hare Krishna movement, is also incarnation of Krishna. Nama-rupe krsna-avatara. Krishna can become incarnation in sound form also. Especially in this age, Krishna is incarnated in His name. So the incarnation has already come. Those who will take shelter of this incarnation of Krishna’s name, they will be never annihilated. Take it for granted. You haven’t got to ask anybody, “Can you show me God? Have you seen God?” These questions are sometimes made. And where is the difficulty to see God? You don’t like to see God. That is the difficulty. Otherwise there is no difficulty..Therefore one should try to become a devotee. Then everything will be disclosed to him. Svayam eva sphuraty adah. God will be revealed, and without any cessation twenty-four hours, you will see God. So as it is prescribed in the authorized shastras and directed by spiritual master, if you follow the principles, it is not very difficult to see God, to understand God. It is very easy.

The three major temple projects in India are languishing, and Prabhupada wants to set them right, get the ducks in a row before he travels there in October. He sends long letters and cables to his field generals in India: Giriraj, Tamal Krishna, Bhavananda, Gurudas, andjayapataka. Here’s kind of how things stood in India in late September 1972:

Bombay: The project at Hare Krishna Land is Prabhupada’s first priority. Since his last visit to Juhu in March, devotees there have lived rough for six months, camping through the pestilential monsoon summer, and many are sick. Some have left, and the rest are a weakened and disheartened bunch. Even stalwart Giriraj hints he’d like to leave. Sumati Morarjee has returned home from London, and she and Giriraj have formed a trust with powerful locals like

Ashok Birla to shore up ISKCON’s efforts in Juhu. But things have become very complicated. ISKCON has paid out two lakhs of rupees on the fourteen-lakh sale price, but landowner Mr. A. B. Nair has not delivered the sale conveyance, claiming that five lakhs of rupees in taxes must be paid to the Charity Commission before they will release the property. Prabhupada sees this as a dirty trick, but advises Giriraj to borrow five lakhs from Bombay banks to pay Mr. Nair off. He also recommends taking out this notice in the Bombay newspapers:

The International Society for Krishna Consciousness has purchased the land in Juhu, Bombay-54, known as Nairwadi, plots, under purchase agreement dated February 8, 1972, and we have advanced the requisite money under the Sales Agreement. The other party, Mrs. A. B. Nair, has not, however, completed the business under some pretext. Under the circumstances, if someone attempts to purchase the land, he does so at his own risk as we are already in possession of the purchase agreement signed by Mrs. A. B. Nair.

On September 30 Prabhupada writes to Tamal Krishna and Bhavananda that Giriraj “is a child in these worldly dealings, so immediately go to Bombay to help him  If things can be settled mutually even by paying 5 lakhs more on the account of purchase agreement, do it and settle up the things properly, otherwise let us go to the court for specific action, either civil or criminal, against the tactics of Mr. Nair.”

Tamal Krishna Goswami was a tough guy. India was maybe the most difficult place on the planet to get anything done, and TKG had been chosen to manage ISKCON’s Indian affairs since Prabhupada had formed the GBC in 1970. If anyone could get the job done, it was Tamal Krishna.

Mayapur: Construction on the guest house continues. Jayapataka and his team in Calcutta are raising funds, buying building materials, hauling them to Mayapur and, though slowed by heavy monsoon rains, the work is going on. Prabhupada writes to Tamai Krishna, “I am very, very much pleased to hear about the progress in Mayapur, and this working with one hundred men both day and night is the American style. If you do something wonderful at Mayapur that will be my credit and I can truly be called the guru of the Americans. But if you do not do anything there, then that will be a great discredit for me and I will not defeat my Godbrothers as I desire.” And then he adds: “You mention ‘bullock for transporting supply,’ and it has cost you Rs. 1800/. I think that is exorbitant price for one bullock ... One bicycle was purchased for Rs. 300/-, but these things anyone will give you, why you are spending money unnecessarily?”

Vrindaban: The village of Krishna’s youth will be our next port of call. Starting in October, Prabhupada wants to stay there for a month in his rooms at the Radha-Damodar temple, lecturing every day in the adjoining courtyard. He instructs Gurudas to get his rooms ready, clean the courtyard, and find living accommodations for an influx of devotees from around the world. Prabhupada, master attorney, even draws up the lease agreement for the Radha-Damodar temple. I type it and send it off, and Gurudas and Yamuna, with their wily diplomatic skills, somehow convince the temple sevites (priests)—who have been feuding with each other for centuries—to sign it:

(1)    The Lease Agreement is for the part of the Temple covered by the renovated house as in the plan annexed hereto for the purpose of occupation by the Lessee;

(2)    The Lease period shall be for the duration of 25 (Twenty-five) years, with option to renew by mutual consent;

(3)    The Lessee will pay Rs. 200/- (Rupees two-hundred) per month inclusive of all taxes and rates such as water, house, etc., whatsoever, and the mode of payment shall be by monthly cash-pay order through the Punjab National Bank at Vrindaban by the first week every month for the Seva Puja at Sri Sri Radha Damodaja Temple. A copy of the letter showing cash order of the Punjab National Bank is annexed herewith;

(4)    The half-finished renovation work still to be done must be completed immediately by the Lessor Sri Madan Mohan Goswami;

(5)    The Lessor will deliver immediate vacant possession of the whole building;

(6)     As personal respect for the Lessor Sri Madan Mohan Goswami, the Lessee will always receive him or his son only as guest at any time he or his son may visit Vrindaban, and either of them will be offered one room for his comfortable stay as long as he likes and while staying as guest he would be supplied with boarding and lodging free of all charges;

(7)    After three years of practical experience, if the Lessee or the Lessor feels any inconvenience, then either of them will be at liberty to cancel this Lease Agreement by serving six month calendar clear notice;

(8) If the Lease Agreement is however cancelled as it is mentioned in clause (7) then the Lessee notwithstanding will continue to occupy the two rooms as marked in the siteplan and will pay rent as usual Rupees Five Only per month as at present.

Over the past few months Gurudas and Yamuna have made friends with the Maharaja of Bharatpur, Sri Brijendra Singh, who is the last in a thousand-year line of Jat ksatriya kings ruling this small princely state in Rajasthan, about thirty miles southwest of Vrindaban. Singh’s ancestors had built a fabulous palace in Vrindaban, fronting the Yamuna River at the spot where Krishna killed the horse-demon Keshi and then took His bath. By 1972 the Keshighat buildings were in an advanced state of neglect and disrepair, the royal coffers were empty, and the Maharaja had offered to sell the place to ISKCON. Prabhupada didn’t want to divert funds from building our own temple in Raman Reti, so he advised his Vrindaban leaders: “They have lost their kingdom but still they have these buildings, so if they are given in our hand it will perpetually commemorate their nice gift to the ISKCON institution and thus to the world The Maharaja will be welcome to stay \[in ISKCON facilities\] as long as he wishes, especially in New York, Los Angeles, Dallas, London, Bombay, etc. In this way, try to induce him... We will occupy immediately and renovate and fill the place with foreign tourists.”

Somehow or other, Gurudas and Yamuna convinced the Maharaja to let us occupy the Keshighat palace, on a trial basis, for the month that Prabhupada will be in Vrindaban, and a team of devotees set to work to clean out the debris, the scorpions, snakes, and bats—at least make the place somewhat habitable.

Meanwhile, London was also calling. Kaushika, Dhananjaya, and others had been looking at big properties in and around London, sending frequent letters to Prabhupada. They even mentioned a huge palace in Berkshire, allegedly built by the Duke of Windsor, now up for sale.

Prabhupada decides to send Karandhar to London to look at the properties and see what can be done. On September 25 he writes to Rebatinandan: “Karandhara is coming there in a few days and he will see how everything is going on, because Shyamasundar must remain with me. But if Shyamasundar’s presence is also required for getting that new place, he can also come there.”

As the GBC man for the UK, I’m torn between rushing to London to assist or happily staying by Prabhupada’s side. I write to Ksirodaksayee in London, soliciting his help with the Indian community: “I understand from Dhananjaya and George that we shall be getting a place, and that it will cost us about £200,000. Most probably we shall take a bank mortgage and make regular monthly payments against the mortgage for about ten years... Your cooperation is very much required at this time to help us meet this monthly sum.”

Karandhar phones Prabhupada from London and says, yes, these properties look good, but Shyamasundar must come here to negotiate with George. Nuts! OK, so we make a plan: We have our round-the-world plane tickets, westward from California to New Delhi via Hawaii and Manila; I will rebook my ticket to fly eastward to India via London, and meet Prabhupada later in Vrindaban; Karandhar will fly from London down to Bombay to help Giriraj in Juhu. And Prabhupada instructs that I should also stop in Bombay en route to Delhi to lend Giriraj a hand. The pace of Srila Prabhupada’s global expansion is getting heavier by the minute.

**\* \* \***

On October 1, 1972, I fly to London, sad to leave Prabhupada, but knowing that he’ll go west around the world while I’m moving east, and that we’ll meet in the middle in two weeks’ time.

At Bury Place, I sleep in the cramped attic with forty other men; first-come, first-served for a 6’ x 3’ space. It’s maybe a foot over your nose to a rafter, so watch your head, mate! And it’s stifling hot up there, five stories above the street. Before dawn each day for a week I wait in a long queue for a one-minute cold shower, and dry off with yesterday’s dhoti. Then, with Kaushika, Jai Hari, and a few other devotees, I go look at a couple of big buildings in London, including Kingsway Hall at 75 Kingsway in Holborn, about a twenty-minute walk from Bury Place. The huge seven-story building was the former West London Mission of the Methodist Church, and we’d been introduced to its minister, Lord Donald Soper, by our friend Lord Fenner Brockway. Soper is a fiery ecclesiastic, known for his radical causes. He’s a loud opponent of alcohol, gambling, and blood sports, often speaking to large crowds at Hyde Park Speakers’ Corner. Prabhupada had met Baron Soper three years ago at Westminster Abbey, where they’d had a bit of an argument about Christianity, but Soper respects the Swami and his disciples and what we’re trying to do in England, and we have high hopes for this place.

Kingsway Hall has a 2500-seat auditorium, large meeting rooms and offices—all great—but only limited residential quarters, and certainly not enough for a hundred devotees. When we see, too, that the roof has collapsed—back in 1969, we’re told—we have to strike this place off our list.

I went out to see George at Friar Park, brought him up to date, and told him that the places we’d looked at just weren’t suitable. He said, “Well, keep looking. Krishna will find us the right place in good time.” He assured me that he’d either pay for a place or stand guarantor, however it worked out. Meanwhile, George said, he’s got Apple Studios booked for the next couple months, starting next week, to record his next album. He wants to call it Living in the Material World, and he grabs a guitar and sings me some lyrics to the title tune:

While I’m living in the material world

Not much giving’ in the material world

Got a lot of work to do

Try to get a message through

And get back out of this material world.

I’m living in the material world

Living in the material world

I hope to get out of this place

by the LORD SRI KRSNA’S GRACE

My salvation from the material world.

“Jeez, George, that’s fantastic! You’ve hit it on the head!”

“Yeah, Shyamsundar, but no matter how good you are, you still need grace to get out. You can be a yogi or a monk or a nun, but without God’s grace you still can’t make it.”

George, the preacher man!

On October 8 I fly to Bombay, frustrated that we still haven’t got a big place in London. How will I report such a failure to Srila Prabhupada? But I’m buoyed, too, by George’s news about his latest album: Man, those words—he's spreading Prabhupada’s message of Krishna consciousness like no one else in history! On the nine-hour flight I dwell on Prabhupada’s amazing power. Despite his sweet demeanor and tiny physical presence, somehow he’s changing the world. I’m counting the hours till I see him again.

**\* \* \***

It’s balmy in Bombay now that the rainy season is over, and I’m happy to see Giriraj, Tamal Krishna, Yadubara, and other strong devotees camped out under coconut palms at Juhu. Karandhar is here too, thank God—if anyone can straighten out this boondoggle with Mr. Nair, Tamal and Karandhar can. But wait. These guys look like earthquake survivors! Even Karandhar looks sheepish, pale, shaking his head, defeated by these affairs. It seems Nair has just canceled the sale agreement! He saw the legal notice Giriraj had placed in newspapers, announcing that ISKCON had purchased the Juhu land, and flipped out. Nair now says the two-lakh down-payment is his to keep—the deal is off. He has shut off the devotees’ water supply, hired hoodlums to harass people coming in and out of the temple grounds, and is circulating nasty leaflets about the Hare Krishnas at local train stations. On top of that, our lawyer, Mr Diwanji, has resigned from the case, and we suspect he’s in cahoots with Nair. Prabhupada has thrown us into the cement mixer of the byzantine Indian legal system, and we’re having a tough time sorting out what’s going on. We’re a bunch of young American guys without a shred of legal experience among us (on this side of the law, that is).

So our first job is to find a good lawyer. Parsi lawyers are recommended, as they are British-trained and notably fair, bridging the emotional barrier between Hindus and Muslims. We ask R. C. Dhruva, secretary to R. D. Birla, who the best attorneys are in Bombay, and on his advice we retain Mulla & Mulla, a big firm downtown. Day after day we taxi an hour from Juhu to the Fort district to consult our Parsi lawyers: four lanky white men in dhotis, clutching briefcases and dashing down Dalal Street and Mahatma Gandhi Road, late for their appointment.

And on the way home each night we always got a laugh seeing the jumbo prow of a Japan Airlines DC-8 jutting halfway across Swami Vivekanand Road, wings buckled and both engines gone. Seems a few weeks back the pilot mistook Juhu Aerodrome for the main Bombay airport, landed on the short runway, jumped the ditch at the end, and halted in the middle of the road. Fortunately, no one was injured or killed.

The legal situation with Nair looks grim, and we feel beaten, in over our heads. Besides that, we all sense that Juhu is simply too far away from Bombay, not central enough for effective preaching, and with all those tenants on the property, the bad vibes, maybe we should just drop this scheme. Sure, Prabhupada wants the place very badly, but is it worth the effort? We write a joint letter to Prabhupada outlining the situation, and on October 18 I hop a flight to Delhi, then take a taxi down to Vrindaban to deliver it to him.

Prabhupada plans to stay in Vrindaban for the month of Karttika, urja-vrata, the holiest season of the year. Hot summer monsoons are finished, the Yamuna River plain is lush and green, crops are flourishing, and everyone is in a celebratory mood. Prabhupada’s staying in his old rooms at the Radha-Damodar temple.

The Radha-Damodar temple was built by Srila Rupa Goswami in 1542, and when he died in 1596, the great saint was buried in the courtyard that now adjoins Prabhupada’s rooms. For the better part of ten years, before coming to the West, Srila Prabhupada had sat in his rooms here looking out at Rupa Goswami’s tomb, communing with his spirit and hatching the Hare Krishna movement. Now he has returned to instruct his foreign disciples from Rupa Goswami’s Bhakti-rasamrita Sindhu, or “Nectar of Devotion,” for the next thirty days. Four hundred and twenty years ago Rupa Goswami wrote his Bhakti-rasamrita Sindhu as a guidebook for devotional service. This sacred text defines spiritual practices and Vaishnava behavior, the stages and ecstasies leading to prema, pure unalloyed love of God and the goal of life for all aspiring Krishna devotees. In 1970 Prabhupada had written a summary study of this work, and he will now speak from The Nectar of Devotion every evening in the sixty-foot space between Rupa Goswami’s tomb and bhajan kutir, the place under mango and tamal trees where Rupa chanted and meditated more than four centuries earlier.

Passing through the narrow lanes of Vrindaban on a rickshaw I observe Krishna pilgrims from all parts of India swelling the otherwise small population. Busloads of mostly small-village Indian devotees are on parikram, walking around the hundreds of temples and holy sites, and the air is filled with Krishna’s names. For the first time in history, fifty Western bhaktas have joined these pious Indian pilgrims for the Karttika season, and Rupa Goswami’s chosen successor will address an international audience. Another huge rhino for Srila Prabhupada, for sure.

The rickshaw stops before the gate at Radha-Damodar temple, and I enter. There’s Malati, fresh from Calcutta, looking good, a flower in her hair—and Saraswati! She’s four and a half now, all grown up, and when I grab her, lift her, and smother her with kisses, she says, “Dad! That’s not appropriate behavior!” She’s shy in her little pigtails, but happy to see her father. Gurudas and Yamuna are here on the porch to Prabhupada’s rooms, the pioneers of Prabhupada’s movement in Vrindaban, thin from austerity but laughing as we swap stories. And Achyutananda—he’s been in India five years now, speaks the language, knows the ropes.

Then Srutakirti comes out of Prabhupada’s room, beaming, and catches me up on recent events: Prabhupada visited the San Francisco and Berkeley temples, went to Honolulu, and from there, on October 11, he flew to Manila to visit its fledgling ISKCON center. He arrived in Vrindaban three days ago, on October 15. “Man, that was a heavy scene in Manila, but the Filipinos loved him! He’s taking lunch now, but you can go in. He’s been waiting for you.”

I ease into Srila Prabhupada’s room, heart in my throat, so anxious to see him again, your incompetent servant reporting for duty. “Ahhh, you have come,” he says. “Shruto! Just bring a little prasad” We sit and eat with our fingers, Prabhupada mooshing dal with his rice, relishing each bite, smacking his lips, chewing real big around a loose or painful tooth, finishing, dipping the fingers of his right hand into a little bowl of water that Sruta brings while he clears away the dishes. Prabhupada leans back on a cushion-tiny burp—takes a toothpick to his teeth, and calmly asks, “So, what are the affairs in London and Bombay?”

Suddenly the frantic events in England and Juhu seem so far away, so miniscule, compared to seeing Prabhupada and what he’s doing here in Vrindaban. After such dread and uncertainty, feeling the ax about to fall, I feel safe and secure in Prabhupada’s presence. I show him the depressing letter signed by his Juhu leaders, and he patiently goes over our points. Sure enough, within minutes he outlines a point-by-point campaign against Nair, convincing me that our position is actually very strong, and my deep worries slide away. Once again Prabhupada’s perfection returns me to peace.

“You just inform the others. Now take dictation.” And he writes this letter to the boys in Juhu: “There is only some delay, that’s all. That will not be sufficient grounds for Nair to rescind the contract. Rather, our position is very strong so long we are in possession and we have paid him the earnest money and more.... Best thing is to follow the advice of Mulla & Mulla and stick to our position very securely. If there is no possibility of settling with Mr. Nair without any risk to ourselves, then we shall stick to the terms of the original agreement and take the matter to court. How he can say we have defaulted? He has purposefully delayed, so if we take the matter to court he must reply to these charges.”

Prabhupada goes into lawyer mode, answering all legal arguments in his letter and, like Krishna speaking to Arjuna on the battlefield, rallies us back to the fight. Then he composes a telegram to Karandhar:

REMAIN BOMBAY FINISH NAIR BUSINESS STOP IF SETTLED PEACEFULLY IMMEDIATELY BALANCE MONEY SOON STOP IF NO POSSIBILITY OF SETTLEMENT GO TO COURT STOP LETTER FOLLOWS—BHAKTIVEDANTA SWAMI

Next, Prabhupada dictates a letter addressed to Mr. and Mrs. Nair—allegedly from me, and which I will sign—which is all sweetness and light: “I beg to inform you that His Divine Grace has reserved two very nice rooms for your comfortable accommodation just adjacent to his quarters at the temple of Srila Rupa and Srila Jiva Goswamins here in Vrindaban, and furthermore, His Divine Grace extends to you both his most cordial invitation to join him here at your convenience and to stay with him for as long as you like during this most auspicious Karttika vrata season. May I also urge you to oblige the desire of your many ‘children,’ who are staying here with Srila Prabhupada, to see you again! All of us, especially my wife, Malati, and little Saraswati, remember you both with great affection and earnestly relish the thought of having you here with us for some time.”

Hah! This is Prabhupada, the ultimate tactician: his bad-guy/good-guy strategy, carry a big stick but walk softly.

I go upstairs to the roof and settle into the room reserved for

Prabhupada’s servants, just next to his bedroom, and hot-wire an extension cord into a light fixture for my electric typewriter. I’ve brought a heavy transformer from Bombay—size of a car battery— to convert 240V to 110V, and hope it works. Srutakirti informs me that the electricity is intermittent, usually coming on about five p.m., so I’ll have to type at night or in the early morning. He also tells me about the fighting going on around town.

Fighting?

“Yeah, Kulashekar got mobbed in Loi Bazar and he’s hiding out over at Keshighat. Seems the locals think we’re CIA agents or something. Prabhupada thinks it’s his envious godbrothers stirring people up against us. Be careful when you walk around town. And up here, watch out for monkeys. Keep all the doors shut and don’t leave anything out or they’ll grab it.”

Ahh, a month in paradise. I’ve missed the first few days’ lectures, so Sruta plays me the recording of Prabhupada’s inaugural Radha-Damodar speech from the night of the 15th, when he is introduced by his old friend and godbrother, Dr. O. B. L. Kapoor:

We are gathered here today at Radha-Damodara temple. This return back to homeland of Srila Prabhupada is particularly significant, because this time he comes back with a host of his Western disciples. This signifies to me the meeting of the East and the West, the victory of spiritualism over materialism, of good over evil. For the first time in the history of the world, Vaishnavism, the message of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, the message of pure devotion, has been carried to the West, and carried so successfully.

I am sure that historians of the future will have a lot to say about Prabhupada and his movement, and they will be even envious of us for living in a time when this movement was begun and for participating in it. I have a secret realization that at this moment, while we are welcoming Prabhupada, Sri Rupa Goswami, Sri Jiva Goswami, Sri

Krishnadas Kaviraj, whose samadhis signify their eternal presence in the sacred precincts of this temple, are also joining us in welcoming Prabhupada, and they are showering their choicest blessings upon him. I can hear them saying, “Long live Prabhupada! Long live Prabhupada!”

We laugh at the parts of his speech where Prabhupada brags it up for the benefit of his spellbound audience of unworldly villagers, telling them that the round-the-world airline tickets for his party of four came to twenty thousand dollars.

Prabhupada: Twenty thousand dollars means how much in Indian exchange? Two hundred thousand rupees—two lakhs. So our expenditure is going like that. And we print at least fifty thousand books. Yes. We are selling our books, average, at the rate of twenty-five thousand rupees per day.... Now we are trying to purchase the Berkshire palace in England. Yes. That was being occupied by the Duke of Windsor. So the price is five hundred thousand dollars.

Dr. Kapoor: Five hundred thousand dollars?

Prabhupada: Yes. five hundred thousand means fifty million rupees according to Indian exchange rate.

Dr. Kapoor: \[laughing\] Fabulous!

The sun is low in the west now, parrots screeching as they flock to their nests, peacocks calling from trees. We enter the packed-dirt courtyard for tonight’s exposition on The Nectar of Devotion. Pilgrims circumambulate the temple grounds, shouting “Radhe-Shyam!” as Srutakirti lays out Prabhupada’s book and drinking water and I set up the Uher tape recorder. Gurudas fiddles with the rented microphone and small amplifier, and Prabhupada sits on his cushion with his back to a low stacked-stone wall. Ahh, Hayagriva is here, and Kulashekar, out of hiding; Saradiya and Vaikunthanath from San Francisco; Sudama Vipra and Gurukripa, tough Vietnam vets freshly arrived from preaching in the Far East; Smara Hari and others from the UK. In the gathering gloom I see Madri, TKG’s ex-wife, and Malati and her friends Visakha and Srimati.

It’s getting cold now. Saraswati sits in my lap and I wrap her in my shawl. Gurudas heightens the drama by holding up both ends of an electric cord—will there be electricity?—then plugs one into the other and yes! The single bare bulb above Prabhupada’s head comes on, throwing shadows back. Prabhupada leads a kirtan, then begins to speak. “Bhakti-rasa, the mellow relished in the transcendental loving service of the Lord, does not finish with the end of life. It continues perpetually and is therefore called amrita, that which does not die, but exists eternally.”

Rasa means “taste”—something Prabhupada calls a “mellow.” There are five basic rasas, or original “tastes” or relationships we have with God, one or more of which is dominant in each individual: (1) The neutral rasa, or appreciating God’s greatness; (2) The servant-master mentality, where one serves in awe and reverence; (3) Having Krishna as our dear friend, enjoying intimate friendly companionship; (4) The parental rasa, where we consider Him like our child; or (5) One may love Krishna in a conjugal state, as a lover. Once we rediscover our particular eternal relationship with Krishna, we develop devotional life to taste this rasa, progressing through stages of increasing ecstasy, each stage marked by definite symptoms such as dancing, rolling on the ground, laughing like a madman, and becoming stunned.

Just before dawn the next morning, I awake to a woman’s loud, melodious singing as she passes along the narrow road outside our quarters, the most beautiful, haunting, morning melody of the Hare Krishna mantra in a pure, high voice, causing all who listen to meditate on the glories of Krishna and Vrindaban. It’s cold, and the air is tinged with wood smoke from early-morning fires. I hear pilgrims shuffling around in the dark. Sruta lights the kerosene lantern in our room, and I rise, still dressed, from thin pads on the floor, splash cold water on my face, bundle into as many sweaters and shawls as I have, and go into Prabhupada’s room to see what he needs, how he’s doing this morning, wait for instructions, thrilled inside for another magic day in Vrindaban. He’s ready to go down, and I grab the tape recorder and follow him, shining a flashlight into the plunging stairway and outside and across the temple courtyard. Srila Prabhupada bows before the ancient Radha-Damodar Deities, gets anointed with sandal paste and kumkum by the pujari, exits right, past the sacred tulasi bush on its elevated planter, steps through the arched gateway and into Rupa Goswami’s garden.

Visakha dasi: Many devotees lived at Keshighat by the banks of the Yamuna, and every morning, very early, Achyutananda Swami would lead a sankirtan party and we’d go all the way to Raman Reti, where the Krishna-Balaram temple was under construction. Devotees living there would join us, and we’d walk together back along the parikram path to the Radha-Damodar temple in time to meet Srila Prabhupada there.

Narayani dasi: The temple looked like it was bombed out; it was all crumbling. There was a lot of rubble around, and the courtyard was just dust. But it was the same dust where the Six Goswamis used to sit to discuss Bhagavatam. So we were sitting in that same place, four hundred years later, hearing Srila Prabhupada.

As dawn touches pink the ancient crowns of temples around us, devotees wiping sleep from their eyes, some with wet hair from early ablutions, and maybe a dozen local Vrajbasis huddle under blankets and chadars, Prabhupada taps the microphone, clears his throat, and speaks from Srimad-Bhagavatam, First Canto, chapter two. He’s discussing Suta Goswami’s replies to the basic question put to him by the gathered sages, “What shall we do now that Lord Krishna has departed from the earth?” I’ve heard Prabhupada comment on these verses on many occasions, but hearing him now is like hearing the truth for the first time. Little wisps of vapor emerge from his mouth as the sun slowly illumines his body and, as the air warms, he sheds his wrap. We’re transported back to that splendid morning of creation, when everything was new, sinless, and sparkling with possibility.

After the lecture Prabhupada wants to walk around the Radha-Damodar temple precincts, take a loop over to the Yamuna River, and show us some of the sights. I record everything as we stroll.

Shyamasundar: Prabhupada, can you tell us a little bit about the temples as we move along?

Prabhupada: Yes. This temple was constructed by Srila Jiva Goswami, one of the first disciples of Lord Chaitanya ... and this is Narottama samadhi ... Here is Krishnadas Kaviraj Goswami entombed ... and here is Rupa Goswami entombed ... Later on, several other devotees, they are not entombed like them. Flowers from their body—they were placed instead. It is called pushpa samadhi. But here, the original bodies are there ... And this is the original well...

Gurudas: Did the Goswamis drink from this well, Srila Prabhupada?

Prabhupada: Yes ... and this is the place for dining. The Goswamis, they used to dine here, in this room.

As we pass by the huge Keshighat temple, where most devotees are currently staying, Prabhupada gives us his perspective on the dangers of institutional overexpansion:

Suppose if Bharatpur Maharaj gives us this temple. Supposing you have got three men here, then how you shall manage? Simply taking over is no good. Because they are giving, means they cannot manage. That Keshighat property is very valuable property, Bharatpur Maharaj’s place... Up to now we are strong. But if our cooperation becomes slack, then our temple also, the same thing will happen. Manager anyone can become, but manpower required. We have no Indian members chanting Hare Krishna, so how much men we shall import? And whether it is feasible by importing men to manage this facility?

There’s a long pause. No one has a ready answer.

It is just like the mouses are afraid of the cat. So they held a meeting and the resolution was passed that let us fix up a bell on the neck of the cat, so that when he is coming the bell will ring and we shall know, “Now he is coming.” Immediately the resolution was passed. Then, now who will fix up the bell? Anyone who will go to bell the cat, he will be devoured. So this is plan-making. Now go in and fix up the bell.

We settle into a routine. Prabhupada lectures at dawn from Srimad-Bhagavatam; then makes his morning walk; then he takes massage. Then business and talks in his room during the day. Evenings he speaks on The Nectar of Devotion. He works late into the night on letters and translating. His position on Juhu is becoming stronger, and he fires off letter after long letter to the boys in Bombay, with bombshells like:

Nair is bluffing. He is trying to avoid this issue by tricks, and he has dominated you and you are little afraid of him, and he has fooled you to think he is in superior legal position so that you will give him some money. But this is cheating. We shall not give him any more money. Don’t pay him anymore. First of all bring a criminal case against him.

If you are thinking hopeless, then how can make you hopeful? Our point is, he has accepted the money, that means he has agreed to terms of SALE. Take police precaution if he is threatening violence ... Therefore I say that you boys cannot deal very well in these matters, because you are too timid. Now whatever you like you may do. Immediately criminal case should be taken, but that you are not doing because he is bluffing you. He says big words and makes threat and you believe him foolishly and do like he says. That I shall not do.

There are so many Vrindaban leaders, prominent sadhus, and so many devotees visiting Prabhupada’s rooms, that I can’t really wander far from his immediate presence. But these letters to Juhu are important, so one afternoon after lunch, while Prabhupada is resting, I decide to post them myself. I walk through the alleyways of old Vrindaban about a half a mile to the main post office, near Banke Bihari temple. The post office is a small, concrete structure in a large open compound, with windows on one side where you line up to do your business under an awning. I’ve just given the clerk some rupees and am waiting for my change when whap! Something hard slaps across my back! I reach around and grab the end of a thick bamboo stick wielded by a surprised little guy in white shirt and dark pants. I pull the stick from his hands and go after him, whacking him about the shoulders. His buddies attack from all sides, tearing at my dhoti as I spin and try to defend myself. Just before bricks start flying, local Vaishnavas come to my rescue, yelling at these kids to lay off. When the dust settles, I gather my change and receipt, tuck my torn dhoti as best I can and, bruised and dizzy from this surprise encounter, make my way back to Radha-Damodar escorted by a band of shocked citizens.

Prabhupada is outraged. He gathers the chief of police, the mayor, and other municipal authorities in his room and lets them have it, yelling at them. “These boys and girls have given up everything for Krishna. How you can allow this fighting?! We are not CIA. They have come here to see Krishna’s place. They are under my protection. You must stop this immediately!” After that, things seemed to calm down. Our presence in sacred Vrindaban is gradually being accepted, even welcomed, by the vast majority of Vrajbasis.

Every day, our devotees served lunch to hundreds of pilgrims over at Keshighat palace. I’d go over there to gossip and to play with Saraswati or to watch her as she helped her mother serve out prasada. Today she has a pot full of chapatis, this little American kid with blond pigtails walking down a line of dark-skinned villagers from Rajasthan or Uttar Pradesh sitting by banana-leaf plates. She stops before each one, reaches into the pot, and PLOPS a chapati—flap!—onto each plate, then rushes to serve the next. She’s near the end of the line now, reaches into the pot, then looks into the pot, and oops, no more chapatis. People reach out for her, grab her leg, and she has to hold the pot upside down and shake it so they see it’s empty. Cracks us up.

Saraswati went everywhere with us, with Prabhupada. She attended all his lectures, walked with us in the mornings, and always seemed to be in Prabhupada’s room, often playing in there alone with him. She brought him marigolds and bowed down; he’d hand her sweets. They’d yak about Krishna stealing butter, and monkeys, and peacocks. Prabhupada’s favorite trick was hiding something from her, making her look for it, laughing with this old friend who was now in a child’s body.

Yashodanandan das: Saraswati was there in Srila Prabhupada’s room, and she was pulling on the shirt of a Bengali gentleman and she was asking the gentleman, “Who is Krishna? Do you know who is Krishna?” And the man was amused that this little girl would ask him that. Then she told him, “Krishna is the Supreme Personality of Godhead, and you should surrender to Him.” And Prabhupada said, “Just see, she is a guru.”

In Vrindaban Srila Prabhupada speaks day and night on very deep matters. Tonight, his words about consciousness—how my tiny consciousness somehow controls the billions of cells in this one small body, and how Krishna knows what’s going on in every single atom in all the universes—has got my mind reeling. He says, “My consciousness is imperfect in this sense that I am eating something, it is being digested in the stomach, so many secretions are coming out. How they are forming into blood, and so many things are going on within the body, I am not directly conscious. But the

Absolute Truth, the Supreme Being, He knows everything, in any corner of the cosmic manifestation.”

After the lecture I ask Prabhupada’s leave to go for a walk. I just want to be alone for a bit, go to the beach beside the Yamuna, and examine my life, try to wrap my mind around the vastness of Krishna. It’s November 6, Diwali season, the traditional Festival of Lights on the night of the dark moon. Night skies over India are usually obscured this time of year by smoke from millions of cook fires and chilling ground fog, but tonight it’s clear and I can see thousands of stars.

I stretch out on the sand by the Yamuna, keeping an eye out for cobras and human waste, and try to connect. I ponder the fervent devotion of the Vrajbasis and note too, that many of my godbrothers and godsisters are advancing in spiritual life. I’m a fraud by comparison. Can I really do this, like, become so devoted, so sincere? Can I really immerse myself in this foreign culture, learn Sanskrit, become the living example of a Krishna conscious person? I’m more the worker type. I feel Krishna most when I’m pounding nails on a huge construction or lost in words on a typewriter, brainstorming the next big thing. Maybe I’ll be OK. There’s always that one verse in the Bhagavad-gita: “If you cannot practice the regulations of bhakti-yoga, then just try to work for Me, because by working for Me you will come to the perfect stage.” I’m not really one for meditation, and even chanting many rounds is difficult, what to speak of the 4 a.m. to 10 p.m. daily temple routine. I’m still attached to so many material ways of doing things. I’m out of control, really, just riding this wild horse to see where it goes.

I look up at the stars stretching from horizon to horizon. So many millions of planets out there, each one balanced in perfect harmony, and think: Wait a minute. Scientists tell us that it takes maybe a million years for the light from that distant star to reach my eyes, so how do I know if that star still exists? Or, what is really out there now? Modern cosmology is wrong, way off; it’s total speculation. Observations are dependent on seeing light from celestial bodies or events that happened before conceivable time, yet they make so many proud claims: “Based on our measurements, such-and-such star has this-and-that properties.” Wait a second! Had is the better word. We have no idea what the universe really looks like now, right this minute. Prabhupada is right! Certainty about anything in the cosmos can only come from higher authority, starting with the creator of it all.

And this is where that creator walked, on these very sands! Krishna played here. We have so many eyewitness descriptions. He was about five-foot-nine or -ten, had bluish skin, the most beautiful person possible. Full moon on His face, eyes dancing, a sideways glance, garlands bouncing on His back as He runs off up the beach. And Krishna is in my heart at this very moment—He’s in every atom, trillions of atoms in each cubic inch of space, from here in my hand to that farthest star, the light from which, traveling at 670 million miles per hour, takes a million years to reach my eyes!

I sense something nearby, and look past my shoulder to see two six-foot banded kraits—the deadliest snakes on the planetwrapped around each other and slithering rapidly past on their way to the Yamuna River—spawn of the Kaliya demon, right before my eyes. Krishna is here! I can feel Him. My skin tingles—Krishna, Krishna, Krishna from the breeze, in every bush, from every grain of sand breathing on the beach—if I could sense Him like this all the time, surely I would go insane.

Whoosh! Blam! In the skies above Vrindaban fireworks flare, multicolored lights strobe over the beach as they flash and fade. Whooshl—illuminating the temple towers of Vrindaban—the singing sounds of Krishna’s names rise louder and softer from the village ... I flick my weak flashlight here and there to avoid thorns and pick my way back to Prabhupada’s room, floating in a pleasant certainty.

During the last week of our stay in Vrindaban, we learn that Richard Nixon has been reelected president of the United States.

That world seems a million miles away as Prabhupada expands on the topic of cosmic creation in his room.

Prabhupada: Here the mahat-tattva, the total material energy, is called avyakta. Then, when it is agitated by the glance of the Supreme Lord, the three modes of material nature acts. And by interaction of these three modes of material nature, the whole cosmic manifestation comes into being. This is not theory. This is the fact of creation. Creation, there is machine, or there is electronic working and the buttons are pushed by the Supreme Lord. Parasya shaktir vividhaiva shruyate. His electronic machine is so subtle and powerful that we cannot understand. We say “nature.” And due to our ignorance, we cannot explain how the nature is working, so we give an evasive reply. So adau, in the beginning, loka-sukshmendriya, for creating the cosmology, Jagrhe paurusham rupam, the creation is coming from the person. Not from nonperson. The modern scientists, their theory of creation is “There was something matter, a chunk,” or something like that. What do they say? What was the beginning of creation, Hayagriva Prabhu?

Hayagriva: Uh, one theory is that there was a mass that exploded, and all of these universes came out in the explosion. The other is that the universe is a “solid state” universe, that it is constantly existing and regenerating itself. These two theories. One’s called the Big Bang Theory, and the other’s called the “Solid State Theory,” or Steady State. In either case, there was something originally there, which they don’t explain.

Prabhupada: But here we find it quite reasonable. Because everything is from the person. Jagrhe paurusham rupam. Rupam means “form.” So we don’t theorize. We take knowledge from the authority. Of course, the scientists also say they take from authority, but as explained by our Hayagriva Prabhu, it does not appear that their knowledge was taken from authority. It is theory. There are so many theories. But we don’t accept theories. We want solid fact.

Later that evening, I corner Professor Hayagriva on the roof to pick his brain about modern cosmology, try to understand more about the realizations I had a few nights ago on the beach.

“Hayagriva, you must know about this. If light from a faraway star takes millions of years to reach my eye, doesn’t that mean that we have no idea whether that star still exists or where it is now?”

“Yes. We know that Andromeda, the farthest visible galaxy, was there 2.5 million years ago. And as of right now we know that the sun was in a particular position eight minutes ago—I can see its reflection in that crescent moon. We know Saturn was there as of sixty-seven minutes ago, but the North Star, Polaris? We only know it was there 434 years ago, in the year, what, 1530 or something? And the star cluster called the Pleiades was there 444 years ago, in about 1528—yeah, about the same time Lord Chaitanya and Rupa Goswami were actually here in Vrindaban. But do these stars still exist?”

“So maybe there’s just a two-way mirror out there, with Krishna looking through at us.”

“Yeah, what a load of fiction these scientists are feeding us!”

One of the favorite Vaishnava festivals is called Jhulan Yatra, or the swing festival, usually held in August, when Deities of Radha and Krishna are placed on a swing and pushed back and forth all day to cool Them in the hottest part of summer. Around noon on November 8 some men drove up to the Keshighat temple and unloaded a large and elaborate jhulan, a swing made of solid silver inlaid with precious stones. They said it was a gift from the Maharaja of Bharatpur, owner of the Keshighat temple, and they asked the devotees who were there to please sign a receipt that they’d taken delivery of this valuable item. Achyutananda, an old pro at dealing with India by now, refused to sign the receipt, thinking it must be some clever Indian ruse to get money from us. Bharatpur Maharaja’s men were dismayed, but they reloaded the swing and took off. When Prabhupada heard what had happened he was furious. “When a great man gives you a gift, you take it,” he said. “Now you go to the king and beg his forgiveness!” Gurudas had told us that the king, Brijendra Singh, was a flamboyant character, schooled in the extravagant ways of the old British Raj, and was quick to take offense. He was also eccentric, drank a lot, and now that he was nearly broke, perhaps had gone a little mad.

Gurudas, Hayagriva, Achyutananda, and I found a car with a driver and immediately set off for the king’s palace in Bharatpur, a one-hour drive from Vrindaban. Knowing the king’s mercurial disposition, we knew we’d better hurry to rectify Achyutananda’s mistake, telling the driver, “Jelde, jelde! Faster, faster!”

We pull in through open rusty iron gates embossed with Queen Victoria’s shield, one side hanging down from a broken hinge, into an overgrown yard before a huge crumbling palace. Old cannons and broken-down Rolls-Royce touring cars are scattered around in the high weeds. The Maharaja’s secretary comes out and tells us that the Maharaja is very angry. He has just left for Delhi, but if we hurry, perhaps we can catch him. We race up the Bharatpur-Delhi road, promising the driver a hundred-rupee tip if he’ll go faster. Suddenly, ahead, we see the distinct profile of the Maharaja’s Rolls convertible, and we pull up close behind it, flashing our lights, trying to pull alongside. The Maharaja speeds up—80, 90, over 100 mph—and leaves us in his dust. This rhino got away. Well, so much for the Keshighat palace. It was probably too big for us to manage, anyway.

Srila Prabhupada is scheduled to finish his Vrindaban lectures and then go to Hyderabad on November 16 for a fourteen-day pan-dal program there. As the Vrindaban days wind down, Prabhupada often walks or takes a rickshaw over to the ISKCON temple construction site at Raman Reti to inspect the ongoing work. One day he even takes up a trowel, mixes some mortar, and applies it to the foundation himself—shows us how it’s done. He gets a telegram from Tamal Krishna in Bombay that he’s filing charges against Nair in both criminal and civil courts. Prabhupada urges the Juhu leaders to stage a big pandal program in Bombay in December so he can make an impression there and apply some public pressure on Nair. He again writes the Nairs to join him, if not in Vrindaban, then “please come and see me in Hyderabad.”

From October 15 to November 14,1972, in just this one month, Srila Prabhupada has delivered fifty-nine incredible lectures— fifty-nine!—summarizing the essence of action in spiritual life. He has resolved problems at the Radha-Damodar temple, accelerated the construction at Raman Reti, and established ISKCON’s presence in Vrindaban forever. This month in Vrindaban has been the most sublime period in the lives of all devotees who were there. Prabhupada shared his deepest confidence, locked us on to Krishna, connected us through our predecessors to the sweetest nectar of eternal knowledge. Here’s the map, Srila Prabhupada said, and the signposts are clearly marked. This is how you develop your own unique personal alliance with God. “I have given you everything; now it is up to you.”

Just before leaving Vrindaban I write a letter to George Harrison, addressed to Friar Park, Henley-on-Thames, Oxfordshire, United Kingdom.

Dear George,

This is the magic Karttika month in Vrindaban, and Prabhupada has decided it’s time to teach us bhakti. This is how it went down:

Radha-Damodar Temple/Rupa Goswami/Nectar of Devotion

Scene One

\[Courtyard, Rupa Goswami Samadhi, almost dark; gold sunset just touching the tops of the trees; outside the walls a cacophony of temple bells, drums, off-key amplified kirtans, firecrackers, crows and peacocks screeching; Srila Prabhupada cross-legged on a cushion to the side of the Samadhi, bare bulb hung from a tree branch illuminating his shawl-covered head, faces maybe thirty seated disciples and a few others, clears his throat, and says:\]

Prabhupada: Soooo, in order to understand the transcendental loving affairs of Radha and Krishna, one has to go through the teachings of the Goswamis. This is the way...

\[Microphone feedback wailing, gets turned down; SP mutters What is this? continues:\]

Prabhupada: Chaitanya Mahaprabhu compared bhakti as a great ocean. So when He was speaking before Rupa Goswami, He said that “It is just like an ocean. So I’ll take a drop of it, and you taste it, and you will understand what is this ocean.” Just like by tasting one drop of seawater we can understand the taste of the whole ocean. Similarly Chaitanya Mahaprabhu described a small portion of Bhakti-rasamrita-sindhu. Bhakti rasa amrita. Bhakti, devotional service, there is a rasa, taste, and the taste is amrita, eternal.

\[“Eternal” echoing through the courtyard, then silence as the word resounds in the minds of intensely concentrating youth; sudden chatter distraction of monkeys, SP looks up from his book, his eyes comically magnified by the horn-rimmed glasses, fine white stubble frosts his chin; SP so beautiful the devotees ache inside and several groans are heard—aside: “Just bring little water” SP lifts his silver lota and pours a slender tendril of water down the back of his throat from a foot above his opened lips, smacks them, sets cup down; suddenly SP’s eyes glaze over, they halt—and fix on a faraway place unblinking, and no one breathes for minutes; a tiny tear rolls out of each eye and hangs, trembling, in his whiskers; blink, blink—SP rubs his eyes, refocuses, then husky-voiced he slowly speaks the secret all of us are waiting for:\]

Prabhupada: “I mean to say, that, when your eyes become, that is, smeared with the ointment of love...then you will see Krishna.”

Scene Two

That night I walk upstream along the Yamuna River, my mind swirling with all this new knowledge. I sit down in the sand and look back at the Magic City, flickering like a dream, Diwali rockets blooming above the temple cones, thinking: We’re so lucky, we are so unaccountably lucky, that among so many trillions of candidates, and after such long eons searching, we have found Srila Prabhupada—and the secret of all the secrets has been handed us so sweetly...

Much love, George, Shyamasundar das

**\* \* \***

On the afternoon of November 16, from the moment we stepped off the plane at Begumpet airport in Hyderabad we could feel that this place was different from the cities we’d visited in northern India over the past two years. Except for Madras, Prabhupada and his entourage had never been this deep into South India. It was uncharted territory, outside our familiar large circle of ISKCON contacts in the north. Hyderabad is the capital of Andhra Pradesh state in south-central India, and at the same latitude as Acapulco,

Mexico. The place has a Persian feel to it: arabesque, Ali Baba and the 1001 nights, intrigue, hidden wealth, deep dark secrets. As our motorcade passed through town, dodging camels, we saw minarets, man-made lakes, bazaars, palaces behind spiked iron fences, and place names like Charminar, Golconda, Begum Bazaar, and Pearl Market—squiggly Telegu and Urdu script on billboards and signs, abrupt colors, veiled women. Hyderabad was a wilder, even more exotic version of India than we had yet seen—and I was quickly seduced.

From the back seat of his Ambassador car I asked our host, Hariprasad Badruka, “What is that, ‘Golconda’? Where I come from, Golconda means a mythical place of great riches, like Eldorado, or King Solomon’s mines.”

“Yes,” he said, “Golconda is a fortress near Hyderabad, which was the world’s diamond trading center in ancient times. All the great diamonds, like Koh-I-Noor, and your Blue Hope diamondbig, big diamonds—were mined nearby here. This was the main source of great wealth for the early Moguls who came here five hundred years back. The British established their residency here in 1798—East India Company—so most educated men speak English. Hyderabad was ruled by the Asaf Sahi dynasty for 230 years; the Nizams of Hyderabad, until independence in 1948, were the richest men in the world. Hyderabad was the largest princely state. We had our own army, air force, currency, even our own postal department and stamps.” (Visions of the mysterious one-anna stamps from my childhood collection, with minarets and Arabic writing passed through my mind.)

Then Prabhupada piped in from the front seat: “Yes, the Hyderabad! men, they are very rich, and they are inclined for philanthropy. Mostly Hindu, but ruled over by the Muslims. Many acharyas have appeared in South India. Ramanujacharya, Madhvacharya, Nimbarka, Vishnu Swami, Shankaracharya—all have appeared in South India. South India is so blessed. Now they are mostly smarta brahmins, impersonalists, but I will convince them about Krishna.”

We pulled into formal English gardens surrounding a magnificent palace, and Badruka announced that this is where we’d be staying, the home of Raja Pannalal Pitti. Now, this was a house! Moti Bhavan he called the place, “House of Pearls.” Looked like a cross between European Baroque and Mughal architecture, three floors, high windows, balconies, and is that ivy climbing the walls? Mr Pitti met us at the door, a tall, thin man, wearing glasses and a black Nehru cap, a high-buttoned coat. He’s soft-spoken, an intense but welcoming man. We settled into comfortable quarters on the second floor, fending off servants who hovered to serve our every need. I’m dazzled by this lavish home, from its heavily furnished English rooms with Palladian crystal chandeliers and old masters paintings, to the Turkish-style dens smelling of musk, myrrh, and cloves, and lit by stained-glass windows. I could almost see men sitting around on brocaded cushions, maybe smoking hookahs, as fountains splashed in atria outside. After the austerities of Vrindaban, this was like a visit to the pleasure domes of Byzantium.

Hyderabad’s most important Hindu leaders gathered in the house to meet Srila Prabhupada, showing him every respect. So many local leaders came I had to seat them in a waiting room and call them into Prabhupada’s room a few at a time. If too many came, we’d move out into the garden and sit among the blooming roses. Most of these men had bright red caste marks on their foreheads, their lips crimson from chewing betel—and they all seemed to be named either Mr. Reddy or Mr. Rao. Tamal Krishna, Subal Swami, and a few other devotees had arrived in Hyderabad three months earlier and had been meeting men like Hariprasad Badruka. They had formed a committee of powerful life members, hungry for Prabhupada’s personal visit to this town well off the normal swami circuit.

The committee had organized a fourteen-day pandal program at the Nampally Exhibition Grounds in central Hyderabad. Prabhupada will speak from the Bhagavad-gita mornings and evenings there—mornings in Hindi and evenings in English. Last month, Prabhupada had dispatched TKG back up to Bombay to help Giriraj with the Nair business, sending Bhavananda from Calcutta to Hyderabad to finalize preparations for the show. The elaborate stage and vyasasan decorations reflect Bhavananda’s masterful artistic touch, for sure. Bhavananda has brought with him a number of devotees from the Albert Road temple. His wife Palika is here, along with Madhavananda, Dinanath, Yadubara and Visakha, Tejiyas, Shrutadev, Anandamaya, Sudama Vipra, Malati and Saraswati, and even Siddhaswarupananda Swami from Hawaii—altogether maybe fifty devotees. There has been good publicity, so about a thousand people show up for the morning lecture, and at night more than five thousand attend.

Right away Prabhupada jumps to the task of convincing these South Indian brahmins that Krishna is a person. He speaks from the second chapter of the Bhagavad-gita. “Here, Krishna says that ‘In the past we are individual persons, in the present we are all individual persons, and in future also, we shall remain individuals.’ So the Mayavadi theory that Krishna is impersonal, how it stands? Neither God is impersonal nor the living entities are impersonal. Every one of us—God also—is a person.”

Following each lecture, Prabhupada’s high-born Hindu audience asks many intelligent questions. Most of these people are from the advaita (nondual) school of Mayavadi philosophy, which claims there’s no difference between oneself and God. We are all One.

Prabhupada: Krishna is inconceivably both dvaita and advaita simultaneously. Just like this finger is my finger. So it is part of this body. So you can say “This finger is also body.” But at the same time the finger is not the body. Is it clear? You cannot say, “This finger is the whole body.” But at the same time you can say, “Yes, finger is body.” This is dvaita-advaita. It is simultaneously one and different. Similarly, the soul and the Supreme Lord are equal in quality. Krishna says, mamaivamsha. The small particle of gold is gold. That is advaita. But the gold mine and the gold earring—there is difference. You cannot say the gold earring is as good as the gold mine. That is dvaita. So in this way, so far our spiritual existence is concerned, we are one, advaita. But so far our quantity of energies is concerned, that is different. That is dvaita. You have no such big energy as God has. You can create one small sputnik and take credit, but God can create innumerable universes simply by breathing. So your energy, your power, is different from God’s power. But in quality, you are one with God.

Indian Man: How? How? How to prove? How?

Prabhupada: That requires brain power. Not dull brain—very fertile brain is required.

Very soon, Prabhupada wins these people over, convincing them by his lucid exposition of scripture and the obvious results of his preaching work all over the world. The audience swells from five thousand to nearly ten thousand each night, and Bhaktivedanta Swami quickly becomes the talk of Hyderabad.

Each night at the Exhibition Grounds, Prabhupada expands on Krishna as a person, and that anyone—not just those born into the brahmin class—but anyone can become a devotee of Krishna, and furthermore, these devotees are qualified to elevate the human society:

A devotee is a first-class pandit because he’s sama-darsinah. Sama-darsinah means he’s feeling for others. Para-duhkha-duhkhi krpambudhir yah. A Vaishnava is very kindhearted, merciful, because he feels for others. He feels for others in this sense: that he knows what he is. He sees every living entity as part and parcel of God. “Now, here is another part and parcel of God. He would have gone back to home, back to Godhead, and danced with Him, and lived very nicely, eternally, blissfully. But now he’s rotting here as a hog or as a human being or as a king—it is the same thing.” So a devotee tries to take him out of this illusion. Therefore he’s called para-duhkha-duhkhi. He’s actually feeling others’ distressed condition. Not like these political or social leaders. What they can do? They make their own fortune, that’s all. And what is that fortune? That is also misfortune. If somebody thinks, “I have got some money, I am very fortunate,” actually it is not fortune. Real fortunate is he who is advanced in Krishna consciousness. He is fortunate. Otherwise, all are unfortunate. All are unfortunate.

Prabhupada publicly extolled the high character of his Western disciples, urging us in private to mind traditional etiquette and to act like brahmins because “everyone is watching you.” But some of the young American devotees had not yet shed their wild hippie ways. Raised in a lax, anything-goes society, we were like a band of gypsies let loose in the candy store of India under the protection of Prabhupada’s personal umbrella. Mahatma Gandhi’s statue cast into the well at Juhu; ruses, pranks, and overblown promises given to potential life members—these were typical American gorilla behaviors. One afternoon shortly after we arrived in Hyderabad, three of Prabhupada’s army-vet surfer disciples hijacked Mr. Pitti’s classic 1956 Chevy V-8 convertible and took it for a joyride around town! When Prabhupada heard about it he was very upset, dressed us all down, and demanded that we mind our manners.

Early on the morning of November 22, Tamal Krishna flew down from Bombay with Mr. Nair to discuss the Juhu situation with Prabhupada. Nair has brought his own guru along for moral support. I didn’t catch the guy’s name, but he was Swami So-and-so, your run-of-the-mill family-guru type with a big white beard, jolly, but obviously outmatched by Prabhupada’s scholarship, fame, and the opulent surroundings at Moti Bhavan. They made small talk for some time, had lunch, then Prabhupada suggested they take a nap in nearby rooms—they must be tired from their early-morning commute from Bombay.

An hour or so later Prabhupada asked TKG to wake Mr. Nair and bring him to him—but don’t wake the guru. Nair came into Prabhupada’s room, haggard and sleepy, and Prabhupada got right to the point, suggesting a revised version of ISKCON’s purchase agreement with him. Nair seemed in a trance and readily agreed to everything Prabhupada said. I typed out a four-page “Supplement to Sale Agreement” in the next room, brought it in, and both men signed. Prabhupada was a genius at using brief legal language to say so much, and the new agreement contained clauses like:

1\.    Notwithstanding anything contained in the said Principal Agreement dated the 8th of February 1972 it is agreed between the parties hereto as follows:

(a)    The sale shall be completed on or before 1st December 1972, time being the essence of the Contract.

(b)    The total price is Rs. 14,00,000/- (Rupees Fourteen Lakhs) out of which Rs. 2,07,500/- (Rupees Two Lakhs seven thousand five hundred) has been paid as aforesaid. The balance of the purchase price shall be paid as follows:

1\.    Rs. 3,00,000/- (Rupees Three Lakhs) upon execution of the conveyance provided said conveyance is executed on or before 1st December 1972.

2\.    Rs. 25,000/- (Rupees Twenty-five thousand) per month for four months thereafter 1st of December 1972.

3\.    Rs. 23,000/- (Rupees Twenty-three thousand) per month thereafter until the entire balance of the said purchase price shall be paid in full.

Under these new terms, ISKCON would pay Nair another three lakhs of rupees, and Nair would execute the deed by December 1— in nine days. Prabhupada signed the new agreement, and Tamal Krishna and I also signed on behalf of ISKCON. Tamai flew back to Bombay that afternoon with Mr. Nair to get Mrs. Nair’s signature, bring our lawyers up to speed, and get the job done. We never did see that other Swami again. Apparently he woke up and left the house.

During these days, the biggest topic of conversation in Hyderabad was the drought. The southwest monsoons, which normally pound Andhra Pradesh from July through September, had failed, and the area was experiencing its worst drought since 1918. Shortly after we arrived in Hyderabad, in fact, Prabhupada received this letter from the secretary of the Andhra Pradesh Relief Fund Committee:

Revered Swamiji,

The residents of Hyderabad are happy to have this opportunity to meet you and your esteemed followers. You may be aware that due to inadequate rainfall during the last two years and its complete failure this year, more than half of our state is in the grip of a serious drought. With a view to supplement governmental efforts to combat this evil, a Central Voluntary Organization of citizens drawn from various walks of life has been set up.
