The members of this organization surveyed the areas affected by drought. The situation is pathetic... with the result that at least five to six million people are hardly having one meal a day.

We therefore appeal to your revered self to consider how your Society could best come to the rescue of these millions of souls who are in unimaginable distress. The Committee would like to suggest that members of your Society appeal to the bhaktas attending your discourses to contribute their mite to the Andhra Pradesh Relief Fund.

On the night of November 22, a cyclone spawned in the Bay of Bengal hurled itself onto the south Andhra coast with hundred mph-plus winds, heading for the interior, and that night it started to rain gently on Hyderabad.

On 23rd morning, three men came to see Prabhupada. I had seen them each night at the lectures asking intelligent questions. They introduced themselves as Balkrishna, Damodar, and Govindas—three brothers—and they requested Prabhupada and his disciples to have lunch at their home one day this week. Prabhupada agreed, and the men asked if I could come to their place in Sultan Bazar, look it over, and make suggestions on how to set up for the program.

The three brothers and I, along with four of their sons, sat around on a white-cloth-covered pad spread wall-to-wall in the huge main room of their house. The only furniture was a small, low, glass-topped table and a big iron safe in the corner decorated with paintings of the Taj Mahal. On the walls hung a calendar, old photos of their forefathers—a plastic garland hung over each frame—and a large painting of Sri Nathji, the stylized murti of Krishna worshiped by devotees of the Vallabha sampradaya. After a while I asked, “Where are your wives and daughters?”

“Oh, they’re upstairs—that is their portion of the house. This is where the men sit for business.”

“Oh, and what kind of business are you in?”

And the magic show began. Their forefathers had been jewelers to the Nizam of Hyderabad, and they painted for me a picture of once-great wealth, the largest hoard of gems and jewels ever assembled. Heirs to the diamond mines of Golconda, the Nizams measured their diamonds in maunds, with one maund equaling fifty-five pounds. When Osman Ali Khan, the seventh Nizam, abdicated his throne in 1947 after Indian independence, he fled to Istanbul with cargo planeloads of jewels, leaving behind the golf-ball-sized Jacob diamond as a paperweight on his desk. When soldiers entered his palace, they found room after room stuffed to the ceiling with international paper currencies, much of it chewed by rats. “Yes, even for his many elephants he had giant strands of rubies, emeralds, and especially pearls. Today you will find that

Hyderabad is the pearl market for all of India, even though we are far from the sea, because of the Nizams’ obsession with pearls.”

Balkrishna then mutters something in Gujarati to his son, also named Shyamsundar, who leaves and returns minutes later with a bucket. He sets it before me. It’s filled with natural pearls, each almost half an inch in diameter, with a hole drilled through each pearl. “These are the necklaces for Nizam’s elephants, and there are hundreds of such buckets. But his favorite gems were the colored diamonds.”

“Oh, there is such a thing as colored diamonds?”

“Yes, I will show you.”

Another son opens the large safe and brings out a couple of wooden boxes, one side lined with small drawers. Damodar opens a few drawers for me to see the loose gemstones in each, then reaches in and plops a stone into my palm. “You see, this is pink diamond. And here is yellow, canary yellow. And green. And here is the rarest of all”—he hands me a deep violet-blue diamond, showing me how to hold it in the morning light streaming in from the north-facing window. Wow, I’m gone, man, absolutely blown away, but trying not to show it.

For hours the brothers and sons showed me box after box of precious stones. And so began my education. We gathered on a shaded veranda for lunch prasad, and they explained that the family had always been strict Vaishnavas, worshipers of Krishna, and that their brahmin cooks had been with them for generations, able to prepare a different vegetarian meal for each day of the year depending on seasonal needs for nutrition and health. Though none of them had ever been outside India, seeing what Prabhupada had accomplished and hearing him speak every day, they were convinced that Prabhupada was the greatest living Vaishnava guru. After lunch, as I was passing down the hall from washing my hands, I glanced in a room to see three of the sons sorting through a two-foot mound of small red stones on the floor. I asked, “What are these? Are they glass?”

“Oh, these are rubies,” Shyamsundar said, “rough crystals. Our fathers have just discovered a ruby mine here in Andhra.”

What?!

“Yes, this is one month’s production—about one kilogram a day we are getting.”

OK, hold on here. This is all too much!

By the time the afternoon ended, I was hooked. The family agreed that I would become their sole representative in the outside world to market their jewels and ruby-mine production. They loaded me up with two eighteen-inch cabochon bead necklaces, one made from pierced deep-green emeralds, the other from carmine red rubies. The gems were in graduated sizes from five to about fifteen carats—antique pieces from the Nizams’ time. They also gave me a 2.23-carat canary-yellow marquis diamond mounted in a white-gold ring, a rose-cut 1.64-carat green diamond, and two topgrade faceted emeralds, deep forest green and nearly flawless. The deal was struck: I would carry these goods to the West on trust, sell them, and give the brothers a certain amount for each piece, keeping the rest as profit. I got a kick out of watching them work out the prices. Without verbal discussion, they each put a hand under a small cloth napkin, and after a series of finger-signals—the cloth jiggling up and down—Balkrishna, as the eldest brother, would announce a final price.

“But what about the rubies from your mine? I think I can stir interest in America and Europe, maybe contract to buy your whole mine production. Can I get an exclusive deal on that?”

Yes, that is also possible, they said, and after protracted haggling under the hankie, they fixed a per-kilo price for mine-run rough and gave me a one-kilo sample, five thousand carats of ruby crystals from their mine.

Bearing my booty in a shoulder bag I walked from the brothers’ place on Bank Street and made my way through a steady rain to the Exhibition Grounds in time for Prabhupada’s evening lecture, jubilant, not caring that I was soaked to the skin.

Afterward, returning to Moti Bhavan, I entered Prabhupada’s room, elated: “Prabhupada, I think I’ve found a way to make a lot of money for spreading Krishna consciousness in England!” As I laid out my new treasures on his desk and related what had happened that afternoon, Prabhupada chuckled at my obvious enthusiasm. He asked questions about the business side of the arrangement, then said, “Just leave these jewels here. It is now late. We shall discuss in the morning.”

At 6 a.m., when I entered his room alone, Prabhupada was leaning back on his bolster, chanting japa and smiling. He was wearing both necklaces! Wow, he looked so beautiful! I thought I’d never seen anyone so beautiful. Relaxed in regal dignity, his smiling golden complexion was lit by an unusual aura, augmented by the crimson and emerald-green precious gems around his neck winking against his saffron-pink silk kurta in the yellow glow from his desk lamp. I glimpsed, and then feasted my eyes on the long-ago splendor of Krishna’s time, when He, His friends, and Radharani were bedecked with jewels like this. “Er, uh, Prabhupada ... you look so beautifull These gems have found their rightful place!” I stammered. And he laughed long and hard, enjoying such rare pleasure: the feel of fabulous cool wealth on his body.

“Yes,” he said, “these special jewels have consciousness. They are born as crystals, so they grow, they are living entities, they have emotions and powers. And they may sit for millions of years, so if you excavate, you polish them and offer to Krishna, they become very pleased.”

He slowly removed each necklace, taking time to study it in his hand, and laid them on his table, laughing, as he said, “But sannyasis should not be seen wearing such things.”

We discussed my proposed business idea. I told him it might be a good way to make a lot of money, especially if we get that new place in London. Finally, Prabhupada said, “You take this opportunity Krishna has given. Now you do it, and let us see what happens.” He picked up the sample handful of recently mined ruby rough, which

I’d left with him the night before, lifted it, and motioned for me to take back the stones. Just above my outstretched cupped hands he released the rubies, and they hit my palms—and bounced all over the room! I scrambled around on my knees, head close to the floor in the semi-dark, picking them up one by one to Prabhupada’s roaring laughter.

It continued to rain all day on November 24—so heavily that we had to cancel that night’s lecture at the Exhibition Grounds. As water sluiced down from gargoyles stationed around the roof of the House of Pearls, Prabhupada dictated his reply to the Andhra Pradesh Relief Fund Committee:

My dear Mr. Kapadia,

Please accept my greetings. With reference to your letter and your personal interview, I beg to inform you that without pleasing the Supreme Personality of Godhead, no one can become happy. Unfortunately people do not know who God is and how to make Him happy... Therefore if you want to perform relief work simply by collecting funds, I think that it will not be successful. You have to please the supreme authority, and that is the way to success. For example, due to the performance of sankirtan here, the rain has begun to fall after a drought of two years. The last time we performed a Hare Krishna festival in Delhi, there was imminent danger of Pakistan’s declaring war However, because of our sankirtan movement India emerged victorious. Similarly, when we held a festival in Calcutta, the Naxalite movement stopped. These are facts. Therefore, through you I wish to appeal to all the leading men of India to accept this movement very seriously and give us all facility to spread this movement throughout the world. Then there will be a very happy condition, not only in India but all over the world.

Prabhupada expanded on this theme in his Bhagavad-gita lectures on subsequent nights:

Krishna, God, has made ample arrangement for our maintenance. Purnasya purnam adaya purnam eva vashish-yate. But it is restricted by nature when we are sinful. We become atheists. We become demons. Then the supply is restricted. Then we cry for: “Oh, there is no rain. There is no this, no... ” That is nature’s restriction. But from God’s arrangement, there is sufficient food for everyone. Eko bahunam vidadhati kaman. He is supplying everyone.

So yajnad bhavati parjanyah: If you perform yajna, sacrifice, then there will be regular rainfall. If you avoid yajna, there will be restriction, and what your plan and fund-raising capacity will help? Now there is sankirtan-yajna and rain is falling. This is a fact. So if you continue performing sankirtan-yajna, then there will be no distress, no unhappiness .... That is the way.

Even the local newspapers exclaimed that because of Prabhupada’s presence in Hyderabad, the rains had come.

Prabhupada and I continued to discuss this new jewels business. My plan was that wherever Prabhupada traveled in the world, along with my secretarial duties I would try to sell some jewels here and there and take wholesale orders on future ruby production from the mine. I never wanted to leave Prabhupada’s side, but selling precious gems seemed easy, like a good sideline. Prabhupada said it was a good idea, Krishna’s arrangement, encouraging me to develop this plan. In my mind, selling gems was like selling good drugs: everyone wants them, so it’s a seller’s market.

I was fascinated by this jewels thing and returned a couple of times to the brothers’ house in Sultan Bazaar to learn more about gems and the history of this richest of all parts of India. One day,

Balkrishna handed me an old, yellowed Time magazine protected in a well-thumbed plastic jacket, like a valued artifact. It was dated February 22, 1937, and had a picture of Nizam Sir Mir Osman Ali Khan on the cover, celebrating the Silver Jubilee of “The Richest Man in the World.”

His jewels have an estimated value of $150,000,000; he reputedly has salted down $250,000,000 in gold bars, and his capital totals some $1,400,000,000, not to mention the fabled “Mines of Golconda.” On the Mohammedan theory that “all are equal before Allah in prayer,” the World’s Richest Man prostrated himself with his subjects at the Great Mosque and everyone prayed. Poems were recited and the venerable Hindu Premier read an address hailing his Mohammedan Monarch as “today the sole relic of Mogul greatness in India.”

Hindu premier? Balkrishna explained, “Yes, the Nizam entrusted the big Hindu families of Hyderabad to manage his affairs. Your Mr. Pitti, for example—he founded the Mercantile Bank of Hyderabad in 1935 with the Nizam’s money, and Pitti was his closest financial advisor. The Pittis, Badrukas, the Bahadurs—these pious Hindu families swam in the sea of money surrounding the Nizam’s court, and they are very generous; they will help your ISKCON so much.” Ah, now it’s all coming into perspective.

The brothers told me about the untapped mineral riches of Andhra Pradesh. “This area is called the Deccan Plain. Dec means ‘ten’ in Sanskrit, and can is ‘jewel mines.’ Besides diamonds and rubies, we have found also deposits of emeralds and sapphires, not far from Hyderabad. This zone is rich in pegmatites, the geological formations that produce precious crystals. For example, when they were excavating gravel to expand Begumpet airport a few months back, they accidentally hit a huge deposit of precious amethyst—you will see that the runway there is purple.”

OK, I’m hooked. I see Krishna’s hand in all of this, meeting these people. It can’t be accidental. I even seem to remember being here before, as a part of this. I can’t explain the strong attraction I have and, on a deeper level, this feeling of familiarity.

Meanwhile, Prabhupada’s no-holds-barred lectures were having their effect: he was changing the parched hearts of so many Hyderabad! Hindus, who had thirsted so long for the real nectar of their tradition. “In Hyderabad they came to fight with me: ‘Sir, you are making brahmins from these Europeans and Americans? This is not good.’ ‘And why not?’ So we had some discussion. So actually it is not that brahmin is made by birth. Brahmin means chatur-var-nyam maya shrishtam guna-karma-vibhagasah, by quality and work... And I have convinced them.”

Sure enough, near the end of our days in Hyderabad several pious wealthy men came forward to offer land and money to build an ISKCON temple here. One man, Mr. Gunampalli Pulla Reddy, had a famous chain of sweet shops in Andhra Pradesh, and he offered a thousand-square-meter property catty-corner to his main shop on Nampally Station Road, just a block from Abids Circle, the commercial center of Hyderabad. Prabhupada saw huge potential in Hyderabad and called for Saurabhi das, his architect disciple, who was designing temples for Juhu, Vrindaban, and Mayapur, to immediately come to Hyderabad and begin work on a temple design for Krishna’s new premises in the heart of the city.

When Saurabhi arrived he brought us up to date on Juhu affairs and suggested that we restructure the land deal with Nair. The way it stood, Hare Krishna Land was divided into two plots, one agricultural and one zoned for residence, requiring two separate conveyances. For the purpose of designing a single temple complex, Saurabhi suggested the land should be transferred to us under one conveyance, and Prabhupada agreed. On November 28, Prabhupada sent me to Bombay to help Tamai Krishna sort out this change to our Agreement.

In just two short weeks Prabhupada had conquered this exotic new land, opening up a vast sector of India to Krishna consciousness. I shared his glow of triumph as I boarded the plane to leave Hyderabad for Bombay. Another rhino in the bag. Along with my typewriter and a briefcase full of carbon paper and correspondence, I carried my gem swag in a bag clutched tight to my body. Srutakirti had said to me the night before, slightly embarrassed, “Prabhupada says you still have a few material fantasies, and by selling these jewels you may suffer, but maybe the fantasies will go away.” Hmmm. And I knew I’d return to Hyderabad again and again.

And yes, the runway is purple.

## Three

From Hyderabad (Xanadu!) my magic carpet left the purple runway, and a few hours later I was in the purgatory of downtown Bombay, where for the next seven weeks we would act out the dramatic but classic struggle between good and evil: ISKCON vs. Nair. We’d be immersed in the insanity of litigation in post-British India, where the transplanted precepts of Blackstone’s law battled the lethargy and corruption of an ancient, old-boy tradition. Nothing had ever gotten under Srila Prabhupada’s skin like this. The next seven weeks read like a Perry Mason legal thriller or an eternal drama straight from the pages of Srimad-Bhagavatam: Krishna vs. Kamsa, the evil king, calling us to the arena where no holds are barred. What will astound me is how quickly Srila Prabhupada perceives the simple logic hidden in the tangled legal issues and counters with his rebuttal; how he forms his argument word by word into a legal brief; how he stands strong before overwhelming forces—a mission impossible—and, as we shall see, rides off victorious into the sunset. It’s a heroic, wonderful story, at least for those who loved him so.

The activities of a person, even if they are not very extraordinary, create an impression of wonder in the heart and mind of the person’s friends. But even very wonderful activities performed by a person who is not one’s friend will not create any impression. It is because of love that one’s wonderful activities create an impression on the mind.

—Nectar of Devotion, p.366.

After months in the company of angels, now, in Bombay, it was time to switch into battle mode.

On Tuesday, November 28, 1972, knowing the deadline for finalizing our agreement with Mr. Nair was in just three days, Tamal Krishna rushed me from the airport to the law offices of Mulla & Mulla, at 51 Mahatma Gandhi Road in the Fort district of central Bombay. In the sunless warrens surrounding Flora Fountain, just behind the High Court buildings, hundreds of solicitors plied their trade. As we pulled to the curb before the Jehangir Wadia Building, we saw lawyers lining the sidewalk in black coats shiny from use, soiled white shirts, and skinny black ties. They were seated at little folding tables drafting minor lawsuits or legal letters for their impoverished walk-in clients who might be embroiled in a dispute over a cow, a meter of land encroachment, or a son-in-law who’d run off with the dowry.

We took the rickety lift upstairs, brushing elbows with black-robed advocates in powdered white wigs rushing off to plead for plaintiff or defendant. As we sat in the waiting area at Mulla &C Mulla, looking across the large, main room, we found ourselves in a Charles Dickens novel. Bundles of dusty documents tied with string were piled on every flat space; lazy fans high in the ceiling fluttered the edges of yellowed papers; the clatter of typewriters was uninterrupted as a dozen law clerks in green eyeshades hunched over their work, feeding huge old Royal or Godrej machines with three pieces of legal-size paper, carbons sandwiched between, hunting and pecking to avoid ineradicable mistakes; chai-wallas ran in and out balancing trays of little china cups of tepid, sweet milk tea. There was a clay water jug with spigot on a stand in the corner, a glass perched on top for communal use. Noise through the open windows—from crows and traffic and vendors hawking their wares in the narrow streets below—was constant.

Our lawyer, Mr. Bhaktiar Bhesania, came into the room to escort us back to his office. He introduced us to his assistant, Mrs. Mehta. Bhesania had been with Mulla & Mulla since 1958 and had just made partner in this prestigious firm, so we were confident in his abilities. We informed him of our new concern: because the land we intend to buy is divided into two portions, requiring two conveyances—one of those plots being zoned for agriculture—the Bombay Municipal Corporation may not allow us to build a temple on it. Bhesania saw the logic, and together we drafted a letter to Mr. Nair’s attorneys asking them to inquire whether “prior consent” would be required by the BMC, and if so, we would prefer the two conveyances be merged into a single conveyance. Bhesania said his clerks would type the letter and courier it over to Nair’s lawyers at Chimanlal Shah &C Co, in the Examiner Press Building at 35 Dalal Street, just four blocks south down Meadows Road.

Pleased with our good day’s work for our spiritual master, and still hopeful the deal would go through in a timely manner, Tamal and I grabbed a quick shot of sugarcane juice on the corner and bought a handful of roasted channa dal to eat on the hourlong taxi grind through heat and traffic back to Juhu. Prabhupada was due to arrive in Bombay from Hyderabad on December 1—in little more than two days’ time. There was no suitable accommodation for him at Hare Krishna Land, so Giriraj had arranged for Prabhupada and his servants to stay at the home of life member Mr. Pranjivan Valia. Mr. Valia’s’s nice, Western-style house was located at 8 North-South Road in the Juhu-Vila Parle Development Scheme, maybe a mile from Hare Krishna Land. Tamal and I set up camp at Valia’s place too, and the next day, the 29th, we returned to Mulla & Mulla to await Chimanlal Shah’s reply to yesterday’s letter regarding the single conveyance. We waited all day, but the reply didn’t come, so we fought our way back to Juhu that evening, a bit worried now: the clock was ticking.

On the morning of Thursday, November 30, we returned to Mr. Bhesania’s office, but there was still no reply from Nair’s lawyers. Taking the initiative, we handed Bhesania a check for Rs. 300,000/- to deposit into the Mulla & Mulla client escrow account, then drafted another letter to Chimanlal Shah:

In order to show their bona fides for completing the sale herein, our clients have requested us to inform you that they have today deposited a sum of Rs 300,000/-, being the amount payable on the execution of the Conveyance Deed herein as per the terms of the Supplemental Agreement dated 24th November 1972.

In the same letter we also asked that Shah & Co deliver the Certificate of Title, “certifying that your client’s title to the property is clear, marketable, and free from encumbrances.” Furthermore, we asked that Nair supply us a copy of a tax certificate for the property “under Section 230A of the Income-tax Act.”

We waited around Mulla & Mulla all day, chanting japa, running in and out for juice and snacks. Finally, late in the afternoon, we got a letter from Shah & Co stating that they had not received our letter of the 28th—asking for one conveyance instead of two— until “29th ultimo at about 4 p.m.” Apparently, our courier took thirty hours to walk four blocks! In their letter Mr. Nair’s lawyers raised the first red flag:

It is surprising that when the sale is to be completed on or before 1st December 1972, time being of the essence of the contract, your clients at the last moment came forward with a suggestion for one Conveyance and thereby making it impossible to complete the sale on or before 1st December 1972. Such conduct on the part of your clients amounts to a breach of the contract on the part of your clients.

The words “breach of contract” in Nair’s letter is a slap in the face. But Mr. Bhesania calms us and explains that Nair’s lawyers are still willing to go ahead with the sale, provided we ourselves draft the single conveyance that combines the two properties and send it over to Chimanlal Shah. OK, looks like only a slight delay. We brainstorm with Bhesania all day to draft the single conveyance deed. Mulla & Mulla will type and deliver it to Shah’s office first thing tomorrow, on December 1, the day of our original deadline. Closing the deal may be delayed until the 4th, but it looks like it can still work. We’ve put money in escrow, we’ve created one conveyance, we’ve requested the clear-title document and tax certificate from Nair—and that should be it. Now we can greet Prabhupada with good news. But “breach of contract” still rang in our ears and worried us, even as we agreed in the taxi back to Juhu that all of this was Krishna’s arrangement and surely everything would turn out well.

Prabhupada arrived in Bombay with a flourish shortly after noon on Friday, December 1, greeted by a hundred devotees and local well-wishers at the Santa Cruz airport. Tamal, Giriraj, and I jumped in the car with Prabhupada for the twenty-minute drive to Juhu, and he immediately asked, “So, today is deadline. What is the position?”

“Everything is in place, Srila Prabhupada, but final closing may be delayed two days until Monday—we will know later today.”

“Hmm,” is all he said.

Prabhupada greeted his beloved Sri Sri Radha-Rasabihari in Their makeshift temple at the front of Hare Krishna Land, then walked the property, catching up on the latest news, making plans for the project’s future as if the property was in our hands, repeating often how much Krishna liked this place. It was such a relief to again be in Prabhupada’s certain presence after our three days of dread and confusion, to be safe and secure with our fearless leader.

By late afternoon Prabhupada had settled in at Mr. Valia’s house. TKG phoned Mulla & Mulla, and Mr. Bhesania said that the draft conveyance had been delivered to Nair’s lawyers earlier that day and he was waiting to hear back from them.

We showed Prabhupada the Shah-Nair letter of November 30. He read it quickly and pointed out two sentences: “Such conduct on the part of your clients amounts to a breach of the contract on the part of your clients,” and, “There is no question of our client obtaining the consent if any of the Municipal Corporation.” Looking at us over his reading glasses, Prabhupada said, “He does not want to settle. He will find every excuse. You will see, this will be a very big battle.”

Despite the threats implicit in Nair’s letter, Prabhupada is relaxed, pumped up by events in Hyderabad. We go over all the legal documents, having fun with the legal posturing, and Prabhupada reminisces about his days as captain of the debate team at Scottish Churches College.

Very early the next morning, we return with Prabhupada to Hare Krishna Land for mangala-arati and a lecture in the small temple, which is really just a concrete pad with a wall at one end behind the altar. It’s open on three sides, with steel posts supporting a corrugated iron roof. Early sun throws long shadows of palm trees as it burns off the morning mist; crows and parrots screech in the new day; kids run to school with their lunch tiffins and little backpacks; dudh-wallas deliver milk to doorsteps in small stainless steel pails. All around us is the wakening murmur of Indian communal life—the dull thump of mortar and pestle as housewives mash their daily spices, the slap! slap! of wet laundry against cement, dogs yapping, a few horns from early traffic on Mukteshwar Temple Road. Prabhupada calls this time of day the brahma-muhurta, the ninety-six minutes before and just after sunrise, God’s hour, the most auspicious time for meditation and worship. It’s a dreamy time for me, calm and magic, hearing Prabhupada’s voice, having some deep realization. Brilliant sunlight suddenly bathes his golden face as he prepares us for the perfect day to come. The Deities are resplendent, and Prabhupada praises the pujaris for taking such good care of Them. He has promised Krishna that he will build a wonderful home for Him here and that he will not back down until this is accomplished.

Later that morning at our lawyer’s office, Mr. Bhesania handed us Nair’s latest letter and said, “This does not look good.” The letter was dated 8 p.m. yesterday, December 1st. Tamal and I read it over, puzzled by the oblique, sinister wording, especially: “Our client says that by reason of your clients’ aforesaid conduct in not continuing Messrs. Ambubhai and Diwanji as their attorneys, your clients have committed a breach of the said Agreement for sale.”

Wait a minute, what does this mean?

“What it means,” Bhesania said, “Is that Nair will not give a clear Certificate of Title because Ambubhai and Diwanji no longer represent ISKCON.”

“What?” Tamal said, “We only hired Ambubhai and Diwanji because Mr. Nair suggested them in the first place! And they resigned as our counsel—we didn’t fire them!” Nair’s letter goes on to say: “We may add that if your clients fail to complete the sale on or before 12th December 1972 they will take the consequence of such failure.”

Confused and distracted, Tamai and I rush back to Juhu to inform Prabhupada of this latest turn of events.

Prabhupada: No, he is mistaken. Just see that supplemental agreement from Hyderabad. I think it is clause three. You have got it? Just read aloud.

Tamai Krishna: OK, here it is, Clause three: “Before the execution of the Conveyance the Vendor will make out a suitable Title to the said property as provided in the said Agreement for sale dated the 8th day of February, 1972 and the Vendor shall be responsible to see that Messrs. Amubhai and Diwanji, Solicitors, Bombay, shall issue their Certificate of Title ...”

Prabhupada: You see? The vendor shall be responsible for obtaining Certificate of Title from Diwanji, not that we have to do it. Vendor means Mr. Nair. He has signed this. His wife has signed. There is no question. This rascal has breached, not we have breached.

Shyamasundar: And he has unilaterally extended the deadline to December 12th. Can he do that?

Prabhupada: No, we have not agreed to that. These delays and threats mean he does not want to sell to us. He will keep our money and sell again and again, just as he has done with this Chhaganlal.

It seems that back in 1966, Mr. Nair had sold the same property to another party, the Chhaganlal Brothers, and had pulled a similar stunt—taking their Rs. 400,000/- down payment and refusing to consummate the sale. In 1968 the Chhaganlals had brought suit against Nair in order to recover their money. Even now, four years later that suit had not yet been heard in the courts.

Prabhupada: Now just read that other clause, Chhaganlal clause. I think it is a part of clause two.

Tamal Krishna: Yes. Here it is, clause two, paragraph c: “The Vendor shall not be entitled to receive any monies which may have accrued in the said Account unless or until High Court Suit No. 94 of 1968 (Messrs. S. Chhaganlal and Co. vs Smt. Chandrabai A. B. Nair) is dismissed or finally disposed of and the claim, if any, of the Plaintiffs in the said suit being satisfied by the Vendor, the Purchaser and the Vendor together shall release whatever monies have accrued in the said Account together with interest to the Vendor, without any hitch.”

Shyamasundar: \[laughing\] I like your words, Srila Prabhupada, “without any hitch”!

\[everyone laughs\]

Prabhupada: He is bluffing. He will frighten these young American boys, taking their money, but he will never sell. We have got many enemies, but we don’t care for them. Still we shall go on. Johnny Walker is still going strong, \[loud laughter\]

Tamal Krishna: What to do now, Srila Prabhupada?

Prabhupada: Just you phone to Mr. Nair and to this Mr. Diwanji. Ask them to come and see me.

The next day was Sunday, December 3, and Prabhupada wrote to Karandhar in Los Angeles: “Still we have not finished the business with Nair, and the case has become very complicated without any settlement. They were supposed to settle the Conveyance Deed on December first, as we had made a supplemental agreement in Hyderabad and Mrs. Nair had signed it. That was very favorable for us. But other things have interfered, and they have defaulted for one reason or another, so we have got the upper hand. Now I have asked Mr. Diwanji to come to see me tomorrow morning, and Mr. Nair is also coming here, so I want to make a settlement with them without further delay.”

We waited all day Monday at Mr. Valia’s house, but neither Diwanji nor Nair showed up. Prabhupada was leaving for Ahmedabad early the next day for a two-week pandal engagement, and Tamal and I were confused and worried. With Prabhupada gone, how could we fight this demon on our own? We were clearly inexperienced kids from the love-and-peace generation, not fighters. A. B. Nair was the king of Bombay hooliganism and crooked dealings; he had organized armies of goondas, mercenaries who did his bidding. He even had a major newspaper behind him. Even Tamai Krishna, the tough guy from New York, was powerless before this monster. That night, Prabhupada gave us his parting instructions.

Prabhupada: So, what did Mr. Nair say to you on phone?

Tamai Krishna: I talked with Mrs. Nair. She says he’s sick, but he wants us to come to their house tomorrow to discuss everything.

Prabhupada: We have given three lakhs rupees to Mulla & Mulla. Now that may be at risk. You tell him these points: number one, we do not agree with his contentions regarding Ambubhai and Diwanji, and number two, we do not agree to his artificial deadline of December 12. He has defaulted. This is how we shall reply. Just make a note. If you cannot settle amicably, then you take back the money from Mulla and put into a separate account at the bank, six months’ fixed deposit.

After Prabhupada left for the airport, Tamal, Giriraj, and I went over to Nair’s house in the Theosophical Housing Colony, just a mile south down Juhu Tara Road from Hare Krishna Land. The house was like a tomb inside, curtains drawn, dark and cold. We waited nearly an hour, sipping on lime juice brought by a servant. Finally, Mrs. Nair came in and said that Mr. Nair was too ill to see us today, could we come back tomorrow? She also intimated that there were other buyers waiting to buy her property for much more money. It was a struggle to remain polite and appear calm in what was becoming a desperate situation, but we said, “Yes, we’ll be back tomorrow at 2 p.m.”

The next day, now December 6, we showed up on time, and after a long wait Mr. Nair came into his front room in pajamas, sweating and pale, and said, gruffly, that we should just let the lawyers work it out, that he’d been instructed not to say anything—and left the room.

The situation was now time-critical, compounded by delays in exchanging letters between law offices only blocks apart. Both Chimanlal Shah and Ambubhai & Diwanji were on Dalal Street, only four blocks from Mulla & Mulla. Either the typing pools were slow or the couriers got lost—but for whatever reason letters sometimes took days to reach their destinations. It didn’t help that Prabhupada’s legal team had to commute hours each day to and from Juhu.

So to be closer to the action, on the evening of December 6, Tamal, Giriraj, and I moved to a downtown apartment in the Heliopolis Building, owned by Mr. Valia. The flat had been built in the 1930s and was old but spacious, with marble floors and high ceilings, near the sea on Colaba Causeway at the southern tip of Bombay, eight minutes by taxi to Mulla & Mulla.

We return to our lawyers’ offices the next morning, and Mr. Bhesania shows us two letters he’d received that day. The first, from Chimanlal Shah, says that because they have received no reply as yet from their two letters to us dated November 30 and December 1, “our client takes it that your client is agreeable to what is stated in our two letters which please confirm immediately.”

The second letter was from Daru & Daru, attorneys for the Chhaganlal Brothers, stating that in the matter of High Court Suit no. 94 of 1968, ISKCON has been added as “party respondents” to a notice of motion brought by Chhaganlal to restrain Nair “from selling or from completing the sale or from dealing with or from alienating or from transferring or from parting with the possession of the property agreed to be sold by the first defendant to the Plaintiffs to any or persons other than the Plaintiffs.” There would be a hearing at the High Court before Justice S. K. Desai on Monday, December 11 to determine this matter.

Bhesania replies to Shah that his clients had not replied to Shah’s letters because “your client’s husband Mr. A. B. Nair approached our clients and has been holding without prejudice discussions with them.... In the meanwhile our clients have instructed us to inform you that they are not at all agreeable to what is stated in your aforesaid letters and they also deny all and singular the allegations and contentions contained in your said letters which are prejudicial to them.” Bhesania also informs Shah that ISKCON is now part of the Chhaganlal notice of motion, and that “our clients wait the result of the Motion.”

What a mess! Tamal, Giriraj, and I itemize and discuss what’s happened to date:

(1) Nair has missed the December 1 deadline. OK, that’s partly our fault for making the two-into-one conveyance change. Still, everything was in place to finish on December 4.

(2) Except that, at the last minute, Nair declared he would not release the Certificate of Title because Ambhubai & Diwanji were no longer our attorneys, implying that we have to rehire them; we suspect A&D are in cahoots with Nair, and so there’s a clear conflict of interest going on here.

(3) The Chhaganlal suit against Nair hasn’t been settled for four years. Until that’s resolved, we can’t move forward with the purchase. Now it looks like Chhaganlal’s new notice of motion will stall the sale to us, possibly for years to come.

(4) Mrs. Nair has hinted that she has other buyers lined up who are willing to pay more money.

(5) We’ve discovered that Nair is entangled in several other pending litigations. Besides the Chhaganlal suit, in the 1960s Nair’s India National Press company was named defendant in a Supreme Court case disputing journalists’ wages, and this case has not been resolved. Furthermore, multiple suits have been brought by or against Nair over the past few years regarding encroachments on the same Juhu property, resulting in protracted litigations.

(6) Everything points to a pattern of cheating with Nair—selling the land over and over and keeping the down payments.

(7) We’ve placed Rs. 300,000/- of Krishna’s hard-earned cash into an escrow account; there is clear and present danger that this money can be impounded or lost if Nair brings some sort of countersuit. Whatever happens, we can’t jeopardize this money!

(8) Prabhupada has repeatedly said, “We shall make no compromise with Nair unless we come to the court,” and, “We are prepared to take back the money and cancel the whole thing.”

In panic mode, on the morning of December 8 we rush to Mulla & Mulla and ask Mr. Bhesania, “How can we get our money back, the three hundred thousand rupees?” He says that to get our money back from escrow means we have to legally cancel the sale.

Tamal asks Bhesania, “Can we do that, cancel the sale?”

“Yes, you and Shyamasundar are both signatories to the sale agreement of November 24, so you are allowed to cancel under clause five: ‘If the purchase is not completed as herein provided owing to willful delay or default on the part of the Vendor it shall be at the option of the Purchaser to rescind this Agreement and in that event the Purchaser shall be entitled to receive back the earnest money from the Vendor and also all the Costs, charges, and expenses of this Agreement incurred by the Purchaser up to that time.’”

“OK, let’s do that, let’s cancel the sale—we cannot risk this money.” Bhesania immediately writes the following letter to Chimanlal

Shah: “In view of the breaches committed by your clients which we propose to set out in a separate letter, our clients have instructed us to terminate the Agreement of Sale dated 8th February 1972 read with the Supplemental Agreement dated 24th November 1972, which we hereby do with immediate effect. Please note that we are returning to our clients the amount of Rs. 300,000/- which they had deposited with us to show their bona fides.”

Later that day, Bhesania delivers to Shah a nine-page letter explaining in detail our reasons for canceling the sale, including our suspicions of Nair’s collusion with Ambubhai & Diwanji: “Our clients note that your client has had access to the personal correspondence between Mr. Diwanji and our Shri Prabhupada. Our clients have not informed your client about the said correspondence and would like to know through whom and how your clients got the said access to the said correspondence.”

Bhesania furthermore denies that we agreed to Nair’s unilateral deadline of December 12, and demands his clients’ right to claim the sum of Rs. 207,500/-, paid to Nair to date, plus damages and costs of construction and improvements to the Nair property. He ends the letter with: “Our clients have instructed us to state that they will always remain open to any suggestions for a fair and reasonable solution in the matter.”

There. It’s done. At least the money’s safe.

That night, Visakha devi dasi tracks us down at the Heliopolis apartment. She has come straight from Ahmedabad by overnight train and has been looking for us all day with a letter from Srila Prabhupada dated yesterday, December 7:

My dear Shyamasundar and Tamala,

Please accept my blessings. I am very much surprised not to hear from you about the Nair affair. I have tried to contact you by phone, but it is understood that you have left Mr.

Valia’s place and they could not say where you are. We have sent a telegram today reading as follows: “Anxious to know progress with Nair. Call urgent 83260.”

So my definite order is as follows: if the conveyance is not yet signed or there is difficulty, then immediately take back the money from the attorneys (3 lakhs), and make a fixed deposit with the bank for six months.

Whew! OK, we’ve done that. But then Visakha says, “Prabhupada’s verbal instruction to you is ‘Do not terminate the sale agreement.’”

Visakha: So Prabhupada asked me to go with a message for the three of them, that under no circumstances were they to cancel the sales agreement. But by the time I found them, they had already canceled it, hoodwinked by the lawyers. The three of them were totally dismal and confused by this. It was a bleak time.

Jeez, Tamai, what have we done?

The next morning, December 9, we received a letter from Chimanlal Shah: “We are instructed to state that by reason of breach of the contract on the part of your clients our clients hereby forfeit the sum of Rs. 207,500/- paid by your clients to our clients and our client reserves her rights to claim from your clients damages for breach of the contract on your clients’ part. Your clients are also required to immediately within 24 hours from the receipt of this letter hand over to our client vacant and peaceful possession of the property.”

War had been declared.

We phoned Srila Prabhupada at the number Visakha had given us, and he told me to come immediately to Ahmedabad with all the documents. I grabbed the first train, and arrived early on the morning of December 10.

Ahmedabad is the capital city of Gujarat state, 330 miles due north of Bombay. For millennia Gujarat has been India’s door to the West. Its people have been traders and businessmen—and largely Krishna devotees—since time immemorial. Two years earlier we’d experienced an overwhelming flood of Gujarati devotion while we were guests of the Jariwala family in Surat, and Prabhupada was always urging his leaders to open centers in this pious corner of India. He had written Giriraj back in June 1972: “Ahmedabad is a great industrial city. It has got more than 100 big, big mills and if all of the mill owners and officers become our members, from Ahmedabad and other cities in Gujarat... you can collect millions of rupees. In Gujarat State we can open a Center in every city because the people are so much dedicated to Krishna.” Giriraj, Hamsaduta, Gargamuni, Dinanath, Paramahamsa, and others had traveled to Ahmedabad over the past two years, and now several devotees there had arranged a two-week program for Prabhupada to speak to its citizens.

After the nine-hour train ride from Bombay, I made my way to the palatial bungalow of industrialist Mr. Navnitlal Patel, who lived in the upscale neighborhood of Gulbai Tekra, in the heart of Ahmedabad city. I was there to report to my spiritual master our humiliating defeat at the hands of Mr. Nair.

Shyamasundar: I’m so sorry, Srila Prabhupada. We had already canceled the sales agreement by the time Visakha found us. Bhesania told us that the only way to get our money back was to cancel the sale.

Prabhupada: Never mind. It is done. At least you have got the money back. In fact, sale agreement is not canceled. I am one of the signatories, and I have not agreed to cancel, so the cancelation is not valid.

Shyamasundar: But...

Prabhupada: No, it is all right. You have made mistake, but our position is very strong. Sales agreement is not canceled because I have not canceled. Nair has openly breached the contract. Now we must meet him in the courts, and he must face charges.

I was so surprised—and relieved—by Prabhupada’s merciful reaction. His anger was directed more at Mr. Nair and the conniving lawyers. At least now Prabhupada could personally guide our legal strategy, through me, by telephone to Tamal Krishna and Giriraj in Bombay.

Srutakirti was very sick—he thought he might have malaria—but he brought me up to date on events in Ahmedabad. Prabhupada had spoken at two Rotary Club meetings, and he was speaking every night in a pandal erected on the Lal Bungalow grounds on Chimanlal Giridharlal Road, just a short walk from Mr. Patel’s house. The leading citizens of Ahmedabad were impressed by Prabhupada’s presentations, and nightly attendance at the pandal had swelled to about ten thousand souls. Prabhupada was speaking very strongly from the Bhagavad-gita, as he had in Hyderabad, winning over these knowledgeable but basically misinformed proKrishna people. I took one look at Srutakirti’s yellow eyes and said, “Man, you’ve got jaundice!”

Srutakirti: Srila Prabhupad, I have jaundice.

Prabhupada: Oh, and what are your symptoms?

Srutakirti: My urine is dark, I have no appetite, and I feel very weak.

Prabhupada: Hmm. Perhaps I have jaundice also.

Srutakirti: The devotees have suggested that perhaps I shouldn’t cook for you because jaundice is highly contagious. Even touching your plates and preparing the food could be dangerous.

Prabhupada: No. That’s all right. You go ahead and cook. There is nothing to worry about.

Ding-ding!

“Shyamasundar?!”

Softly said, but cutting through a door and down the hall and through another door and into my sleeping skull like thunder— Wham! My body flies from bed, fresh from a dream—Prabhupada calls!—and I hit the ground running, literally dashing down the hall, struggling to remember where I am and how to reach that voice, my heart leaping with joy and dread (did I do something wrong?) as

I grip Prabhupada’s door latch, squeeze a breath and plop to the floor. I catch a quick read of his mood: ahh, he’s calm and thoughtful—maybe I’m safe. As I scoot across the rug and compose myself before his desk, he points to his Dictaphone—that big gaudy watch someone gave him hanging loosely upside-down from his swanlike wrist—and says, “The letters are ready for typing.”

I’m grinning from ear to ear, always filled with delightful wonder at seeing him again—it’s been, what, four hours—and this morning I take an extra pause to drink in the beauty of his face. Is it the flickering fluorescent table light that casts his cheeks in blue? A couple of crows call through the predawn curtains rustling softly behind him. Outside I can hear distant temple bells, a taxi horn, the scurry of early traffic. His big teeth flash white and gold in the shadows behind the desktop pool of light, full face moving now into the light. Those soft doe eyes, so like the sacred brahmin cows of India, his velvet neck bristled with downy grey in the early morning—what matter of creation is he to possess such perfect youthful radiance?

“Sooo, you have taken rest?”

“Yes, Prabhupada, but I got lost in the hall, trying to remember where I was.”

His face cracks open in a laugh, eyes opening very wide in surprise. Then he shuts them tight and his cheeks stretch back to impossible limits, and his head bobs up and down in a full belly laugh. His eyes are open again now, moist with delight, as he contemplates the strangeness of our high-speed journey, and he says, between gasps of laughter: “You are just like the, what is that, this goat, tied to my string, and always bumping.”

“Yes, Prabhupada, even if you lead me to the slaughterhouse.”

“That is the position of guru, to lead you to Krishna, even you do not want to go.”

I’d brought a big packet of letters that had arrived in Bombay for Prabhupada. We sat to answer them, and it was such a relief not to think about Juhu legal problems but to focus instead on this vital lifeline to Prabhupada’s worldwide movement. Prabhupada dictated seventeen letters over the next three days, several of which are unforgettable classics:

To Balavanta, who was running for mayor in Atlanta, he wrote: “I have had a good laugh and enjoyed your tricking the opponent candidates in the election race.” And then he put the whole matter of politics and fighting (and thus, unsaid, the current situation with Nair) into an eternal perspective: “But one thing is, we have not got anything to gain by ‘fighting the demons in the streets and courts’ ... No, our process of solving the matter is simple.... Just like Krishna. He did not call for fighting until after every chance for settlement failed. So we shall try to overcome our opponents by, first, our words and our behavior, and all means of friendly approach we shall attempt by sober planning, and only later, all else failing, shall we actually fight. That is our philosophy. And if we stick to these lines of politics and diplomacy as set out for us by Krishna, we shall expect always victory, without any doubt.”

To Shukadeva in Seattle Prabhupada wrote: “My request to you is that you enter into the universities and colleges wherever possible and preach there with a view to recruiting some first-class devotees for helping me manage and push on this movement all over the world ... That will be the best contribution. Now spread this idea also to the other leaders: Appeal yourself to the high class of men, not the mass. Mass, too, we do not eliminate anyone, but if best men are there to run on things, mass will follow later.” He asked me to handwrite a note at the bottom: “Kindly xerox and distribute copies of this letter to all temple presidents, GBC, and sannyasis.”

To Tusta Krishna in Australia Prabhupada wrote: “All of my disciples are pure devotees. Anyone sincerely serving the spiritual master is a pure devotee.... Amongst ourselves one should respect others as Prabhu, master, one another. As soon as we distinguish here is a pure devotee, here is a nonpure devotee, that means I am a nonsense.” And here was the kicker: “Your next question, after leaving this material realm does the devotee remain forever with his spiritual master? The answer is yes. And if somebody does not go \[to the spiritual sky\], then I shall have to come back to take him there. One should remember this and every one of my disciples should act in such a way that they may go with me and may not have to come back to take another birth.Siddhasvarupa will go, you will go, Shyamasundar will go, all others will go. We will have another ISKCON there. Of course, Mr. Nair must stay.”

On the evening of December 11 Tamal Krishna phoned from Bombay. That afternoon he and Giriraj had attended the Chhaganlal vs. Nair Notice of Motion hearing before Justice Desai at the Bombay High Court. The judge granted an ad interim injunction in favor of Chhaganlal, restraining Nair from selling the property and thus effectively freezing the situation at Hare Krishna Land.

Giriraj: During the course of the proceedings, Justice Desai appealed to Mrs. Nair to execute the conveyance in favor of one or the other parties from whom she had taken money. But her solicitor replied that she had found still another purchaser for considerably more money, and that she wanted to sell to an unnamed third party.

'When he hears the news that Nair would be restrained by the courts from any further action at Hare Krishna Land until the Chhaganlal case was settled, Prabhupada is very happy. He comments that now he has Mr. Nair right where he wants him. Prabhupada wants to immediately launch a civil suit against Nair, and he dictates a letter of instructions to Mr. Bhesania, which I mail to Tamal Krishna in Bombay:

Because of the vendor’s breach of contract we are compelled to hereby officially direct you to immediately institute civil proceedings against the vendor for breach of the contract in order to claim back from her the sum of Rs.207,500/- paid by ourselves to the vendor for earnest money and toward the purchase price, and to further claim damages of the sum of Rs. 90,000/- spent by us on the said land for construction and improvement, and further to claim repayment from the vendor for all amounts we may have to pay now and in the future to the courts for the due process of the law to be carried out in this matter.

Prabhupada writes to life members in Bombay, calling them to a strategy meeting at 4:30 on the afternoon of December 18, when he is due to arrive back in that city: “We have neither changed our plan nor have we any intention of changing our plan of developing Hare Krishna Land in Bombay. We shall stick to the place to the last moment and we require your cooperation very much. I shall be the last man to give up the Hare Krishna Land to the rogue Mr. Nair.”

Meanwhile, in Ahmedabad, Prabhupada’s nightly lectures at the Lal Bungalow pandal were amazing. This was without doubt the most learned audience we had yet seen in India. The question-and-answer period following each lecture went on for up to an hour, and the questions were, for the most part, pitched to Prabhupada with Sanskrit scriptural references. For example, one man from the audience asked, “Then guru has been defined like this: ajnana-timirandhasya jnananjana-shalakaya, chakshur unmilitam yena tasmai sri-gurave namah... But now I have understood everything from Your Grace, and now that ajnana has been removed. I have now become more Krishna conscious than before. So should I give credit to all those gurus who have helped me to understand? Or should I now select, or should I accept this guru or that guru only? So that doubt should be cleared.”

Prabhupada’s replies were complete, deep, scholarly, and laced with Sanskrit quotations. In fact, in his lecture on December 14, the final night of the program, Prabhupada quoted more than seventy Sanskrit verses from a dozen scriptures, astounding this erudite audience. They had never seen or heard a swami who knew this much. Nearly fifteen thousand people attended that night.

If you titled each of the Ahmedabad evening lectures, the list would read something like this:

December 6: Krishna Gave Bhagavad-gita to Get Us Out of Ignorance

December 7: Eleven Signs of Ignorance of Self

December 8: What Can Max Muller Understand About the Vedas?

December 9: What the Human Form of Life Is For

December 10: Classless Aryanism

December 11: From Blockhead to Bhakta

December 12: Conclusion of the Yoga System

December 13: Kanishtha, Madhyama, Uttama Stages of Devotion

December 14: The Theory of Uncertainty

On the morning of Saturday, December 16, the Ahmedabad life members arranged for Prabhupada to speak at the Sabarmati Central Jail, where Mahatma Gandhi had famously been incarcerated in 1922. It was an active jail, and the prisoners were given leave to enter a hall, where Prabhupada spoke to them. For months afterward, Prabhupada would comment on that experience:

The prisoners, they received us, they chanted Hare Krishna mantra. So it was a good function. So we went to jail. But what we have got to do with the jail? We have no business with the jail. Similarly, sometimes we are invited in a very rich man’s house, or governor’s house. We go there. That is heaven. So we have nothing to take, either from this heaven or from that hell. We have to chant Hare Krishna mantra anywhere. That is our business.

Gandhi’s room, where Gandhi was staying they showed me, and I sat down there. So, there is very good arrangement for the comforts of the prisoners, if they abide by the laws. Similarly, this material world is just like prison house, and we living entities, we have come here as punishment: krishna-bahirmukha hana bhoga vancha kare, nikata-stha maya tare japatiya dhare. Anyone who is within this material world, he is condemned. Simply there is division. Just like in Sabarmati jail also, there are first-class prisoners, there are second-class prisoners, and third-class prisoners. The first-class prisoners, just like political prisoners, they are given separate bungalow and servants, newspaper, books. But they cannot go out of the prison. That is called first class. Similarly, there are second class. And third class, ordinary.”

On December 18, 1972, we returned to Bombay at 11:30 a.m. on Indian Airlines flight 461. We drove straight from the Santa Cruz airport to “Sea Face Park,” an exclusive enclave of ten, six-story apartment houses built in art-deco style in 1938 by the Mafatlal family, on the western edge of central Bombay. Most residents at Sea Face Park were relatives or employees of Mafatlal’s vast textile business empire, and one of them, Mr. Kartikeya Mahadevia, had offered to house Srila Prabhupada and his servants in his spacious apartment there, at number A-11.

The Juhu devotees were planning another big pandal program at Cross Maidan, in the center of Bombay, and they’d booked the grounds from January 12 through 21,1973. To finance the upcoming show, they were compiling a souvenir book of paid advertisements from metropolitan businesses. To more easily solicit these ads, a number of devotees had moved from Juhu downtown to the Heliopolis apartment in Colaba. Both Prabhupada and the majority of devotees were now located in central Bombay, both to be closer to the Cross Maidan action and to more directly conduct our growing legal battle with Nair.

Kartikeya Mahadevia was an important life member in Bombay, a fervent supporter of ISKCON since its earliest days. He and his wife and two children loved Srila Prabhupada very much. Kartikeya and his wife gave Prabhupada their master bedroom, and Srutakirti, Pradyumna, and I set up camp in the living room. Sea Face Park was on Warden Road, just a few blocks north of our old headquarters at Akash Ganga, right by the Arabian Sea — a neighborhood very familiar to us. The US Consulate, “Lincoln House,” was practically next door, and in this brilliant, sunlit seaside setting, in the tranquility of a previous gentler age, we felt right at home.

Kartikeya was a real character. Ahead of his time, he was an ardent environmentalist, natural food health nut, an early advocate of self-sustaining villages and technology, and probably the only person in India who subscribed to Mother Earth News. He grew wheatgrass in trays under every window in the place, and constantly urged us to drink the foul green juice. In 1902, his great-grandfather, Chandulal Mahadevia, had partnered with Gagalbhai Mafatlal to found their first textile mill, and over seven decades the two families diversified into chemicals and engineering and built a considerable industrial empire. Gagalbhai’s grandson, Arvind Mafatlal, along with Arvinds eighteen-year-old son, Hrishikesh, were also very attracted to Prabhupada, so Prabhupada began giving regular classes and kirtans in Mahadevia’s living room, speaking intimately to this close-knit group. Mornings and evenings these prominent men, sometimes with their wives and children, gathered at Sea Face Park, often joined by devotees who came over from Colaba. To this captive audience Prabhupada spoke from The Nectar of Devotion in a powerful way, often blowing our minds with his forthright, candid rhetoric.

Giriraj: Prabhupada was on fire in his lectures, especially about cow slaughter in India, the land of religion, and how India was so fallen now she had to import brahmins (us) and milk powder. Hearing him made my hair stand on end just from his forcefulness.

Now the question is whether this kind of black marketing, bribing, is required for spiritual life. And the answer is that yes, everything is required, provided you can satisfy Krishna. Just like Marshall Foch. He was in charge of the French centers in the First World War. So there were many refugees from Belgium, mostly women and children—they came to France. Then Marshall Foch became very angry. “What can I do? This is a battlefield!” So it was ordered that they should be killed. So actually it so happened that all these women and children, they were assembled together, and with four guns from four sides they were blown up. You see? Their own allies. So these things take place in war sometimes. The point is that as people act, by hooks and crooks, they do everything for satisfying the senses, to gain some material profit. Similarly, if need be, everything can be accepted for Krishna’s service. We have to satisfy Krishna only. “Krishna is asking me to tell lie. Yes, I shall tell lie.” That is bhakti. If I consider, “Oh, I have never spoken lies in my life. How can I speak lie?” that is not bhakti. It is pratikula, against the desire of Krishna. Common people cannot understand this. They will shudder.

Prabhupada’s utterances were always based on time, place, and circumstance. He’d say things to specific persons, at certain times, under variable circumstances to fit a particular occasion. To try to understand Srila Prabhupada by pointing to “he said this” as opposed to “he said that” will only limit Prabhupada’s inconceivable, limitless knowledge about everything. For example, on December 25, the day before he made the above comments on Marshall Foch—“in Krishna’s service, anything goes”—Prabhupada wrote to Sri Govinda in Chicago: “So far this making some false story for collecting money or selling book, of course we may do anything for Krishna, but that is supposed to be reserved for very advanced experts in Krishna Consciousness—they know how to catch the big fish without themselves getting wet. So it is not very much advisable to make lies just to sell books. If we simply stick to describing how wonderful is Krishna, then whatever we may lie or exaggerate, that will not be lie! But other things, lies, they will not help us to train ourselves in truthfulness. Lie to some, not to others, that is not a good philosophy. Rather the brahmanas are always truthful, even to their enemies... That art you must develop, not art of lying. Convince them to give by your preaching the Absolute Truth, not by tricking, that is more mature stage of development of Krishna Consciousness.”

All paradoxes were resolved within Srila Prabhupada’s consistent theme: Krishna. Every day, at every moment, whatever Prabhupada thought, spoke, or performed was always about Krishna. And Prabhupada’s consistency was at the same time always different, ever fresh, at all times new and surprising.

After four days at Kartikeya Mahadevia’s home, we moved to a nearby apartment, number B-11, recently vacated after the untimely death of Kartikeya’s brother Kumudchandra, who had been married to Arvind Mafatlal’s sister, Ushaben. We would stay at B-11 for the next thirty-two days. Mornings, we walked around Sea Face Park, or sometimes I drove Prabhupada in Mahadevia’s white Ambassador to the Hanging Gardens on Malabar Hill or to stroll the seaside esplanade at Worli. Some mornings Prabhupada commuted to Juhu to lecture at the temple there. At Juhu he initiated several new Indian devotees. One young local boy was given the name Haridas. I was amazed to hear Haridas’s story. He was an orphan, living as a petty thief on the mean streets of Bombay, and had seen the gold watch on Prabhupada’s wrist at the Cross Maidan festival in March 1971. Thinking, “If I join these people I may get an opportunity to steal that watch,” Haridas began to hang out with the devotees. Now, two years later, Prabhupada had stolen Haridas’s heart instead.

Malati showed up at Sea Face Park one day, in a truck from Jaipur, with two wooden boxes containing extra-large, 48” Radha and Krishna deities. As the crates were unpacked in his room, Srila

Prabhupada gazed at Radharani and said, “She is very beautiful, she has Mexican eyes. So these Deities shall be shipped to Mexico City.”

By January 1, 1973, Mr. Nair’s attorneys had not yet replied to our nine-page letter of December 8 detailing our reasons for canceling the sale agreement. Two weeks earlier Prabhupada had instructed Mulla & Mulla to file suit against Mr. Nair, but Mr. Bhesania wanted to wait for Nair’s reply to our December 8 letter before making such a rash move. Meanwhile, Bhesania suggested we bring suit in the Bombay City Court to block any possible eviction action at Hare Krishna Land. For this suit we retained yet another law firm called Harshad M. Shah and Associates, located in the Dahanukar Building on Kalbadevi Road in downtown Bombay. Our H. M. Shah lawyers bled suit on behalf of ISKCON against the Bombay Municipal Corporation, restraining the BMC from taking any action at Hare Krishna Land until our dealings with Nair were resolved. Suit No. 32 was heard in the Bombay City Civil Court before Judge Thakar on January 5, 1973, and this was the court’s decision:

THIS COURT DOTH ORDER THAT in the meantime and until the hearing and final disposal of the said Notice of Motion the defendants \[BMC\] their agents and servants be and they are hereby restrained from taking any action ... or removing and/or pulling down the shed \[temple\] ... that they will not deal with, dispose of, alienate, encumber or part with possession of the suit premises in any manner whatsoever and will not carry out any construction, addition, alterations, or extension of any nature whatsoever to the suit premises.

Now it was really getting complicated. Half a dozen law firms were involved, several courts, and legal documents flew back and forth. I was totally confused, but Prabhupada seemed to have a handle on it all and calmly carried on. Prabhupada was like a lion during these weeks; he was at war with Nair, feeling his oats, preaching strongly at Sea Face Park or Juhu or to impromptu clusters of well-dressed gentlemen who gathered around him on the paths at Hanging Gardens or on the esplanade at Worli.

One morning at Worli, we passed a man who was lying on the sidewalk—all over India, tired, skinny men slept where they fell—but when we returned he was still lying in the same position. Prabhupada stopped, pointed with his cane, and said, “This man is dead”—then walked on without further comment. Srutakirti and I were thunderstruck by this incident, and later that night I wrote to Rupanuga in South Carolina: “We stepped over a dead body this morning on Prabhupada’s walk, eyes glazed over, one arm stiffly askew, abrupt upward from the elbow, fingers poised gracefully in rigor mortis as if modeling a new watch, legs opposed and bent as if he clicked his heels and froze there standing, breathed his last and plopped down backward into nothing much unusual.” I was flustered—it was the first dead body I’d ever seen—and ran ahead to start the car for the return to Sea Face Park. I jammed the key into the ignition and tried to turn it again and again, but the key just didn’t seem to fit. Prabhupada and Srutakirti got into the backseat, and I said, “Prabhupada, the car will not go,” and I got out to hail a taxi.

Srutakirti: After a few minutes, two well-dressed Indian men opened the car doors and sat down in the front seat. I became frightened. Srila Prabhupada, smiling, appeared very calm while he spoke to them in Hindi. Suddenly, they put their key in the ignition and the car immediately started. They drove away. I finally realized that we had been sitting in the wrong car. “We are sorry,” Prabhupada told them. “No problem,” they said. “We’ll take you back to where you are staying.” “This is the difference between India and America,” Srila Prabhupada told me after getting out of the car. “In America they would have said, Hey, what are you doing? Get out of my car!’ Possibly beaten you. In India, still there is some culture. In India they see a sadhu and they have some respect.”

As GBC for Great Britain I was always attentive to what was going on in London. Prabhupada, too, was anxious that we find bigger premises there. Back in November Rupanuga had sent $30,000 to London as a loan for a down payment on the Duke of Windsor property. When that deal fell through Prabhupada encouraged Dhananjaya to accelerate his search for another place. In late December Dhananjaya had phoned from London to ask if they could keep the $30,000 loan, because George Harrison was now looking at a monastery, and there was “every chance” that they would get it “within one week.” Prabhupada had heard this sort of excuse before, and he replied by letter: “But if there is no solid program for getting the place, simply we are depending upon agents and George to do everything, then we may not be favored by Krishna to get a place very quickly. Better to take the upper hand and begin very energetically attempting to get some place, at least by springtime. Otherwise Krishna may take away this golden opportunity, being disappointed by our lack of enthusiasm and labor.” In other words, Dhananjaya, get your butt in gear!

Dhananjaya also reported that George had sent a carload of fruit to the Bury Place temple on Bhaktisiddhanta Saraswati’s Disappearance Day, December 23. On January 4 Prabhupada writes a long, remarkable letter to George, thanking him for remembering his spiritual master and instructing him:

Yes, if one simply comes to that platform of spontaneous love for the spiritual master, then very quickly he will get love of God, or Krishna, and his all hankerings and lamentations of life will be finished. That is the process. Just like when two friends meet and, if the friend is also with his dog, the gentleman pats his dog first, is it not? So the friend becomes automatically pleased, his dog being patted. I have seen it in your country sometimes when I used to walk in the Regents Park early mornings. So it is like that, the spiritual master is the pet dog of Krishna, and if he is pleased so Krishna is also pleased.Therefore I say that you are a great soul, not because you have got some material name, fame, or wealth. That will vanish at the time of your death, so what good it is? But because you are serving Krishna in devotional loving mood, therefore I say great soul. There is one Bengali proverb that the person who is actually famous is the Devotee who is known by all other Devotees to be great............ So that will be the supreme perfection of our existence, to come to that point of being best friend, best devotee of Krishna. And how is that? If we become simply engaged in His preaching work, trying with whatever talent or education or experience or material advantages we have got, to spread Krishna Consciousness message all over the world. That is best friend of Krishna, that is Krishna’s pet dog... I know you are very intelligent boy, and I like you very much, you are better than my own son, and I am so much pleased that you are displaying gradually more and more the qualities of becoming Krishna’s pet dog as described above. If you have got any time, you may kindly write to me, or you are free to come to India to live with me for some time, as you like.

During the latter part of December and early January, Prabhupada wrote nearly seventy letters, guiding his disciples around the world, including gems like these:

Krishna does not like to see His sincere devotee suffer or become frustrated or depressed. He will not stand idly by in any such case, so do not fear on that account. Krishna has got some plan for you, always think in that way, and very soon He will provide everything to your heart’s desire and you will meet Krishna face to face, you may know it for certain.

Do not centralize anything. Each temple must remain independent and self-sufficient. That was my plan from the very beginning, why you are thinking otherwise? Do not think in this way of big corporation, big credits, centralization— these are all nonsense proposals... Our leaders shall be careful not to kill the spirit of enthusiastic service, which is individual and spontaneous and voluntary. That is the art of management: to draw out spontaneous loving spirit of sacrificing some energy for Krishna.

A letter came from Rameswara in L.A. informing Prabhupada that over the three-day Christmas weekend the Los Angeles devotees had distributed more than 17,100 books, valued at $9,500, exactly one lakh of rupees. Srila Prabhupada was over the moon, and replies: “That indicates to me that people are at last becoming a little serious about this Krishna consciousness movement in your country....Understanding is one thing, and practical application of that understanding is another. But as devotees of Krishna, being engaged in the practical work of spreading Krishna consciousness, that is already the highest realization. That’s all right, that is our real mission, to deliver the world by preaching Krishna’s message to others, but even higher realization, the highest realization, is to save oneself.”

Besides typing many letters I’m busy trying to organize Prabhupada’s travel itinerary. After the Cross Maidan festival we’ll go to Calcutta on January 23. Then, if Madhudvisa can arrange tickets and a program, we’ll proceed to Jakarta, in Indonesia, then Australia and New Zealand. Prabhupada wants to be back in India by the first of March to celebrate Lord Chaitanya’s Appearance Day in Mayapur.

The Bombay devotees were working day and night to organize and promote the Second Cross Maidan International Hare Krishna Festival, so I threw my hand in to help. Tamal Krishna and I met with Mr. Bhattar, the pandal contractor, and designed an innovative round pandal, like the circus tents of our youth—something they hadn’t seen before in India. I worked with Yadubara and Visakha to design eye-catching billboards, posters, and leaflets with slogans like: “Krishna Consciousness: World’s Greatest Spiritual Culture!”, “Knowledge is Bliss!”, and “These Young Westerners Had Everything—Do You Think They Would Give It Up for Less?” Since India’s 1971 war with Pakistan, airspace over Bombay had been restricted, so this time they wouldn’t allow us to fly our “Hare Krishna” balloon over Cross Maidan.

Prabhupada always dealt with each disciple in the manner that best suited that disciple’s temperament. For example, he rarely got angry with me, knowing that his anger would devastate me, maybe cause me to leave. But Tamal Krishna Goswami was raised on the streets of Manhattan and was hard-hitting and emotionally strong. Prabhupada knew this, and he could be very critical of Tamal sometimes, to toughen him up and shape him for leadership in India. Prabhupada openly blamed TKG for canceling the sale agreement with Nair, and Tamal would sit in front of Prabhupada’s guests, deeply humiliated, as Prabhupada said, “And this boy is so foolish and inexperienced that he has canceled the agreement.” Tamal Krishna had a real gift for getting things done, bullying you one minute and sweet-talking you the next. I thought he was doing a great job under the circumstances. But the stress of Prabhupada’s criticisms, the daily grind of dealing with lawyers and lawsuits, the pressure on him to build four major temples—plus the responsibility for Cross Maidan—was beginning to tell on TKG. His sweet side faded and he became even more abrupt than usual as he argued, demanded, grew petulant, even pouted to get his way. I was one of the few guys around him who could say, “Cool out, Tamal, you’re fulla crap and you know it.” Usually he’d grin, roll his eyes, and say, “You’re right, but don’t tell anybody else,” but these days it was hard to get a smile out of him.

The Nair litigation became increasingly complicated. Letters between lawyers overlapped; the Chhaganlal and Bombay Municipal Corporation suits were in play; our lawyer’s father died, forcing a long postponement; then the Christmas and New Year holidays intervened. It was getting so complex now that neither side really knew what was happening. I thought perhaps this had been Srila Prabhupada’s intent all along—to bring litigation to such an impossible mess and thus force a simple solution. Prabhupada dictated a letter to Mr. Bhesania on January 9, which Tamal Krishna signed: “We have advised the vendors that the sales agreements have been terminated. However after further consideration we feel it would be more to our interests to purchase the property after all. Therefore we would like you to represent our interests in such a way that may make it possible for us to eventually be able to purchase the property.”

Prabhupada was the master tactician. The next day, on January 10, Mr. Bhesania wrote a clever, three-page letter to Mr. Nair’s attorneys denying that Nair had any legal basis to, among other things, force us from the land until he had refunded our down payment. How’s this for obfuscation: “We are instructed to state that the attempt on your clients part to terminate the contract and forfeit the amount is only an afterthought in as much, as before this letter was dispatched by you on the 9th December 1972, our letter of the 8th December 1972 terminating the contract was received by you, as is evident from the postscript to your letter under reply. It is also pertinent to note that the alleged reason for terminating the contract by your client is nothing but our letter simply stating that our clients were not agreeable to what was stated in your letters of the 30th November 1972 and 1st December 1972 unilaterally extending the time for completion of the sale to 12th December 1972.”

Chimanlal Shah’s reply the next day, January 11, was just as hazy: “We are in receipt of your letter dated 12th December 1972 \[one month ago!\]. The said letter contains several false allegations and contentions. Our client repeats all the statements and contentions in our three letters to you, one dated 30th November 1972, the second dated the 1st December 1972, and the third dated the 9th December 1972, and deny each and every allegation and contention contained in your said letters which are contrary to or inconsistent with what is stated in our said three letters.”

Etc., etc., etc.
