Meanwhile, the stage was set for Cross Maidan. The Second International Hare Krishna Festival would bring a welcome tide of public sentiment to ISKCON, reinforcing our claim to the Juhu land. On December 31, 1972, the Sunday Standard printed a favorable article about Srila Prabhupada with the headline, “From Big Business to Krishna Cult, Swami with a World Mission.” There was an entire page describing Bhaktivedanta Swami, his background, his accomplishments in India, and his vision for the world. Meanwhile, advertisements and propaganda for the Cross Maidan festival were being plastered all over Bombay; some of the billboards featured an artist s rendition of the future temple, school, and hotel to rise out of the coconut groves of ISKCON’s new land in Juhu.

Now, in Bombay, there were many more devotees and life members—and more money—to prepare this second grand Cross Maidan festival. Giriraj, in his humble but insistent manner, had made friends with the mayor and many of Bombay’s biggest industrialists, a number of whom agreed to help finance the festival and even speak onstage. About twenty thousand people flocked to the novel round tent at Cross Maidan on opening night to hear Mayor Ravji K. Ganatra introduce Prabhupada and extol his accomplishments. The mayor presented Bhaktivedanta Swami with the Emblem of the City of Bombay as a token of respect and honor. This gold coat of arms is an open lotus blossom topped by a lion puissant and inscribed with symbols in four small separate panels, depicting the Gateway to India, a factory in a cog wheel, three sailing ships, and a sketch of the Bombay Municipal Corporation building. Mr. Ganatra said, “Your Society alone is counteracting the degradation of our modern youth who are falling prey to permissive life. You have already surpassed the greatest of saints in propagating Krishna consciousness around the world, and now with your foreign disciples you are helping Indians themselves remember their own great heritage, and so you have practically become a national hero.”

In his opening lecture that night at Cross Maidan, Prabhupada got right to it: “Anyone, beginning from Lord Brahma down to the small ant or microscopic insect—everyone, every living entity—has come here in this material world for gratifying their senses.... You are not this material body. You are not Indian, you are not American, you are not brahmin, you are not shudra. You are eternally part and parcel of Krishna. So your business is to serve Krishna. Understanding this is the purpose of all Vedic literature. ... We want that everyone, every thoughtful man, should read Bhagavad-gita As It Is. These books are very much welcome in the Western countries. I have got report just now from Los Angeles: within the Christmas holidays, we have sold one lakh worth of books within three days.... So I am struggling alone. I require many Indians to join and spread this movement all over the world.”

After the program Mr. Ganatra came to meet the Swami at Sea Face Park. Prabhupada turned on the charm, giving the mayor the gracious VIP treatment he reserved for few visitors. Ganatra was genuinely moved to see this unique Indian swami surrounded by loving foreign disciples, especially when Prabhupada introduced him to Atreya Rishi das, a smart Iranian boy from a Muslim background, and Atreya’s blond, blue-eyed American wife, Vaidhi Bhakti devi dasi.

On the evening of January 14 Prabhupada laid the groundwork for the next seven nights: “Our country, India, is called punya-bhumi, ‘the land of piety,’ because on this land all the great saintly persons appeared. The incarnations of God, such as Lord Ramachandra, Lord Krishna, Lord Buddha, Lord Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, and all the big, big acharyas—Shankaracharya, Sri Ramanujacharya, Madhavacharya, Vishnu Swami, Vyasadeva—all of them appeared on this sacred land of Bharatvarsha. But the present Bharatvarsha has degraded so much that we have lost our Vedic culture. We are now eating meat. We are drinking wine and we are having illicit sex life and indulging in gambling. This is India’s position. Otherwise the land is punya-bhumi. Therefore in spite of so much fallen condition, still you are anxious to hear about Krishna. You ladies and gentlemen who have come here, sacrificing your time—why? Because still the Vedic culture is twinkling within your heart and you are anxious to hear about Krishna.

“Just like we have our dealings. In Bombay there are so many businessmen, business dealings. But our staying, our dealings in Bombay city are temporary. But there is another place which is called sanatana-dhama. That place is eternal, and the parties, namely God and the living entities, both of them eternal. Their dealing is also eternal. That eternal dealing is called nitya-lila, eternal pastimes... Just like you do business in your business establishment, but you have to take permission, license, from the government. Similarly, although this body is yours—you are the proprietor of this body, you have been given freedom to utilize the body to your best interest—still, you cannot do anything without the permission of Krishna. That is the subject matter to understand spiritual life. Sarvasya caham hridi...”

Following each lecture, after Srila Prabhupada had left, hundreds of people remained behind, gathered in front of a second, small stage set up for “Questions and Answers.” This was a new concept: people could present their innermost spiritual questions to a bona fide representative of Krishna and receive personal answers. Achyutananda Swami, Tamal Krishna Goswami, and Giriraj sat in shifts on that stage until the last person had left—often after midnight.

Jagatpurush das was a tall, intelligent, laid-back American boy, and his sister Mary was the wife of my ex-drug-dealer partner in San Francisco. Jagatpurush had met a new young Indian devotee at Juhu named Gandiva dasi and proposed to her. Their colorful marriage ceremony took place onstage one night at Cross Maidan, with Srila Prabhupada presiding. It made a great show—an American devotee marrying a pretty Indian woman—and the newspapers had a field day with it.

Jagatpurush and others added a new dimension to the festival by staging dramas based on age-old Krishna themes. Prabhupada’s vyasasan was moved down into the audience for these dramas, and he jeered, laughed, and clapped along with twenty thousand members of the audience as Krishna got a terrible headache that could only be relieved by applying dust from His devotees’ feet. A businessman, an intellectual, and a yogi were each asked to give Krishna dust from their feet. All three refused, not wanting to offend the Lord. Finally, when the gopis heard about Krishna’s dilemma, they rushed to place their feet on Krishna’s head, thus removing His suffering. Guru Kripa and Yasodanandan Swami led rousing kirtans after the dramas.

Srila Prabhupada said many amazing things every day, every moment of every day. I couldn’t possibly remember them all, or even a tiny fraction of them, and I had no time to write them down, the pace was so fast, so I tried to record everything. It was a constant battle to find enough tape reels and cassettes. Each Uher 5” reel was good for two to five hours, using both sides of the tape and depending on spool speed. If we were running short of reels, sometimes I had to pack eight hours onto a single tape using 15/16 inches-per-second, the slowest of four speeds, so the sound quality was pretty bad. With Prabhupada’s accent and especially, with the heavy background noise of India, faster speeds were better. Best fidelity-vs-tape-space speed was 3.5 ips. The problem was, we had brought only ten reels from L.A., and new ones were not available, even in a big city like Bombay. Even tape cassettes were scarce. Most cassettes available in India were thirty minutes a side, one hour total. From time to time devotees from other countries would bring me new cassettes, but these days few devotees were arriving from abroad. No one had anticipated Prabhupada’s prodigious output. Since his arrival in India on October 15 until now—in only three months—Srutakirti and I had recorded 101 lectures! I soon realized that recording our beloved room conversations and walks was a luxury we could no longer afford; there just wasn’t enough tape. Even labeling the tapes was a problem. I’d have to find a few minutes of blank space here and a few minutes there, sometimes divide one lecture onto two or three separate reels or cassettes, erasing or adding to labels in tiny, almost undecipherable script. I and other devotees scoured Bombay for cassettes, coming up with a handful—just enough to almost keep up with Prabhupada’s spoken bounty. But now, after the opening-night January 14 lecture at Cross Maidan, there were no more tapes in town! It was devastating to know that the upcoming nights’ lectures would go unrecorded and be lost to posterity.

Visakha: The last day of the pandal was Sunday, January 21, and more people attended than ever before—tens of thousands. The final kirtan was especially enthusiastic. When Prabhupada came before the Deities, very gracefully he put his arms in the air and began to dance. It was almost as if he were weightless, he danced so effortlessly, and everyone was electrified by this. It felt as if all the sinful reactions of all those people suddenly lifted, just like birds leaving a tree when there's a sudden noise: the burden of karma lifting all at one time.

The Cross Maidan Festival had rallied the good citizens of Bombay to ISKCON’s camp; we were the darlings of the public eye. Everywhere in Bombay, wherever A. B. Nair went, Srila Prabhupada’s face was staring down at him from a billboard. Who knows why, but late one morning near the end of the festival, Mr. Nair showed up at Sea Face Park to meet Prabhupada, maybe to get more money or somehow convince him to leave the land so he could sell it to someone else. When Nair came into the room he was civil enough, but he radiated anger and disdain. We could see, too, that Prabhupada, usually so cordial with the Nairs, was no longer Mr. Nice Guy. We brought a chair for Mr. Nair—his leg was swollen and he was limping—and Prabhupada asked us to leave the room. Our ears were glued to the door, though, so we could hear the two conversing in Hindi peppered with English phrases like, “But in Hyderabad you have hypnotized me!” The voices rose abruptly, and the conversation became a shouting match.

Giriraj: Mr. Nair started shouting, “You are a trespasser. Get off my property!” And Srila Prabhupada shouted back, “It is not your property. It is Krishna’s property.” Both of them became angrier and angrier. Prabhupada pointed out that Krishna was already on the land and could not be moved. Then Mr. Nair went too far. He yelled, “I will break your Krishna!” and stormed out of the room.

Three weeks later, Mr. A. B. Nair had a massive coronary, and in a month he was dead. Prabhupada was in New Zealand when he heard this news, and he wrote to life members in Bombay: “This morning Srutakirti received telephonic message from Bali Mardan. It is understood that Mr. Nair is dead. So it is good news that Nrisinghadeva has killed a demon like him. Prahlada Maharaja said that even a saintly person becomes pleased when a scorpion or a snake is killed. So if it is a fact that Nair is dead it is a matter of great pleasure for all devotees.... Unless Mrs. Nair pays back all our invested money in the Juhu land, do not vacate the land under any circumstances. If you stick to your principles certainly Krishna will help and the indications are already there.”

But Mrs. Chandrabhai Nair didn’t give up so easily. Thousands of pages of legal documents testify to this bitter adversarial dispute over the Juhu plot of land. In early June 1973 Mrs. Nair loosed the police on Hare Krishna Land, and they tore down the temple in a lightning raid. Only last-minute intervention by the Bombay Municipal Commissioner saved the Deities from total destruction. Six more months of litigation followed before, finally, Mrs. Nair succumbed to the inevitable: Krishna lives here; you must relent. On November 19, 1973, the sale deed was executed and Mrs. Nair—14 lakhs of rupees richer—begged Srila Prabhupada’s forgiveness, thus ending two and a half years of protracted agony for both parties.

Prabhupada’s disciples learned many lessons from this singular dispute. On the one hand, we saw Prabhupada as the living example of equanimity under fire, and of tolerance and mercy toward others: “Therefore a sadhu is advised, titikshavah, tolerate! Tolerate all this nonsense! What can be done? Nobody’s coming to help us. Our business is so thankless task. Because we are trying to create one temple, so many enemies, they are giving hindrance. Therefore titikshava, tolerant. Titikshavah karunikah. Karunikah is merciful. At the same time, you have to become merciful. You know what has happened in this place, Hare Krishna Land. So much attack by the police, by the Municipality, ‘Break this temple.’ So we could have said, ‘What is the use of taking so much botheration? We have got hundreds of temples outside India. If Bombay people are not liking, let us go away.’ No. Karunikah. We have come to distribute Krishna consciousness movement. We must tolerate and give this message to the people. Very merciful, in spite of all trouble.”

And on the other hand we saw Prabhupada as the instrument of divine wrath, retribution, but then forgiveness. He personified for us the tales he’d told us from Krsna book, how Krishna kills, then gives salvation, even to His enemies, turning the whole ugly business into a kind of transcendental sport—Prabhupada in his lion mood. “Your real enemies are lust, anger, greed, and illusion,” he said. “This Mr. Nair affair is a temporary diversion, that’s all.” We saw our spiritual master in full-scale battle on the larger stage of good vs. evil. We saw Prabhupada’s expertise and grim determination defeat the most hostile and entrenched real-world interests we could imagine—and best of all, we got to see Krishna personally intervene to save His devotees on the battlefield.

To this day, in Juhu, just near ISKCON’s thriving Hare Krishna Land temple-hotel-theater-restaurant-school complex, there’s a two-block street named A. B. Nair Road.

Hare Krishna Land was a very major rhino for Srila Prabhupada.

On January 23, 1973, we’re on a plane about halfway from Bombay to Calcutta. Srila Prabhupada is by the window, I’m seated next to him, and Tamai Krishna is across the aisle from me. During the journey I noticed Prabhupada playing with his mouth, moving his tongue around. Suddenly he plucks out a large molar, looks at it, hands it to me, and says, “Dispose of this.”

I’m looking at this big tooth, roots and all, and looking at Prabhupada for some sort of explanation, but he continues to stare out the window. Tamal leans over and says, “Give it to me!” and grabs the tooth from my open hand—and stuffs it into his kurta pocket. OK, I’m not a relics person; if I’d wanted Prabhupada souvenirs I’d have a roomful by now. But when I ask Tamai if I could look at the tooth again, he says, “No, it’s mine.” Like a snotty kid, sullen and withdrawn. Hmm.

At the Albert Road temple in Calcutta I settled onto the veranda outside Srila Prabhupada’s room, organizing stuff accumulated from this long tour of India. I was overjoyed to see Malati and Saraswati again—I hadn’t seen them since Hyderabad—and Swati and I horsed around and cuddled while devotees in the temple room adjacent to Prabhupada’s room buzzed with the excitement of his arrival. Jayapataka, Madhavananda, Dinanath, Bhavananda, Kaushaliya—many old friends were there.

Shyamasundar: Hey, Dinanath, what’s happening, brother? You don’t look so hot.

Dinanath: Yeah, well, I’m getting kind of burned out. Been in India for two years now.

Shyamasundar: Why don’t you go to London? You’re the best kirtan man. George would love to chant with you!

Dinanath: George Harrison?

Shyamasundar: Yeah, man, really. Kirtan with you and George, you on the drums—wow, outta sight! I think I can get you a plane ticket. Whaddya say?

Tamai Krishna overhears us, and runs to Prabhupada’s room to complain that I’m trying to poach his best men for the UK zone.

Dinanath: In heated excitement he told Prabhupada about our conversation. Without batting an eye Prabhupada said to Tamal, “It’s not Shyamasundar’s fault for trying to get Dinanath to go; it is your fault for not keeping him.”

Prabhupada is relaxed here, back in his home town with his intimate family, the battles of Bombay behind him. There are no big public programs arranged, so Prabhupada focuses on fortifying the Albert Road temple and its leaders, who are running a pretty loose ship. He will speak in the temple twice a day: mornings from The Nectar of Devotion, evenings from Srimad-Bhagavatam. But there are no more empty tapes, no reel-to-reels, no cassettes.

On January 24, our first morning in Calcutta, I run downtown and comb the shops and bazaars to look for blank cassette tapes. After hours of searching I wind up in a dirty alley where recent rains have clogged the drains and raw sewage puddles on the pavement. There’s a Chinese feel to the place: crowded, rundown stalls scattered in a haphazard fashion, with everyone bargaining at a fever pace. I wade through the filth in my open chappals and yes! This guy has two cassettes. He sends a runner to other stalls, and within ten minutes I’ve scored a dozen unused cassettes! Overjoyed, I return to Albert Road, race up the stairs and, full of cheer barge into Srila Prabhupada’s room. But a blanket of darkness has settled in, a feeling of doom. Prabhupada is sitting quietly in one corner; Bhavananda, Bali Mardan, Tamal Krishna, Achyutananda, Gopal, Panchadravida, Gargamuni are sitting or standing around the room, and as I enter, they glare at me like I’m some sort of pariah. What’s going on here?

Srutakirti: All of a sudden you entered the room doing a dan-davat in front of Prabhupada’s desk. You were so happy to be back in his room after what must have been a tiring time. You sat up with a big smile, saying, “Mission accomplished” and showing the cassettes. There was nothing but silence in the room—you could cut it with a knife. Srila Prabhupada looked at you with sadness on his face and didn’t say a word. You looked around, realizing something heavy was going on and said, “What’s wrong?”

Tamal Krishna said, “Prabhupada, we can no longer accept Shyamasundar as your secretary. We feel it is time for all of us, especially GBC men, to take turns being your secretary.”

I was stunned. Was this a bad dream? I looked at Prabhupada, like, Is this your decision? He started to speak, but TKG spoke over him, loudly arguing his well-prepared coup, stating all his reasons why I should be replaced, my other godbrothers nodding in support. He accused me of manipulating Prabhupada—and the thought flashed in my head: Are you saying that your spiritual master is not 100 percent conscious of everything he’s doing? That he’s an ordinary man, to be manipulated? A hundred other thoughts flew by—What would I do now? Is my life over? I was sobbing with deep concussive breaths before Tamal had even finished.

Prabhupada cleared his throat, and said, softly, “They have decided in this way. What can I do? We are a democracy.”

I replied, “I don’t care what they think. Srila Prabhupada, my whole life is serving you in this way. What have I done that’s so bad?” “They are not giving me a choice. They are demanding I remove you as my secretary.”

“But Prabhupada, what shall I do now?”

“You have your responsibilities in London, you have your jewels business—or you can stay with me, travel with me as long as you like, let the others type my letters.”

\[long pause, sobbing\] “No, it wouldn’t be fair, Prabhupada, for devotees to pay for my plane tickets if I have no service.”

In such a power struggle, I am shattered, but the first to cave. The very will-to-power that makes these guys good leaders is too much for me to overcome, and I see the writing’s on the wall. One by one I look at them. Et tu, Brute? I try to strip their envy from this emotion-charged putsch and see Krishna’s hand in all events, but it’s more than I can handle ... and I slowly bow down, rise, go to my workplace a few feet outside Prabhupada’s door, wrap myself head to toe in my chaadar, lay down, and bawl like a baby.

Srutakirti: You were devastated! Prabhupada’s heart was breaking. I was also devastated. It was my first experience of how political things could be in a spiritual society.

For almost two days I lay there, frozen in my thoughts, crying from time to time, unable to make any decision whatsoever or even to eat. Malati or Saraswati would come and try to cheer me up, bring me a sweet, discuss a plan, but I was unapproachable in my misery. Contrary emotions charged around my brain like freight trains. I wallowed in self-pity, then guilt, then self-loathing, then anger at my godbrothers’ betrayal. I even got mad at Krishna and Prabhupada—why didn’t they defend me? Then I’d remember I’m not this body, I’m an insignificant nobody, that I deserve this trouble—then quickly return to feeling sorry for myself. Worn out after two days of introspection, I finally came to this: “Who do you think you are, Shyamasundar, you whiny little ass? OK, maybe you can get through this”—and I started chanting round after near-silent round of the Hare Krishna mantra, knowing it would help even if Krishna had abandoned me.

On January 27, Prabhupada said in his Calcutta temple lecture, speaking from The Nectar of Devotion: “Even in our society, Krishna conscious society, if there is no mutual cooperation, then it will fall down immediately. So as Rupa Goswami advises, the first thing is enthusiasm, utsahan. Utsahan dhairyat tat-tat-karma-pravartanat... \[aside: Why Shyamasundar is not here?\]... Tat-tat-karma-pravartanat. If you want actually to make progress in our devotional life, the utsahan, enthusiasm, is the first thing. If you are lacking enthusiasm, then you should rest instead of making too much agitation within the mind. Just like, something has dropped in the river. You cannot see the things dropped within the water by agitating the water. Just stand still for some time. As soon as the water is settled up, you’ll see the things as they are. So as soon as our enthusiasm is agitated, it is better to sit down in any temple suitable and chant Hare Krishna. There is no question of being disappointed. After all, we commit so many mistakes. That is human nature. To err is human. That is not fault. But try to rectify with cool head. That is required.... You should cooperate for the common cause. The common cause is Krishna. For Krishna’s service we should submit to the immediate officer or commander. Just like soldiers. Soldiers, there is no question of discrimination. Whatever is ordered by the commander: immediately done.”

Later that night, Srila Prabhupada heard me sobbing in the dark and softly called my name. When I sat before him, head down to hide my tears, he said: “I also cannot sleep. Simply tossing and turning. Some pain in the stomach—I think my sister Pishima has not cooked the coconut in her kachouris”

That did it, broke the spell—and I burst out laughing. Prabhupada’s relationship with his lookalike younger sister was always like a sit-com to me. Within moments my tears had dried under the overwhelming rush of Prabhupada’s compassion and love for me, and the way forward seemed clear again and full of hope and promise.

Prabhupada: You have done the best job as my secretary. No one can do as you have done. But what can I do? They have ... Practically, my hands are, I mean to say, tied up.

Shyamasundar: Yes, Prabhupada, I understand all this, but—

Prabhupada: You will see. It is Krishna’s arrangement for you, some good opportunity is there.

Shyamasundar: Shall I go back to London from here?

Prabhupada: Now you have got still your plane ticket up to Los Angeles. You go there, and take Saraswati with you, and I will provide ticket for her. Malati is here, she has got service, so you have not to worry. You take Saraswati to the gurukula in Dallas. Then you go on to London and get that big house from George. I will come there and stay as long as you like. Is that all right?

Shyamasundar: Hmm. OK. And I can try to sell some of these jewels along the way.

Prabhupada: Yes, this is Krishna’s plan. Now go with enthusiasm. Wherever I am, you may always come and live with me. You will always have my blessing.

As soon as the water is settled up, you'll see the things as they are.

In my mind, this event signaled the end of Prabhupada’s guiding his Society by detailed personal letter. Revolving secretaries meant that the deeply personal letter flow between Prabhupada and his disciples would be disrupted, diminished somehow, but maybe this was for the best; the Krishna consciousness movement was expanding at high speed, and Prabhupada now needed to shift responsibility for managing thousands of devotees to his senior leaders. He wanted to travel and preach, translate books, and not be so involved in writing personal advice in a dozen long letters a day. It was time for a sea change. It was such an unexpected change, and I was badly hurt. But within days I saw it as the right thing to do. I was married, with a child. I was the GBC man responsible for ISKCON in the British Isles. And I had a new ruby business—so many projects going that I couldn’t take care of any one of them properly. So I surrendered to the inevitable.

It was decided that one GBC man would stay with Prabhupada as his secretary for one month at a time, depending on where Prabhupada was in the world. Bali Mardan, GBC for the East Coast of the United States was in Calcutta, so he would serve as secretary until Prabhupada went to Australia, then Madhudvisa Swami would take over, and so on. Though at first they were hesitant to meet my eye, gradually Prabhupada’s men began to warm as they sensed my surrender. I showed Bali Mardan how to use the electric typewriter, how to make two carbon copies of each letter, and I gave him the small file of unanswered mail and, with Prabhupada looking over our shoulders, guided him through the process.

I suppose every devotee thought that in his lectures Srila Prabhupada was speaking to him or her alone, but over the next few days I couldn’t help but think that he was instructing me personally, and I listened closely. “If somebody is advancing, making improvement, his neighbors, his friends—even most intimate friends—they also become envious: ‘Oh, this man is becoming so rich, so popular.’ Envious. So Vaishnava is not envious. If somebody advances more than him, he appreciates: ‘Oh, he’s so nice that he has advanced more than me. I could not serve Krishna in such a nice way.’ That is Vaishnavism. And if one is envious—‘Oh, this man is going so fast. Let us put some impediments on his path’—he’s not Vaishnava; he’s hinasya jantuh, he is animal. Vaishnava cannot be envious.”

My paltry feelings of victimhood and personal failure were gradually crowded out by understanding that, yes, I was probably getting too big for my britches—the Greeks called it hubris—and so I had met a well-deserved nemesis. I should have known it couldn’t last forever, this dream job of being physically by Prabhupada’s side at every moment of every day. And Prabhupada was no doubt right when he told Srutakirti that I still had a few material fantasies I needed to work out. I felt like Prabhupada was speaking about me when, the next day, he said, “Rupa Goswami says phalgu vairagya. There is a river in Gaya city named Phalgu. On the bed of this river you’ll find only sand, but if you push your hand within the sand you’ll find water. Similarly, phalgu vairagya means the so-called sannyasis—they have taken the dress of renounced order, but within the heart they have got all desires to fulfill. Within the heart. If you push your hand within his heart, you’ll find he has got all desires for material enjoyment. That is called phalgu vairagya”

Jeez, except for small jobs as a carpenter I had never really worked to earn a living, unless you call selling illegal drugs an acceptable profession. Now I’d have to go out into the world and earn my keep, earn money by selling gems and rubies. But that sounded kind of exciting, too. I took to heart what Prabhupada said in his Calcutta lecture on January 29: “There is a very practical example. Just like if you find all of a sudden a certain amount of money, say, one hundred rupees note lying on the street.Three things. The one thing is bhoga, if you take for your sense gratification. And if you don’t take, that is tyaga, renunciation. And if you pick it up and deliver to the right person, Krishna, that is bhakti, devotion. So everything belongs to Krishna. Krishna says, bhok-taram yajna-tapasam sarva-loka-maheshvaram: ‘I am the supreme proprietor.’ This is unknown to the nondevotees. The karmis think that the resources of the world are given by nature for our enjoyment. This is the modern theory of economic development. Everyone is thinking that by nature we have got the gold mine, so we shall take it and use it and enjoy. Just like, what is that land where there is too much gold, in South America? Brazil. There is too much gold stocked there. And every nation is hankering how to take it. What is the struggle in this world? Struggle is, the gold mine is there, and everyone is trying to exploit it.”

How deeply these words would affect the rest of my life.

As I rose from my bed of thorns and started packing my few possessions, I thought about failed dreams and new beginnings. Gone was the dream of perfect sanctity by remaining at Prabhupada’s side and chasing rhinos shoulder to shoulder with him forever in the great cities and deep jungles of Krishna’s amazing creation. No more waking to his little brass bell, that first morning glance at Prabhupada’s stubble-lined cheeks, hearing his soft command, “Shyamasundar, take dictation.”

I thought, Prabhupada, you said you’ll always be with me wherever I am. I thought, too, of unlimited horizons, of reaching my fist deep into the earth and, Srila Prabhupada, watching your eyes light up at the gleaming sun-red gems—and buying you a Rolls-Royce, maybe a satellite to broadcast your glories around the world. I’ll get that magnificent house in England, hang chandeliers, bring the queen to you for tea and talks so that you’ll come and live there with me again. And so the die of the next phase of my life was cast.

Wounds healing, Saraswati and I headed east. It was time to chase some rhinos on my own.

## Four

Saraswati and I stand in the long line at Honolulu airport. I’m praying to Krishna that US Customs won’t find the jewels and the kilo of rubies hidden in my suitcase. This is 1973, and and passengers from Vietnam are being carefully screened for drugs. They’ll look especially at a weird-looking guy in white robes with paint on his face and a shaved head. Taking a cue from our KGB-deception entrance at Moscow’s airport in June 1971, I had piled Saraswati’s toys on top of our clothes in the suitcase. Sure enough, when the Customs guy opens the overstuffed bag, balls, dolls, spinning tops, and jigsaw puzzle pieces spray all over the counter and onto the floor. Saraswati squeals, jumps up and down, runs around to pick things up. The official is flustered, the long line behind us is screaming with impatience—and we’re home free, back in the good old USA.

We’d flown from Calcutta to Saigon to hook up with Pan Am flight 002 eastbound to America. Squadrons of F-4s circled Tan Son Nhut airport, and dozens of idled B-52s, with six huge engines hung from drooping wings, lined the runways. After the Rolling Thunder carpet-bombing of North Vietnam that Nixon had ordered in December, the Paris Peace Accords were signed on January 27, 1973—just four days before our flight—and a ceasefire declared, so the Saigon airport was a madhouse of military and civilian activity.

I didn’t really know what was happening, but it looked to me like many rich Vietnamese, with piles of luggage, were standing in long lines to flee the country.

On January 31, we flew from Honolulu to Los Angeles, where Karandhar greeted us with open arms—a bit puzzled to see me, not really knowing what had happened in Calcutta, but showing me a letter he’d just received from Bali Mardan: “From now on, the twelve GBC men will be acting as Srila Prabhupada’s secretary on a rotating basis.... Shyamasundar prabhu will be going to London via Los Angeles. You will probably be seeing him there shortly. He has many big ideas for increasing the preaching work, which he will probably tell you about.”

Karandhar was fired up by my schemes to make a fortune selling gems, to line Sunset Boulevard with billboards broadcasting the glories of Krishna consciousness, and to meet Hollywood stars and enlist them as devotees. I asked Karandhar to show me the fanciest jewelry store in town, and he took me to Kazanjian Jewelers on Rodeo Drive in Beverly Hills. Michael Kazanjian bought my emerald cabochon necklace for cash, a few thousand dollars, and I had my grubstake.

Saraswati was nearly five now, and my folks hadn’t seen her since she was two, so I decided to take her for a short trip up to Salem, Oregon, to see her grandparents. Sudama Goswami, my friend from the early Haight-Ashbury days, was in L.A., and he said, “I’ll go with you! We can stop and visit Ken Kesey. He’s at his farm near Eugene right now.” Sounded like a plan. So I rented a car and we motored a thousand miles north. Passing through Sacramento, 385 miles north of L.A., we stopped and visited Malati’s parents, Al and Eleanor Nagel, then headed toward Oregon.

At my parents’ big house in Salem, Saraswati seemed happy to hang out with my mom, so Sudama and I left her there for the day and drove seventy miles south to the Kesey dairy farm on Route 58, near Pleasant Hill, Oregon.

Brent Selden—who’d become Sudama Goswami—was Ken’s old prankster buddy from the Grateful Dead house at 710 Ashbury, and Ken was happy to see us. I hadn’t seen Kesey since the 1968 Christmas party at Apple Records in London, when I’d first met George. “Yeah, I remember when that English Hell’s Angel got thrown down the stairs!” he laughed as he ushered us into his kitchen. Ken had done some time in jail, fled the “Hashbury” scene, and was now leading a hermetical rural life with his school-age kids and wife, Faye. He seemed reflective, a little confused by it all: What happened to the Psychedelic Utopia I pioneered and martyred myself for? Ken was still a commanding presence, with his wrestler’s build and intense intelligence, but was nearly bald now and a little tired around the eyes. The unique passion of this man, trying to patch the pieces of his genius torn apart by the pinball game of speed and acid, just a few years ago a blazing comet in the skies of change, was now back to green fields and a herd to milk and maintain. He was compiling his recent writings into a book (Kesey’s Garage Sale) and working on a literary magazine he and his friends were putting together, Spit in the Ocean. He showed us the layouts, with their psychedelic art and stream-of-consciousness essays. The theme of the first issue was “Old in the Streets” (Ken was now 38.) We spoke about the Acid Test Graduation Party at Winterland, celebrated on Halloween 1966, how they’d chanted Hare Krishna after all else failed. “The Krishnas and the Jesus freaks, all you guys are the same halfway-through-a-step acid-casualty kids who won’t go ahead and complete what they started... One guy grabbed that stick and another guy grabbed this stick, and it set us to fighting each other in all manners and ways,” was Ken’s remark.

From the barnyard fence Kesey pointed past a few Holsteins grazing in a field to the infamous “Further” bus, now a rusting memory sitting on flat tires in a grove of sapling alders by a marsh, and he said, “Maybe we’ll drag that thing outta there one-a these days.” I thought of asking, But what about your eternal soul, Ken? then changed my mind. He had our sympathy, but we saw everything now from a stiller place, outside the Acid Trip That Knew No Bounds but always brought you back to what Kesey called the “demon box.” Ken Kesey was a legend in our times, self-chosen to lead his generation down this shell-blasted path. He was indeed a great soul, but deeply cynical about organized spiritual beliefs. Sudama and I realized how lucky we were to have escaped maya’s terminal velocity. Another full circle.

Sudama said he’d make his own way south, so the next day I took Saraswati with me to visit Mukunda and Janaki in Kelso, Washington, forty-some miles and across the Columbia River from Portland. They had left London and moved to this small paper-mill town in the summer of 1971, mostly, from what I could gather, because Janaki wanted to have children, pack her husband a lunch for work, and lead a normal life. Having pioneered the San Francisco and London temples together, we hadn’t seen each other now for more than two years. Mukunda had been my hero since our college days; he’d brought me and many others to the Swami— we’d been so close!

It was a sweet reunion, but the air was charged with complex undercurrents. I’d heard that Mukunda was helping Danavir at the Portland temple from time to time, and he sometimes wrote to Prabhupada, but he had a regular job now, selling tombstones, coffins, and graveside services, and he and Janaki were living in back-woods suburbia. They’d tried to have children, but there had been an unhappy string of miscarriages. I could see that Mukunda was not really happy; Janaki seemed a little angry that we—and the Swami-had abandoned her in London, and though she loved Prabhupada very much, the simple sweet days with few devotees and unlimited access to the Swami’s door had ended and she felt adrift in the now worldwide movement, just one of a thousand disciples.

While Janaki and Saraswati romped in the yard in the sulfu-rous air from the Kelso pulp mill, I showed Mukunda my kilo of rough rubies, told him there was a whole mine of these in India, and that I’m going to sell them all over the world. “Come on,

Mukunda, we were great partners in our essential oils business, Flash Transactions. Join me on this, man! You can sell these rubies in New York—all over America. I’ll mine them in India and ship ’em to you. We can’t miss. Whaddya say?”

Mukunda never carried much expression on his face, but I could see his eyes light up—Janaki’s too, when she saw possible big bucks—and we made a plan.

A few days later, winging east, I stopped in Dallas, Texas, to leave my four-and-a-half-year-old daughter with the devotee teachers at the gurukula boarding school, my heart breaking at the impending separation. Saraswati seemed all right with it, though. She’d known only a life of travel, strange places, and an everchanging crowd of Krishna people around her; she was resilient, smart, and curious, and wanted to learn, and she seemed happy to melt into the little gang of kids her age. I held and kissed her, I told her I’d see her soon, and I hoped her sense of time could not yet conceive of months ahead without her parents. Fighting back tears, I caught a cab to the airport, sad, alone. Was this the right thing to do? On the flight to New York I meditated on my mother’s childhood. As a young girl, Pauline had been shifted from school to school in Orange, Port Arthur, and El Paso, Texas, winding up in a convent school in Las Cruces, New Mexico, while her dad, Jean-Paul, went to Alaska to work in the gold mines and tend remote lighthouses. Generational repetition...

Waiting in New York for my flight to London, I spent two days at the Henry Street temple in Brooklyn. Bali Mardan was back from Calcutta. He told me Prabhupada had left India on February 2 and was now in Melbourne, Australia. My reunion with Bali Mardan was awkward; that Bali had sided with the others to oust me as secretary still hurt. But our overriding love for Srila Prabhupada prevailed and we were still good friends, fellow servants in our spiritual master’s grand plan for expanding ISKCON around the world.

Bali Mardan thought a lot like I did: nothing but the biggest and best for Krishna. He took me into Manhattan to show me the skyscraper, in the heart of the world’s greatest city, that he was hoping to buy for Prabhupada. The Columbia University Club at 4 West 43rd Street was for sale. This elegant, seven-story building had been built in 1900—during the gilded age—and I felt self-conscious walking around the vast, quiet lobby in my shaved head and dhoti as old men on overstuffed couches smoked cigars and read newspapers in the subdued light. “Prabhupada told me to offer them $900,000 with a fifteen-year mortgage,” Bali told me, “They’re desperate to sell—they need the cash—but I don’t think it’s enough.” (A year later the Columbia Club was purchased by the Unification Church of Reverend Moon; on November 9,1975, ISKCON New York opened in its own twelve-story building, at 340 West 55th Street, with three hundred rooms, including a giant temple room, exhibition hall, restaurant, and a 250-seat theater.)

Leaving 43rd Street, the spirit of transcendental competition was high as we walked over to the nearby Lincoln automobile showroom so Bali Mardan could show me the chariot he was hoping to buy for Srila Prabhupada. The tan-and-cream Continental Mark IV limousine was nearly twenty feet long and eight feet wide, and we imagined Prabhupada’s tiny figure sunk into the backseat of this giant land-boat as he rolled down Fifth Avenue, confetti and streamers snowing down from the buildings above.

**\* \* \***

After two days in NYC, I was off to London. On the seven-hour flight I thought, OK, I have to help shift the UK affairs back into high gear. As the GBC for Great Britain, I’ve left it long enough. Bali Mardan is getting a skyscraper, Karandhar’s got his big church building in L.A. Now we need a big place in London, a bigger and better one than all the other ISKCON temples—show these guys how it’s done. I was sniffing the wind, alone now, without Prabhupada’s personal protection. Well, not exactly. I knew in my heart that Prabhupada was always with me. Optimistic, and convinced beyond a doubt that Krishna personally intervenes to help His devotees who risk the most, I flew into the great unknown. I didn’t know if Prabhupada missed me or not, but I missed him deeply, hoping against hope that his secretaries would screw up so badly he would call me back to his side, like a general whose front lines have collapsed, like the Lone Ranger riding in to save the day. But I also knew that this was unlikely to happen.

I arrived in London in mid-February 1973 to very good news: Dhananjaya has found the perfect place! Not only that, but George has approved and his agents have submitted an offer for £220,000 ($550,000). On the way from Heathrow to Bury Place, Dhananjaya filled me in. Piggot’s Manor, on seventeen acres, is a large, Tudor-style house surrounded by smaller buildings, gardens, and a lake. This was the most excited I’d ever seen this rock-hard Scotsman. “Quite suitable,” he said.

“When can we move in?”

“Well, that’s just the thing,” said Dhananjaya. “George wants his charitable trust to buy the place so he doesn’t have to pay taxes, and his solicitors are in the process of forming the trust, so it may take a couple of months.”

George’s Bangla Desh benefit concert in August 1971 had been a smashing financial success. The Concert for Bangla Desh triple record album stayed near the top of the charts for much of 1972 and would be awarded Grammy Album of the Year. But most of the money had been siphoned off by lawyers, record companies, and taxes, never reaching its intended famine-stricken benefactors. The litigation and outright greed following the concert deeply colored George’s mood, and he was in the process of forming the Material World Charitable Trust to legally protect his altruistic ambitions.

Dhananjaya phoned the owner and made arrangements to meet us at Piggot’s Manor the next day. It was cold and drizzling as Dhananjaya, Kaushika, and I motored north to Hertfordshire from Bury Place, up through Swiss Cottage and Finchley on the

A-41, exactly twenty miles and forty-five minutes to the village of Letchmore Heath. On the way, Dhananjaya told me about the half a dozen properties he and George had inspected during the past few months, describing how George would steer his Aston Martin one-handed at 100 mph up the motorway, the other hand in his bead bag, chanting intensely. But whenever the agents saw George, the price would suddenly skyrocket. So finally he’d told Dhananjaya, “You find a place on your own. I needn’t be there. I trust you completely.”

Entering Letchmore Heath was like traveling back five hundred years or more. The quaint village of about a hundred homes—some still with thatched roofs, nestled beneath huge oak and beech and chestnut trees, surrounding a village green—harkened back to Saxon times and looked like something out of a Robin Hood movie. The grounds of stately Piggot’s Manor bordered Letchmore Heath to the south, and perhaps at one time the lord of the manor owned all this land, the villagers bound to him with medieval fealty—a blacksmith, a greengrocer, an ironmonger, perhaps, all working for the lord, who in turn, provided their daily bread—or so I imagined it.

The manor itself was three stories high, with about thirteen thousand square feet of floor space. It was built in mock-Tudor style, with overhanging gables and a half-timber, half-stucco exterior. We could have been in Shakespeare’s era. You could look out through leaded bay windows to imagine lords and ladies stepping through the huge oak front doors into carriages and off to London for king and crown. Though built in 1884, Piggot’s Manor stood on the site of earlier manor houses on land that had once been owned by Lord Geoffrey Picot in the thirteenth century. The current owner of Piggot’s Manor was a Scottish woman named Mrs. Ruggles, so Dhananjaya was the perfect go-between—he spoke her language and carried her same brand of frugality and dry humor.

It was dark inside—parsimonious Mrs. Ruggles had stripped the place of light bulbs—but we were inwardly thrilled (and trying not to show it) to see three large rooms on the ground floor (perfect for a temple room, prasada hall, and a reception room), all paneled in oak and with huge carved, stone fireplaces. There was a large tiled kitchen, a billiard room and, what Dhananjaya really liked: a walk-in steel-doored safe, and a pay phone in the hall. On the southwest corner was a glass-enclosed orangerie, where gentlemen would lounge in weak sun on a winter Sunday amid potted palms, sipping tea and reading The Times to catch up with Livingston’s cross-Africa expedition and other colonial affairs. (“Psst, Kaushika, this is the perfect place for an altar!”) There was a wide hardwood stairway leading to halls and bedrooms upstairs. The large, south-facing master bedroom would be perfect for Prabhupada’s room. Across the circular paved driveway from the main entrance was a spacious duplex servants’ cottage with eight rooms—perfect for married couples. There was a coach house over the stables, garages, storage sheds, greenhouses, even a tennis court. “Yeah, Dhananjaya, your forehand needs some work.” (He didn’t get the joke.)

I wasn’t sure, but from my research into Indian history, I thought maybe this house had once belonged to the same aristocratic family that produced George Piggot, governor of Madras for the East India Company in the 1760s. Governor Piggot obtained a hundred-carat diamond from the Golconda mines, and when he returned to England he was made a baron, and the huge rough stone was faceted down to forty-eight carats. On his death, by an act of Parliament, the Piggot’s diamond was sold in a public lottery. The price was set at £24,000 and tickets to win it cost two guineas each.

Taking leave of Mrs. Ruggles, we tightened the scarves at our necks and toured the grounds. The property was dotted with mature Scottish and Roman pines, some big western red cedars (memories of Oregon), overgrown rose and rhododendron gardens, shady dells; leafless weeping willows draped over the lake by a dilapidated boathouse. We followed a neglected path through ivy and brambles to the high, moss-covered stone wall and ornate iron gates that separated the manor from the village green and the four-hundred-year-old Three Horseshoes pub across the street. On the west side of the manor, quaint turnstiles led out to apple and pear orchards and six acres of lush green pasture.

“Whose cows are those?”

“Oh, they belong to Tom over at Battlers Green Farm—he’s got a grandfathered grazing lease on this pasture.”

Hmm—we’ll see about that.

Bury Place was more crowded than ever. Bless their hearts, even in this freezing weather teams of devotees pounded the streets of downtown London day and night with sankirtan. Though crowded and hectic, there was a sense of deep calm about the place. Radha-Londonishwara lived here, and temple affairs revolved around Their comfort and well-being. The pink-cheeked British devotees at Bury Place were advancing in Krishna consciousness, and I was happy to again be part of the mix.

I set up my office and living quarters in Srila Prabhupada’s familiar upstairs room, sleeping on the floor by his bed. After dark I turned on his table lamp and felt him there, in half-shadow, across the desk, giving advice, nodding his head at a point I was making, that secret small laugh of his to a fellow conspirator.

I sorted rubies in the lamplight while everyone else was in bed. On these late nights I dreamed and schemed again with Prabhupada. He’d throw me an idea, I’d roll it around, toss it back with my little spin on it, and hear his pragmatic decision. Sometimes Prabhupada seemed to veer off a topic, go around it, say something in Sanskrit or, “Just bring a little water,” and then he’d drive his point hard. When you saw that happen, you knew he was the ultimate game master, losing a pawn to gain the queen. My eyes reared, I’d sense him across the desk and whisper, “Oh, Prabhupada, you’ve never lost the thread in all the years I’ve followed your millions of words— you are without error, perfect in every way.” And “Now this rubies business,” he’d say, “it may be a good one. Krishna has given you. But men in this trade are, I mean to say, very astute, and they are mean-spirited. They will kill you for a small profit, so you must be very careful.”

I was so proud of Dhananjaya, Tribhuvanath, Balagopal, Rebatinandan, Bhajahari, Ranchor, Lilashakti, and all the others— how they’d stuck it out, kept Bury Place alive, expanding, and almost solvent. And now Dhananjaya had found this marvelous new Hertfordshire headquarters for Krishna in the UK. He would often come to my office to discuss daily affairs, mostly how, in heaven’s name, are we going to get the money to maintain this new manor? Dhananjaya was more pumped up than ever, riding the high of seeing Krishna in action. Jai Hari, too, came often to my room. For years I’d wondered how this young devotee from a Pakistani Muslim family had so much energy. He was always as busy as a bee, didn’t question, just got the job done, surprising us with his daring accomplishments. Jai Hari right away became my personal assistant. I could point him in any direction, say “Do this,” and he’d usually exceed my expectations.

**\* \* \***

Shortly after arriving in London I phoned George at Friar Park. He said, “Come on out, I’m putting the finishing touches on the next album.” He had named the new recording studio in his house FPSHOT—Friar Park Studio Henley-On-Thames—and proudly showed me his huge sound-mixing console and all the latest technical marvels, watched over by Krishna on the wall. George looked good in his neck beads, his face glowing, unencumbered by mustache and beard. We talked about Piggot’s Manor. George was thrilled, and said, “Yeah, I wanted you Krishnas to have a place like Friar Park, something in the countryside. I promised Prabhupada. You can chant as loud as you like and no one bothers you. And I can come over there, and no one’ll bother me”

One by one, musicians and sound engineers begin to arrive at George’s studio. “Sam the Shyam, I want you to meet Zakir Hussain, the tabla player.” (One of our shared favorite songs was “Wooly Bully” by Sam the Sham and the Pharaohs.) This afternoon, they are overdubbing Indian instrumental tracks onto the finished song, “Living in The Material World.” From the raucous horns and honky-tonk piano behind “I got born into the material world / Got caught up in the material world / Getting worn out in the material world,” George pauses, moves to a higher key, and segues into whispering flute, tamboura, and tabla, creating an ethereal backdrop for

From the Spiritual Sky,

Such sweet memories have I

To the Spiritual Sky

How I pray

Yes I pray

that I won’t get lost or go astray

Takes my breath away.

“George, you’re too much. But when is the album coming out?”

“Maybe in a couple of months. I’m going to Los Angeles in a few days to do the business thing, get the promo going. I want to put japa beads and a bag in every album—whaddya think? At least some pictures of Krishna, make it special. I don’t want to mess up manor negotiations by going out there just now—once we’ve signed the deal, when I get back from L.A., then I’ll go out there.”

Later, during a tea break in the kitchen downstairs, I showed George and Pattie my gems, and they were both very excited. A ruby mine in India. Wow! The maharaja’s jewels. Splendid! Fool that I am—and always a sucker for a pretty face—I draped the priceless Nizam ruby cabochon necklace around Pattie’s neck and said, “Here, I brought this for you”—not even thinking how I was going to pay for it. Everything belongs to Krishna, Prabhupada had taught us, himself often taking the most precious object he had, like the advance copy of his latest book or an expensive watch someone had just given him, and simply gifting it to the first person he saw. Years later, Pattie wrote in her memoirs: “Syamasundara went to South India... and came back to England with a sack of ruby chips ... and an uncut ruby necklace. He also gave me a handful of rubies that I thought I would have made into necklaces to give to my nieces when they reached eighteen.”

I returned to Bury Place on top of the world. George’s amazing Krishna-themed music is broadcasting around the globe, we have a new palace for Radha and Krishna in Hertfordshire—Prabhupada, you’ve done it again! And rubies. If I’ve got to work in the material world, what a cool thing to be doing, mining and selling rubies.

Krishna wears the giant Kaustabha ruby on His chest. In Srimad-Bhagavatam Krishna says, “Among jewels I am the ruby.” Prabhupada told us that the ruby symbolizes the Lord’s potency, and a ruby is the center jewel in a navaratna, the traditional, Vedic nine-gem cluster representing the planets that surround the sun. Carat for carat, a good ruby is more precious than a diamond. No wonder I saw this ruby mine as a God-given gift. What a rhino for me to chase, I thought. It fits with my natural inclinations, and I’ve been trained by the master of rhino-chasing. What I chose to ignore, perhaps, were other things Prabhupada had said, like, “Activity for a Vaishnava should be easy, without extreme, undue, or too much endeavor,” and, “Too much business means always struggling, misplacing energy that should be utilized for realizing Krishna.” Anyway...

I took the rubies and other gems to Asprey & Garrard and other fancy jewelers on New Bond Street. They could see I was an amateur, and their offers were very low. They suggested I take my goods to Hatton Garden, London’s wholesale gem district near Bury Place. I learned quickly that, like other commodities, there are two markets for gems, retail and wholesale. I could sell single faceted pieces, one or a few at a time, to retailers, but even then, no matter how perfect an individual gem might be, there was fierce competition to supply the few high-quality pieces that retailers sold. Hmm, this might not be as easy as I expected. Rough rubies would have to be sold in bulk to wholesalers, who would cut and polish the stones and sell them onward. I made the rounds at Hatton Garden and discovered that my ruby crystals had a good crimson color but were opaque, translucent at best, considerably reducing their value. Size was good—my rough crystals went up to forty or even fifty carats in size, with an average size of about five carats. On the minus side, picking through an entire kilo (five thousand carats) of rough rubies, the Hatton Garden dealers would select only two or three stones they considered good enough for cutting, and even those would hardly be worth $20 to $50 per carat finished weight. “You’re going to lose fifty to seventy percent weight in the cutting to get a polished piece,” they told me. “The only market for these goods is to round and polish them for cabochons, or as beads in cheap jewelry.” One dealer summed it up for me: “Most high-quality rubies come from the old, established mines in Burma, Ceylon, and Thailand. There are few ruby mines on this planet, which is why rubies are more precious per carat than diamonds or emeralds. Your color is good, size is good, so you may have something there, if you can find a more transparent grade of rough somewhere in the mine. But what you have here is corundum, the generic name for aluminum oxide crystals. Only when corundum is transparent can it be called ruby.”

Heartbroken that we weren’t going to get rich overnight, I returned to Bury Place to ponder this information and study the stones late into the night. At a hundred rupees per kilo (about $15), I was paying the Hyderabad brothers less than one cent per carat, but if I could only make a few dollars on the half a dozen good stones in a kilo of rough, there just wasn’t enough profit for all the trouble. Most of the crystals were a nice hexagonal shape, but flat, and nature had imprinted several overlapping triangles on the crystal faces. I thought, Well, they look nice in their natural form. Maybe I could polish them as is and create a whole new market for these stones, call them “Krishna Ruby,” launch a PR campaign, make them more valuable through propaganda and advertising. Anyway, I’d better learn more how this business works.

The Hatton Garden boys gave me contacts for gem cutters in Belgium and Germany, so I bought a fancy Indian silk kurta and a peacock-colored vest, and took a train from Victoria Station to Antwerp. I rented a big Mercedes and made the rounds, affecting the attitude of a callous, veteran gem dealer fresh from the jungle: “Hey, we’ve got a new ruby mine here, if you want to get in on the ground floor.” The gem cutters in Antwerp scoffed at my stones (fueling my desire to show them a thing or two), so I raced across Germany at 120 mph on the autobahn to the quaint village of Idar-Oberstein, Europe’s traditional center for cutting semiprecious gemstones.

Herr Wild at Wild & Petsch Lapidary was more positive about the rubies’ chances for widespread appeal as a semiprecious product, and I left him with a dozen stones to cut and polish into various shapes. Though the cost of cutting was probably more than the pieces were worth, at least now I would have some finished samples.

Everyone I met said I had to go to Zurich to meet Dr. Edward Giibelin, who had the only certified gem laboratory in Europe at that time, and who had written the definitive book on precious stones, Edelsteine. He was the world’s foremost authority on rubies, and had visited ruby mines all over the globe. When I met him at his family’s posh showroom at 36 Bahnhofstrasse, Dr Giibelin was cautiously excited By this new find, and said it showed good potential. He said that ruby deposits often improve at depth, where there was more heat and pressure at the time of formation. His explanation of geological forces sent me in a new direction, urging me to find out more about how nature works to create the perfect, rare gemstone—and how this might apply to our current mine workings in India.

I then showed my stones to dealers around Zurich. A few of them bought samples, and most seemed receptive to developing the Krishna Ruby as an appealing new product. Since my student days at Albert Schweitzer College, I had a special fondness for Switzerland, renewed now by the well-mannered, practical, and prosperous Swiss professionals I was meeting. Zurich seemed like the perfect headquarters for my new “Krishna Ruby” company.

Several Swiss gem dealers said they would buy kilos of rough ruby if I had them, so I needed more stock. They also said there was a heavy tax on rough gemstones entering Switzerland, and suggested I rent a locker at the Zurcher Freilager, a tax-free warehouse at Kloten airport. This was a convenient hub where potential customers could examine the goods and pay tax on any purchases—or goods could be shipped from Kloten to anywhere.

After renting a locker at the airport, I jumped on an overnight flight from Zurich to Hyderabad. Sitting in the large front room at the brothers’ house off Bank Street, I told them everything I’d learned. “These are not really gem rubies, but because they are large size and have nice hexagonal crystal shapes and a bright red color, I’m optimistic that with clever processing and marketing I can still turn them into a profitable business.”

The brothers’ entire mine production of rough ruby, totaling some fifteen hundred kilos to date, was stored in a pile of five-gallon metal biscuit containers in the yard behind their home in Sultan Bazar. We bargained for several hours, and finally agreed on a price of fifty rupees per kilo for their entire stock of mine-run rough.

Since Mukunda and I had met at his home in Kelso last January, I’d been trying to get him involved in this ruby business. We had been great partners in Flash Transactions, our essential oils business in Haight-Ashbury. Mukunda is a natural, low-key salesman— you take one look at his deep brown eyes and winning smile and can’t refuse him. Six years earlier, when he’d told me about this swami he’d met in New York, I bought what he was selling hook, line, and sinker. If I could get him selling these rubies, making some money, it might even resolve his situation with Janaki and at the same time maybe lure him back into Krishna conscious activity, help me govern the UK zone’s affairs, take some of the load off.

From the Central Post Office in Hyderabad I called Mukunda in Washington state and said, “Mukunda, look, there’s a ton and a half of rough rubies here in India that I can buy for $7.50 a kitol There are five thousand carats in a kilo, so that’s 7.5 million carats. And the best place to sell this stuff is New York City. Manhattan is the center of the world gem trade. I wanna ship the rough ruby by sea freight to New York. You know New York like the back of your hand. Why don’t you go there and sell them? They’ll go like popsicles on a hot day at Coney Island. There’s no tax on rough gemstones in the US, so you just pick ’em up at the docks, get a cab to 47th Street, sell them, send some of the money to London, and you keep the rest.”

Mukunda said, “OK, do it!”

Next, I called Dhananjaya in London and asked him to transfer almost twelve thousand dollars from the ISKCON building fund in London to the brothers’ bank account in Hyderabad. We were now proud owners of about a million ruby crystals.

The brothers gave me a room, and for five days and nights I sorted through one and a half metric tons of rubies, holding each crystal in the yellow light below the rim of a table lamp to check for color and transparency.

Ruby and sapphire are both aluminum oxide crystals. The red in rubies is caused by a minute presence of chromium; the very same crystal with a trace of titanium in its latticework structure turns the crystal blue and is then called a sapphire. Blue, of course, is the color of Krishna, but red is the color of Radharani. After hours of staring intently into ruby after ruby I began to feel Radha’s presence—see the crimson of Her cheeks, Her passion and anger for Krishna, Her pouting lips, the kumkum sprinkled on Her torso, Her ruby nails, Her eyes red from crying, Her scarlet sari loosened by play with Her beloved.

I selected a hundred kilos of the best rubies, packing them into two metal trunks to take back to Zurich as checked luggage on the plane. The brothers would ship the balance of the ruby rough by sea freight to Mukunda in New York City. To avoid raising suspicion at customs, we poured motor oil over the stones along with handfuls of grime to hide their color, and declared them as “rough corundum, for industrial abrasives.” The game was on.

**\* \* \***

In the past one week I’d flown to Hyderabad, purchased one and a half tons of rough rubies, dispatched most of them by sea to New York, selected a hundred kilos of rough to take with me, and flown back to Switzerland. I made a few sales to dealers in Zurich, stashed my hundred kilos in the Freilager, and then returned to London. It was mid-March and still cold when I got back, and there wasn’t much that was new. Paperwork for Piggot’s Manor was still being shuffled around, and it might be another six weeks before we can move in.

Prabhupada had often said, “You just approach the leaders of society, convince them about Krishna, and millions will follow,” quoting Krishna’s instruction in the Bhagavad-gita: yad yad acharati shresthas tad tad evetaro janah: “Whatever action is performed by a great man, common men follow in his footsteps.” This strategy had worked very well here in London. But Mukunda was gone, Gurudas was gone, and now almost no one around me still had this vision except Jai Hari. He loved the idea of cultivating great men, and people like George and John Lennon were comfortable around him. Jai Hari was bold but not arrogant, upbeat, always giving and never asking. British rock stars and entertainers were the easiest celebrities for us to approach, as most of them knew George and knew he liked the Hare Krishnas.

One day, as Jai Hari and I were browsing a copy of New Musical Express, we saw the lyrics to a hit song called “Stairway to Heaven,” sung by Robert Plant in a band called Led Zeppelin. The article gave the location of Plant’s home, so as a test run, I said to Jai Hari, “Why don’t you go out there and meet Robert Plant?” A few hours later, Jai Hari returned to Bury Place and said, “Yeah, I met with Robert today. He’s keen on Krishna, and wants to help.”

I gave Jai Hari a short list of George’s friends that he should try to meet and cultivate, starting with Peter Sellers, Eric Clapton, Mick Fleetwood, and Stevie Winwood. I had met these guys when I lived with George at Friar Park in 1970. I already knew Eric pretty well and sometimes visited him at Hurtwood Edge, his place in Surrey. Mick Fleetwood, the drummer in Fleetwood Mac, was married to Pattie Boyd’s sister, Jenny. Winwood had a band called Traffic, and he later joined Eric in a band called Blind Faith. Big stars like this are one-on-one people; they spook easily if they’re approached by more than one person at a time. They’re just people like you and me and, for the most part, believe in magic—how else did they get so suddenly rich and famous?—and the best ones are looking for spiritual answers. So I let Jai Hari loose: “Just go to them in some private place and tell them about Krishna. Lose your ego. Let Krishna take over. Don’t be attached to the results. Be happy and they’ll sense it. Above all, give, give, give—and don’t ask for anything.”

While I was in Europe and India, Jai Hari met a few of the famous people on his list, and was becoming especially good friends with Peter Sellers. I didn’t know Peter, but George was always talking about him and I knew he was also good friends with Ravi Shankar. Peter had co-starred with Ringo in a 1969 film called The Magic Christian. Jai Hari told me that Peter had been stationed as a soldier in India during the last days of WWII and that he was fond of everything Indian. He also believed strongly in reincarnation and karma, was a vegetarian, and practiced yoga.

Now that I was back in London, we decided to go see Peter together. We arrived unannounced mid-morning at Peter’s tiny converted carriage house on Lyall Mews in Belgravia, a few blocks from Buckingham Palace. Peter answered the door in pajamas and bathrobe, rather timid and unbalanced at first, but always a gentleman, he rose to the occasion and entertained us. “I was about to do some yoga,” he said, and popped into a headstand in the corner. Upside down, he continued chatting with us, “Now, who is this Krishna fellow again?” It was unintended perhaps, but Peter soon had us howling with laughter.

His cramped living room smelled of fried food and was littered with tossed clothes, multimedia equipment, and Peter’s latest toys. On the walls hung pictures of his “guru friend” Venkateshananda Swami. After his headstand Peter ran around straightening things, lit some incense, and asked questions about Prabhupada in an increasingly American accent. I said, “Peter, do you realize you’re speaking like a Yank?” He looked a bit perplexed, and I could understand he was the perfect mimic, like a chameleon taking on the voices around him. When we talked about India, his voice adopted an Indian lilt and he rolled his eyes and wiggled his neck in the Indian way—and again he had us in stitches.

We discussed cryogenics—he was seriously considering freezing his body after death—and bhakti yoga. And then a sleepy gal tottered downstairs from the loft in her nightgown and high heels, blond of course—“I have this thing for blonds,” Peter said—and we saw the time was not right for high philosophy. We soon bowed out, leaving him with garlands, a Bhagavad-gita, and sweets, promising to meet again soon.

Jai Hari: So I began to visit Peter and encourage him in his spiritual quest. I didnt come on strong. I was very easy going with him, and he appreciated that. He could see I was not affected by his superstar status at all. But I found he became distracted very easily; one of his biggest weaknesses was women. I often went there and found girlfriends and hangers-on in various stages of undress.

Another day, not long after I was back at Bury Place, Dhananjaya came into my room, out of breath, saying that one devotee—this guy works as a teller at the Mitsubishi Bank on Ropemaker Street in the financial district—anyway, this devotee had just told him a deep dark secret and he wasn’t supposed to tell anyone, but here’s the thing: “On Monday, March 26, 1973, the government of Japan will legalize private ownership of gold up to one kilo—the price of gold will skyrocket on that day, for sure! Why don’t we ..

In October 1972, at Prabhupada’s request, Rupanuga had wired thirty thousand dollars from New York to London for a down payment on a new building. That money had been sitting for five months in a Midland Bank savings account, and now that George had volunteered to buy us a place, we didn’t really need it, so we came up with a plan. Even cautious Dhananjaya conceded that we couldn’t miss as he handed me £10,000 in thick packets of Bank of England £20 notes.

I called Bali Mardan in New York and told him, “Hey, we’ve got a sure-fire deal here, brother. Bring the money you’re saving for that Columbia Club building, meet me in Zurich, and we’ll make a killing!”

“Hmm, maybe,” is all he said.

On Thursday, March 22, carrying a thin, hard-shelled briefcase stuffed with cash, I was quite surprised—and relieved—when customs officials at Zurich’s Kloten airport saw this shaven-headed freak in an Indian dhoti, sandals, and peacock-pattern vest and waved me through. I’d never done anything like this before, but I’d seen it done in the movies: I’m on a roll to break the bank at Monte Carlo. Beware, ye gnomes of Zurich. I was hot stuff, Krishna’s bagman.

Before leaving the Zurich airport I looked up banks in the yellow pages of a phone book. Swiss Credit Bank had the biggest ad, so I went directly to their main branch on Paradeplatz, Bahnhofstrasse, in downtown Zurich. I played the exotic millionaire and talked my way to the new accounts manager, Mr. Schildknecht, who led me to a private room and said, “We do not open new accounts for Americans. It is very difficult.” I opened the briefcase and dumped my impressive pile of cash on his desk. He took one look and said, “But considering you live in England, let us see what we can do.”

Within the hour I had two checking accounts, #443702-01 in Swiss Francs, and #443702-02-1 in US dollars. The bank did not pay interest to depositors, and certain fees were to be expected; I guess that’s the price you pay to have a Swiss bank account. “Now, I want to buy gold with this money,” I told him.

Schildknecht said, “Then I shall take you to Herr Rudolf Shriber. He handles our bullion sales.”

Seated in a comfortable room, with his immaculate suit and polished English, Herr Schriber exuded the ease, security, and precision exclusive to Swiss bankers; you feel almost privileged to be in their hallowed presence. He assured me that any gold bullion I purchased would be held in their vaults, and, “Yes, you may sell it or take possession of it at any time you like.” The price at the moment was $81.70 per ounce. “But we sell only in one-kilo bars.” “OK,” I said, “how much gold can I get for about twenty-three or twenty-four thousand dollars?” By end of that March 22 day, Krishna had paid $23,638.10 to own nine kilos, or 289.328 troy ounces of 999.9 fine gold bars.

The next day, Friday, I sat all day in the lobby of Swiss Credit Bank with a dozen other curious citizens, watching new-tech red digital readouts flash past on a long black wall screen—stock and commodity prices. By the end of the day, the price of gold had jumped to $84.50 an ounce. I thought, Wow, we made more than eight hundred bucks today, doing nothing.

Monday, March 26 was D-Day according to our hot tip, so I returned to the bank lobby when the doors opened at 8 a.m.— it was five hours later in Tokyo—and sat glued to a seat, heart in my mouth. By day’s closing gold jumped to $90! The $5.50 one-day rise in gold price was one of the steepest jumps in history up to that time. Dhananjaya’s clandestine source was right! In the following days the price seemed to plateau at just above or below $90.

Meanwhile, news of what I was doing had been leaked to Prabhupada, who had arrived in India from Jakarta earlier in March and was now in Bombay: Shyamasundar has taken the

Society’s money and is in Zurich speculating on gold. When I phoned Jai Hari one evening for my daily briefing, he told me that Prabhupada had cabled London and wanted to come immediately to Switzerland.

I phoned Prabhupada at Kartikeya Mahadevia’s house in Bombay and assured him that, “We’re making a lot of profit on the gold. It’s not a wild gamble, Srila Prabhupada.”

“I will come there, and we shall see And what about Piggot’s Manor? When it will be ready?”

“We’re hoping to move out there soon, maybe in a couple of weeks. Meanwhile, I think you would enjoy a nice vacation in the Swiss Alps, Prabhupada, like we’ve talked about many times. How much time shall I book in a nice hotel for you?”

“I shall stay ten days. Then I may go to London. Or, if you are not ready there, I will go to New York.”

I was so excited. Another major rhino chase with my pirate commander! I phoned Jai Hari in London and said, “You’d better come over here right away and help me arrange things!” I then sent a telegram to Srila Prabhupada in Bombay:

OBEISANCES STOP INFORM ARRIVAL DETAILS C/O HOTEL SCHWEITZERHOF/BAHNHOFPLATZ/ZURICH

Through a travel agency I booked a ritzy, three-bedroom apartment in St. Moritz, 125 miles east of Zurich and six thousand feet high in the Swiss Alps. The brochure looked great: the six-story Skyline House stood across a lake from the fairytale village of St. Moritz, a favorite ski and spa destination for upper-class Europeans for a hundred years. “Weather up there has been clear and sunny for the past two weeks,” said the travel lady, “Cold, crisp, good for the health.” Jai Hari raced over from London on the train-ferry. I said, “Look, Jai Hari, I have to stay in Zurich to keep a close eye on the gold price. So you have to take Prabhupada by train up to St. Moritz and look after him there.”

On March 31 Prabhupada wrote to Karandhar from Bombay: “I am not going to Johannesburg, rather I am leaving tomorrow for Zurich for maybe 10 days while they are getting my place ready in London. As soon as I am informed, I will send you my address in Zurich.”

The next day, Sunday, April 1, through tear-filled eyes, I watched Prabhupada walk through the Ankunft gate at Kloten airport with Srutakirti and Pradyumna. The past two months without him—the longest we’d been apart for nearly two years—had only increased my love for him. I made prostrate obeisances, and laid a costly Italian gardenia garland around his neck.

Prabhupada was happy to see me too, I could tell—there was that special glint of looming adventure in his eyes—but he had a stern look on his face as he said, “So, what is the position?” I assured him that Krishna’s money was safe. “In fact we’ve made a few thousand already, Srila Prabhupada, and we can sell the moment the price starts to drop. It’s Sunday, banks are closed today, so Jai Hari will escort you guys to St. Moritz, and I’ll drive up after the market closes tomorrow. Your train leaves in an hour.”

As I steered the big rented Mercedes through downtown Zurich, I sensed that Prabhupada Was a little disgruntled to be shuttled away from the action but, mollified by my repeated assurances that St. Moritz has the healthiest climate in Europe—“You can get a good rest, Srila Prabhupada (translated: no one will bother you!)”— he put himself in our hands and boarded the train at Hauptbahnhof for a scenic, four-hour trip to the famous Swiss health resort.

That night, Zurich was hit by cold torrential rains, and when I phoned Skyline House the next morning, Srutakirti said it had snowed heavily all night in St. Moritz. In fact, it was still a blizzard out there. Prabhupada had taken his morning walk in the hallway outside the apartment and was complaining about the cold. Oh, jeez, what have I done? The gold price stayed pretty steady all day on Monday, April 2, closing at $89.25. About 4 p.m. I took off for St. Moritz. Polizei stopped me on Highway 3 east of Chur and said that the Lenzerheide Pass to St. Moritz was closed due to heavy snow. I remembered an alternate route to St. Moritz, but I’d have to go through Liechtenstein and Austria to get there— another 170 miles one way. I drove north to Vaduz, then Feldkirch, Austria, where it started to snow. Luckily, the highway east over 5880’ Arlberg Pass was plowed, but the going was slow. The sev-enty-five-mile stretch south, up the Inn River valley from Landeck, Austria, to St. Moritz was the worst: six to twelve inches of snow on an icy, winding, two-lane road. It was late at night by now, and no towns and no other traffic. The last thirty miles were driven through a whiteout.
