I was hypnotized by headlights reflecting off the blinding white curtain, and sliding through curves in that big Mercedes. I thought, “This is it, Krishna. You gotta get me through this, Prabhupada is waiting!” And just as I entered the Engadin basin, the blizzard lifted, a thousand stars popped into the sky, and I could see the twinkling beacon atop the Corviglia-Piz Nair funicular four thousand feet above St. Moritz. Prabhupada is somewhere close by in this frozen white tableau.

By the time I find Skyline House on Via Sela, the normally six-hour trip has taken eleven hours. It’s now three o’clock in the morning and too early to pound on Prabhupada’s door. I curl up in the dark carpeted hallway and fall into exhausted sleep. Tap, tap — something is tapping my leg. My eyes fly open: “Ah, it is our Shyamasundar! You have come for your vacation?” says Prabhupada, laughing and holding his cane, bundled in cap and shawl, wisps of vapor coming from his mouth. “You will join us for morning walk in the hallway?” Laughing so hard his eyes are closed. OK, Prabhupada, your impetuous fool is busted again.

We walk up and down the corridor, pushing the light switch each time we reach the end of the hall—the lights are on a three-minute timer—and Prabhupada jokes about the weather. “Just see, they will pay so much money to come to this hellish place, thinking to enjoy the bitter cold!” He wants to leave—there’s no talking him out of it—so we make a plan: Jai Hari will book Prabhupada and his entourage on the mid-morning train back to Zurich, I will race back through Austria with the car, find hotel rooms, and pick them up this afternoon at the Zurich train station.

Swiss Credit Bank was just a few blocks from the train depot and directly en route to our rooms at Hotel Sonnenberg, so Prabhupada wanted to stop at the bank first and check out the action. We were a strange-looking lot as we entered the lobby and sat down in a row of chairs. Is that a swami? From India? Those four young men, they must be his disciples? People tried not to stare, but you could see even the cosmopolitan Swiss were blown away by the sight of us. We watched the red-on-black digital display roll by high on the wall. “These are stock prices, Srila Prabhupada. The plus or minus after each price indicates the current increase or decrease. Ah, now we’re coming to the commodities. You see? Metals ...”

Prabhupada saw it first: “Gold, $89.70, it has increased today by forty-five cents.”

By now it was the afternoon of April 3, and as we sat and watched the screen for nearly two hours, Prabhupada seemed mesmerized by the red electronic numerals flashing by. This was a whole new daring alliance with Prabhupada—as business partners throwing the dice, giving each other excited looks, like, the price just went up ten cents! Long silences, watching the ticker, thrill building, a shared moment of pleasant intensity best left unsaid. I’d been on a hundred rhino chases with the Swami, but this was a first and more exotic field as colleague, his partner in this wild deal, apprentice to the master businessman. And I was extraordinarily happy.

At 4 p.m. the gold price settled on $90 even, and I said, “Prabhupada, that’s it for the day.”

“Why is that?”

I explained that daily gold prices are fixed in London, and that the exchange there closes at 3 p.m. London time. He chuckled at this example of British cleverness, and said, “Let us go.”

Hotel Sonnenberg was a ritzy place across the Rhine River from Swiss Credit Bank and up a hill, and from Prabhupada’s room you could look west across Lake Zurich to the city below and the high Alps immediately behind. Waiting at the hotel were three of Prabhupada’s GBC men, Hamsaduta, who’d come from Hamburg, Bhagavan, from Paris, and even Bali Mardan had shown up from New York City. They’d heard that Srila Prabhupada was in Zurich gambling on gold with the notorious Shyamasundar, and they’d flown in to see what was going on. Up in his room Prabhupada was glad to see his front-line generals, and they caught him up on ISKCON’s affairs in their various zones. But there was tension in the air, and finally the topic of gambling—one of the four forbidden activities of Vaishnava life—was raised. Their disapproval showed as they sat around and took potshots at me, criticizing my outrageous behavior. Prabhupada listened to everything they said, seeming to agree with them, then asked me to leave the room. My heart fell, my chest seized up. It was Calcutta all over again. I left, but I kept the door ajar a few inches and leaned in close to overhear Prabhupada say, “Yes, this purchasing of gold for speculation is a gambling business. You should not do it. You cannot do it. \[long pause\] But Shyamasundar, he knows the art, he may do it.”

Thanks, Prabhupada! My heart swelled with love for my pirate chief, defending me in this way. Heart pounding, I feigned no emotion as the men slowly filed out, casting befuddled glances as I studied my fingernails. After they’d left for their rooms I crept back into Prabhupada’s room and sat down with one eyebrow raised. So? He said, “Tomorrow we must sell the gold. They are objecting. Apart from that, price seems to be staying the same.”

On the morning of April 4 we returned to the Credit Suisse Bank lobby, watched the price display for an hour or so as gold continued to hover at $90 an ounce. Then Prabhupada rose and said, “I want to open one account here. We shall sell the gold and put into my account.” Herr Schildknecht was very impressed by Prabhupada’s presence and graciously opened a US dollar checking account in the favor of A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami. Herr Schriber sold our gold at $89.75 a troy ounce, and credited Srila Prabhupada’s new account with $25,967.17, a profit of $2,329.07—nearly 10 percent in just twelve days. (By May 15 gold was at $110 an ounce, where it stayed for much of 1973.)

Srutakirti: Srila Prabhupada sat and listened and said as little as possible. He always had to do his best to satisfy so many different personalities. But I could tell he was proud of Shyamasundar, and he was proud of his Swiss bank, account. It was the type of pride, seeing his society becoming a world force from its humble beginnings. It was certainly a wild adventure.

I never knew what happened to that $26K. Prabhupada probably used it to print books or put it toward a new ISKCON center somewhere. His new Swiss Credit Bank checkbook disappeared into his little white case, but often, years later, he would pull it out and show it, saying, “I have got my Switzerland bank account.”

**\* \* \***

The Piggot’s Manor deal had still not gone through, so on April 5 Prabhupada flew from Zurich to New York, bypassing London. I stayed in Zurich for a few more days, and then returned to London.

Over the next few weeks in New York, Prabhupada would say stuff in his lectures like, “I went to Switzerland. Everything is imported. No supply. The supply is only snow. Take as much snow as you like. You see? Similarly everything is under the control of Krishna. If you become devotee, then there is no snow supply—simply food supply. And if you don’t become devotee, be covered with snow. That’s all. Covered with cloud. Everything is under Krishna’s control.”

Or, “In Zurich, we saw that every bank is by television giving the price quotation. They are speculating on the price of gold and they purchase and sell, so they must know the price of the gold every moment—how the market is changing. Their brain is always in that way. So everyone is inquisitive, every one of us, even the animals, birds, beasts, everyone, inquisitive. But when one becomes inquisitive to understand God, then his human life is fulfilled. Then he is actually in human life. Otherwise, to simply be inquisitive what is the price of gold, that means selling and purchasing, make some profit, and when there is profit, then there is sense gratification. This is their aim. Diva charthehaya rajan kutumba-bharanena va. Our life is being spoiled.”

Or, “One division of military phalanx \[in ancient India\] required sixty thousand horses. Akshauhini. So many horses, so many elephants, so many chariots, and so many infantry soldiers—that will compose one division of soldiers. So all the horses were welldecorated with golden ornaments, svarna-bhushitaih. So just imagine all the saddles of the horse, if they are golden ornamented, how many ounces you will require to decorate the horse. And what is the price of gold now? Just see, at least fifty ounces will be required to decorate one horse. And one ounce is $120. So what is the price of fifty ounces? Six thousand dollars. So sixty thousand horses— how much it comes? \[laughter\] Where is that gold? They are very much proud, advancement of material civilization, but instead of gold we find plastic.” \[laughter\]

In Zurich I’d sold some rough rubies, but not enough to pay the expenses at Bury Place, what to speak of outfitting Piggot’s Manor if the deal went through. Prabhupada’s room at Bury Place was still my business office, and Dhananjaya installed a three-foot iron safe he’d found someplace so I could lock up the rubies and other jewels. The Zurich precious metal scheme had gone so well that we decided to put whatever cash we could scrape together into platinum. Dhananjaya purchased platinum bars at Argos Metals Ltd., 64 Coleman Street, London, at $140 an ounce, and we stashed them in the safe. (The price of platinum stayed steady for a couple of months. We sold our stock in early May and broke even—right before the price shot up to $160 an ounce later in the month.)

One day, as I closed the safe, one of the Hyderabad brothers’ precious cut emeralds, given on credit, tumbled off its shelf and was crushed to fragments by the heavy door. What a klutz! Oh well, Krishna makes all things happen. Don’t be attached. Or so I told myself.

Peters Sellers phoned, inviting Jai Hari and me to dinner at his favorite Indian restaurant in Knightsbridge. In a quiet corner booth, over piped-in sitar and the background clatter of dishes, we discussed the difficulties of being a vegetarian in this blood-and-guts civilization. Peter broke into his wheelchair-bound Dr. Strangelove character: “Sir! I have a plan!”—standing up from his chair—“Mein Fuhrer! I can walk!” He mimed the Indian waiters, and we could scarcely eat we were laughing so hard.

I told Peter about the ruby mine in India. “This is a good chance to finance the spread of Krishna consciousness throughout UK, Peter. I’m basing my ruby selling from Zurich, but I’m having trouble getting the business started.”

Peter said, “Aha, then you need to meet Denis—Denis O’Brien— my new business manager. He’s saved my life.” Peter wrote Denis’s phone number on a napkin, and I stuffed it into my bag. We left Peter with more books, made a date for him to see the temple and, still laughing, made our way back to Bury Place.

Jai Hari: We gave Peter Prabhupada s books, and after he began reading them, I think he got the message. As he became more serious about it, I invited him along to the Bury Place temple. It was a very quiet day, no one around, and when Peter came out from the temple room he was positively glowing. Although this made him really receptive and very supportive, he still personally found it very difficult to practically apply Krishna consciousness to his own life on a day-to-day basis. Film commitments and the like always kept him tied up.

Let’s face it, Jai Hari, there are just not going to be many George Harrisons out there who are so perfect, so ready for Krishna.

Peter’s latest film, The Blockhouse, had just been released in the spring of 1973. Director Clive Rees would later recall: “We asked Peter who we should pay \[his acting fee of £20,000\] to, and he said, ‘Pay it to Hare Krishna in Zurich, Switzerland.’ That’s what happened, and I haven’t the faintest idea.... He was amazingly helpful and sympathetic once we got his money off to Hare Krishna.” (Later, when I heard about this, I asked Denis O’Brien, “That money never showed up in Switzerland—what happened to it?” And Denis explained, “Peter was very short of money at that time of his life. So it was impossible to give the Hare Krishnas that money as I had put him on a budget to restore his financial status.”)

Prabhupada had left New York on April 12 and was now in Los Angeles. On April 26, the “Material World Charitable Foundation” was officially registered in the UK, and the paperwork for Piggot’s Manor quickly moved forward. Dhananjaya said we could probably start moving out to Hertfordshire around May 1.

On April 27 I went to meet Denis O’Brien at his “Euroatlantic Ltd.” offices in a red-brick Victorian building on posh Cadogan Square in Knightsbridge, very near Peter’s place. Denis was a tall, gangly American from St Louis. In his mid-thirties, he was already an accomplished international lawyer and financial consultant and had worked in Paris with the Rothschilds as a merchant banker. Peter Sellers as a client must have been a real challenge for him, with his extravagant ways and costly divorces. But as Peter’s money manager, Denis had turned Peter’s desperate finances around, and Sellers hailed him as a godsend, a lifesaver. I could see why when I met him: Denis exuded confidence and success. As I went over the short history of my ruby-mine business, Denis doodled corporate boxes and graphs, saying over and over, “No problem.” He said, “I will make this business a grand success as long as you play by my rules.”

Wow, this was such a relief to hear—I’m in the hands of a topflight business professional now. Thank you, Krishna!

Over the past couple months I’d been phoning, cabling, and writing Mukunda in Kelso to please come over to England. “You are Srila Prabhupada’s first disciple. We came to Prabhupada because of you. He’s counting on you, Mukunda. Come out of retirement, help me out with the rubies, we’ve got a top manager to guide the business now. Help your spiritual master here in London. George has given us this huge country estate, man— everything we dreamed together is coming true. We’re having a ball! Please get over here!” I’d even sent him a plane ticket. Finally, Mukunda relented and wrote to Prabhupada in L.A. announcing his decision to return to England.

Prabhupada replied:

4/27/73

Kelso, Washington

My Dear Mukunda Das,

Please accept my blessings. I am very pleased to receive your letter dated 4.26.73 and the steps which you have taken is worthy of one who is my disciple. So please revive your old Krishna conscious activities and make your life successful. Do not spoil your life for a woman. According to Vedic culture one must give up association of woman which is known as renunciation or sannyasa. For Krishna’s sake Lord Chaitanya gave up the association of his wife, although she was never against Lord Chaitanya Mahaprabhu. According to Chanakya Pandita one who has no mother and the wife is not obedient, such a home is as good as living in the desert.

So you immediately go to London and help in organizing the great temple which we have already purchased. It is understood by telephone conversation that George has already paid the full amount for the new house and Shyamasundar is going to take possession of the house by May 1. When it is actually in our possession, it can be made into a great attraction especially for the tourists. Practically Shyamasundar, yourself, and Gurudasa started the London yatra together with your wives. Now Gurudasa is in charge of Vrindaban construction, so you and Shyamasundar can take charge of the London affairs..I am very pleased by your noble action. Now you do the needful and be happy in Krishna consciousness.

Mukunda flew to London at the end of April 1973, and all the UK devotees were very happy to see him again. Mukunda was my hero, my touchstone. Whenever Mukunda was there, all things became possible, without much effort. Naturally, Mukunda was a little reluctant to take charge of affairs, so he eased into things gradually. There was so much to do! The next few months may have been the busiest, most intense period in the history of the UK yatra. Devotees shuttled back and forth between Bury Place and Piggot’s Manor—now renamed Bhaktivedanta Manor—between their sankirtan squads on Oxford Street and cleaning and building the new property at Letchmore Heath. With half a dozen new temples in the UK and Ireland, we were spread pretty thin. Tribhuvanath came down from Manchester with some men, Prabhavishnu was called from Dublin to take charge at Bury Place, Ksirodaksayee’s team was hustling the Indian community for donations, young Bhaja Hari was appointed president at the Manor, with Mukunda as his close advisor. The empty, cavernous rooms at Bhaktivedanta Manor rang with the sounds of power saws and vacuum cleaners. Mrs. Ruggles had stripped even doorknobs and light fixtures, so there was a lot of work to do. A temporary temple room for Gaura-Nitai was set up in the billiards hall. Devotees slept where they fell. Money was scarce. I had expected to pay for all of this by selling rubies, but here I was at nearly thirty-one years old and still had very little idea how to run a business.

“Hey, Mukunda,” I said in the midst of all this, “what about our old friend Nick Poulos, in San Francisco—the guy who bought our Flash Transactions business? Maybe he’d like to get into the ruby business?” Nick had changed our company’s name to Sunshine

Natural Products and built it into a huge enterprise. I phoned Nick and invited him to Zurich. “You gotta take a look at these rubies, Nick! You’ll make millions!” Nick agreed to come over and have a look. In Kelso Mukunda had also met some guy interested in buying rubies—Lester or Earl or something like that—who was now stationed at a US Army base in Germany. Mukunda phoned him, and he also agreed to meet us in Zurich. The four of us hooked up at the Schweizerhof Hotel on Bahnhofplatz on Saturday, May 5.

When I opened my briefcase stuffed with diamonds and emeralds, all my cut and polished rubies, and maybe a kilo of premium rough, their eyes bulged and negotiations began. We went out to the Freilager and looked at the hundred kilos stored there. There was an undercurrent of mistrust—none of us really knew this soldier-type guy from Germany. He looked very fit, disciplined, maybe a Green Beret. Neither he nor Nick knew much about gems, so they balked at our prices. It got late, so they retired to their rooms to discuss our offer. We’d meet in the morning to finalize things, then I’d take them to the airport in my rented Mercedes, and Mukunda and I would catch a train back to London. What happened next was so hilarious it should have been in a movie.

Scene: \[Overhead shot: downtown Zurich, zoom to Paradeplatz: white Mercedes sedan in heavy traffic, clanging electric trams whizzing by from four directions. Fade to:\]

\[Interior shot, Mercedes: Shyamasundar driving, with shaved head, dhoti, fancy vest; Mukunda in front passenger seat, full head of hair, in American suitcoat, slacks, tie, street shoes; briefcase sitting upright between the front seats; Nick and Earl/Lester sitting in back:\]

Nick \[frustrated\]: Sorry we can’t make a deal on this, Sam. Your prices seem too high, and I don’t even know if I can sell the stuff yet...OK, bottom line, what will you take for the briefcase?

Shyam: Five thousand bucks, Nick—everything in the briefcase.

\[Long silence from the backseat. Mercedes halts for tram; briefcase suddenly yanked back, right rear door flies open, Earl/Lester jumps out with briefcase and runs for the sidewalk, dodging trams; Mukunda opens front door and dashes off after him; Nick and Shyam staring at each other in disbelief, stunned into inaction. Horns honking. A hundred feet down the sidewalk, in front of Swiss Credit Bank, Mukunda leaps and tackles the army-hardened thief. Mukunda returns, briefcase in hand, brushing his clothes, out of breath, and says:\]

Mukunda: I guess we’d better get Nick to the airport.

It was so great to share adventures—and laughs—with Mukunda again! His quiet reliability, competence, and courage in the face of enormous new challenges—breaking with his wife, the Manor, the ruby business—were so welcome back in my life. He had me in stitches constantly with his understated remarks. “The moichandise, it’s all about the moichandise,” he said, mimicking Max Bialystock in Mel Brooks’s movie The Producers, as he sat back in the car and clutched the briefcase full of gems to his chest.

Mukunda and I returned to London and, the following day, George’s much anticipated single, “Give Me Love,” was released in the US and quickly shot up in the charts.

Give me love

Give me love

Give me peace on earth

Give me light

Give me life

Keep me free from birth

Give me hope

Help me cope, with this heavy load

Trying to, touch and reach you with,

Heart and soul

Om mmmmmmmmmmmmm

M m m my lord ...

Please take hold of my hand, that

I might understand you.

Things were somehow coming together at the Manor. Since we’d come to the UK back in 1968, our practice of Krishna consciousness, devotional service, was inspired by a spirit of having fun—we were all volunteers, so we had to enjoy our service. At the same time, there was always a sense of urgency, too— keep a stiff upper lip, let’s dig in and get the job done, Krishna will help. And He always did. This dependence on Krishna’s magic, work as hard as you can but leave the results to Krishna, and then seeing Krishna’s personal intercession just when our ship was on the rocks—this was the tonic that nourished the UK yatra. We expected magic. Only magic could explain how this wild show stayed afloat. Sure, we ran a pretty loose ship, but we’d had a good run so far and the future looked bright. Some of my GBC godbrothers, bless their hearts, disapproved of the way we did things in England. One German devotee even wrote to Prabhupada in Los Angeles: “In short, Shyamasundar has implicated the whole England Yatra, if not the entire society, in so many dubious dealings by smuggling, speculating, and taking the society’s funds to enterprise in ruby business. So now instead of depending on Krishna London is depending on Shyamasundar and his schemes to sell Rubies........ It appears that Shyamasundar like so many other devotees who have neglected the basic principles and practices of Krishna Cons, has gone so far out on a limb that the only way to save him is to saw him off. This will probably bring him to his senses by your Divine Grace.”

My response? “Mein Fuhrer! I can walk!”

But Prabhupada’s more sober response: “Thank you very much for your report of the London situation... So, now what can be done? I expect to be going to London sometime after the first week of June. You may come there at that time. I am very pleased to note that you are opening a center in Zurich.”

**\* \* \***

Meanwhile, it was fog season in Los Angeles, and Srila Prabhupada had come down with a bad cough. In a Srimad-Bhagavatam class on May 16 he spoke about this: “So I have caught cold and cough. So why shall I deride upon it? It must be created either you say by bodily nature or by God. So long it is there, let me suffer patiently. It has come, it will go. That is the instruction in the Bhagavad-gita: agamapayinah anityah. In material world there is no happiness. Everything is distressed condition. But we are so foolish that we consider distressed condition as happiness. This is called maya, distressed condition as happiness.”

Prabhupada sometimes said that his illnesses were due to the “sinful activities” of his disciples, and that by taking us as disciples he had to personally accept our bad karma. Prabhupada operated on a different, higher energy level—obviously transcendental-unbound by physical laws, yet sometimes the burden of his children’s sins was just too much to bear, and he visibly suffered for us.

Prabhupada decided to return to India for his health. From L.A. he wrote to Bhavananda in Calcutta: “My health is broken down.... Unfortunately since I have come to the Western countries beginning from Zurich (Switzerland) then to New York then Los Angeles, but everywhere the climate is not at all suitable for me. I have received one letter from Madhavananda, who was instructed by the Calcutta physician that I should come back to India immediately. So I am coming back, and I will stay at Mayapur.”

From Los Angeles Prabhupada called Mukunda to the pay phone in the Manor’s main hall and said, “On my way to India I wish to stop for one week at London to see the Manor house that George has given.” Mukunda yelled out, and the whole place exploded: “Prabhupada’s coming!”

**\* \* \***

On the morning of May 23, face pressed against the lurching, rain-spattered plastic bubble, I struggle to see the M1 motorway somewhere below. “A little more to your right,” I holler at the pilot. “I think we’re almost there!” I had blown my last £400 on this ten-minute, fifteen-mile helicopter ride from Heathrow to the Manor. “Prabhupada has a bad cold,” they had said. My thought was, if we go by chopper he’ll be spared the hour-long drive from the airport in this nasty weather. Now I’m very anxious, sorry that I’m putting him through this gut-wrenching experience. I look over to see Srutakirti: ghost-white face, jaws clenched, as the three-passenger battered Bell 206 skitters and claws its way through the storm. Leaning forward I glance at Prabhupada buckled to the front seat, expecting the worst. His eyes dart from instruments to trees and hedges, the green blur close below, whispering Krishna at a rapid pace. But his face is lit by uncommon joy, calm, taking it all in—that special look that means, “Yes, another adventure!”

Being in that copter is like being inside a bouncing plexiglass basketball—it’s all I can do not to heave my breakfast. “Yes, there are the power lines!” The Manor suddenly emerges through a wall of water: “That’s it! Land on that big lawn in front!” A sea of devotees swarms onto the lawn, dancing. “I can’t land there—they must move!” yells the pilot. I wave my arms, signaling the crowd below to move while the chopper hovers, flattening the grass with circular downwash, blasting garlands from devotees’ hands. They get the message, move well back, and we land.

The helicopter thunders off, and in the welcome silence Prabhupada stands beaming beneath a held umbrella, greeting his British children lying face-down on the drenched lawn. Up to his chin in rhododendron garlands, he raises his eyes to the elegant Manor facade and says, “Ohhh, so nice, this house Krishna has given.”

We led Prabhupada through the cavernous rooms, pointing out our hurried improvements: “The Deities will one day be in this gallery room, Srila Prabhupada, and your vyasasan will go over there.... This will be the prasad hall.... That paint smell? We’re refinishing the walls.... The temporary temple room is in here. We’ve installed some small Deities.”

Prabhupada mounted a simple vyasasan in the billiards room and led us in kirtan. There wasn’t a dry eye in the place. For most devotees, it had been nearly a year since they’d seen Srila Prabhupada, and many had not yet met him at all. For me it had been just six weeks; it seemed like forever, so much had happened, but at the same time it was like yesterday. Our familiar father, best friend, and protector was now home.

We led Prabhupada up wide oak stairs to his big suite of rooms, and when he entered he said in loud voice, “All glories to George Harrison!” These were the only rooms in the Manor with heat, a rug, fresh soap, a mirror, flowers in vases, pictures of Krishna, and a bed with bedsheets and blankets. We sat wall to wall around Prabhupada, awaiting his remarks. He gazed from his windows across emerald lawns to the lake draped by yellow-green weeping willows, flaming-pink rhododendrons blooming along the treeline, tall pines and cedars behind them. Turning to us—he didn’t have to speak, we knew—he was so proud of his loving disciples, and he said, “Thank you very much.”

Prabhupada walked the Manor grounds each morning. Some days we led him through the village or through sprouting wheat fields dotted with giant oaks, which surrounded the Manor. He lectured from Srimad-Bhagavatam in the mornings, but still suffered from his cold, so we tried to muffle the construction noise and didn’t burden him with engagements or guests. (We did try to get Peter Sellers out to meet Srila Prabhupada, but Peter had recently met Liza Minelli, and their whirlwind affair made tabloid headlines every day. Peter was a bit too busy at that moment.)

It was cold in the Manor—along with everything else, Mrs. Ruggles had stripped the place of heating oil and coal—so we kept paraffin and electric heaters blasting in Prabhupadas room twenty-four hours a day. During his morning lecture on May 24 Srila Prabhupada said, “Just like it is dark now. Any country which does not get the full facility of sunshine, it is to be considered condemned. It is shastric injunction. I think I remarked this long ago, when I first came in the BBC television. They asked me in London that ‘What is your conception of hell?’ And I said, ‘This is hell, London.’ Always dark, moist, drizzly. No sunshine. Cloudy, misty. That’s all. There must be clear sunshine.”

Back in early April, right after Srila Prabhupada had left Zurich for New York I’d seen a window display at Herr Gubelin’s fancy boutique on Bahnhofstrasse for the “Quartz Digital Watch,” a brand-new concept. You pushed a button on the watch and a Pulsar LED chip displayed the time in red electronic digits on a black background, just like the stock price display at Swiss Credit Bank that had so intrigued Prabhupada the week before. A limited edition of a thousand watches had been specially prepared for Gubelin at five hundred dollars apiece. I thought, wow, I gotta have one of these for Prabhupada. It would look so cool on his wrist! (In June 1973, the James Bond film Live and Let Die would feature Roger Moore reaching his bare arm around the blond head of his gorgeous lover and punching the button on his watch—and the world would see time as a red digital readout, changing timepiece history forever.)

So at Bhaktivedanta Manor I gave Prabhupada the watch. The band was loose on his wrist, clunky, but he wore it, constantly pushing the button to see the illuminated display, like a kid with a new toy. “So now it takes two hands to tell the time instead of one,” he said, demonstrating his new watch to everyone who came to his room. Srila Prabhupada, global trendsetter.

The rain eased on May 27 and the temperature rose to 75° F. Prabhupada was already ticketed to leave for India in three days, but he promised to return when the Manor was ready and his health better. Each night Prabhupada met with me and Mukunda to discuss the future. He was so happy that Mukunda—practically his first disciple—was active again in Krishna consciousness and had returned to help govern ISKCON’s affairs in the UK. Prabhupada approved, too, that Mukunda had joined me in the ruby business. I was obviously a profligate spender, a reckless partner in any venture, and Mukunda’s natural caution was a good fit.

On May 25 and 26 I took Mukunda into downtown London to meet and confer with Denis O’Brien. We made a plan with Denis to travel to India with him on June 1—right after Prabhupada’s departure for Calcutta—to meet the Hyderabad brothers and see the ruby mine. May 30 dawned, and it was a sad day for all of us as Prabhupada stepped through the departure gate for his twelvehour flight to Calcutta. Later that same day, George’s new album Living In the Material World, was released in America, selling a million copies in its first two days.

Before leaving, Prabhupada had set the date for London Ratha-yatra: Sunday, July 8, 1973. Now, on top of maintaining Bury Place and renovating the Manor, we needed to make huge preparations for a Ratha-yatra that was little more than five weeks away. Rath carts had to be built, we had to advertise the festival — there were a thousand things to do. Where are we possibly going to get the money for all of this? The day after Prabhupada left, a young devotee named Mahavishnu informed me that his uncle had just died and left him £10,000. “Ah!” I said, “Then you will be in charge of Ratha-yatra this year!” And just like that, we had money again. The devotees much appreciated Krishna’s latest prank, His last-minute rescue as our ship had again begun to founder.

Mahavishnu: My uncle left me ten thousand pounds in a will, which was a lot of money in those days, 1973. So there I was — I was a young brahmacari, never had money before, and I was spending like water, walking around with bills hanging out of my bead bag.

On June 1, Mukunda and I flew from London to Hyderabad with Denis O’Brien, our new Krishna Ruby Mines managing director. I was so relieved that Mukunda was with me on this ruby deal. We joked all the way down. “In Zurich you looked like O. J. Simpson out there, Mukunda, that broken-field run down Bahnhofstrasse— you tackled the Green Beret guy!”

“Yeah, did you see the look on Nick’s face when I got back to the car?”

**\* \* \***

It was pre-monsoon season in South India and hotter than blazes. The brothers hastily organized a trip to the mine, about 140 miles east of Hyderabad in Andhra Pradesh. It would be a very long day’s journey in their old, much-repaired WWII US Army jeep, “provided we have no breakdowns.” Seven people, including two servants and all our gear, packed the tiny, groaning vehicle.

Denis O'Brien: Through my client Peter Sellers I went with Sam and Mukunda on this mad chase to India. On the way to the ruby mine the axle broke and we heated the old part over a fire and pounded out a new axle. We slept on a concrete slab that night and awoke to monkeys trying to steal our wallets. Then there was some kind of riot in this little town, and our hosts had to pack us up and run. At the mine, only the women gypsies were doing the work of digging and carrying out the rough material. It was one of the hottest days in my life with very high humidity.

We parked the jeep by a couple of bamboo huts, and Govindas pointed at ruby crystals visibly scattered over the bare ground of a former chili farm. On hands and knees I crawled around the field, picking up the stones and stuffing them in my pockets, while the others unpacked our luggage. I lost track of time—this is a real treasure hunt! Am I dreaming?—Krishna’s magic pulsing in my head and near-delirious in direct noonday sun. I was on the verge of unconsciousness when suddenly a cold, wet towel was wrapped around my bare head and I looked up to see Mukunda smiling, gently lifting me by the arm. “Come into the shade, Shyamasundar. The rubies will still be there. Don’t fry your brains!”

We lay down to rest on sleeping mats laid side by side in the hut to let the worst of the day’s heat subside, and dozed off. I awoke suddenly, looked to my left, and saw a—a what!—a small snake with triangular head—Russell’s viper!—emerging slowly from beneath Mukunda’s mat, turning and about to crawl over his ankles. I touched Mukunda’s face and, when his eyes opened, whispered: “At the count of three, jump as far from here as you can. One, two ... three!” Mukunda leaped and landed on another sleeping form, and the hut erupted as I yelled “Snake!” and a servant killed the deadly creature with a shovel. Mukunda and I, watching each other’s backs, brothers to the death.

The labor boss’s name was Ismail. With his kofi cap and beard, he was the only Muslim on a crew of Hindus. Ismael was a no-nonsense guy and lived rigidly under Shariah law. He barked orders and hit lazy workers with his stick, and I liked him immediately. We swapped verses from the Koran and the Bhagavad-gita, and I trusted this man of Allah. Ismail was also the local snake doctor. While we stood on piles of dirt and watched the miners below, a farmer came running to Ismail with a goat convulsing in his arms, apparently just bitten by a cobra. Ismail ran into the jungle and came back with a branch from some plant in his hand. He sat down by the goat and went into a trance, reciting Urdu mantras in a keening wail—passing the switch back and forth over the wound—and suddenly, the goat stood, shook its head, and danced off into the bushes. You can’t make this stuff up.

The mine ... I was fascinated to see how about thirty workers, mostly women, had dug a trench maybe twenty feet deep, five feet wide, and a hundred feet long, across the fields. Govindas explained the geology to us. Within a white bed of soft, aluminum-rich kaolin clay, a giant trunk of green tremolite, an iron-rich silicate carrying chrome, had burst and sent waving green branches spreading over several acres, each thin branch bearing small green pods enclosing ruby crystals, like cherries on a tree. From down in the pit, the walls looked like a subterranean orchard—Krishna’s perfect arrangement. The laborers worked with short hoes and pry bars to mine the soft ore into baskets, and a line of women, in vivid saris and covered in bangles and silver ornaments—Rajasthani migrants, but to Denis, “gypsies”—carried the baskets of ore out of the pit on their heads and dumped it onto a pile. You could easily see red stones in the white matrix—it was like plucking maraschinos from melting vanilla ice cream.

I related to Govindas what Herr Gubelin had told me in Zurich—that maybe this near-surface material cooled too fast during its formation and that transparency would likely improve at deeper levels, where the crystals cooled slower and under more pressure. Govindas agreed this was probably the case. So our plan evolved. Let’s try to create a market for this poor-quality surface stuff, raise money for a bulldozer and water pump and, later, go deeper and see what’s below.

We inspected the last two months’ production, about 120 kilos, and yes, because they had gone deeper, this ruby looked better than anything mined before, so we loaded it into the jeep. On the return trip to Hyderabad the jeep repeatedly overheated. Govindas’s solution was to pour a handful of rice into the radiator. It will cook, he said, and seal water leaks in the gaskets and hose. No petrol was available in the small towns en route, and we ran out of gas twenty miles from Hyderabad. Govindas’s solution was to drain our lanterns—this vehicle will burn kerosene. Thanking Krishna, we limped back in our jeep to safety. For both Denis and Mukunda this was their first trip to India, and transitioning from English gentility to broiling, snake-infested jungles was a big step. They’d both done pretty well, I thought, rolling with the punches.

Over the next few days I sorted the new rough material into three metal trunks, and on June 13 we hauled them to Bombay by train. Denis flew on to London from Bombay airport, and Mukunda and I went out to Juhu, arriving to a state of chaos. Tamal Krishna and Giriraj filled us in. At Mrs. Nair’s instigation, on June 1 the police had descended on the temple and demolished it, all except a small area where the Deities stood. It was a classic tale of demonic destruction, heroic bravery under fire, enlistment of allies, and pious men coming to the rescue. At the last moment our life members and sympathetic city officials had intervened and stopped this ungodly act. Things were still a mess, but Juhu was rebuilding from the ashes. Mrs. Nair had given it her best shot and failed.

It appeared as if Srila Prabhupada didn’t want to be much involved. His simple written advice was to let the life members in Bombay “carry the fight,” adding, “Had it been the case of some Mohammedan, there would have been great riot. But Hindus are such cowards, therefore no action has been done.”

Poor Prabhupada, pestered by incessant problems from such a rapid expansion of Krishna consciousness. My heart ached to see him. Meanwhile, I caught up with old friends and had a great time showing Mukunda around Bombay.

Whenever we ran into British devotees in Juhu I’d sing the praises of Bhaktivedanta Manor, encouraging them to return home to help us there. On June 18, Tamal Krishna showed me a letter he’d just received from Prabhupada, who was still in Mayapur: “I have received your telegram regarding Shyamasundar asking devotees to leave India to be guests in the new London temple. So let Shyamasundar come to Mayapur and we can discuss this and correct any difficulty.”

Whoopee! We stashed the ruby-filled trunks at Juhu, and Mukunda and I flew to Calcutta on June 19, invited by our spiritual master. When we got to Mayapur we found Prabhupada at ease, lounging in his freshly painted rooms in the Lotus guest house, which was still under construction, a huge smile on his face, two bottom-front gold teeth flashing as Mukunda and I entered and bowed down.

Prabhupada looks so good! You can’t take your eyes off him— he’s shirtless and glistening from his morning massage, fresh clay tilak daubed on his arms, chest, and belly, leaning back, his head resting on a pillow and his feet thrust out, ankles crossed, toes pink and scrubbed. Gone was the tension, the overquick response to stimuli. His once-darting eyes are half-closed now, still, content, the rested guru. A buzz of pleasure crawls over my neck and shoulders.

Prabhupada: So, Mukunda-ji, how you are liking India? \[motioning\] Just bring little prasada.

Mukunda: It is everything I have dreamed, Srila Prabhupada, but very hot.

Prabhupada: Yes, this is season, but this building they have designed very nicely, always cool breeze from the corridor. Only thing is, they are working, there is always some noise, tak! tak\\ ... And Shyamasundar, what about our George Harrison?

Shyamasundar: He was still in Los Angeles when we left London, Prabhupada. He hasn’t seen the Manor yet, but his new record album is number one in the world just now, selling millions of copies. It’s called Living In the Material World. I sent you the lyrics to some of his songs.

Prabhupada: He will get out of this place, by Lord Krishna’s grace.

Shyamasundar: \[laughing\] Yes, that’s the one!

Prabhupada: Now I have got one telegram, so you and Tamal, still some fighting is going on?

Shyamasundar: Yes, Prabhupada, I am still poaching and he is still whining—nothing new there! \[laughter\]

Prabhupada: Actually I wanted that some competition must be there, but it is transcendental competition, as incentive, to increase my service for Krishna better than my godbrothers. And there is some complaint from Germany?

Shyamasundar: Yes, Srila Prabhupada. This ruby business is getting big—it’s early stages, but there is good hope. I have sent more than one ton of rubies to New York City, and Mukunda will go there and sell them next month. I should be able to repay you the ten thousand dollars sometime in July.

Prabhupada: Let us see.

Shyamasundar: Oh, and here’s a set of four ruby buttons I had made for you, for your kurtas, from our best rubies.

That night Prabhupada spoke from Srimad-Bhagavatam: “Here is the statement. Kamam parjanyah vavarsa parjanyah sarva-kama-dugha: everything you’ll get from the land, even luxury articles. What can be more luxurious article than the jewels? The jewels are also produced. The medicine is produced, the minerals are produced, gold is produced, diamond is produced from the earth. Sarva-kama-dugha. You get everything. Make your civilization very perfect, very luxurious, simply by satisfying Krishna. This is Krishna consciousness movement.”

Over the next couple of days Mukunda and I reported to Prabhupada on the progress at Bhaktivedanta Manor, the Ratha-yatra preparations, and the ruby mine, yakking like excited schoolkids. Prabhupada told us that his health was better but still some coughing, eyes itching, some ringing in his ears. A few locals wandered in, and Prabhupada amazed them with the story of his thousand-dollar helicopter ride, his eyes big as saucers. He flashed his new watch at these startled godbrothers here in the boonies of Bengal, and their eyes lit when he pushed the button at his wrist.

Shyamasundar: Srila Prabhupada, you must come to London for Ratha-yatra! Summer weather is so nice in England, and the Manor will be the perfect place to relax and translate. No one will bother you—and many, many important people want to meet you!”

Prabhupada: Ach-cha?

Shyamasundar: Yes, people like George and his friends, other big stars like Peter Sellers, lords and ladies, philosophers, great statesmen, Nobel prize winners, the Archbishop of Canterbury, Prince Charles, maybe even the queen—they all want to meet you!

Prabhupada: Hmm, let us see.

We laid it on thick. We could almost see Prabhupada’s health return as he contemplated a relaxed summer in his mansion in the English countryside, surrounded by his loving spiritual children, meeting daily and advising the movers and shakers, the highest minds of Britain. And the idea took root. Malati was also in Mayapur, and she wanted to see Bhaktivedanta Manor, so Malati, Mukunda, and I flew from Calcutta to Bombay on June 22 and booked our flights to London.

On the evening of June 22 Prabhupada wrote to Kirtanananda in the US: “Shyamasundar came here and he also wants me to come to London for talking with some important men. I am thinking of going there by the first week of July but wondering what shall I do if some new symptom of my bad health threatens me in so many ways?”

From Bombay I phoned Jai Hari and said, “Send Prabhupada a glowing invitation for Ratha-yatra, and have all the London devotees sign it. And send him copies of our publicity brochures. Airmail everything to Calcutta. I think he’ll come.” Knowing a bit how Prabhupada thought—that a ticket in his hand meant a fixed itinerary—I sent a telegram to Prabhupada in Mayapur:

FLYING LONDON WEDNESDAY WILL TELEX YOUR TICKETS BOAC CALCUTTA OFFICE SHYAMASUNDAR

On Wednesday, June 27, Malati, Mukunda, and I stood in separate booths at the Santa Cruz airport, each toting a nearly hundredpound metal trunk full of ruby rough for customs inspection before boarding our flight to London. I was worried I’d have to pay an exorbitant tax on the rough gemstones, so again we had mixed the crystals in oil and dirt to disguise their beauty and worth. There were two hefty padlocks on each trunk, and I had all the keys. The inspector in my booth said, “Please open,” and I fiddled with six identical keys on a ring, trying to find the right key for each lock. Just then, Mukunda, in the next booth over, shouts, “Hey, Shyamsundar! I need the keys!” and I toss the keyring over the partition, apologizing to the officer before me, “Please wait one moment, sir.” And just then Malati also shouts, “Hey, Mukunda, I need those keys!” And he tosses them over into her booth. After about ten minutes of this, keys flying back and forth over the partitions, no one successfully finding the right keys to open the trunks, the frustrated customs guys wave us through and say, “Never mind.”

When we arrived at Heathrow customs, the three trunks were hefted onto a long table and I opened the locks and lifted the lids. In the UK there was a big tax on imported rough gemstones. The British official said, “Alright, then, what’ve we got ’ere?”

“These are sacred stones from India, sir—we use them as part of our ancient Hindu ceremonies.”

“Hmm. They smell kind o’ funny t’ me. Eh, and what’s this, then?” He picked up a peacock-feather fan that Malati had brought. “Feathers, eh? Can’t ave this. This has to go in quarantine—fourteen days!”

And, again the rubies slipped through, and they didn’t even notice the fabulous yellow diamond ring on Malati’s left finger.

That day, Prabhupada wrote to Tamai Krishna in Bombay: “There is a suggestion by Shyamasundar that I may go to London for meeting very important men there in the new house given us by George. Regarding your complaint that Shyamasundar was canvassing some of our Indian workers to go to London, that I have already stopped.... So far my health is concerned I think I am 80-90% fit to move, so there is no cause of anxiety.”

The next day, June 28, Srila Prabhupada received a large airmail packet from London. Inside was a 16” x 20” heavy card paper invitation in the form of a triptych, artfully created by UK devotee Sakshigopal das. The two side panels were montages of paintings cut from the Krsna book and opened from a central panel designed like a medieval illuminated manuscript, decorated with filigreed capital letters and, in gothic black-letter calligraphy, humbly requesting Prabhupada’s presence at Bhaktivedanta Manor for Ratha-yatra. It was signed by seventy UK devotees. That did it.

Prabhupada immediately replied to Jai Hari: “I am in due receipt of your wonderful literature, ‘Ratha-yatra ’73, London Summer Festival.’ The collage of the pictures has been wonderfully set up and I very much appreciate it. I can understand from your attempt that you are doing something wonderful this year. So I think I must accept your invitation.”

Srutakirti: In Mayapur and Calcutta Prabhupada was very ill, and Shyamasundar had invited him to London. I was massaging him one day, and he said to me, “So, what do you think? Should I go to London for Ratha-yatra or should I go to Australia and rest?” I said, “Well, if you go to London, there will be thousands of people there at the Ratha-yatra festival that you can preach to.” He just said, “Yes, that’s right.” Later that afternoon, he called everyone in and told them: “We are going to London for Ratha-yatra.”

As soon as I was back in London I bought plane tickets for Prabhupada and his servants. They will arrive in the UK on July 7, the eve of Ratha-yatra. George had himself just returned to England from Los Angeles, so I phoned him at Friar Park and said, “Mukunda is here, and Prabhupada is coming!” George was delighted, and said, “Come on out. I want to show you guys something.” We went out to Friar Park on June 29.1 hadn’t seen George since early March, and Mukunda hadn’t seen him in about two years.

George tended to keep his rock-n-roll friends and spiritual friends in separate circles. When he was with the rockers it was all about music; when he was with devotees it was all about Krishna, with his full attention. When he saw Mukunda, George threw his arms in the air and shouted “Mukunda!” Their reunion was ecstatic. We thanked him profusely for Bhaktivedanta Manor, and George said, “Yeah, well, you Krishnas are country gentlemen now, and for me it’s just a short hop round from Henley on the motorway.”

By the third week in June 1973, George’s single, “Give Me Love,” and his album, Living In the Material World, were #1 on both the American and British charts. George rushed to show us the new album art and liner notes. “I wanted to include chanting beads and a bag, but they shot that down in L. A. I wanted to show that we’re living in a material world, but it’s spiritual at the same time, see.” George explained the front cover, which showed a Kirlian photo of his right hand holding a medallion of many-armed Kalki mounted on His horse and laying waste to the unbelievers of this degraded age. The back cover depicted George’s left hand holding two quarters and a silver dollar. “See? And the album is dedicated, All glories to Sri Krsna,”’ The first page of a four-page gatefold insert was a painting of Krishna and Arjuna on the chariot at the Battle of Kurukshetra, Krishna holding the reins of three charging horses, Arjuna reaching back for an arrow. This picture was reproduced from the cover of Prabhupada’s Bhagavad-gita As It Is. The next two pages of the sleeve insert were the lyrics to George’s songs, and the back page was a big OM sign.

Mukunda said, “Look at these songs: ‘The Light That Has Lighted The World,’ ‘Don’t Let Me Wait Too Long,’ and look at this one—‘The Lord Loves The One, That Loves The Lord!”’

George replied, “Yeah, that one I wrote right after Prabhupada came out to Friar Park that day last summer.”

I said, “Wow, George, you really did it. Now the whole world knows about Krishna!”

“And look at the labels on the record,” George said, pointing beside the headshots of Krishna and Arjuna in the center of both sides of the record. Etched around the run-out groove on the A-side was “Hare Krsna” and on the B-side “Hare Rama.” Mukunda and I sat with eyes closed as George played the record:

I’m living in the material world

Living in the material world

I hope to get out of this place

by the LORD SRI KRISHNA’S GRACE

My salvation from the material world.

“My original lyrics were ‘Got to get out of this mess, with Krishna Consciousness/ but ‘place’ and ‘grace’ sound better,” George said.

We sat stunned, realizing the enormity of George’s devotional achievement, and seeing clearly now Prabhupada’s impact on the modern world—how this one exceptional man from India had changed the world forever, enlisting us as chosen but undeserving servants, instruments for his mighty endeavor.

We had lunch, and George asked, “How’s the ruby business going?” We filled him in about our recent trip to the mine, how the rubies were getting better as we mined deeper, and how “Peter introduced us to his business manager, Denis O’Brien, and this guy really seems to know what he’s doing. With Denis looking out for us, I think this business will succeed. We’ll see.”

George went on about how his own business affairs were now in shambles since the Beatles had disbanded and fired Allen Klein. I said, “Why don’t you meet Denis. Maybe he can help you? I can set up a meeting.” George agreed that that might be a good idea. We discussed the upcoming Ratha-yatra, and George said he would come into London for the parade and, of course, “I can hardly wait to see Prabhupada again. I’ll come out to the Manor right after he gets here.”

Meanwhile, Mahavishnu had lavishly spent his inheritance on the festival. A huge Ratha-yatra car was nearly complete, and devotees were pouring in from all parts of the UK and Europe to prepare for the gala occasion. Ratha-yatra, a new ashram in the country— and Prabhupada will be here too! We published a full-color souve-. nir program and launched a citywide advertising blitz, with two hundred London double-decker buses carrying big posters that announced “London’s Summer Festival.”

Ranchor: We had stickers made, orange circular stickers, and we stuck them on every available surface—the lampposts, shop windows ... Teams went down into the underground, the subway stations, and stuck them on all the doors of the trains, and the whole of London was just covered in the things.

On July 6 Mukunda and I sat chatting with Denis O’Brien in his office on Cadogan Square. George rushed in wearing jeans and sneakers and said, “Sorry I’m late!” I introduced him to Denis, and the two seemed to hit it off right away. “We have to take off, you guys. Lots to get ready for Prabhupada’s arrival,” I said, and we left George and Denis to their discussions. (Denis remained George’s business manager for the next twenty years. Later, George described his meeting with Denis: “A Hare Krishna friend of mine discovered this ruby mine in India and was wondering how he could use it to support the temple. He’d met Peter Sellers, who put him in touch with Denis, and the Krishna guy put us together.”)

We got a message that Prabhupada had developed a touch of dysentery in Calcutta, and we were worried that he’d be too weak to participate in the Ratha-yatra. We fixed up a nice vyasasan on the rath car so he could ride in comfort, and I rented a little red-orange Ford Capri to follow closely during the parade in case Prabhupada needed to bail.

July 7, 1973: On pins and needles, we wait at the arrivals gate at Heathrow, more than a hundred ecstatically chanting and dancing devotees, to greet the glowing form of our spiritual master, who has so kindly come to spend with us this most glorious summer of our lives.

## Five

Nobody could believe it when Srila Prabhupada raced into the arrivals hall at Heathrow, vital and glowing with energy. Wasn’t he supposed to be recovering from some kind of illness? Flight BA 952 from Calcutta had arrived at 11 a.m., and Prabhupada hit the ground running. We drove him to Letchmore Heath in the red-orange Ford Capri, and he talked nonstop all the way. When he stepped into the Manor he announced, “All glories to George Harrison!” Then he made a quick tour of the construction work, entered the temporary temple in the billiards room, greeted the Deities, and sat on his vyasasan.

We are given the facility of assembling in this nice house by this good boy, George Harrison. We must give him all glories.. .. I have come here within twelve hours from Calcutta to London. You know the speed of mind. You can go on the mind millions of miles within a second. That, science has not yet discovered—how to go in the speed of mind. The yogis know. The perfect yogis, they can travel on the speed of mind. That is also material science. So even on the speed of mind, or on the speed of the velocity of the air, if you try to go to find out where is God: panthas tu koti-shata-vatsara—even you go many millions of years with the speed of mind you will not find God. So Krishna is not so easy to approach by the nondevotees. But to His devotees He’s very easy. If He reveals Himself by His causeless mercy, being pleased upon you, then you can see Krishna within a second, immediately.

It was July 7,1973. Tomorrow would be the 5th Annual Ratha-yatra in downtown London. Seeing Prabhupada in such a lively mood, we knew it was going to be good. Like his arrivals in Sydney and Mexico City in 1972, Prabhupada just didn’t want to shut down or rest. All day and late into the night he talked in his room with devotees, often asking that snacks be brought for everyone. We showed him George’s new Living In the Material World record album, the jacket and inserts, and he was especially pleased with the full-color image of Krishna and Arjuna on the chariot. Perhaps because of this, or the fact that the average age of his audience was maybe twenty years old, Prabhupada decided to lecture each morning from the very beginning of the Bhagavad-gita, “Observing the Armies on the Battlefield of Kurukshetra.” This was Theology 101 for these youngsters, so eager to learn. Next morning in the temple room Prabhupada set the scene, laying out several themes he would return to again and again during his next nine weeks with us, such as “inner television”:

Sanjay was a very faithful secretary. And he is explaining the Bhagavad-gita by television within the heart. That art is not yet developed. You have got television through machine, but there is another television—you can see within your heart everything, what is going on outside. So that television was known to Sanjaya—by the grace of Vyasadeva, he learned this televisioning, and he was sitting with his master within the room and he was actually seeing how the fighting is going on.

Especially in this scientific age, when television, radio messages are broadcast thousands and thousands of miles away, and you can hear—now why Krishna cannot hear your prayer, sincere prayer? Just like thousands of miles away you can transfer the television picture or your radio sound, similarly, if you can prepare yourself, then you can see always Govinda. It is not difficult. This is stated in Brahma-samhita: premanjana-cchurita-bhakti-vilochan-ena. Simply you have to prepare your eyes, your mind, in that way. Here is a television box within your heart. This is the perfection of yoga. You do not have to purchase one television set. It is there, inside, and God is also there. You can see, you can hear, you can talk, provided you have got the machine. You repair it, that’s all. The repairing process is Krishna consciousness. Otherwise, everything is supplied, the complete machine you have got within you. And just like for repairing, an expert mechanic is required, similarly, you require the help of someone expert. Then you’ll see that your machine is working. It is not difficult to understand.

Right after the July 8 morning lecture, Prabhupada went up to his room. He had his own staff now: Srutakirti and Pradyumna were with him, and Satsvarupa Goswami, GBC for the midwestern US, had flown over to be Prabhupada’s secretary for the month. As soon as Prabhupada left the temple room, some devotees dismantled his vyasasan and rushed it downtown to mount on the rath car, preparing for the big day. I went to the little office I’d set up off the ground-floor hallway and began directing operations through short meetings with the devotees running the Ratha-yatra. Prabhupada’s bags had not arrived with him on his flight from Calcutta, so I also made several phone calls to the airlines to track them down.

After the Ratha-yatra preparations were in motion and Pradyumna had left for the airport to find Prabhupada’s luggage,

I had time to reflect on the spiritual mystery Srila Prabhupada had referred to in his class as “televisioning.” I had experienced brief moments of “televisioning” myself, and knew without a doubt that Prabhupada was wired directly into Krishna’s subtle television network. In a purport in the Second Canto of his Srimad-Bhagavatam translation, Prabhupada had introduced this idea:

The vision of the devotees is not ordinary; it is purified by the process of devotional service. In other words, as the universal elements are both within and without, similarly the Lord’s name, form, quality, pastimes, entourage, etc., as they are described in the revealed scriptures or as performed in the Vaikuntha-lokas, far, far beyond the material cosmic manifestation, are factually being televised in the heart of the devotee. The man with a poor fund of knowledge cannot understand, although by material science one can see things far away by means of television. Factually, the spiritually developed person is able to have the television of the kingdom of God always reflected within his heart. That is the mystery of knowledge of the Personality of Godhead.

Before meeting the Swami in early 1967, I’d often start my day with a hit of LSD, mescaline, or psilocybin, powerful hallucinogens, to ride out the next twelve hours in a pageant of impossible beauty and pleasure. I felt I was visiting the mysteries of a cosmic realm, which were as real—or more real—than the tiny black-and-white world into which I had been born. These inner journeys introduced me to a higher reality, the deep buzz that permeates everything, so much so that when Bhaktivedanta Swami walked into my life, I was convinced that he was the one living person I’d met who could see existence through this inner lens, without drugs, and he would instruct us, his willing adepts, how to envision in this way.

After seven years with him—and especially during the past two years being with him nearly every day—I began to see Srila

Prabhupada as the action hero in a blockbuster transcendental film. His life, everything around him, was a movie. His activities were extraordinary, almost mythical, and they flowed from scene to scene in perfect transition. He suspended our disbelief, made this play seem so real, as if all of us were cast for our particular roles by a master talent director. Now, nearly a half a century later, as I sit at my computer, I clearly see the streets of London in the summer of 1973:

London was a world capital of modern hedonistic life. The British populace was locked into traditional, approved, and widely advertised trends in sense gratification, like replaceable civilian robots in the endless stream of conformity. And into their midst comes this small pack of childlike, shaven-headed men and brightly clothed women, singing aloud the same song over and over, some indecipherable, strange words with no apparent meaning, pulling some giant wooden contraption with strange, painted—what are they, idols up there? It’s so foreign, this parade, so shocking, irresponsible, vaguely threatening. Escapism? Revolution? Who are these crazy people?

Prabhupada told us, “Just do it.” He said, “I want you to glorify the Supreme Personality of Godhead, enjoy this glorious Ratha-yatra day with Krishna and His family, and celebrate Them being here with us.” And we didn’t question much beyond that.

SCENE ONE:

EXT. CENTRAL LONDON, EARLY AFTERNOON,

ESTABLISHING

Camera panning: ‘‘Speakers’ Corner3 sign. Marble Arch in background. Giant rath car, a sea of devotees chanting around it; sudden silence as red-orange Capri creeps slowly into the mob. Stops.

SRILA PRABHUPADA disembarks. Garlands around his neck. Stern look on his face.

1st DEVOTEE

(voice over—whispering) Prabhupada looks worried about something.

Srila Prabhupada gazes at Jagannath deities for ten long seconds, then falls flat on the pavement in obeisances. Slowly rises, turns to camera, his eyes brimming with tears.

2nd DEVOTEE

(voice over—whispering)

No, not worried—he’s in some kind of trance, like some kind of very deep love!

1st DEVOTEE

Yeah, like seeing his beloved after long separation.

SP with big smile, raises his arms; kirtan erupts; SP slowly turns in a circle, then moves forward; rath car jerking slowly forward behind him as SP dances down Park Lane.

END SCENE ONE.

He even started jumping from time to time. I couldn’t believe it! Last year, Prabhupada had ridden the rath car the entire length of the parade. This year, no one was sure—he’d been ill in India. So I was driving the Capri real slow just behind the rath car, in case Prabhupada got tired and wanted to leave for Bury Place to rest. Four men carried a heavy set of wooden steps ahead of me, in case our master wanted to mount the rath car. Defying all expectations, though, Prabhupada kept walking and dancing and sometimes twirling and leaping up and down.

Ramadevi: When everyone realized that Prabhupada was going to walk in front of the cart with everybody else, it was like a wave went through the devotees as they heard that this was happening, and everyone started to dance. It was quite amazing that there was this seemingly very small old man in the middle of these tall, young, strong Western men, and he was completely directing them. Just with a flick of his finger they would lift off the ground and dance and spin ... From time to time, he would himself start dancing, jumping off the ground with his arms raised. And when that happened, the devotees just went wild, and it was as if we were flying. There was no ground beneath our feet, there was no time, no space, no nothing. There was just pure joy and happiness—and Prabhupada.

For more than an hour, our iron-man spiritual master dances his way down Piccadilly, giving all who see him a glimpse of some higher energy in action. We approach Piccadilly Circus, the Times Square of London:

SCENE TWO:

EXT. CENTRAL LONDON—MID-AFTERNOON

CLOSE UP on street sign: “Piccadilly Circus W1, City of Westminster.”

ZOOM to EROS FOUNTAIN STATUE: shining aluminum youthful winged CUPID on left tiptoes, right leg in the air, left arm clutching bow, right arm raised, having just released his arrow of love.

ZOOM OUT to high-angle crane shot behind EROS FOUNTAIN, looking west... shallow focus.

TITLE: July 8, 1973.

A hundred backpacking, itinerant hippies are lounging on steps and terraces below Eros Fountain—talking, smoking, reading, napping in hot sun, happy for some respite.

In the background we hear traffic noise, horns honking.

Gradually we begin to hear drums, cymbals, singing voices approaching ... Indistinct, approaching.

Now discernable as HARE KRISHNA KIRTAN. The soundtrack continues, getting gradually louder, throughout the scene:

Hippies turn to face oncoming sound of singing, gather belongings, run across the square to merge with the crowd. Kirtan soundtrack now very loud. Deep-focus shot over Eros to Piccadilly:

ZOOM IN TO:

Five-foot painting of Krishna dancing and playing flute, being held aloft, swaying back and forth—hold ten seconds during...

BHAKTIVEDANTA SWAMI

(voice over)

His beauty is surpassing many millions of Cupids. You have heard the name of Cupid. He is a very enchanting person, loving person, but here it is described, kandarpa-koti-kamaniya-vishesha-shobham. If you assemble millions of Cupids in one place, still it cannot be compared with the beauty of Krishna. Krishna is so beautiful.

ZOOM OUT TO:

The heat-shimmering six-story dome of the rath car filling the sky between tall buildings, bright gold and crimson cloth billowing with jerky movements—being pulled by two hundred people on two thick ropes—enters the open square.

Thousands of celebrants in front, surrounding, and behind, mostly young Londoners, a sprinkling of Indians, carrying instruments, banners, signs, or clapping their hands, like a river of ecstatic humanity singing the Hare Krishna mantra. Dozens of helmeted bobbies trying to control vehicle and pedestrian traffic.

ZOOM INTO:

A quiet vortex in front of the rath car: a dozen devotees have linked arms to create space for Bhaktivedanta Swami as he walks slowly forward with faraway eyes, heedless of the noise around him...

HE TURNS TO FACE THE JAGANNATH DEITIES, RAISES HIS ARMSAND HOPS UP AND DOWN, TWIRLING, WEARING SIX HEAVY GARLANDS.

THE CROWD GOES WILD, KIRTAN EVEN LOUDER. THEN HE DROPS HIS HANDS, EXCHANGES LOVING GLANCES WITH DEVOTEES AROUND HIM, AND TURNSAND WALKDANCES FORWARD.

SLOW ZOOM OUT:

TRACKING SHOT

The giant rath car maneuvers around Piccadilly Circus. Signs carried by devotees read: “HARE KRISHNA HARE KRISHNA KRISHNA KRISHNA HARE HARE’’ and “CHANT AND BE HAPPY”

They contrast with WALLS OF NEON ADVERTISING: GORDON’S GIN “STANDS SUPREME”—SPEARMINT GUM, “CARRY THE BIG FRESH FLAVOUR”-JAMES BOND, “LIVE AND LET DIE” AT THE ODEON.

END SCENE TWO.

We make the sharp right turn onto Haymarket and start down the slight hill in front of Her Majesty’s Theatre.

Bhakti Chaitanya Swami-. Then the Ratha-yatra car began to go out of control—the braking system was not working properly, so it started to accelerate down the hill! The devotees were blissfully unaware of what was going on, but the police were in anxiety. One policeman approached me in a mood of great concern, asking, “Who’s in charge here? Who’s in control?” And I turned around and said, “Krishna is in control,” and went back to the kirtan.

I’m still in the little Capri, following along, wondering at Prabhupada’s incredible display of energy, and still wondering if he’ll want to get in the car and leave. Suddenly, the rath car starts to pick up speed down the hill—I park and run out to help devotees slow it down; I go around to the front of the cart to ask Prabhupada if he is getting tired, and he just motions me away, turns, and keeps on dancing. After several tries we negotiate a left turn at the bottom of Haymarket, and slowly creep into Trafalgar Square. The 170-foot Nelson’s Column dominates the Square. This granite, Corinthian column celebrates Admiral Horatio Nelson’s 1805 victory over the combined French and Spanish fleets off Cape Trafalgar on the southwest coast of Spain, ushering in Britain’s rule over the seas for the next 135 years. But today, our bright yellow awnings stretch for two acres across the Square, tethered by ropes to bollards and trees—some ropes even anchored to the necks of the huge bronze lions at the base of the column. Nelson’s Column is faced on four sides by bronze relief panels, each eighteen feet square, made from captured French guns. The west-facing panel depicts the Battle of St. Vincent, with Nelson accepting the swords of defeated Spanish admirals. We have covered it completely with a dark-blue banner with two-foot yellow letters, saying simply,

HARE

KRISHNA

Two hours after leaving Speakers’ Corner, a dozen devotees now swarm over the massive base around the column, setting up speakers, half a dozen microphones and a sound system, and more than twenty rented mature palm trees in big pots. Thousands of people gather and chant loudly in the Square as Lord Jagannath, Lady Subhadra, and Lord Balarama are escorted from the rath car by teams of burly men and set down in three separate pagodas atop the highest stage of the plinth, just above Prabhupada’s vyasasan.

SCENE THREE:

EXT TRAFALGAR SQUARE-LATE AFTERNOON

ZOOM IN TO:

Closeup of Jagannath, Subhadra, Balaram.

Camera moves down to Srila Prabhupada, wearing six garlands as he mounts steps. He sits down, surveys the crowd.

Camera pans and stays on THOUSANDS OF SPECTATORS seated and standing before him, while:

Sound-track begins: SP leading kirtan, the huge crowd responding. After thirty seconds, kirtan ends.

ZOOM IN TO:

Srila Prabhupada begins to speak.

VOICEOVER a video montage of Men O’ War in naval battles; British kings and viceroys; Jagannath as king of Dwarka sitting with His citizens.

BHAKTIVEDANTA SWAMI

So formerly, when there was fight between two kings, it was on the principle of who is giving best protection to the citizens, not for personal profit. Who is able to give good protection, security for life and property, he should become king. That is birthright. Government should give security of life and property. That is government. Not only for the human being, but even for the ant. Government should be equal to everyone, just like God is equal to everyone. The king or the government must be representative of God. Therefore, according to Vedic civilization, king is called naradeva. That means “God in human form.” Why? Because he acts as the representative of God. Just like in our India, when somebody was going as viceroy, representative of the king in England, he was being offered all the respect of a king. Nobody said, “Oh, he’s coming from England. Why we should offer respect to the viceroy?” No. They never grudged. That is the right system of government. The representative of a king, if he can be offered such respect, then how much respect should be offered to a person who is representative of Krishna? \[pause\] Yes?

As Prabhupada speaks, I begin to meditate on what’s really going on here. Trafalgar Square is the very symbol of British strength. The British lion dominated India for more than two hundred years, and here we are, shaded by tarps tied around the lions’ necks, listening to truth delivered to us by this amazing swami from India who was born under the British Raj. So much history between the two countries, good and bad. When the Swami was young, everything good seemed to come from England. Now here he is, delivering the real goods back to the citizens of this once proud, now beleaguered nation that has foundered on the rocks of time. Great Britain has lost its empire, much of its wealth, and the British people are now hungry for the real wealth of India, the very essence of spiritual knowledge, which they have overlooked in their scramble for tea, cotton, jute, the jewels and shiny baubles of India.

CLOSE UP:

Srila Prabhupada sings one verse of the Hare Krishna mantra, playing kartals.

CUT TO:

CKANE-SHOT over Trafalgar Square: thousands of people joining in response... Final ka-thump of a drum.

Silence.

CLOSE ON SP:

Srila Prabhupada Thank you very much.

Handheld camera following SP as he walks through the huge crowd.

Hundreds of Britishers with their hands folded in front, stunned with awe and reverence, some yelling “Bravo!” or “Thank you!”

Many bow down, trying to touch his feet, as he slowly steps to the parked Capri, turns around, waves one more time.

Srila Prabhupada slides into the back seat. Slow fade as door closes.

Car drives off.

END.

Srutakirti: This was one of those very special events. This was conquering London. Prabhupada conquered the British that day. He admired the British very much, but was sometimes critical of their politics and history in India. So now he came back and took over Britain. That’s what he did. Prabhupada really, really enjoyed his special triumph in Trafalgar Square that day.

By all accounts it was the best Ratha-yatra ever. The next day, Monday July 9, The Guardian newspaper carried a front-page photo of the rath car parked next to Nelson’s Column, with the caption: “A rival for Nelson.” Prabhupada had conquered England. He was now established in his gentleman’s estate in Hertfordshire, a peer of the realm, poised to meet on equal terms with Britain’s finest citizens.

That afternoon David Wynne came to visit Srila Prabhupada. David was George’s friend, and he’d helped us choose the marble for Bury Place temple. He was also the Sculptor Laureate of England. Britain had switched to a metric money system in 1971, and David was designing the new fifty pence coin. Queen Elizabeth II would sometimes sit for him so he could sketch her face from various angles. When David told me about this two months earlier, I’d given him a copy of Bhagavad-Gita As It Is and a handful of my very best rubies to give to the queen during one of their posing sessions. Now we sat with David in Srila Prabhupada’s airy room in Hertfordshire.

Prabhupada: Days were there in British Empire that Nelson was a hero. Now Krishna is going to be hero in this country, \[laughter\] It is a good admission, “A Rival for Nelson.” Actually, Krishna should be rival to Nelson.

Shyamasundar: Prabhupada, Mr. Wynne has personally given Queen Elizabeth a copy of your Bhagavad-gita. What did she say, David?

David Wynne: Yes, she read Gita for about half an hour and then said, “How marvelous it would be to completely trust Krishna.” She said, “How marvelous to be able to give all responsibility to Krishna. What a relief it would be.”

Prabhupada: Yes. Krishna is ready. But the thing is, from our side Krishna expects something. \[David Wynne laughs\] Krishna is not a dead stone. The relationship is reciprocal. If you fully surrender to Krishna, then Krishna takes full responsibility. If you keep some reservation, Krishna also keeps reservation.

David Wynne: Yeah.

Prabhupada: But that one has to learn, how to surrender to Krishna. The sentiment is very nice. It is just fitting from the royal ... Because she’s after all favored, she has taken birth in the royal family, so she is not ordinary woman. And her intelligence must be extraordinary. So she has remarked like that, how nice to give all responsibility to Krishna.

Shyamasundar: It’s funny, too, Prabhupada. When David went to see the queen one day, he gave her fifteen of our small rubies. They’re not worth much compared to her crown jewels. She said, “Well, what shall I do with them?” And David said, “Consider them, Your Majesty, like flowers offered to you by one of your subjects.” And she became very pleased to see it like that.

Prabhupada: Yes. What is the value of those jewels to her? But if some citizen offers in good faith and love, she accepts. That’s all. Just like Krishna is pleased when you offer a flower or a little leaf.

This July 9 conversation with David Wynne was just the first of nearly fifty conversations recorded with Srila Prabhupada during his 1973 summer in London. The previous month in Mayapur I’d promised Prabhupada that if he came to the Manor we’d bring important and intelligent people to converse with him in his room. These London conversations together form the most intense body of recorded dialogue during Prabhupada’s years with us, and so I’ve selected portions of these extraordinary talks to include below. They will demonstrate Prabhupada’s expertise on any subject, his tact, and his ability to match philosophy and wits with anyone.
