These conversations illustrate, perhaps better than any single period in Prabhupada’s long career, that any person, whether humbly born or highly placed, agreeable or disputa-tious—whoever talked with Prabhupada left the room comforted, elated, enlightened. These discussions reveal Prabhupada’s humor, his grasp of the absurd, his finesse and total awareness. And always, in every conversation, Srila Prabhupada never forgets Krishna even for a moment.

As guests entered his upstairs room they’d see a large room, painted all white, with white sheets spread over the nearly wall-to-wall carpet. On every wall were photos and pictures of Radha and Krishna, Lord Chaitanya, and Prabhupada’s spiritual masters, some with garlands draped over the frames. In the southeast corner of the room was a wicker rocking chair with white foam pads on seat and backrest. On the south wall, directly across from the door, three large, Tudor-style bay windows with leaded, diamond-shaped panes lighted the room. Below the central window was Prabhupada’s seat and workspace—an upholstered white pad on the floor, with royalblue bolsters on either side for him to lean on. Before him was a low glass-topped table, five feet long and three feet wide, with his work neatly arranged on top. You saw his Dictaphone, manuscripts from his translating work, a pile of letters waiting to be answered, his table light, pens, glasses case, snuffbox—everything organized for efficient access.

David Wynne is here today, and as he sits on the floor across the low table from Prabhupada, their conversation turns to sculpture, inspiration, and the creative act.

Prabhupada: You cannot do anything. Sarvasya chaham hridi sannivisto mattah smritir jnanam apohanam cha. Krishna is guiding everyone, even an ant. Krishna is giving information, “Mr. Ant, there is a grain of sugar, you can go there.” Otherwise, how he gets the information? For him it is like one hundred miles away, a grain of sugar, and he goes there. Who has informed him?

David Wynne: You feel this very much when one is trying to make sculpture, because it is impossible for a man; one can’t do it. One has to wait and be passive.

Prabhupada: If Krishna withdraws the intelligence, you cannot work. So Krishna is guiding at the present moment according to your whims. “You wanted to do this? All right.” But when you will agree to act according to the whims of Krishna, that is perfection.

David Wynne: How does one agree to this?

Prabhupada: There is process. You have to learn it. In the conditioned state we act according to “my” whims. In the liberated state we act according to Krishna’s whims. That is the difference. Mayadhyakshena prakrtih suyate sa-characharam. Everything is created by God. So when you create something, is it your property or God’s property?

David Wynne: Yes. It feels like when one creates something, one is surprised and didn’t know what it was going to be like, and therefore cannot claim it.

Prabhupada: No. God supplies the ingredients. Just like you are working on stone. So who has supplied the stone? Then you are working on the stone with your intelligence, but who has supplied you the intelligence? You are working with your hand, but who has supplied you the hand? In this way, if we consider that everything belongs to God, therefore the result must be given to God.

David Wynne: Sometimes, when one’s own wishes intrude, one can spoil what’s being made by trying to put one’s own ideas at the wrong time. But at other times, even with somebody who’s as bad at it as me, something perfect does come through, doesn’t it?

Shyamasundar: What is inspiration, Srila Prabhupada?

Prabhupada: That inspiration means you created an idea, that “I want to do like that,” and because you are persistent to act, so God helps you, “All right, you act.” But you are responsible. Unless it is desired by God, we should not act anything. That is bhakti. You should not have any desire for your satisfaction. Then it is all God’s responsibility.

Shyamasundar: What if there’s some desire to make something beautiful to give to Krishna?

Prabhupada: Well, Krishna will not accept from any ordinary man, unless you are devotee. If you think that “I shall do something for Krishna without having any relationship with Krishna,” how Krishna will accept it? Krishna accepts only from the devotee. He is not in want.

David Wynne: Yes, exactly! \[laughs\]

David stayed and talked with Prabhupada for three hours. After he left, I went out to Heathrow with Srutakirti to finally retrieve Prabhupada’s misplaced luggage.

More than sixty devotees live around us at Bhaktivedanta Manor, including fifteen or so transient devotees from continental Europe. Others live in homes and flats in Radlett or other neighboring communities. Another twenty or thirty devotees live at Bury Place and shuttle back and forth to the Manor each morning to attend Prabhupada’s lectures and take morning walks with him. Mukunda has a small room in the main house. His presence inspires calm and order in the chaos of Manor activity, and he’s slipped easily into his role as advisor to Bhaja Hari, the young temple president. Malati and I have moved into a room in the servants’ cottage across from the manor house. Were now living together in the same room for the first time since we’d left London to join Prabhupada in India in September 1970. I sleep there, shower, change clothes, but most of the time I’m on the phone in my office doing ruby business and trying to convince leading persons to come out to meet Srila Prabhupada. I have to raise money, direct renovations at the Manor, and try to keep the whole show afloat, but whenever an important guest comes, I rush to Prabhupada’s room.

Prabhupada set aside the time after 4 p.m. each day to speak with outside visitors in his room. Mukunda, Mahadeva, Jai Hari, and others ran around London to find and schedule important persons to come out and meet Bhaktivedanta Swami. Over the next several days, guests and devotees streamed in and out as the Swami chatted with a wide variety of visitors, sometimes for half an hour but often late into the night. Midway through conversations he’d call for lights to be turned on as summer twilight settled over Hertfordshire. Discussions were mostly casual, but sometimes formal if the guest carried a certain prestige. Prabhupada spoke with authority, on occasion calling for Pradyumna to read his guests a relevant verse and purport from the Bhagavad-gita. During the first week of Prabhupada’s stay we recorded ten separate conversations, notable for their wide variety of guests and subject matter. Here are some examples:

With Mr. Wadell, headmaster at a boys’ school near the Manor:

Mr. Wadell: But are there not things about which in the mortal life one can have no clear idea?

Prabhupada: But if you get clear idea, why you do not take it? We are always in wrong idea because we are born ignorant. Therefore we send our boys, children, to school, to correct the wrong ideas.

Mr. Wadell: But some of the teachers may not be correct.

Prabhupada: No, then he is not teacher. If a teacher is wrong, he is cheater. That is our proposition.

With three college students, who ask basic questions about yoga:

Student: I don’t follow at all.

Prabhupada: Then you’ll never be able to follow. Just like, why do you accept the queen as the supreme?

Students: We don’t.

Prabhupada: Oh, then, then you are not very sane.

With an Indian swami from a London Hindu organization:

Prabhupada: So it is a great pleasure for us. Your Holiness visits us voluntarily. Although I could not invite you, but still, you are so kind, you came. So I am doing my bit, following in the footsteps of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu. That’s all.

With three “educationists,” who’d traveled a long distance to hear from the Swami:

Prabhupada: So our process is to force the student to hear. Then he’ll be awakened, by hearing. Therefore Vedic literature is called shruti. Shruti means it has to be received by hearing. You may be uneducated. It doesn’t matter. If you simply hear from the right source, you get right knowledge. There is no need of education. Simply by hearing.

With several citizens of Indian origin—in Hindi and English they discuss their children, their dreams, and cannibalism:

Prabhupada: So you have got your own house there in Dehra Dun?

Guest: No, I tried to buy a house. I made deposit and everything. Then I came to England to raise the money and went back to India. By the time I went back, there were four litigations going on over the house.

Prabhupada: Oh! \[laughter\]

With a Protestant minister:

Father Tanner: I think I’m mostly teaching them to love one another.

Prabhupada: You cannot be successful to teach people to love one another. That will never be successful. That has never been successful. But if you can teach people how to love God ...

With two Buddhist monks—Prabhupada hears them patiently, agrees with many of their precepts, avoids controversy, but still makes his point:

Monk: We do not believe in a God. We believe that incomparable bliss and shanti, peace everlasting, is the most perfect.

Prabhupada: At least, you follow the leadership of Buddha. First of all you have to follow a perfect leader. Then whatever he says, that is perfect. You have given a definition of peace. Krishna is also giving definition of peace: “I am the proprietor of all the planets, I am the friend of everyone, I am the enjoyer of all fruitive activities, and when one knows Me like that, he gets shanti.” This is the peace formula given by Krishna.

With a teacher named David Lawrence, who learns:

Prabhupada: You have to become little free from biased ideas of Christian philosophy. You have to come to the common sense platform.... So teachers were not allowing Prahlad Maharaj to speak philosophy to his fellow students. But he was taking opportunity in their tiffin hour.

Shyamasundar: What?

Prabhupada: Tiffin hour. Recess. When the teacher is gone and students are free to eat their lunch.

Shyamasundar: Tiffin hour! \[much laughter\]

The next sixty days would be the highest point, the perfection of my years by Srila Prabhupada’s side. I watched with awe and supreme enjoyment as he shared his love for Krishna and for philosophy with a broad spectrum of people from British society. Prabhupada was the relaxed country gentleman, watching the seeds he’d sown blossom and spring into abundant fruit. Of course, wherever Prabhupada was, was perfect, but this haven of English soil in Hertfordshire seemed to be extra special to my spiritual master, and he was enjoying these soft sunny days, the challenge of sharp minds, with a very special relish.

Each morning, a pack of us walks with Prabhupada around the Manor grounds and out into surrounding fields. Early mist is rising behind giant oaks, and we see silhouettes of tall power lines emerge over by Aldenham Reservoir. When the path is narrow we walk single-file behind our Pied Piper in his orange wool cap and overcoat—then spread again to flock around him when the road widens, tripping on one another’s heels to hear everything he says. Most of us are in dhotis and saris, hands in bead bags, but there is usually a hippy or two alongside, with long hair and bell-bottoms. The fields of wheat are maturing now—it’s almost August—browning fast from the lack of rain. There is talk of drought in the newspapers. Cabbage patches are ripe blue-green; other fields are fallow, brown and dusty. As we return to the Manor just after dawn, we can still see lights in a few windows as Prabhupada makes his way to the billiard room temple to greet Gaura-Nitai and speak to us, this morning of elephants and envy:

“In the material world politics are always there, and enviousness, jealousy. Envy means you have something, and I want it. Jealousy means that I am rightful owner of something, I won’t allow you to take it. As soon as there is accumulation of more things, there is competition. And that is the cause of war. The two big wars in your Europe were started by German people because they were very much envious of the English people. Now the British Empire is finished. So if you want to acquire more, then you become jealous and others become envious. If you are satisfied with your minimum or maximum needs, nobody will be jealous. Just like an elephant is eating forty kilos of foodstuff at a time. We cannot eat even onefourth kilo, but we are not envious of the elephant because we know he needs to eat so much. Neither the elephant is envious to us. Spiritual world means there is no jealousy, there is no enviousness. So the Krishna consciousness movement is a movement to train people how to become not envious.”

**\* \* \***

On the morning of July 18 I took the train out to Henley-on-Thames to inspect some marble slabs George Harrison had stored in his greenhouse at Friar Park. These were pieces left over from construction of the altar in his home, and he thought we might be able to use them at the Manor. There were three slabs of “Norwegian Rose’—bright pink marble stippled with white. They’d come from a quarry in Fauske, Norway. Each highly polished piece was about

2x7 feet and three inches thick, and I could imagine them set into a nice stairway-like effect.

“Hey, these look perfect, George!”

“OK, let’s go tell Prabhupada,” George said, and we hopped in his car for the one-hour drive to Letchmore Heath. This would be George’s first trip to the opulent country ashram he’s purchased for the Krishnas, and his first meeting with Srila Prabhupada since August last year.

George, in ponytail and with a tight band of Vaishnava beads around his neck, is dressed in white Indian kurta and bellbottom jeans. A bubbling gang of devotees greets us in the Manor parking lot, and someone daubs George’s forehead and nose with bright white tilak. He is ebullient and talkative as we stroll around the grounds, taking the full tour. George especially appreciates the trees and flower gardens, exchanging horticultural knowledge—he knows the Latin names of almost every plant and shrub—with the devotees in charge of landscaping. A purple satin bead bag dangles from his right hand as he gives tips to Devadatta on the proper care of maples and Anemone Japonica.

George, Mukunda, Pradyumna, and I enter Prabhupada’s large room and bow down. Fresh-cut lilacs fill the room with wonderful fragrance, and we rise to see Srila Prabhupada seated in slanted sunbeams pouring through the lead-paned Tudor windows, his face aglow on this glorious English summer late afternoon.

Prabhupada: Hare Krishna! \[laughs\] Jaya'. First, when I entered this room, I said, “All glories to George Harrison!” \[laughs\] Yes. You have given us this shelter, and Krishna will give you shelter at His lotus feet. We shall pray always like that. Yes. Not only future, but you are sincere and Krishna has already favored you. So just intelligently utilize your favorable condition. Then everything will go on rightly.

George: Well, everything feels so exciting at the moment, what’s happening. The future is just going to be overwhelming. There’ll be more temples. In the end there’ll be more temples than hotels!

At their first meeting at John Lennon’s place in 1969, Prabhupada’s instruction to George was, “Anything which is accepted by the leading persons, ordinary persons will follow them.... Just give them something actually nice, Krishna, and the face of the earth will change.” So for the past four years, George has been passionately following this instruction to spread God consciousness to the world, in so many significant ways. But now he is having some problems—perhaps even some doubts—and he struggles to get help from his spiritual master.

“Prabhupada,” George says, “I seem to be going around in circles. Maybe it’s something to do with me being, you know, the Pisces. They show one fish going this way and one fish going that way. I have periods when I just can’t stop chanting, and then other periods where, you know, I turn into a demon again and then forget to—”

“You are not demon, you are demigod! \[laughs\] Some way or other you have got attached to Krishna. That will help you. Are you reading Bhagavad-gita?”

“Yes, I’m reading. I read it over and over. You know, just one piece over and over.”

“Then you just keep reading. All answers will be given there.”

They talk about Sanskrit, and hearing, and then Prabhupada reminds George of his duty to preach Krishna’s message to the world. This brings up the second of George’s quandaries: “You know, Shyamsundar and I were just talking on the way over here that when you commit yourself to something, in a way it’s like putting your head on the chopping block. I find that the more commitment I make—even though, relatively speaking, it’s such a little commitment—now I’m getting in the area where I find that people are agitated, I’m provoking a bad reaction. Like the stronger the commitment, the stronger the animosity becomes. You know, sometimes I get the feeling that maybe there’s one person who it means something real to, and ten people who it doesn’t mean anything to—and I’m not sure if it all balances out in the end, whether reaching that one person is worth the ten or twenty people getting annoyed with you. Maybe, if you don’t say anything, all of them will be quite friendly!”

“Yes,” Prabhupada replies, “in the preaching propaganda there is possibility of creating animosity. Mukhaya upadecho hi propapaya chantaya. There is a verse that if one is foolish, if you give him good instruction he becomes angry. Just like if you keep a snake and if you give him milk, his poison will increase. But when one preaches he must tell the truth. Just like Lord Jesus Christ. The people did not like his preaching, but he did not stop his preaching, even they killed him. Especially in God consciousness, such things are possible, you see? We have to pay sometimes in this way. But if you take risk for Krishna, even you stand to lose everything, Krishna always comes to help and protect. Just like Arjuna, because Krishna is there he comes out victorious. Krishna always protects his devotee.”

George nervously jiggles the beads in his bead bag—and then comes out with this startling realization: “There is one sort of problem that I found when chanting all the time, and that was that I start relating less and less to all the people I know. I mean, there’re only certain times when I see Shyamsundar or just a few devotees. And that’s OK, but most of the other people in my life, my friends—even my wife—I suddenly found myself on such a different level that it’s hard to relate to them! It’s like I’ve reached a point, I have to decide to either slow down and pull back towards these people in order to try to bring them with me—maybe because I’m not really ready to go myself?—or just cutting the whole thing off and just going completely! You know what I mean? Just in day-to-day things. More and more the mantra builds up in me, and the effect is so subtle in a way that there’s a point where I just can’t relate any more to anybody! There’s so much urgency involved. The realization that everybody is wasting their time, doing mundane things just to have a little bit of mundane fun.

I’m benefiting so much from chanting that suddenly I’m out on a limb and it’s hard to pull these people with you. You know, there’s a point where suddenly I’m not going to know them anymore. So the problem is this: Where to find a balance?”

The room is silent, all of us pondering this heavy, classic question. From outside we hear the cry of a child, the chatter of a jay flying by the open windows. All ears in the room are anxious for Prabhupada’s reply.

“Yes, you keep balance. You keep balance. Don’t spoil your position. Keep your position. Just like external, internal. The example is Raghunath das Goswami. He was a rich man’s son, a big zamindar, manager over so many villages. He became attracted to Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, so immediately he wanted to leave home and go with Him. Chaitanya Mahaprabhu advised, ‘No, no, don’t do it. It is not required. Externally you remain as you are. Krishna will help you.’ Antha nishtar badge loko babu ha. Within, you just keep yourself always Krishna conscious. Outside, you just deal like an ordinary man. This is the tactics. Because your talent is not ordinary talent. So you are getting money, fame, so why you should refuse to get it? No. Wealth and fame can be utilized for Krishna. Our policy is not that you don’t touch money. Why not? For Krishna’s sake you have to do so many things.”

One more question plagues George. He’s still uncertain about Krishna’s place in the pantheon of gods. If the person Krishna is the absolute cause of all causes, how does this relate to Vishnu, Buddha, and all the other incarnations of God? “Ravi Shankar and I have just been making some music in California, and one of the songs we’re doing, based on the Vedic thing, is called ‘Jaya Jagadisha Hare,’ which describes the ten avatars of Vishnu. It’s a beautiful song. But I was wondering during that song, why there is no mention of Krishna?”

“Krishna is not incarnation. Krishna is the origin of all incarnations. Incarnations are called avatara. Krishna is avatari. This is the difference. Avatari is source of avatara. Therefore in that song it is invoking ‘Keshava.’ Keshava is the name of Krishna: ‘Keshava, You have appeared, and You have incarnated as Buddha, Rama, and so many others.’”

“OK. Is there any place where those words have been printed? Where I can see the words?”

“Yes, I can give you immediately. Satsvarupa? Where is he? Just bring the words to that song ‘Jaya Jagadisha Hare’ in typewriting, for George.... And also in Bhagavad-gita Krishna says, ‘Neither the hosts of demigods nor the great sages know My origin, for in every respect I am the source of the demigods.”

“That’s great!” George says. He just wants to see the written proof.

Things lighten up as George describes his recording sessions with Ravi and then suggests a line of Krishna postcards. Malati and Rebatinandan bring in the prasada, bowls and plates loaded with goodies, and we all begin to eat with great enthusiasm, yakking between mouthfuls. George comments or asks questions about nearly every item on the menu: What is this? This is great! What would you call this one? Where can I get the recipe?

Prabhupada: In Vrindaban there are many pandits, and they eat too much. So one pandit, an old man, after eating he was practically dying, \[laughs\] So he instructed his son, “Don’t be sorry, I am not dying out of starvation. I am dying by eating, \[laughs\] To die of starvation is inglorious. Better to eat and die.”

George: As long as you’re not thinking about indigestion as you go! \[laughter all around\]

Prabhupada: Yes, so I will have to transmigrate. I can transmigrate to ordinary life of starving or eating to death, suffering. Or I can transmigrate to the kingdom of God. Everything is open. It is my choice.

George \[mimicking a businessman\]: It’s a good deal. It’s a good, fair deal, \[laughter all around\]

Prabhupada rises, goes to the bathroom to wash his hands, and George leans over to me and says, “He’s fantastic! He is just so beautiful! And he’s what, almost eighty? It’s hard to believe, isn’t it? I mean, he’s writing all those books, always traveling, talking to people, he almost never sleeps—and still he looks so fresh and young! Prabhupada is just fantastic!”

One more thing bothers George: What will happen to the Krishna consciousness movement in the future, implying, what will happen to the devotees—and himself—when Prabhupada leaves his body? Prabhupada returns, sits down, and George sort of shifts sideways, a stern look on his face as he addresses all of us.

“You’ve really got to be prepared for the future, you know. I don’t know what made me think of that the other day. I was just thinking about the difference between now and a few years ago. I mean in a relatively short time, ISKCON, it’s gotten to be such a huge organization, and there’s going to be a point where, you know, it’s going to need such fantastic management. Whether you can hold on to what you’ve got now? ... At the moment, you see, you have Prabhupada there, who everybody—you know, he’s like the figurehead and the spearhead and the one who everybody can draw their energy from. But sooner or later, everybody’s going to have to be out there on their own, doing, you know, carrying out what everybody’s learned. And at that time... ”

We try to answer George’s concern in various ways—how ISKCON is being managed now, how each center is separate and self-sufficient yet united with the others by a common philosophy and purpose, but it’s Prabhupada who steps in to nail it on the head:

“If my disciples remain strictly to the spiritual principles, they’ll get strength. This is the Vedic injunction, that one should have full faith in Krishna and the spiritual master. Then everything will be revealed. That is revelation. Nobody can understand spiritual life by academic study. The more we become sincere and stick to the spiritual principles, it will be revealed. Just like the sun. Now it is darkness. By your scientific method you cannot get the sun to rise now. The sun will rise automatically in due course. Similarly, spiritual method is we have to work for the mercy of Krishna, for the sun to rise. Therefore, this devotional service. Krishna is pleased by service. Krishna is not in want of money or anything. He is complete. But He becomes very much pleased by one’s service. Now, what is that service? Ya idam parama guhya: ‘For one who explains the supreme secret to the devotees, devotional service is guaranteed and at the end he will come back to Me. There is no servant in this world more dear to Me than he, nor will there ever be one more dear.’ Explaining the supreme secret, this is highest service. By preaching this Krishna knowledge, automatically the sun appears, and you go back to Krishna.”

Srila Prabhupada adds, “Sometimes it is a little difficult, this preaching service. We are promoting restrictions. We say God consciousness can be awakened proportionately as one becomes pure. Purified. Because Krishna is the purest. So we begin with four restrictions: no meat-eating, no illicit sex, no intoxication, no gambling. Right away they will say, ‘Please go away. Don’t preach!’ So animosity will be there. Never mind, you go on preaching. Eka chandras tamo hanti—if there is one moon, that is sufficient. There is no need for millions of stars. One person enlightened is sufficient to give light. There is no need for many glowworms, \[laughs\] Glowworms can be important only in darkness, but when there is sunshine they have no value. So my request to all devotees, you just go on chanting, distribute Krishna to everyone, become yourself purified, and Krishna will reveal Himself like the sun, and everything will be all right.... You have to go now or you will stay the night?”

After a long pause, George replies in a voice hoarse from emotion, “No, I must go.”

“So you are going alone or somebody is going with you?”

“I go alone. Well... \[laughter all around\] a little bit of you will be with me.”

Almost every day I don my peacock “sales” outfit—gold-embroidered Rajasthani vest, brocade-bordered dhoti, and white chappals with turned-up toes—and run around London in the little rented Ford Capri trying to sell my inferior-grade rubies. Sometimes Mukunda comes with me. We’d outfitted him a few months back with fancy kurtas from an Asian boutique in Zurich, but he usually chooses to wear his suit and tie, his hair groomed for business. We’re selling a few pieces here and there, barely enough to pay the electricity bill at the Manor and buy food for the hungry devotees. The rubies have a good carmine color but little transparency, and there’s just very little market for raw stones in London’s Hatton Garden wholesale gem district.

A cabochon is a gemstone that’s been smoothed, rounded, and polished, without facets, and with a flat back for mounting into jewelry. The market price for rubies is much higher for finished goods, so to improve the value of my rough stones I set up a cabochonmaking “factory” in the old horse stable behind the manor house. I had no practical experience in the “cab’’-making art, but I’d watched others do it. How hard could it be to round a crystal into a pleasing shape and keep its back flat? I bought a how-to book, some supplies, and carefully copied the procedures. I made dop sticks from hardwood doweling and bought the special wax to mount individual crystals to the sticks to prepare them for grinding. I bought an electric grinding machine, with two carbide wheels, to shape the stones into rough cabochon forms. Noisy work—I had to wear earplugs. I also bought a lapping machine, with a ten-inch round, horizontal, grooved ceramic plate (lap), which spins at high speed. You impregnate the lap with various grades of silicon-carbide and diamond powders to gradually bring a high polish to the finished stone. This is lapidary.

Late at night, exhausted from hours of grinding and polishing rubies in the stables, I’d turn off the lights and walk across the courtyard to my cottage. I often saw Srila Prabhupada’s light still shining in his windows and, hungry for his company, I’d slip through the darkened manor, mount the creaky wooden stairs, and knock softly at his door. One night, well after midnight, I was in the stables working to finish an order I had for fifty, standard-sized (6mm x 8mm) cabochons when Srutakirti entered the stables rubbing his eyes and said, “Srila Prabhupada wants to see you.”

His room is dark. His shawl-shrouded figure dim behind the desk, beads clicking, his smiling face moves forward into the pool of light from his desk lamp, and I feel faint with sadness and pleasure as he says, “So, you are working so late? I did not see you on morning walk, or through the whole day.”

“Yes, Prabhupada, I have an order for polished rubies I must deliver tomorrow, for £250.”

“Your fingers—there is blood.”

“That’s from the grinding wheel. Sometimes I push on the stone too hard and my finger goes psshhht onto the wheel.”

He chuckles. “Yes, I can hear your machine from here. So there is good scope for your business?”

“I think so, Srila Prabhupada. Sales are increasing, and our business manager, Denis O’Brien, is trying to get us a long-term contract from a big buyer. Mukunda will go to New York soon to try to sell that ton of rubies we shipped over there. He has some buyers lined up.”

My spiritual master leans back, disappears from the lamplight, and then I hear from the dark space: “Actually, my Guru Maharaja ordered me when I was twenty-five years old that ‘You go and preach.’ But I thought, ‘First of all, I shall become a rich man, and I shall use that money for preaching work.’ So that’s a long history. I got good opportunity for becoming very rich man in business. And some astrologer told me that ‘You should have become like Birla \[an Indian billionaire\].’ So there were some chances, very good chances. But everything was dismantled. I was forced to come to this position to carry out my order of my Guru Maharaja. And now I am realizing that I have not lost. I’ve gained. That’s a fact. So to lose material opulences for Krishna’s sake is not loss. It is the greatest gain. This position, that ‘Krishna, except Yourself, I have nothing to claim. I have nothing, no possession, so don’t neglect me because You are my only possession—this position is very nice.”

During the long silence following Prabhupada’s words I ponder the risky, faint path to wealth I’ve recently chosen and where it might take me, but youthful pride and arrogance win the war over sadness in my head and I reply, “I am sure this business will succeed, Srila Prabhupada.”

So we chat about the perfect weather were having and about my days as his secretary. I have noticed that the volume and size of Prabhupada’s letters to his disciples has greatly diminished in recent months, so I drop hints, hoping against hope, that I’m available to take up my old post should he need me. “And your GBC secretaries, they are doing a good job?”

“Yes, it is sufficient.”

We talk about the important people scheduled to visit him in the upcoming days. “Prabhupada, there is one man in London— he’s a famous historian named Arnold Toynbee. We’ve spent days tracking him down, and he really wants to meet you. His books on Western history are required reading for college students in America. He’s been to Haight-Ashbury and supports the hippies’ search for spiritual values. He believes religion can save humanity. But he’s eighty-three now and can’t move about very well. He wonders if you can go into London and meet him, at his office.”

“Yes, we can go. Why not?”

“OK, I’ll phone him tomorrow and make arrangements. I have an article he wrote—it’s here in my pocket.” I read bits and pieces aloud to Prabhupada from Toynbee’s erudite study of historical forces, ending with: “The present threat to mankind’s survival can be removed only by a revolutionary change of heart in individual human beings. This change of heart must be inspired by religion in order to generate the willpower needed for putting arduous new ideals into practice.... I hold that the goal of education ought to be religious, not mercenary.”

“That is very nice. He is a great sage. Just leave it. I will study.”

Prabhupada asks about the current status of the temple and altar construction in the main room downstairs, then says, “We shall install our Radha-Krishna Deity on August 21, which is Janmastami, Krishna’s birthday, almost one month from now.”

OK, we’ve got our deadline. It’s a lot to get ready, but the good news is that Prabhupada will stay at least one more month!

We’ve got some good carpenters now, so I’m not too worried about the construction. I just have to worry about paying for the materials to make this the most glorious temple on the planet. The large temple room, formerly the estate’s living room/ballroom, is wainscoted in oak, very beautiful, so doesn’t require much renovation. But we have to build a vyasasan in an alcove at the north end of the room, which will look across to the orangerie, the glass-enclosed dayroom once used for tropical plants. We’ll enlarge and modify the orangerie to house the Deities. So a wall has to come down, the ceiling reinforced, and an altar built in the center of the gallery. There is a lot of empty wall space in the temple room, so on July 20 Prabhupada writes to Bali Mardan in New York, “There are no nice paintings immediately available for the Temple room here. So I want you to remove all of the paintings that are presently hanging in my rooms at Brooklyn Temple and dispatch them to Letchmore Heath or London immediately by airmail. The temple room is very large, I think about 70 feet by 40 feet, so many paintings are required to decorate it nicely. And if you can send me besides additional of the newer paintings that will be your great contribution.I am awaiting anxiously your report on the meeting with the Japan emperor.”

**\* \* \***

Even after meeting so many world leaders in Srila Prabhupada’s presence, I was especially excited and humbled to walk into the hallowed, book-filled sanctum of the great sage, Arnold J. Toynbee.

In his twelve-volume opus, A Study of History, Toynbee had defined to my generation the vast interplay of civilizations over thousands of years. The venerable gentleman rose to greet the Swami, and there was an instant exchange of respect, as if these two men were already acquainted. Prabhupada got right to it, explaining how the most successful society in history had been guided by Vedic principles.

Arnold Toynbee: Yes, yes. You would apply the teachings of the Gita to all human societies at all times. Yes, yes.

Prabhupada: Yes, yes. Yes, that is my ambition.... In your Western countries they are very well organized, so you are not feeling now so much confusion. But it is coming.

A lot of yeses are exchanged between these two great elders— they’re on the same wavelength. Prabhupada points out that India is the prime example of a first-world culture in an advanced state of decline, and that the same confused state awaits the West—“It is coming”—unless society somehow embraces deep spiritual values. Toynbee agrees that civilizations improve when there is widespread renunciation of material pursuits, and he sees a spark of hope in the youth of today.

Toynbee: There are some signs in Western countries of people turning away from just money-making professions. Small signs, but distinct signs, perhaps. But it’ll take a long time, and probably only through a great deal of suffering will people learn to change their attitude to life.

Prabhupada: Renunciation means that as every son has got a right on the property of the father, similarly, every living entity has got right to live at the cost of God. But they should not encroach upon others’ right. That is renunciation.

Toynbee: Yes. I agree.

They laugh a lot, these two great historians, enjoying each other’s company. To seize this rare opportunity I ask, “Srila Prabhupada, what is your view of the future—the future of history?”

Prabhupada: That is described in the Srimad-Bhagavatam. Governmental power will go to the rascals and thieves, rogues.

And their only business will be how to exploit the people. So on one side, by not sufficient rain, there will be scarcity of foodstuff, and on other side, the government will tax like anything. In this way, people will become so much harassed that they will give up their hearth and home and go to the forest.

Toynbee: Yes.

Shyamasundar: In that case, is there any hope in a movement like ours to rectify the situation?

Prabhupada: Yes. There is always light and darkness. So the light party will be also there. Kaler dosha-nidhe rajann asti hy eko mahan gunah. Only Krishna conscious people will be free from all these calamities. Others will have to suffer. Now they are going to the forest, the hippies. Eh? Yes, this is the future history. You can predict in your writings. Yes?

Toynbee: Yes.

Prabhupada: This Krishna consciousness movement—we are getting response. It will increase. That is also stated, that for ten thousand years Krishna consciousness movement will increase. Yes.

Shyamasundar: Ten thousand years ...

Prabhupada: Yes. Within next ten thousand years, if they become Krishna conscious, then life is successful. After ten thousand years, the gloomy picture of Kali-yuga will return. Still there is time. Ten thousand years is not a small period.

These two wise men traded thoughts back and forth for more than an hour, and I had to change cassettes twice. They discussed the cycles of history. Toynbee compared the conflict between ancient Athens and Sparta to the US vs. USSR nuclear confrontation in today’s world—the forces of light and civilization vs. the armies of darkness. “Today you will find Athens a flourishing metropolis, and of Sparta only a few columns standing in the ruins.” Similarly, Toynbee said, the West would triumph over the Soviet colossus. “Because civilizations that develop the spirit, in mystic ceremonies, will survive and thrive, prevailing over demonic forces.”

Wow! Prabhupada loves this, and he says, “Yes, yes, yes.”

Furthermore, Toynbee adds, great civilizations always perfect a particular art form to its pinnacle. The Greeks perfected the plastic art, or the art of sculpture. “You Americans—what I have observed—your young people are perfecting the art of music, of sound vibration. And this will be your great achievement.”

“Yes,” says Srila Prabhupada, “transcendental sound vibration, the maha-mantra.”

The following day, Malcolm Muggeridge visited Srila Prabhupada at the Manor. Muggeridge was seventy years old, a well-known British author and public figure. He was a teacher in India in the 1920s and a spy during WWII. A lifelong agnostic, he’d converted to Christianity at age 66, and then written his best-selling book, Jesus Rediscovered. These days he was an outspoken advocate of the Christian faith.

He approaches Prabhupada in very humble, reverent mood, but his complex intelligence had me straining to understand what he was trying to say.

Muggeridge: If he would then, when he is a grown person, recapture or re-feel the link to his reason for being, he must go back in his experience or thought to the point at which he cried to feel again the feeling of being lost. Now, the Western youth seem to have been forced to accept their position, and the position that they have accepted gives them no freedom to go back and find the point at which they felt the experience of the thing they had lost. So in their minds they would say, “There is something missing. There is no God. But there is a God. Yet I must find him with my mind.” And then they know through the search that it is only through going back through their own years of experience to the point at which they had lost that they will find it. But for the Westerner, it clashes with his total Western heritage, the thing imposed upon him by his senses, which is that he may not isolate himself from his community in order to go down. And the young people cannot move, because this point holds them—

Prabhupada: No. The young people, they are moving. They are coming to Krishna consciousness.

Great Britain is such a special place for Srila Prabhupada, and he is at home with English language, customs, laws, and the manners of gentlemen. On the morning of July 23, after the Bhagavad-gita class, Mr. Robert Arnold, the unlikely middle-aged banker who’d become Bhaktipramode das, stood in the temple and presented Prabhupada with a badge of honor, a pin made of twenty-four-carat gold, saying, “And hereby we present it on behalf of the members of the guild to you, our most beloved leader.” Prabhupada replied, “Thank you very much,” and the assembled devotees shouted Hariboll in unison. Then Prabhupada asked, “So how it is to be used?” Bhaktipramode said, “Just like this, m’ Lord,” and he pinned the decoration, a symbol of valor and accomplishment carefully to Prabhupada’s kurta. How very British.

That afternoon Lord Fenner Brockway, the Baron of Eton and Slough, came to visit Srila Prabhupada. Since his entry into British politics in the early 1920s, Brockway had been an outspoken critic of established values. He’d led the antiwar and anticolonial movements, and was a founding member of War on Want and the Campaign for Nuclear Disarmament. Lord and Lady Brockway had attended our “Guess Who’s Coming to Dinner?” party at Betterton Street back in January 1969, and a few days later Brockway admitted Gurudas and me into the House of Lords visitors’ gallery at the Houses of Parliament so we could observe democracy in action. The queen wasn’t there that day, but an ancient elevated throne sat as silent testimony to her power. I studied that austere yet somehow elegant throneeven made a sketch of it—thinking I’d like to build one like it someday for Srila Prabhupada.

But that day we watched with great amusement as the Lords of the Realm fought heated verbal battles over seemingly minor issues, yelling at each other with scarcely disguised imprecations, like knights dueling—and then breaking into uproarious laughter as they walked out, arm in arm, for a drink at the nearest pub.

Over the years, Lord Fenner Brockway had been our “inside man” if we needed a special political favor. Now, as he sits in a comfortable chair across from Srila Prabhupada, we learn that he’s eighty-five years old—he’d been born in 1888 of missionary parents in a village in West Bengal.

Prabhupada: Baharampur? Oh, that is not far from our temple at Mayapur. That road from Calcutta to Mayapur is called Baharampur National Road.

Brockway: Yes, and I became a vegetarian, oh, seventy years ago, following ascetic, humanitarian ideals. And I was an athlete too. I’ve proved one can be just as healthy without meat.... I would quite welcome death. I’ve no fear of it at all.

Prabhupada \[laughing\]: Because you are pure, you are a little advanced.

Brockway: I, uh ... And I say that, though I have no picture in my mind at all of what would happen after death, or if anything happens. I love the description which was given by my friend Bertrand Russell, that life is like being born in a spring on the hillside, and the stream becomes a river.

Prabhupada: Just like, your childhood body is no longer existing. Your youthhood body is no longer existing. That’s a fact. And it is also a fact that you had such and such body. Therefore you, as the soul, you are permanent, even changing so many types of bodies. Similarly, the conclusion should be when you give up this body, you’ll be in another body. It is common reasoning.

Brockway: Yes, I have listened to you with very great interest and almost complete agreement. What you’ve said has impressed me. But I wouldn’t say it has convinced me—one has to think...

Prabhupada: After this body is annihilated, we do not know what kind of body we are getting next. We must prepare. Just like you are elevated to the position of lordship. So you had to prepare yourself. Not that this lordship is offered to anyone and everyone.

Similarly, we should know how we are becoming qualified for the next life. We must prepare.

Brockway: I’ve listened, and I think I understand. And one has to think about it. And I acknowledge I do not know. And I am personally satisfied with trying to do what I can while I’m living in this life for the betterment of mankind. And I believe that’s the best preparation for any future life, if there is a future life.

They switch topics, discussing the House of Lords, and then Prabhupada drops a bombshell—really blows Lord Brockway’s mind:

Prabhupada: No, democracy we don’t approve.

Brockway: Oh, I approve of it very, very strongly! Democracy must be the basis of all good! I am saying that a people will advance only as it is a reflection of the progress of the people themselves.

Prabhupada: That is not possible. My proposition is that either the king or the elected person should not commit mistake. But if you try to educate the mass of people to elect the right person, that is very difficult. But if a king is educated nicely, that is easier. That is my point of view. Because people are godless at the present moment, they are suffering.

Brockway: It’s not only because of that.

Prabhupada: That is the only cause.

Brockway: No, it’s not the only cause. I know people who are very God conscious, beautiful people, kind in all their human relations, but they haven’t got a beginning of an understanding of how one can bring brotherhood and peace to the world. And therefore, in addition to God consciousness, you must have understanding, knowledge.

Prabhupada: God conscious means understanding. Without understanding, how there can be God consciousness? Perfect consciousness means full understanding also. That is perfect God consciousness. Yasmin vijnate sarvam evam vijnatam bhavati. This is the Vedic injunction. If you understand only God, then you understand everything.

Devotees bearing huge plates of prasada suddenly appear, and the argument ends with Krishna’s perfect timing. Prabhupada, as usual, has the last word.

Prabhupada: Have you got your natural teeth? Your teeth?

Brockway \[between bites of food\]: No, not natural. I used to play rugby football, and my teeth got kicked out of my mouth. \[laughter\]

Prabhupada was exceedingly gracious to Lord Brockway and— as he rarely did with visitors—he stood to bid farewell to this senior British wise man. To this day, Lord Fenner Brockway’s statue stands defiant at the entrance to Red Lion Square in central London.

**\* \* \***

It’s high summer now in the charming green fields of England. After years of hectic travel with him, it’s a relief to see Srila Prabhupada relaxing in this bucolic setting. My adventures these days were pretty much solo. I dashed around London so that Prabhupada could remain still and serene in one place—in flight, so that my spiritual master could rest in what I hoped was tranquil routine. I sensed that our relationship might never be this close again, and I savored the perfect somnambulant summer afternoons when Prabhupada sat on his saffron shawl on the vast lawn in front of the Manor, chanting japa as Srutakirti softly read to him from Caitanya-caritamrita. Sometimes I sat with them, quiet, listening to the ancient words, humming bees, faint quack and rustle of ducks on the lake, rattle of grass being mowed beyond the hedges. Men worked bare-shouldered in gardens around us, and across the lawns you could see splashes of bright saris as girls giggled and picked roses for garlands. Prabhupada has named this place New Gokula.

Gokula is the small village (nowadays, with four thousand inhabitants) near Vrindaban, India, where Krishna spent His childhood. Krishna’s father, Nanda Maharaj, kept huge herds of cows, and Krishna and His brother Balarama herded cows and performed amazing pastimes in the pastures and forests that surrounded the village. Gokula is also the name of the most exalted region in Krishnaloka, Krishna’s abode in the spiritual sky, described in the Brahma Samhita as resembling a lotus flower with thousands of petals. In the inner portion of Gokula there is an elaborate arrangement for Lord Krishna’s residence, along with His eternal associates. New Gokula is now our pastoral paradise in Hertfordshire.

Brahmacharis loitered on the paths, women strolled by with their babies, and one by one they would slowly walk over, shy, looking down at their feet, but drawn like iron to a magnet, ducklings to their mother. They’d sit and spread out around their spiritual master in the sun. Prabhupada would listen, eyes closed, absorbed in Srutakirti’s words, then open with a wide smile, reach over and tickle a child, or ask if someone’s cold was getting better. He especially liked to talk with his new disciple Bhadrakrishna das, the jug-eared older man who had been Father Bernard, a Cistercian monk who had lived under a vow of silence for twenty-three years. Bhadrakrishna reminded me of one of those monk statues out at Friar Park with that fringe of gray hair around his bald pate. No longer in brown robes, he sat in dungarees and a mud-spattered T-shirt to discuss with Prabhupada in reverential tones the ascending steps to heaven. The women would take out their knitting or string garlands to pass the quiet time. If too many men gathered, Prabhupada would sometimes turn to me and say, “What is this? Why these men are not working?” And I too would get the hint and dash off to my phone or drive into town or polish some rubies in the stables.

Sometimes invited guests would join us on the lawn. On the afternoon of July 25 Mrs. Critchley—her son, Michael, has just been initiated as Mahadeva das—comes to visit. She has an aristocratic bearing, wears a summer straw hat picked out with tiny fruits and feathers. Someone fetches her a chair. She brings with her the family’s Jesuit priest, one of Michael’s instructors at the Catholic school he attended much of his life. Their purpose is to convince her son to return to school, rejoin his rightful station in the long tradition of rational British behavior. Mrs. Critchley is a hospital administrator, and she sheltered Indian troops rescued from Dunkirk in the dreary days of 1940.

Mother: Michael has had a very happy home with brothers and sisters. And we’ve all been very happy. And I hope he will remain happy.

Prabhupada: He was happy; now he’s happier. Because your son has come here, you may not be happier, but he is happier. That is the difference.

Mother: I’m very disappointed that he is not continuing with education.

Prabhupada: But what is the purpose of education? Our Krishna consciousness movement is not depriving people of their education. You get education how to eat, how to sleep, and that’s all right. But side by side, you take education how to know God and how to love Him. There is a balance. You take some students and train them as medical men. But I am training to become preachers. Why you interfere with my business? You do your own business. That is our proposition.

Mother: Yes. I agree with you. But, well, my son is my business. He was snatched out of the university by your people going round the universities.

Rebatinandan: Now wait a minute. We didn’t snatch Michael. Michael came to the temple in London, sat down, and didn’t want to go away.

Mother: He’d been taking LSD and he was very sick. And somebody took him in.

Prabhupada: So when he was taking LSD, what did you do for him?

Mrs. Critchley cannot deny Michael’s obvious happiness. She gestures to her priest, hoping he can turn the tide against Prabhupada’s unswerving logic. Prabhupada asks the priest, “So next life, how it will be ascertained? What kind of body I am going to next life?”

Priest: I don’t think it matters very much. I couldn’t care less what’s happening after I’m dead. All I know, there’s not annihilation. I’m going to be joined with almighty God. Until that time, I must lead the good Christian life.

Prabhupada: Then the question becomes, What is the good life?

Priest: Obeying the commandments of God.

Prabhupada: So if the commandment is “Thou shall not kill,” if somebody kills, so that is good life?

Priest: But you kill the potato.

Prabhupada: Now, suppose there is potato, and there is your child. So would you like to kill your child in preference of potato? Why this discrimination?

Priest: Animals do not have rationality. “Omnia animalia intel-lectu carent.”

Prabhupada: Even the animal is not intelligent, you cannot kill. Because your child is also not intelligent, so that does not mean you can kill your child.

Priest: Would you say that because I eat meat and bacon and so on, does that make me sinful? If I didn’t eat those, I would be less sinful?

Prabhupada: Then you become pure. You become pure.

Priest: That’s very interesting.

The learned Jesuit shuts down for the rest of the afternoon, perhaps thinking about killing and sin and how this might apply to him. Both Mahadeva and Bhadrakrishna sit quietly, understanding both sides, their inner peace radiating conviction that they’ve done the right thing.

These conversations on the lawns and in his room were very enlivening for Srila Prabhupada. In fact, he wrote to Karandhar that “Wherever I shall go now this policy of important men being invited to talk with me about our Krishna Consciousness movement should be implemented.”

We’ve lined up some really important men to see Prabhupada over the next couple of days. On the evening of July 26 we’re joined in Prabhupada’s room by John Papworth and E. F. Schumacher, distinguished pioneers in the environmental movement. At the dawn of the energy crisis and emerging globalization both of these men spoke out against “bigger is better,” promoting instead a new society based on small, self-governing communities and enterprises. Papworth was a Church of England clergyman; in 1966, he founded Resurgence magazine, the popular, bimonthly journal of the environmental movement—real cutting-edge stuff in those days. It was a new science, how humans should live within the bounds of nature. E. F. Schumacher’s book, Small Is Beautiful: A Study of Economics As If People Mattered, had just come out earlier in the year and was already established as a bible for sustainable living.

Schumacher thought that human society should aim “to obtain the maximum amount of well-being with the minimum amount of consumption.”

Prabhupada: Thank you very much for your coming. I have read some of your ideas. So from your writing it appears you are nice, thoughtful men. Muni, the Sanskrit word is muni. I was just reading your article, “Cars, Profits, and Pollution.” On one side we make profit, another side we make pollution. This is the nature of material activities. Profit and pollution. Anything you do material it is the same. Therefore the remedy is to act for spiritual realization. Then you will avoid pollution. This is the sum and substance of Bhagavad-gita: do not avoid material activities, but become free from the material pollution. This is the secret of Krishna consciousness movement.

Papworth: How do you envisage one should act, if I may ask that?

Prabhupada: We do not say, “Now stop all cars driving.” That is impossible. But we say, purify it.

Papworth: What means do you suggest for purifying it? Prabhupada: By chanting of the maha-mantra.

At this point three devotees interrupt and begin to dominate the conversation, steering the subject matter into a passionate diatribe against Christian priests and animal slaughter. Prabhupada sits back and watches his senior preachers take the floor.

Schumacher: Well, I happen to be a vegetarian. But I would hesitate a long, long time before I would make meat-eating the touchstone on which I would judge a Jesuit. And the evils that are going on that have to be fought are, by comparison with meat-eating, gigantic. And therefore, to refuse to accept that even a meat-eating Jesuit may be a far better man than a vegetarian who is engaged in all sorts of nefarious practices, I think one should be a bit careful. I mean, a fellow who builds himself a huge house when we have twenty thousand homeless people without a roof over their head in London Now I would like those things to be raised into real spiritual problems.

Once again devotees interrupt, derailing Schumacher’s concerns as they lash out against animal killing and other matters, showing little sensitivity to these mens accomplishments and interests.

Papworth: I found this the other day when I was here, and you’ll forgive me making this point, but when I was in discussion, it was impossible for me to make a point without being interrupted so frequently that I failed to make my point at all. Now, can I beg for courtesy for our guests to listen to what Mr. Schumacher has to say first, and then answer him?

Prabhupada steps in to save the day. He exudes charm, and by his gentle manner invites forgiveness for his young and zealous disciples, inwardly proud of them but needing to show them how it’s done with diplomacy and tact.

Prabhupada: John, Mr. John. That we follow strictly the Vedic injunctions, and unless we become God conscious, there cannot be any reformation in this world.

Papworth: I agree with that.

Prabhupada: That’s all. And that God consciousness cannot be achieved without being pure. In the Bhagavad-gita it is said, param brahma param dhama pavitram paramam bhavan, God is the supreme pure. You cannot approach God in impure condition. And without God consciousness, there cannot be any purification. You please try to understand this simple fact, that without God consciousness you may prescribe so many things—they will be all failure. God consciousness cannot be achieved without being pure. This is the problem. Now you can think over it.

Schumacher: I agree with that.

Next on our parade of eminent British intellectual all-stars was Sir Alister Hardy. Alister Hardy was one of the world’s foremost marine biologists and a fellow of the Royal Society. In 1925-27 he’d sailed aboard the RSS Discovery to explore Antarctica. Hardy was Prabhupada’s age and was a deeply spiritual man. He was fascinated by religious experience and had founded the Religious Experience Research Unit at Manchester College, in Oxford. Hardy attempted to reconcile science with human spiritual yearnings, and his hypothesis was that religious experience is an awareness that has evolved through natural selection because of its survival value to the individual but that the spiritual is also a genuine dimension of reality—a blend of Darwinian and creationist thought. When we’d contacted him a couple weeks earlier, Sir Hardy asked for a list of topics to be discussed, and Prabhupada had sent him a short list of questions, starting with “What is the problem of the human life?”

Sir Alister Hardy has come to Bhaktivedanta Manor today to conduct research for his upcoming book, The Biology of God,

Prabhupada: We are very glad to meet together, and we are ready to help you in your research work, provided you take our help. That will be very nice for you.

Hardy: Well, I’d very much like to have records of experience, accounts of present-day religious experience. I’m hoping to get scholars who are really Sanskrit scholars and those people who can really understand the language of oriental affairs.

Prabhupada: No, first thing is, this is a different science. Science of God is not material science. Simply a material, academic career will not help. For example, this zoology is another subject matter—whether life began from matter or matter began from life.

Hardy: That’s a great problem in biology: how did life arise from the inorganic matter?

Prabhupada: Life did not rise from inorganic matter.

Hardy: No? I say, that’s ...

Prabhupada: Life came from life; matter also came from life. That is explained in the Vedanta-sutra, janmadyasya yatah. And it is explained in Bhagavad-gita: aham sarvasya prabhavah. So there is no difficulty.

Hardy: But life may have arisen from the inorganic by the breathing in of the Divine into it. And there’s no doubt that our bodies are material, but within our bodies is this divine, what I call the “divine flame” in one of my books.

Prabhupada: Why not the “divine person”? Wherefrom the flame comes?

Hardy: That is exactly what we are trying to do, understand, from the manifestations of God in the experience of present-day people.

Prabhupada: No. Why manifestation? Why not directly from God? Why you are after manifestation? If there is direct knowledge you can approach.

Hardy: I think we are studying directly from God through present-day man.

Prabhupada: Present-day man, that is all right. But one who knows God, that is who you should approach. You cannot understand the science of God from a storekeeper. That is not possible.

Mukunda and I drive Sir Alister Hardy back to Paddington Station, where he will catch a train to his home in Oxford. He seems blustery, a bit confused. He got something positive from his talk with the Swami, but he’s not sure what it was. Mukunda and I try to answer his questions, but we leave this great scholar with an empty feeling that so much more could have been accomplished by their meeting. When we get back to the Manor late that night we convey these feelings to Prabhupada, and the next morning he summons me to dictate a letter to Sir Alister Hardy, which I hand over to Prabhupada’s new secretary, Hamsaduta (GBC for Germany) to type. This letter, dated July 28,1973, is a six-page masterpiece. With precise scholarly argument Prabhupada not only addresses each of Hardy’s concerns for the modern dilemma, such as overpopulation and food scarcity, but he gives authoritative solutions to all contemporary problems. If the good professor is prepared to accept his guidance, the Swami can show him the path he must take to crown his research with success.

Srila Prabhupada writes:

“You have tried to explain in your book, ‘The findings of such research might spark off a new phase in religious history, people might be induced to try the experiment of approaching this power in their own way, not by prayer or the alteration of physical events or for national safety or material aims, but for spiritual strength and guidance for a better way of life, or perhaps how best to deal with some difficulty.’ This is indeed very good, when you say ‘People might be induced to try the experiment of approaching this power.’ Power is energy, so when you speak of approaching the power it means the Powerful. Power is not independent; there must be a supreme Powerful. This is reasonable, to search out the powerful. Without the powerful no power can exist. A politician or a big general exhibits his power as the powerful by his commandment or by his order. Therefore your understanding of the power is not complete. You must induce people to approach that supreme Powerful.

“So all these problems are due only to a lack of God consciousness. Therefore if you can actually help people to know about the supreme powerful that will be a great help. But I see that your method is not very satisfactory. You are making research by accepting the statements of common peoples’ expression of religious sentiment. There is no need of research, the result of research in this matter is already there, perfectly presented in Bhagavad-gita. All we have to do is accept it and the whole problem of research is solved. You want to establish your conclusion of religious experience by taking the opinions of laymen. A layman’s sentimental expression about religious problems is not a practical understanding of religious problems. Religion as we have explained means the orders of God, therefore it must be scientifically studied, what are His orders, how to abide by them. Simply by taking statistics of the sentiments of common men we cannot come to the right conclusion.”

**\* \* \***

Early morning walks with Srila Prabhupada are especially lively as he expands on yesterday’s room conversations, praising or critiquing the great minds of Britain as the rising midsummer sun wakes grasshoppers and sends them leaping across our path.

Srutakirti: These were the most enjoyable walks in all of my time with Srila Prabhupada, walking through the wheat fields around the Manor. Prabhupada would discuss everything from the natural things we would observe along the way right up to the person he talked with the day before.

On late afternoons before the arrival of guests Prabhupada would pace his room, chanting japa, stopping to gaze from his windows across the rhododendron gardens, green lawns, and ripening fields, meditating on the perfection of Krishna’s creation. One afternoon he asked Srutakirti, “What is the most beautiful animal?”

Sruta answered, “The cow?”

Prabhupada pointed beyond our fence to the shiny brown thoroughbred grazing in a neighboring pasture and said, “No, it is the horse. Just see its perfect body and muscular structure.”

In the evenings, after the guests had left—dusk descended after nine o’clock—Prabhupada left his windows open to crickets fiddling and a chorus of frogs from the lake, the silent crackle of dry lightning in faraway cumulus.

Installation of the Deities at Bhaktivedanta Manor is scheduled for August 21, just three weeks away. I’m worried that Srila Prabhupada may leave right after the installation, so, together with Mukunda and other temple leaders, we make a big plan: the installation ceremony will launch a week-long Hare Krishna Summer Festival at the Manor. We will erect an India-style pan-dal on the Manor grounds and invite the public. There will be a different theme for each day of the festival, and celebrities from all walks of British life will come to open each day’s show. Many important people will want to meet Prabhupada during and after the festival, and he will extend his stay with us. This will be a huge rhino. Hey, if we do this right, we can keep Prabhupada here for months!

Prabhupada writes to Kirtanananda in the USA:

“I beg to acknowledge receipt of your telegram from New York ‘Festival extended per letter expect Your Grace 23rd August, send details.’

“Here, Shyamasundar has arranged a gorgeous festival beginning 21st till 27th August. Just see the program:

August 21st: Chief guest, Indian High Commissioner.

August 22nd: Chief guest, George Harrison.

August 23rd: Ecumenical day, chief guest, Malcolm Muggeridge and representatives from all faiths.

August 24th: Young people’s day, advertised 4 days before the event on all young peoples’ programmes on BBC radio. At least 5000 young people expected to come.

August 25th: Businessman’s Day, chief guests Mr. Toyota, and Baron Rothschild.

August 26th: Neighbor’s Day, chief guest Graham Hill.

August 27th: Intellectual’s Day, chief guests, David Wynne, William Burroughs, Professor Alister Hardy.

So please excuse me because I shall not be able to come there. When I return to America I will come there.”

We re building a magnificent Deity room and altar; we’ll erect a colossal tent in the gardens with the best stage, sound, and lighting systems money can buy; we’ll have elaborate decorations and a ton of advertising. The press, the world, will come to sit at Prabhupada’s feet, and they must see his surroundings like a palace, like a visit to royalty. But how are we going to pay for such huge arrangements? We scarcely have money for lightbulbs. “Mukunda, you’ve got to go to New York and sell those rubies!”

On April 22 I’d consigned 1144 kilos of “Rough Corundum,” sent from Bombay harbor in twenty-five metal boxes to “Int’l Society for Krishna A/C M. A. Grant” in New York. The Dutch ship Hoegh Dyke arrived in Manhattan on June 7, and our rubies had been sitting in a warehouse there for almost two months. Now, in early August, under pressure—even Prabhupada is asking him, “When you are going?”—Mukunda flies off to New York City to try to sell the ton of rubies gathering demurrage in a dockside warehouse on the East River.

The Hare Krishna Summer Festival is going to cost us a lot of money.

## Six

On the afternoon of August 5 George Harrison visits Srila Prabhupada at the Manor, and this time he brings three of his friends from the extended Shankar family: Lakshmi, Viji, and Viji, and Shubho. George and Ravi Shankar have been colla since last April on their first studio album together, Shankar Family and Friends. Lakshmi Shankar, a vocalist, is married to Ravi’s elder brother, Rajendra, and their daughter, Vijayashree (Viji), is married to the Indian violinist, Lakshminarayana Subramaniam. Ravi’s eldest son, Shubho, plays sitar. Music is in this family’s genes, going back many generations. They bow and seat themselves quietly behind George. A few devotees trickle into the room, and there are maybe ten people arranged before the Swami’s desk. Prabhupada asks after George’s health and then remarks on recent conversations he has had with British scientists and religionists.

George: Who’s Alister Hardy?

Devotee: He has an institute at Oxford. He’s a religious experience researcher.

Prabhupada: Now I propose to start a Society. I will explain to you, Shyamasundar. \[clears throat\] “The Society of the Sinless Man.” How do you like this idea?

George: Good idea, but it mightn’t get many members, \[laughter\] Prabhupada: Unless people become sinless, it is not possible to understand what is God. That’s a fact. Just like without becoming fire you cannot enter into fire.... When there is fire in the forest all the animals become disturbed, especially the snakes. Other animals, they have got legs, they can jump over. But the snakes, they have to crawl. So when there is fire in the forest, these snakes, they die first. And the snake business means envious. Without any fault it will bite, and the man will die. Envious. There are two envious beings: one is the snake and another is the envious man. So the envious man is more dangerous than the snake. Why? Snakes you can bring under control by herbs and mantra. But the envious man cannot be brought under control; therefore, he is more dangerous. So we are living in such world. Whole world is surcharged with enviousness. Nation to nation, person to person, there is competition. So how they can be happy? Therefore Krishna Chaitanya Mahaprabhu suggests that “Let them chant Hare Krishna maha-mantra. Then this blazing forest fire will be extinguished.” Simply by chanting ... So we shall chant? Yes? Bring kartals.

Prabhupada begins clapping, leading kirtan, and some of the sweetest voices on the planet join for eight minutes of pure harmony, lost in the transcendental rhapsody of Krishna’s name. The final kartal ringggg fades, and in the silence Prabhupada mutters thanksgiving prayers to the spiritual masters, the holy places, the sacred rivers, and the goddesses of all good things, ending with “All glories to the assembled devotees.” The air seems clarified, surcharged with pleasant expectation. Now it’s George’s turn. He’s brought a tape of songs for the new Shankar Family album, fresh from the studio. We first hear a four-minute, uptempo version of the traditional mantra “Jaya Jagadisha Hare” with tabla, flute, and tamboura. Next, we hear Lakshmi singing a song Ravi wrote called “I Am Missing You”:

**\* \* \***

I am missing you, О Krishna, Where are you?

Though I can’t see you

I hear your flute all the while, Please come wipe my tears And make me smile.

At first I thought the tune kind of corny, with the high falsetto voice you hear in Hindi film songs—the words are nice, but why’s George so involved in this kind of music? Then the song starts multitracking, with voiceovers, electronic doodling, and wait, guitars, a saxophone cuts in, building to a Phil Spector-like wall of sound. Now I get it! You’re taking us on a path here, aren’t you, George, from East to West? The next two songs are in Hindi, with increasingly complex instrumentation, and with voiceovers in English:

Where has my glorious Krishna gone, my only love? Without Him I feel so restless. There’s no peace, no joy. Aching for Him, I cry out, but He does not hear. O my cruel love.”

You came to me in my dreams, O my beloved Krishna, and whispered such sweet and tender things to me. I awoke startled: “Where is my love? Where has He gone, my Krishna? Oh, how can I face this night alone?”

I’m thinking, wow, George, you’ve broken the mold again! This far-out fusion of East and West has never been heard before. You’ve blended the best singing and spoken voices of India, with backing vocals, heavy rock guitars, violins and flutes, sarods and sitars, even cowbells, tweaked here and there with a Moog synthesizer. You flow from bhajans by a hut in rural Bengal to the electronic sky of your Wonderwall album; from Lakshmi’s lilting soprano to a gospel surge with Billy Preston on organ and Ringo on drums. You’ve got everything in these songs, George: Indian music, jazz, funk, and rock-n-roll, from Beethoven to the single sound of Krishna’s flute in a distant perfect forest.

Prabhupada sits quietly for nearly twenty minutes, absorbing George’s artistic devotional sounds. The look on his face says everything: he is content. Eyes closed, he moves his head from side to side with the beat, and when the last song ends Prabhupada glances at George with deep love and says, “There is a verse ...” And he begins to croon a Sanskrit song:

he radhe vraja-devike cha lalite he nanda-suno kutah

sri-govardhana-padapa-tale kalindi-vane kutah

Prabhupada explains to his enraptured audience, “The Goswamis were executing their devotional service in Vrindaban, and this is their devotional attitude: ‘Now I have come to Vrindaban. О son of Nanda, where You are?’Just like the same thing: ‘Krishna, where You are?’ \[laughter\] It is very nice. That is best motive—to feel separation from Krishna. So these songs are following the footsteps of Radharani, feeling the separation of Krishna.”

George: A lot of people are gonna hear Krishna’s name, maybe for the first time, through that song.

Prabhupada: Yes, yes. But they will be benefited. You have done nice. You are distributing Krishna’s name and feeling separation, which will have good effect to the people.

George: One of the devotees from the Los Angeles temple came to play kartals on the record. I forget his name. “Rent-a-kartal.” \[laughter\]

Prabhupada: So you are all qualified artists. So by your art even on material platform you get so much adoration. This material adoration will be finished with the finishing of this body. But if you work in spiritual platform with the same art, if you are recognized by Krishna, spiritual adoration, then that will go with you. That will go with you... Whenever you find time, please come here. It is your temple.

George: It wasn’t my money that I bought it with, \[laughter\]

Prabhupada: \[laughs\] Yes, it is Krishna’s money \[laughter\], but you are the cashier! \[laughter\]

That evening Srila Prabhupada was joined by Professor Ronald Lewcock and Mrs. Barbara Sansoni. Lewcock was on the faculty at Cambridge University, specializing in Islamic architecture. Mrs. Sansoni is a well-known Sri Lankan artist, founder of the textile design company Barefoot.

Lewcock: So we both of us work with the senses, and we deal with the sense of vision. Do you consider that sensual responses to vision and color can be a handicap to spiritual advancement?

Prabhupada: You cannot deny the senses. Use the senses, but for spiritual purpose. Purify the senses, use them in service to God, then you will gradually understand best use of senses.

Lewcock: To what extent would doubt be regarded as a sin, or an element in sin?

Prabhupada: Doubt is good provided you use doubt to make progress. If you stick to your own understanding, tradition, then doubt is not very beneficial. It sets you back. When there is doubt, then you go to a person who can mitigate your doubt and explain. That is the Vedic injunction: to clear your doubts you must go to a guru. Who is a guru? Brahma-nishtham: he is fully convinced in the Absolute Truth. That’s all.

Sansoni: You say that Krishna created these two energies, the spiritual energy and the material energy, and that in the material energy there is illusion, also imperfect. How can God create something which is imperfect?

Prabhupada: The creation is not imperfect, but it is imperfect for my understanding. Just like the sun is always shining, but there is a cloud. The cloud is created by sun, so it cannot be imperfect. But it has created an imperfect position for me to see the sun.

Sansoni: Yes, our vision is imperfect.

Lewcock: Master, how many thousands of devotees of Krishna consciousness are there?

Prabhupada: That is very difficult to say. So suppose I am talking to you for the last more than one hour; you have not become my devotee. So we have to spend gallons of blood to make a devotee. Therefore we cannot expect many in number. You have to talk like this for years; then one man becomes a devotee. But if we can make one person devotee, then we think our preaching attempt is successful. Yes.

**\* \* \***

Bhaktivedanta Manor is a natural for creating publicity, so we decide to milk it for all it’s worth. On August 4 I write a two-page stream-of-consciousness press release and send it out to media reps all over Britain. After a paragraph announcing Bhaktivedanta Swami’s arrival at his seventeen-acre ashram in Hertfordshire, newly gifted to him by none other than George Harrison, I go for the rhino:

Imagine, if you will, a 55-room Tudor-style manor house set in the midst of manicured formal gardens, a beautiful 1 1/2 -acre lake, surrounded by fine paddocks, orchards, and lawns, once the exclusive domain of lords and ladies... Imagine it now populated variously by swans and peacocks, cows (at home in the rose beds or in the hallways!), by sari-clad young women and by saffron-robed, bald (and pale-faced!) Indian-style sadhus or monks (one of them a Cistercian monk, a genuine “renegade” who broke 20 years of avowed monastic silence to join the Hare Krishnas!) nearly a 100-strong, strolling about absorbed in their “ japa,” or chanting meditation, or working individually and collectively in the fields, gardens, and stables, and in the manor house itself, in one of the outlying “householders” cottages, in one of seven tropical greenhouses on the property...
