Imagine the enormous oak-paneled Hall-cum-Temple where, commencing at 4:30 a.m., all of these devotees of Krishna meet several times a day to chant loudly and dance together with drums and crashing cymbals; imagine theirs, the frenzied (watch out, it’s highly infectious!) ecstasy as mortals shed the trappings of their body designations, lose themselves to find their self: transcendent Spirit soul—(or so they say, that is)—but if you look upon such faces, only once, an evidence is written there: glee, excitement, a special kind of happiness is theirs, invoked, it seems, by only Krishna’s name, and strange but unmistakably apparent gleam indicates at least, perhaps, there’s something to it after all...

Imagine this, and then see yourself being led up spacious flights of 400-year-old solid oak staircases to HIS rooms, the Master’s Chambers, and entering them— high-ceilinged, instantly serene and welcome—to find yourself seated cross-legged (chaired upon request) on some oddly comfortable satin pillow, your pen and pad— or microphone, or camera—in hand, and then, across His simple table, face to smiling face with Who’s behind, above it all: His Divine Grace, our Prabhupada (a Sanskrit term, affectionate, it means “the Master at whose feet all other masters sit”), jovial, relaxed, and ready to impart advice, as well as conduct an INTERVIEW! His Grace will answer any question, parry every thrust, make you laugh, yes, even after once or twice he may have got your dandruff up— He’ll fully entertain, satisfy, demystify you all!

Over the top, of course, but that’s show biz. The response was overwhelming, and my phone rang off the hook. On August 6 the first reporter showed up in Prabhupada’s room. He’s after the scoop for a major London tabloid, more interested in controversy than philosophy. I was running around London that day, so I missed the interview, but others told me that the guy wouldn’t take a chair, that he sat on the floor or stood for an hour, pen and pad in hand and, in rapid order, fired terse questions at Srila Prabhupada, receiving equally brusque replies.

How did you meet George Harrison?

Do you have a very expensive motorcar?

Why do you need a fifty-five-room mansion to carry out your work?

Do you smoke pot?

Do you ban sex here?

The reporter read off the celebrities’ names we had listed in our press release, famous people who might be at the Manor, and asked, “When these important people come here, what do they do?”

Prabhupada: They talk about our philosophy. Why don’t you talk about philosophy?

Reporter: OK, philosophy. If I wanted to join your group, would I have to give up my job?

Prabhupada: You are giving ordinary reports, but if you give daily a report what God’s activities are, then you are devotee. This is our movement: that you do everything as you are doing, but do it for God. That’s all. The same business. If you want to report God’s activities, we can give you daily big, big columns of activities.

Reporter: Do you regard yourself as human?

Prabhupada: What do you think?

Reporter: Well, your followers seem to regard you as being superhuman.

Prabhupada: Yes. Superhuman means I am not acting as ordinary human being. You can regard also.

Reporter: Your followers seem to regard you as being, uh, some sort of... holy man.

Prabhupada: Because I am holy.

Reporter: Have you talked to other members of the British press at all this week? We’re the first, are we? The Daily Mail—when are they coming?

Prabhupada: Give him prasada, a sweet-ball.

Reporter: No, no. I’ve had these before. They’re too rich.

Prabhupada: So give him something poor, \[laughter\]

The Daily Mail reporter came the next day. He was an older man, respectful. He asked a few mundane questions (“Are you a rich man, sir?”), but quickly sensed there was a more important story here. He asked, “What is the message, in fact, of Krishna?” and “What does ‘Hare Krishna’ mean?” The reporter warmed to Prabhupada, became interested on a personal level: “Have you any older members, like me?” and “Do you enjoy life, sir?”

Prabhupada: What do you think?

Reporter: I think yes, I think yes.

Prabhupada: Now give him some prasada, this sweet-ball.

Reporter: Thanks very much!

That night Srila Prabhupada’s old and dear friend Allen Ginsberg appears at the Manor. He’s in the UK for the Poetry International Festival in London and is on his way up to Glasgow to read poetry for the Scottish Arts Council in Blythswood Square. Allen leads a lively kirtan in Prabhupada’s room, and then he and the Swami talk and laugh deep into the night. I’m reminded again how much this gentle, brilliant man has influenced my entire generation—and how much he has helped Bhaktivedanta Swami spread Krishna consciousness all over the world.

Prabhupada will leave England on August 9, in two days, for a week in Paris. There’s much to do to get ready for the Manor Deity installation and festival, which will begin on August 21, and though we re unhappy to see him leave, Prabhupada’s absence will give us that much more energy to dive into the massive preparations that face us. I’m caught up in all that’s happening here—the prime instigator, if you will—and happy to see Prabhupada so happy; but at the same time a deep sadness is growing in me, made prominent by Prabhupada’s departure for Paris. I sense, somehow, that these will be our last great days together. I’m way out on a limb with this ruby business, and I know it’ll take my full-time energy to make it succeed. Prabhupada constantly reminds me that I’ve borrowed money from the Society to pay for some of my goods and that it must be repaid. At the same time, I’m the GBC man for the UK, and I’m supposed to manage the properties, activities, and spiritual well-being of hundreds of British devotees. Prabhupada is our living example of someone who can perfectly perform a thousand things at once, but he is a self-realized soul, and my spiritual stature is miniscule by comparison. Just to find time for everything we’re doing here now I’ve neglected my personal sadhanas; I’ve practically stopped chanting and attending temple services and reading scripture, relying instead on a few one-on-one moments each day with Prabhupada to carry me through.

And after nearly three years on the road, I at last have a semblance of a home life. Malati and I live together in the cottage, and the only thing missing is our daughter. On an impulse I buy a one-way plane ticket for Saraswati Speerstra and send a telegram to her keepers at the gurukula in Dallas to please put her on the next available flight to London. There, that’s done. Saraswati is barely five years old, and she’s been separated from her parents for nearly seven months now. Am I being sentimental? Attached? Am I trying to relieve my guilt for having so abruptly dropped her from my life? I want to again see the joy that lights her face when she discovers something new, maybe through her to revive my own sense of magic in Krishna’s creation. Just sending the ticket lifts my spirits.

And this thing about “transcendental competition” with my godbrothers. Prabhupada has encouraged it, and I have responded, trying to outdo all others in pleasing our spiritual master. But this whole attitude has worn me out. Some of my godbrothers are deeply envious, and I’m just not good at dirty tricks, gossip, and conspiracies. I thrive on signs, omens, coincidence, Krishna’s magic, to guide my decisions—like seeing sunlight reflected from a passing car—but this kind of guidance is dimming. I miss playing with Saraswati. How will all this work out? Will this story have a happy ending?

I drowned these depressing thoughts in activity. First we had to build an altar and vyasasan in the temple compatible with the existing formal, oak-paneled decor at the Manor. We wanted Radha and Krishna and Srila Prabhupada to feel at home in the glory and tradition of royal England. We found some photos of the queen’s throne at the House of Lords—the one that had so impressed me when Gurudas and I had been there four years ago—surrounded by an elegant, neogothic canopy and facade, everything gilded in gold. This became our model.

Svayambhur was a rangy, amiable Aussie who’d fallen in love with Krishna while touring Britain two years back. He had a blond sikha and eyebrows, and his face, flushed red with health and devotion, always wore a toothy smile. He was a good carpenter, had worked on a couple of rath cars, and so was now charged with transforming the orangerie into a Deity room and building the altar. The south wall of the temple room had been removed and roof supports installed. The altar would be centered in the 24 x 40-foot orangerie around the fixed dimension of three 2 x 7-foot rose marble slabs, leaving space around the altar for devotees to circumambulate the Deities, in the Indian tradition. The marble pieces would rise like steps, with Radha and Krishna standing on the top tier, five feet above the black-and-white-checkered tile floor. There was plenty of wood left over from Ratha-yatra to build stout supports beneath the marble. The flat facade behind the Deities would be formed from thin plywood cut and overlaid in panels, windows, and silhouettes resembling a Moorish castle, rising sixteen feet into the arched skylight overhead. A turreted canopy would project from the facade over the Deities’ heads, much like the queen’s stall in the House of Lords. Facade and canopy would be entirely gilded with gold paint.

But building Prabhupada’s vyasasan, his royal throne of instruction, was another problem altogether. The logical site for the vyasasan was an 11 x 7-foot alcove, nine feet high, on the north wall of the temple room, facing the altar. But this space was currently occupied by the estate’s principal fireplace. Svayambhur, Mahavishnu, Sakshigopal and I gathered before the alcove and studied the situation. Here, the lord of the manor had once relaxed on a velvet settee before a roaring log fire, puffing his favorite pipe while branches scraped the roof on cold stormy nights and scullery maids padded down a hall somewhere. Now the place resounds with Krishna’s names, the excited voices of a hundred barefoot devotes running up and down the stairs. Fireplace? Why do we need a fireplace? We’ll have to cover it anyway if we’re going to put Prabhupada’s vyasasan in front of it, so why not just tear it out? The fireplace was a magnificent structure, surrounded by carved oak boiserie: fluted columns on each side, sculpted corbels supporting an ornate lintel and mantelpiece. Look, those parts will be perfect for building Prabhupada’s throne. We only have ten days to build this thing, and we have no money, so this fireplace has to go.

With Prabhupada in France, we got going with our careful destruction. With hammers, crowbars, and mindless enthusiasm, we attacked this Victorian masterpiece and, within hours, the dismantled pieces lay scattered around us covered in plaster dust. Studying our photo of the queen’s throne, we sorted them: OK, these will make two tall legs to support the back, but we need legs for the front, and something for the sides. We scurried to the other fireplaces, smaller but equally ornate, one in the reception room and another upstairs, and demolished these as well. The bannister on the back stairs between the first and second floors was taken down. In this mood of creative destruction we scoured the house to salvage more parts—a finial here, some balusters there—and within a couple of days we had a pile of well-wrought materials for Prabhupada’s amazing throne.

While Svayambhur and his helpers worked on the altar, Mahavishnu, Sakshigopal, and I assembled the throne. Day and night we hammered, sawed, chiseled, nailed, and glued with intense concentration as the thing took shape. Stout, fluted columns formed the four legs. On the crest rail topping the triangular pediment at the back, we mounted eight ascending crocketed finials, four on each side, which would have the appearance of lightning bolts, or chakras, above Srila Prabhupada’s head. The apex was a globe finial, an amalaka, symbolizing the king’s power over his domain.

Meanwhile, Mukunda had arrived in New York City and cleared the ruby hoard through customs. By phone I’d advised him to remove the dirt and grease we’d used to disguise the value of the stones, and soak them in clear mineral oil to enhance their red color. August was the worst time of year to sell anything in New York; most of the gem dealers were on vacation. After a few days Mukunda had sold maybe two hundred kilos of rough ruby and collected a few thousand dollars. He wired the money to us in the UK and, just in the nick of time we were able to pay some Manor construction bills. Mukunda had now pretty much flooded the New York market, so he dispatched the remaining nine hundred plus kilos of rubies by sea freight to London—we’d deal with them later—and he flew back to the UK.

**\* \* \***

Prabhupada returned to the Manor from Paris on August 15, 1973. His laughter and praises as he inspected our work made us near faint with pleasure, and we doubled down to finish everything by August 21, now just a few days away. Prabhupada continued his morning Bhagavad-gita lectures from the billiards room, but there was no time now for room conversations. I had been in these tight-deadline situations before, and I knew how much spectacular results pleased my spiritual master, so I urged the devotees to just work, work, work—don’t think about eating, sleeping, chanting rounds, or anything else until we’ve created the perfect place for Radha and Krishna to live. One of the temple leaders complained.

Srutakirti: Srila Prabhupada was silent for a moment, and then replied, “Shyamasundar, he is very busy. Arjuna, when he was fighting the battle of Kurukshetra, he did not chant his sixteen rounds. When you are fighting a battle, where is the time to chant your rounds? Anyway, you should see it like that. As long as he is in charge, you should follow, and encourage him to chant if he is not chanting.”

But there was so much to do! Teams were assigned to tackle each project. Some money was coming in to our central treasury, but for the most part, devotee teams also had to raise the cash for their individual projects. I made a list:

(1) Deities

(2) Cows and peacocks

(3) Outdoor program—stage, lights, sound, and publicity

(4) Temple room—paintings, lights, decorations.

(5)    Altar

(6)    Vyasasan

(1)    Deities: At the November 1971 pandaliin Connaught Circus, New Delhi, Prabhupada had installed a beautiful set of four-foot Radha and Krishna Deities. They’d been carved in Jaipur from pure white makrana marble. After the program, these Deities had been shipped to England along with seven elegant outfits, clothing, and jewelry. We’d carefully stored Radha and Krishna in the pujari room at Bury Place; They’d been in Britain for more than a year now, waiting for us to find Them a place to live.

Malati: I picked Them up from Heathrow airport. We had this Russian Lada car, very small. I was worried that if customs opened the wooden cases with a crowbar, \[the Deities\] might be broken. The officer said, “All right, now, what’s this?” I said, “This is the Supreme Personality of Godhead and His eternal consort,” and they didn’t flinch—they’re British!—and they let Them go, unopened. Customs men even helped me load those two big cases into that little car, which wasn’t easy.

Malati had been cooking for Srila Prabhupada all these weeks, and she was her usual busy self. We crossed paths now and then, but we were each on our separate journeys. Malati and Balagopala were supervising the preparation of Radha and Krishna for the big day, with help from Gangamayi, Sarvamangala, and a few other girls. They had set up a sewing room just down the hall from Prabhupada’s room, and lights burned in there all through the night.

(2)    Cows: Some devotees were out looking for cows and peacocks. Prabhupada wanted cows, and we had advertised cows in our press release. We should have cows. Bhaja Hari found a milk cow for sale in Letchmore Heath, and Partha went to some Indian supporters and raised the cash. A few days before the festival, an Ayrshire cow with brown and white patches was herded through the village and settled into our horse stables next to my ruby polishing operations. Her name was Pradyumni.

(3)    Outdoor program: This huge responsibility was assigned to Jai Hari and Mukunda, and they were doing a great job with it. Stevie Winwood recommended the place where his band, Traffic, rented their equipment, so the three of us drove down to Maurice Plaquet’s music shop in Shepherds Bush to select the biggest outdoor sound system we could possibly afford. We went to Earth Star Structures for the stage and lights, and contracted G. Pugh Builders to transport, construct, and dismantle the stage. Everything would be delivered to the Manor on August 21 and removed on August 28.

Jai Hari has been trying to get Badfinger to play at the Manor on August 24, Young People’s Day. Badfinger recorded on the Apple label and had enjoyed a recent string of top ten songs. Their biggest hit was “Day After Day,” produced by George Harrison.

We also needed half a dozen fancy chairs to set on or in front of the stage for special guests, and there wasn’t a proper chair in the house. Ringo had purchased the Tittenhurst estate from John Lennon in August 1971, and on a previous visit to his house in Ascot I’d seen a set of six elegant matched chairs, perfect for our purpose. I phoned Ringo’s wife, Maureen, and she said, “Sure, you can borrow them. Come on out.” I’d need a devotee to help me, but taking anyone to meet George or any of the Beatles was always a risky business. I had to be careful. Everyone has an ego, and someone saying one wrong thing or making the slightest error could cause big problems. Mukunda, Jai Hari, and Dhananjaya were all busy with other matters, so I asked Pradyumna—the personification of deep humility—to accompany me to Tittenhurst in the temple van.

Pradyumna: Yeah, Ringo had about fifty pinball machines in his living room—looked like an arcade in there. He was busy playing Fireball while he talked to us—lots ofclacking and bells going off—but he wished us all success and said he might come out to the Manor soon.

(4)    Temple room—paintings, lights, decorations: The flautist from the Quintessence band, a boy named Gopala, had painted a nice life-sized portrait of Srila Prabhupada, and Saradiya dasi had done a painting of the Pancha-tattva. These and some recently arrived canvases from New York were being framed and hung above the wainscoting on the temple room walls. For our gothic theme we needed stained glass windows, so Sakshigopal and Parameshwari painted the six-foot mullioned mock Tudor windows on three sides of the altar room in transparent rainbow colors, dark blue at the bottom and rising up through the spectrum—purple, red, orange to yellow, light green, then clear glass on top. The girls then hung floor-length white curtains over the windows.

Prabhupada recommended chandeliers. We needed fine chandeliers to illuminate our royal motif, so on August 18 I drove to London and shelled out £1200 for four gorgeous, Edwardian crystal basket chandeliers, in the empire style, praying the check wouldn’t bounce. They were delivered the next day, and we hung three from the ceiling in the temple room and one above the slowly-rising altar.

(5)    Altar: Svayambhur and his team used columns, corbels, and lintels scavenged from fireplaces to frame a nice, three-stage altar. They built six tiered steps on the right side of the altar, covered in gold satin cloth, so the pujaris could access the Deities. The elaborate floor-to-ceiling facade, with its gabled canopy, gradually rose into place. The workers used a jigsaw to cut four five-lobed Moorish windows into the design, with five intricate arches at the bottom edge of the canopy below filigreed spandrels. A few of us went over to Friar Park in the temple van to fetch the three pink marble slabs George had donated.

(6)    Vyasasan: We centered the vyasasan on a platform two feet high, which extended six feet out into the temple room in a pentagon shape. Two wide stairs on eight-inch risers rose up to the platform, leaving space before the throne for devotees to bow prostrate before their spiritual master. Platform and stairs were upholstered in crimson fabric. For the back of Prabhupada’s throne I carved three, round, wooden cartouches—rondels—and encased them in the pediment. The central rondel was sixteen inches in diameter, a convex, many-petaled lotus flower floating atop its own concave reflection, framed on each side by a smaller, sixteen-rayed disc, or chakra.

Padmalochana upholstered the throne in crimson velvet, sewing four gold, satin lotuses to the velvet at Prabhupada’s back. The girls created a fancy sapphire-blue cushion, two burgundy velvet bolsters, and a footstool. I didn’t sleep for three days as bits of pilfered wainscoting, molding, and cornice found their way to the front of the throne and, at the last moment, on deadline night, Monday, August 20, we sprayed gold paint onto everything. We hoped it would be dry by morning.

It was Krishna’s birthday, Janmastami day, and the skies were lighting as I staggered to my cottage dizzy with noxious fumes from the paint, and passed out, unwashed, on my bed. In what was becoming habitual fashion—working myself to exhaustion then missing the main event—I slept through most of the Deity installation. I later heard that when Prabhupada approached his new vyasasan that morning, noting the almost Tibetan tantric flames striking up from the back of the throne, he said, “Oh, is this for Lord Shiva to sit?” Then he sat and welcomed Radha and Krishna to Their new home in Hertfordshire:

So this Deity worship in the temple means worshiping Krishna, the Supreme Personality of Godhead. Krishna is everywhere. Just like the waves of sound. As soon as I produce one sound, within a second the sound goes round the earth seven times. So if a material thing has got so much power, just think how much power God has got. So Krishna is everywhere. Simply you have to catch Him. And He’s also ready for being caught. Yes. Therefore, He has very kindly accepted to assume a form which you can handle. We can see stone, we can see wood, we can see earth, we can see water, we can see color. Therefore Krishna comes before us just quite suitable for our vision. But He is Krishna. We never should think that here is a statue. No. You must always think that here is Krishna, personally. He has come here to accept your service. You can dress Krishna, you can offer Him food, you can chant His glory. If you want to catch Krishna, He comes forward ten times more than your desire. Therefore we have simply to receive Him.

I woke up, ran to the temple and arrived to stand at the back of the crowd of ecstatic devotees while Prabhupada performed the last graceful circles of an arati before Their Lordships, now named Sri Sri Radha-Gokulananda. Then Prabhupada beckoned us forward, and we swarmed in loud procession, chanting and dancing with him around and around the now proudly crowned proprietors of New Gokula, lustrous in Their pink and gold dresses.

That afternoon two lorries arrived bearing our pandal equipment, and we started to erect the stage on the west side of the front lawn, facing across open lawns to the lake. I paid £400 to Maurice Plaquet Ltd, £285 to Earth Star Structures, and £366.26 to G. Pugh Company, again hoping the checks wouldn’t bounce. I also wrote a check for £50 as an installment on the overdue Manor milk bill.

**\* \* \***

For most pious Indian people, Krishna’s birthday is the biggest holiday of the year, and widespread advertising brings more than a thousand guests to roam the grounds and halls at Bhaktivedanta Manor on this perfect, late-August day. Stage assembly is not complete by 7 p.m., so Prabhupada’s Janmastami speech, inaugurating the week-long festival, will be delivered in the temple room. Our chief guest tonight is acting Indian High Commissioner to Great Britain, Maharaja Krishna Rasgotra. Sri Rasgotra is a distinguished, fifty-year-old gentleman dressed in a tan Nehru jacket over white pants. He sits on the floor to the side of the vyasasan as Prabhupada speaks.

His Excellency, the High Commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, I thank you very much for your coming here and participating in this ceremony, Janmastami, advent of Krishna. Krishna says in Bhagavad-gita, janma karma cha me divyam, yo janati tattvatah. “One who knows the transcendental nature of My appearance and activities does not, on leaving the body, take his birth again in this material world, but attains My eternal abode.” The advent of Krishna, it is very important thing. Why? The aim of life is to achieve immortality. So Krishna advents to teach us this lesson, that you are immortal by nature. “As spirit soul you are part and parcel of Me. As I am immortal, so you are also immortal. Unnecessarily, you are trying to be happy in this material world.” So especially Indians are meant for para-upakara, doing well to others. Indians are not meant for exploiting others. So I shall specially request the Indians, especially His Excellency, kindly cooperate with this movement and try to make successful your life and others’ life. That is the mission of Krishna, advent of Krishna.

After the program, Mr. Rasgotra accompanied Bhaktivedanta Swami to his room, and they chatted for almost two hours. Afterward, Prabhupada wrote a letter to Tamai Krishna, in Bombay: “Please immediately send me the names and addresses of all the foreign devotees in India, with their passport numbers and nationality. I am just now speaking with His Excellency the High Commissioner Rasgotra Maharaja Krishin, and he has promised to help us with the devotees who are being harassed by the Indian authorities.”

After Mr. Rasgotra leaves, a few devotees remain behind in his room, and Prabhupada is in a joking mood, so I say, “Srila Prabhupada, you looked just like Lord Shiva today, sitting on your new throne.”

The sides of his smile begin to tremble. He chuckles, and softly we begin to chuckle with him. Then he laughs aloud, pointing at me, “You ...” but he can’t say more he is laughing so hard. Then he rolls back on his elbows, raises his face in the air and laughs until tears flow from his tight-shut eyes—and we join him, near-convulsing on the floor in helpless mirth.

“You, yes, you have made. It is like Lord Shiva—but that is the power of king, he should show power, and that the people will accept. Throne is very nice. English king, it is proper for me.”

Next morning the subject comes up again. On our early walk through the village I say, “So you don’t like your new throne, Srila Prabhupada? It is too much like Shiva?” Prabhupada stops, leans both hands on his cane, turns to me and just smiles, so big and so loving, so rare that four different cameras capture the moment.

**\* \* \***

Today is August 22, Prabhupada’s birthday, the day we celebrate the advent of our guru. Prabhupada conducts his first morning Bhagavad-gita lecture in the new temple room. “This throne is called vyasasan, sitting place of Vyasadeva. So one who is representative of Vyasadeva, he can sit on this throne. So guru, by parampara system, guru is seated on the vyasasan because he is the representative. Just like in the high court, the bench is to be used by the head of the executive power, the king or the president.”

He describes the past wealth of India—that formerly, the palaces and temples of India were filled with jewels. “Now all these jewels have been taken away when British government was there, and they are now protected in the British Museum, so far I have heard.” But now the tide has turned. “Formerly this Manor was known as Piggot’s Manor. Now we have named it Bhaktivedanta Manor. What is the difference? Formerly, it was for sense gratification; now it is meant for elevating one to the spiritual standard of life.”

Prabhupada is seventy-seven years old today, though he says he’s seventy-eight because in India they count the nine months in the mother’s womb. During the afternoon guru-puja ceremony he sits atop his royal throne while devotees, one by one, approach and bow and offer him flower petals, sprinkling them on his lap and feet. After nearly a hundred devotees have made their offerings, Prabhupada addresses them in an intimate voice: “So my dear sons and daughters, I am so much obliged to you that you have become so enthusiastic for offering Vyasa-puja.”

Then Prabhupada praises his own guru and all the gurus who came before him, and then he gets personal: “I am very much indebted to my beloved disciple Pradyumna. He edits and he works very hard, and not only that, but his wife also helps in this connection. Here is Bhagavan dasa, he’s also married man with children.... Or you go from village to village preaching the message of Krishna, as our Sriman Rebatinandan Maharaja is doing. Or you install Deity at home, as our Ksirodaksayee Prabhu is doing. Anyone who is connected with our movement, he’s not ordinary living being. Actually, he’s liberated soul. You, all my disciples, everyone should become spiritual master. It is not difficult. Just follow the principles and speak to the general public. Then you become Krishna’s favorite. Na cha tasman manushyeshu kaschin me priya-krittamah. ‘One who is doing this humble service of preaching work, Krishna consciousness, nobody is dearer to Me than him.’ So if you want to become recognized by Krishna very quickly, you take up this process of becoming spiritual master, present to others the Bhagavad-gita as it is, and your life is perfect.”

After Prabhupada’s talk the devotees offer him a marzipan cake, fancifully shaped like temples in Vrindaban. He saves a piece of it for later and eats it in his room with great relish. As twilight gathers over the copses and meadows of Hertfordshire, Prabhupada watches from his windows as hundreds of lights ignite in the gardens and trees around the stage below—perhaps inwardly smiling at our fantasies to please him. I’ve sensed that Prabhupada doesn’t really care for this outdoor pandal that much—it’s cold in the evening, there’s a lot of audio reverb off the trees—a very informal setting for such high talks. But his devotees have made elaborate arrangements, and he doesn’t disappoint. Hundreds of devotees, friends, and neighbors mingle on the lawn on this Vyasa-puja evening, as Prabhupada walks across the lawn, mounts the stage, and delivers his ultimate definition of a guru:

Ladies and gentlemen, I know for most of you this is your first appearance here at Bhaktivedanta Manor. I’d like to extend this welcome to you .... So guru’s business is to bring his disciples from darkness to light. That is guru’s business. Every human being is suffering in this material world. So guru’s business is to take the torchlight of knowledge into the darkness of ignorance and relieve him from suffering... Hundreds and thousands of acharyas have come and gone, but their message is one. Guru cannot be two. Guru is one. Real guru will not say that “In my opinion, you should do like this.” Guru has no “own opinion.” No. This is simple definition: guru cannot change any word of his predecessor...

Who needs a guru? An ordinary man doesn’t require a guru. Like you keep dog as a fashion, don’t keep guru. One who is very serious to understand spiritual life, he requires a guru. Guru is a question of necessity. When we are very much serious to understand spiritual life, then we require a guru. First you find out a bona fide guru, then you establish your relationship with guru, and act accordingly....So guru’s task is very great responsibility. He has to guide the disciple how to make him quite eligible candidate to get the perfect position, immortality, and guide him back home, back to Godhead.

August 23 was our Ecumenical Day. Malcolm Muggeridge, scheduled as our chief guest, called and canceled at the last minute, but every other priest, minister, vicar, friar, rabbi, or monk we could find was assembled on the lawn for Prabhupada’s evening lecture: “What Is Religion?”

He defines religion as dharma, the constitutional occupation of the living entity: “Just like this microphone. Sound production is the dharma, the religion, the natural characteristic, of this microphone... If you think that by your principle you have understood God and you have learned how to love God and you have learned how to obey God, that dharma, that religious system is perfect. It may go on under any name—it doesn’t matter. Just like if you pass your MA examination, it doesn’t matter whether you pass it from London University or Calcutta University or Berlin University. You have passed your examination..... So dharma means first-class dharma, which teaches the followers how to know God and how to love Him.”

But the next day, Friday, August 24, was the date I’d been anticipating most: Young People’s Day—rock-n-roll day! Posters and BBC radio announcements have spread the word that Badfinger will perform at the Manor tonight, and we hope for a large crowd.

To kick this day of rock and roll off, Donovan, the famous poet-troubadour sometimes called the “Bob Dylan of England,” and well-known for records like Catch the Wind, Sunshine Superman, and Mellow Yellow, arrives at the Manor in the afternoon to meet Srila Prabhupada. Donovan had been to Rishikesh with the Beatles in February 1968 to hang out with Maharishi, and he is George’s good friend, a fellow spiritual aspirant. I haven’t seen Donovan since our little party with him at Stowe School in May 1970, but George has been talking with him about Krishna and last Christmas had given him a copy of Prabhupada’s Bhagavadgita As It Is. Today, Donovan is dressed in a blousy paisley shirt, and he’s brought with him his wife, Linda, and Gypsy Dave and his girlfriend. Gypsy Dave (Gyp Mills) is Donovan’s tour manager and right-hand man.

Donovan sits forward, right in front of Srila Prabhupada’s desk, his open face hungry for knowledge. His friends sit apart, behind him, kind of surly, as if tolerating Donovan’s interest but not really wanting to be there. Both women kneel in their tight miniskirts, clearly uncomfortable, shifting, legs tucked under or to off to one side.

Prabhupada starts right in with, “So, what is your philosophy?”

Donovan isn’t quite sure how to answer, and he says something about Maharishi, breathing, and mantras.

Prabhupada: Eh? You do not know?

Donovan: No, I know as a musician I am a student of spiritual sound.

\[long awkward silence\]

Prabhupada \[gesturing to Linda\]: So you don’t feel cold by this dress? \[everyone laughs\]

Donovan: What would be the situation of a young man who has already been initiated by another guru, and now he’s doing something else?

Prabhupada: Other guru... Whether he is guru or not, first of all you have to see.

Donovan: Hmm.

Prabhupada: So you can test. Just like if you are eating, you can understand how much you are getting strength, how much you are satisfied. You haven’t got to ask anybody. Similarly, anyone can see whether I am increasing my attachment for dog or whether I am increasing my attachment for God. That’s all. This is test.

\[long awkward silence\]

Prabhupada: Yad-uttamashloka-gunanuvarnanam. So God is to be described in choicest poetry. Now, you are poet, singer of songs, famous, you can take it up like your friend George. He has sung so nicely about Krishna. We can give you many themes ... Bhaktih pareshanubhavo viraktir anyatra syat. That is bhakti. Bhakti means God realization. Bhakti means you become mad after Krishna. Everything I may have—my thoughts, my words, my money, everything—to become mad after God.

Linda: But money is material!

Prabhupada: What do you know what is material and spiritual? \[turning to Donovan\] Do you understand?

Donovan: Well, I, uh, I am very thick-headed ... but I’m trying.

Visibly upset, Linda leans forward and whispers in Donovan’s ear, and the four visitors stand up as if on signal. Donovan somewhat reluctantly says, “I guess we have to go now, master.” They leave, and Prabhupada comments that Donovan’s wife is worried that if Krishna gets him, she will lose him.

Meanwhile, that morning, two more cows have arrived in a lorry, and our little herd is growing. We’ve just about maxed out the space we have for cows, though. Until our lawyers can abrogate the

1948 grazing lease with nearby Battlers Green Farm, most of our pasture is occupied by the neighbors’ cows.

**\* \* \***

“Hare Krishna!” says Pete Ham, lead singer and guitarist for Badfinger, as he breezes into the Manor parking lot and climbs out of his van. I haven’t seen Pete—with his trademark long mane of burnished auburn hair—since the Bangla Desh concert two years back, when he and George played that sizzling acoustic guitar duet during “Here Comes the Sun.” “Tom’s here, and Mike,” he continues as two of his bandmates jump out and start unloading drums and guitars, “but Joey’s not gonna make it tonight.”

From the stage it must have looked like a cozy English summer evening lawn party. A dozen elegant, small round tables covered in white cloth—and jeez, candelabras—and matching wrought-iron chairs. We’ve rented fancy crockery, linen napkins, champagne glasses, and devotees in white aprons and black vests scurry from table to table: “A glass o’ the bubbly, then, sir? Will that be red or white?” as they pour from bottles of expensive sparkling grape juice.

We’ve hired the best sound system available, and refrigeratorsized speakers line the stage. When the band does a sound check I think, “Uh oh, this may be too loud, things could get out of hand.” As Badfmger hits the E-A-B riff on “Come and Get It,” I imagine the teacups rattling in Mrs. Leary’s cottage down the road. When the band sweeps into their big hit “Day After Day,” I race over to the main house to monitor decibel levels as' Pete sings “Looking out from my lone-ly ro-o-om, day after day ...” and his slide guitar break shakes every window in the house. Bhaja Hari hustles up to me and says, “You gotta turn it down, man. Prabhupada says it’s too loud!” We reduce the volume as low as we can.

After half an hour of pristine rock-n-roll I thank Pete and the boys and ask them to join us as Rebatinandan and other devotees mount the stage and begin kirtan. I keep glancing up to

Prabhupada’s room to see his glowing silhouette, wondering what he thinks of our outrageous efforts to spread Krishna’s name and fame. Hey, it’s happening. We’ll deal with the fallout later—but right now it feels pretty good to hear the Hare Krishna mantra broadcast for miles in every direction.

Ringo couldn’t make it tonight, but Maureen Starr sits with me for a while at a table. We click fluted glasses, she wishes me “Salut!” and I wish her “Hare Krishna!” After the show, she meets and chats with Srila Prabhupada for some minutes in his room, and then I escort her to the Manor’s back door. She puts on her platinum stilettos and takes a few steps backward, pranaming and saying what a pleasure this was—and her heel catches in the perforated rubber doormat and she falls to the pavement, badly spraining her ankle. We wrap it in an icepack and tape, and she insists she’s all right. We help her to her car, and she drives off into the night, but I’m deeply worried until she calls later to say she made it back to Tittenhurst okay.

I sit around with Srila Prabhupada late that night and go over the day’s events. He reminds me of the money I borrowed from the Society to buy rubies.

Prabhupada: Now, how much you have got in your ruby business account?

Shyamasundar: Well, I’ve just paid the festival expenses and most of the milk bill, so maybe there’s about £1600 left.

Prabhupada: Karandhar and others are objecting that you have borrowed. The householder should give at least fifty percent of his income to Krishna. So you just give me one check, and that will reduce your debt.

I write out Cheque #621275 on The Chartered Bank to “A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami” for a thousand pounds and lay it on his desk, laughing with relief. He’s laughing too, both of us off the hook for a while. I have no idea if more money will come any time soon, but that’s Krishna’s responsibility, and surely He will somehow provide.

Saturday, August 25 was scheduled as Businessmen’s Day, but all the chief guests we’d invited were unreachable or had called to cancel, so we decided, after last night’s epic rave, to take the day off. Mr. Toyota was probably busy building cars in Nagasaki, Baron Rothschild relaxing in Cannes, the Rockefellers at a regatta for the weekend ...

George Harrison phoned me in the morning, sorry he couldn’t be here for yesterday’s events. He’d been in meetings with his lawyers into the night, he said, very annoyed that he had to write a check to the British government for a million pounds, his tax on income from the Bangla Desh concert. “They’re buying a battleship with that money!” he said.

Best news of all: Saraswati arrived early this afternoon! I was so happy to see her I thought I’d burst. Srila Prabhupada was happy too, I could tell. Though he questions why I would remove her from school, he seems to tolerate my mundane fatherly attachment for his daughter and laughs and plays with her in his room, just like old times. He asks her what she’s learned at gurukula, and she recites for him some Sanskrit verses from Bhagavad-gita without too many mistakes.

I take Saraswati for a walk around the Manor grounds. She’s a big girl now, and she shakes off my hand when I try to help her through the brush, saying, “I know the way.” She’s reserved, maybe a little angry that I’d left her alone for so long. Her coolness gradually thaws as we walk through the trees. A quick game of hide-and-seek and we go limp with laughter when I introduce her to the ducks, saying, “This is Neville, and over there is Lucille, and this guy here is just plain Quack!” I see my own love of nature and laughter in this tiny being, and the strong determination of her mother. She’s chanting rounds in her little bead bag while a soft, unamplified kirtan floats from the stage and across the lawns like perfume through the gardens of New Gokula. We’re glad for the afternoon respite, because tomorrow’s program will be huge: Sunday, August 26, is Neighbors Day at Bhaktivedanta Manor.

Our neighbor Graham Hill came to see Prabhupada on the afternoon of the 26th. Graham was the world’s most successful Formula One race car driver, the only winner of the triple crown of motor racing (24-hour Le Mans, Indianapolis 500, Monaco Grand Prix), and he lived about a mile down Aldenham Road in Elstree, near the small civil airstrip where he could fly his Piper Aztec in and out to races on the continent. Graham was another of George’s friends, and with his mustache, wit, and pirate swagger, he reminded me of Errol Flynn. He appeared to be a mild-mannered country gentleman, but the steel look in his buccaneer eyes was forged by cold, raw courage. Graham brought his wife, Bette, and their three children to meet Srila Prabhupada. The discussion that followed could be called “The Race Against Death.”

Prabhupada: But death is compelling me to die. There is no science, no philosophy, no hero to conquer over this death. This is our philosophy: how to conquer over the race between life and death. Janma karma me divyam, yo janati tattvatah, tyaktva deham punar janma naiti mam eti kaunteya. This is Sanskrit language. What it says?

Shyamasundar: “One who knows the transcendental nature of My appearance and activities does not, upon leaving the body, take his birth again in this material world.”

Prabhupada: “Does not take his birth again.” Yes, this is the winning over death. Simply if you know what is Krishna, if you try to understand, then you conquer over death.

Graham: And you go back to the Godhead. You go back to home.

Prabhupada: Yes. The actual aim of life should be back to home, back to Godhead. Then this human form of life is successful. Just like your son. Now he is under father’s protection, he is very happy. But if he declares his independence? Just like Shyamasundar. He is very rich man’s son. His father—I met him—his father is a big lawyer, big businessman. But he declared independence. And I know his life history, how much he had to go through so many serious things. Similarly, we are also sons of God. We have declared independence, and we are going through so many chapters of life and death in different bodies. Suppose you have got now a nice Englishman’s body, but next body you do not know. That will depend on your karma and desire.

Graham: How do we attain our spiritual body?

Prabhupada: You must act now. Learn who is Krishna, become His devotee. Otherwise, there is no guarantee. Just like—don’t mind— when you drive your car, it is not a very good position, \[laughter\] Yes. But you are doing, taking that risk for winning over. But the position is not very good. At any moment there can be accident.

(And after surviving 179 Formula One racing starts, Graham Hill lost the race against death two years later while trying to land his Piper in fog at the Elstree airport. Daring Fate to the very end, his pilot’s license had expired and he was uninsured.)

The weather turned blustery that afternoon, so Prabhupada addressed our neighbors that evening from his throne in the temple room. “So we invite all ladies and gentlemen to come here and try to understand what I am, what you are. We are not this body; we are spirit soul. As spirit soul we are all eternal, blissful, and cognizant. This house has been very kindly given to us by George Harrison. He is a nice boy. He is taking to Krishna consciousness. So we shall simply request you to take advantage of this center. You haven’t got to pay anything. If you come here and take part with us, we hold classes. We are engaged twenty-four hours in this temple. We don’t say that first of all you qualify yourself, then come here. No. Any condition. Even a child can take part. And by taking part in this movement, automatically you feel some ecstasy, spiritual ecstasy. Then you begin to dance. We don’t ask you to dance, but the chanter automatically dances.”

August 27 is the last day of our festival, and we’d saved it for Intellectuals Day. But it was a Monday, and both David Wynne and

Sir Alister Hardy have phoned to say they can’t make it—and God knows where William Burroughs is. After a week of hectic activity, our interest to showcase Srila Prabhupada to large, outdoor audiences has waned anyway, and when severe thunderstorms threaten, we cancel the stage program and move it indoors. Prabhupada speaks in the temple room that evening from first verse of First Canto of Srimad-Bhagavatam. Next morning, the Pugh Company people arrive, and I feel a surge of relief as they remove the now rain-soaked plastic sheeting we’d laid over the stage, lights, and sound equipment and haul everything away.

The Hare Krishna Summer Festival is over. Srila Prabhupada has installed Radha-Gokulananda in Their permanent HQ in Great Britain. The pandal venue was a good opportunity to enjoy summer days and nights outside, meet the neighbors, and make a statement about what we’re doing here to them. We were the biggest landholders in Letchmore Heath. The Manor was now a mon-astery—not unusual to the old traditions of England—but ours was an evangelical mission and our boisterous proselytizing behavior was foreign to most of our neighbors. It was time to set ground rules for our overwhelming presence in the heart of this small provincial village.

Harry Edwards, the local constable, came to see Prabhupada in his room. Harry was a jolly man, strong when he had to be, but solicitous toward all sides, like an old-fashioned peacekeeper. He said to Prabhupada, “I think that the festival week has done a lot of good. There has been a lot of bad feeling because you had nearly a thousand people here on Tuesday, wasn’t it? The sound or the echo was going that way, toward the village. I didn’t hear anything. My inspector Mr. Turner didn’t hear anything. And we were quite satisfied. But as I say, you can please some people, and others you can never please. So with those people, I say, well you’ll just have to get on with it. That’s all there is to it. Right.”

We then talked about potential threats to Manor security, including the Gypsies, the tinkers—locally called Diddycoys—and

Harry said, “If you get any trouble with them, let me know, ’cause I’ll soon warn them off.”

Harry mentioned an incident that had occurred earlier in August, when Rebatinandan Swami had clocked a farmhand with a wooden fencepost in a dispute over the neighbors’ cows grazing in our pasture.

Shyamasundar: Well, in America, we’re used to shooting rustlers, you know. “String him to the nearest tree! Lynch him!”

Harry: Well, they wanted to shoot you, but I said, “Over cows? This is England, not America!”

Shyamasundar: This is England, after all, a civilized country.

Prabhupada: Yes.

Harry lives directly across the road from the Manor gates, and the conversation becomes hilarious.

Harry: You know what the villagers say, don’t you? “Harry Krishna on this side, and Harry Edwards on the other side.” \[laughter\]

Prabhupada: So good neighbors are chanting your name. So when somebody’s disturbed, he can say, “I’m calling Mr. Harry.” \[laughter\]

Harry: Oh, there you are, yes. They are chanting my name. When I ring up the station in the morning, they say, “Ah, Harry Krishna.” And I say: “Hare Krishna to you!” \[laughter\] You know, I must keep in liaison with you. So if I come over here, gentlemen, you must realize I am not here for the purpose of, say, spying, if you may think this. I don’t suppose you do?

Prabhupada: Certainly, Mr. Harry, when we are in difficulty, we must take shelter of you.

In the days following Janmastami, Srila Prabhupada initiated twenty-five new disciples. I was pleased to see how all the devotees had blossomed, how they’d risen to the occasion, met deadlines, and somehow pulled off this amazing celebration. We were all way over our heads in massive activity, completely dependent on Krishna’s magic. And miracles had happened— many, and every day—taking us to higher levels of conviction and spiritual animation. Most of all, I was happy to see Mukunda back in action. Just to again witness the excited look on his face as he raced from place to place, taking care of Krishna’s business: “Hey, look at this, Shyamsundar, I got a letter today from the Vatican, and the Pope has agreed to a private audience with Prabhupada!” We were a good fit—Mukunda’s caution and my extravagance—and we never seemed to disagree on anything. Nothing rattled Mukunda. His stiff upper lip or well-timed Lenny Bruce monologue carried us through times of very high tension. My best friend was back at my side, fighting the good fight. Under the guidance of our master again, together we chased rhino after impossible rhino.

**\* \* \***

We’d been trying to get Peter Sellers out to meet Srila Prabhupada. Earlier in the summer Peter had spent some time with devotees in Cancun, Mexico, but film commitments had prevented his coming to the Manor, and now he was about to dash off to Cyprus or someplace. Before he left, Peter called and said, “Here’s a man your master should really meet: Rubeigh Minney.” He gave us Rubeigh’s phone number in Manningtree, Essex, and on August 27 Mukunda, Jai Hari, and I met Minney for lunch at the Savage Club on Whitehall Place in downtown London. The Savage Club was an exclusive gentlemen’s club, a home away from home to British explorers and bohemian journalists “since 1857.”

Rubeigh James Minney. This distinguished, debonair Englishman in navy-blue blazer and yellow cravat led us past huge paintings of British redcoats with their Iroquois allies at the Battle of Fort Niagara, the blood and smoke of the Zulu wars, and Shackleton’s party at the South Pole, and sat us at an elegant table. Many of the men around us were dressed in tuxedos. I felt out of place in my dhoti and flip-flops. But gazing across the vast dining room I noticed a turbaned Sikh and some unshaved men in wrinkled khakis—correspondents fresh in from Cambodia?—so began to relax, charmed by the atmosphere and obvious elan of our host. It turned out that Rubeigh Minney had been born in Calcutta in 1895, only one year before Srila Prabhupada. He was a seasoned veteran from the golden age of early twentieth-century exploration, and he described his trip across Tibet on horseback, the first flight across India in a plane assembled from crated pieces, and his time with the Duke of Connaught (Queen Victoria’s third son) at the 1921 foundation-stone ceremony for Parliament House in New Delhi. We told him about Abhay Charan De, his fellow citizen of Calcutta, who had become Bhaktivedanta Swami, and who’d adventured to the West with no friends or money and was now spreading Bengali Vaishnava culture around the world.

Minney told us of his film career. He’d written and produced Clive of India for Darryl F. Zanuck in 1935, starring Ronald Coleman and Loretta Young. Wow! This was one of my father’s favorite movies! We told Rubeigh that Bhaktivedanta Swami had himself written more than a dozen books, and we gave him a copy of Bhagavad-gita As It Is. We told him that the Swami was now at his manor estate in Hertfordshire, recently gifted to him by George Harrison, “And we cordially invite you, sir, to come at your convenience.”

“Yes,” he agreed, “I must meet him!”

Minney said he would consult with his wife, Hetty, but it looked like they could come on the Friday, four days hence. This man was such an extraordinary character that I wanted to tell Srila Prabhupada more about him before they met. I consulted our much used British Who's Who, and this is what I discovered: Rubeigh James Minney had written more than thirty books, from adventure stories (Distant Drums, Midst Himalayan Mists) to romanceintrigue novels (How Vainly Men, A Woman of France) to best-selling biographies like Chaplin, The Immortal Tramp and Rasputin. For decades he had been editor of several of Britain’s most popular magazines and journals. He also produced blockbuster movies, like Madonna of the Seven Moons (1944), starring Stewart Granger, and The Wicked Lady (1945), with James Mason. He was one of the most prolific and well-regarded British men of letters, and Srila Prabhupada was really going to enjoy talking with this rare soul.

When Rubeigh and Hetty Minney entered Prabhupada’s room and sat in two hastily arranged chairs, they represented the epitome of British refined taste and manners. They were exceedingly polite, and as Mr. Minney began to describe his 1956 and 1972 journeys through China and Russia, Prabhupada showed interest in everything he said, punctuating Minney’s insights with lots of “Hmm’s.” The Minneys engaged Prabhupada’s strong intelligence, and he was relaxed in their gracious company, delighted by the con-gruities between them across time and place. They’d both stayed at the National Hotel in Moscow, had brushed elbows in the same Calcutta neighborhoods, and had several friends in common.

Prabhupada: My birthdate is 1896.

Minney: 1896? Well, I was born in 1895, so I am older than you. \[laughter\] And in Calcutta I was an assistant editor of The Englishman newspaper, and I disagreed with the policy of that paper, because that paper was for showing how the English should rule the Indians.

And I was against that. I said, “This is the country of the Indians. It is for us to restore the country to them.”

Minney knew Motilal Nehru, father of independent India’s first prime minister. He was friends with Motilal Ghosh, the outspoken advocate of Indian independence and editor of the Amrita Bazar Patrika, The Englishmans rival newspaper in Calcutta.

Prabhupada: Yes, their office was in Bagh Bazar. Motilal Ghosh’s grandson came here two days back.

Minney: Really?

They spoke of human suffering—of drought and hunger in India, and drunkenness in Russia—and Prabhupada proposed his ancient solution: “So the human form of life is especially meant for getting out of all these sufferings, provided he takes education, and acts accordingly. Then, after giving up this body, he does not accept any more material body, which is subjected to all these sufferings. He remains in a spiritual body and enters into the spiritual world and lives eternally, blissfully. This is our proposal. Hmm? What do you think?”

Hetty: So when you have had your suffering in various forms and through various lives, you then reach that—

Prabhupada: No, not that—not that because you have suffered so much, automatically you’ll go there. Not like that. You have to prepare yourself to go there. Unless you endeavor for going to Krishna, there is no possibility. Human life is meant for trying for that thing which he has not achieved by wandering up and down. So a living entity is wandering up and down, and in this way he is suffering. But by fortune if he gets in touch with a bona fide representative of Krishna, guru, by the mercy of Krishna, then he gets the seed of devotional service. And if he cultivates that devotional service, then he goes back. This is the path. Not that because one has suffered so much, therefore automatically he gets liberation. No, not like that.

Prabhupada always has a fresh angle on something I thought I’d heard a hundred times before. One of the underlying themes of hippiedom was “If we suffer enough, we’ll find the light.” The Swami says, no, suffering in and of itself does not lead to spiritual freedom; only when renunciation and sacrifice are practiced under the guidance of a proper guru can suffering be transformed into blissful, liberated activity.

The conversation between Prabhupada and the Minneys went on for more than two hours, late into the evening. As the Minneys reluctantly rose to leave, Prabhupada ended the evening with this advice: “Every enlightened gentleman, thoughtful man, they should study this philosophy and science scrutinizingly, understanding it. Just like you are an author: you can contribute to the people such knowledge, and it will be tremendous benefit for them. Otherwise, if we produce some fiction, it may be for some moment some so-called pleasure, but actually literature should be presented in which people can get relief from the sufferings of this material existence.”

**\* \* \***

During these two idyllic summer months with Srila Prabhupada, the rest of the world seems to fade away. In the quiet green English countryside Prabhupada has been insulated from the pressures of his expanding Society, and he’s more relaxed—and mentally lively— than I have ever seen him. ISKCON seems to be quite stable around the world; even the perennial hotspot, India, was mostly quiet. But as September approaches things are heating up there again, especially in Juhu. Prabhupada books a ticket to Bombay, and will leave London on September 15. On September 5 he will fly up to Stockholm to inaugurate his new ISKCON center there, returning to London on September 11 for the last big rhino of his summer with us in the UK: a gala launch party for Prabhupada’s books at one of London’s biggest bookstores.

On August 3 Srila Prabhupada had written a letter to Rameswara in L.A.: “There is no doubt about it, to distribute books is our most important activity. The temple is a place not for eating and sleeping, but as a base from which we send out our soldiers to fight with maya. Fight with maya means to drop thousands and millions of books into the lap of the conditioned souls. Just like during war time the Bombs are raining from the sky like anything.”

Distribution of Prabhupada’s books was the emerging priority for ISKCON devotees around the world. In the UK we were deeply in debt to The Bhaktivedanta Book Trust for books we’d taken on credit. Mukunda took the responsibility to increase our UK book sales and start paying off the BBT debt. He’d made friends with Tom Maschler, the CEO of Jonathan Cape, a major London publishing house with titles by Ernest Hemingway and James Joyce, as well as Ian Fleming’s James Bond novels, and Joseph Heller’s

Catch-22. With Tom’s connections, Mukunda and Jai Hari had secured Dillon’s Bookshop on Oxford Street for the afternoon of September 12. Macmillan Company would advertise the formal release of their newly published unabridged edition of Bhagavadgita As It Is. “Free Feast! Swami’s Autograph in Every Copy!” say the invites we sent to members of the press.

On the afternoon of September 3 George Harrison came over to the Manor, and Prabhupada said, “So, I am told you are reading Krsna book?”

George: Yes, I’m in the fourth chapter now, just where Kamsa is killing... I’ve had it here so many years, just looking at the pictures. I’d never really read it before—I wrote the foreword and I’d never even read it! \[laughter\] It’s just so amazing, the book.

Prabhupada: You have seen the Deity down in temple—our Radha-Gokulananda?

George: Oh, yes. And They are so beautiful. They look so great up there—and the pink marble is nice. They seem to be at home here. Everyone has done such a good job.

Prabhupada: Shyamasundar tells me you are going away to some place?

George: Portugal. I’ve rented a nice villa there, in the Algarve. It’s very quiet and sunny. I’m going to drive there and just, you know, chant, and read Krsna book for a few weeks, undisturbed. I’m leaving day after tomorrow, on the 5th.

Prabhupada: Oh? I am also leaving on 5th, to Sweden. And after, I am going to India.

George: Then I will bid you a fond farewell. I’m going to India this winter with Ravi—maybe I’ll see you again, down there ...

On September 5 Srila Prabhupada celebrates Radhastami, Radha’s birthday, at Bury Place, then catches his flight to Stockholm.

On September 6 we got notice that eighteen crates of “rough corundum” had arrived for “Int’l Society for Krishna” at London’s King George V docks on the River Thames. These were the goods Mukunda had shipped to the UK from New York City in mid-

August. At the docks in Woolwich we loaded the heavy metal crates into the temple van. As we ground our way forty miles back across London to the Manor, the van chassis groaned and wheel wells rubbed on tires as we turned corners, weighted down with more than two thousand pounds of rough rubies.

At the temple we stacked the crates in the steel-doored safe adjoining the prasada hall on the ground floor, nearly filling the room-sized vault that had once housed the Piggott family’s silver. I pocketed the key and tried to decide what to do with this huge quantity of ruby rough that I’d dared purchase with Krishna’s money. As mentioned, these low-grade goods were just not very saleable in Western markets, so I didn’t really need the rubies in the UK any longer. What to do with them?

Srila Prabhupada returned to the Manor on September 11, enlivened by his favorable reception in Sweden. The girls at the Manor cooked all night, and on the morning of September 12 we hauled Prabhupada’s old vyasasan from the billiards room—along with huge amounts of prasada—down to Dillon’s Booksellers on Oxford Street near Marble Arch. Under Jai Hari’s supervision we set everything up in a meeting room behind the shop’s retail showroom. We stacked three tables in a pyramid effect, covering the whole thing with white cloth. On top we placed copies of Macmillan’s new unabridged Bhagavad-gita As It Is, and below that an impressive display of eleven hardbound books by the prolific author, His Divine Grace A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami: six volumes of Srimad-Bhagavatam, Krsna book in two volumes, The Nectar of Devotion, Teachings of Lord Chaitanya, and Sri Isopanishad. A paperback trilogy of Krsna and other smaller books were placed around the edges. People in the book trade who attend such launchings are accustomed to seeing one new book by an author. This would certainly blow their minds.

By the time Srila Prabhupada arrives, the place is mobbed by reporters, publishing reps, and curious passersby. The mysterious Swami mounts his elevated throne and, adjusting the microphone, instructs his disciple to read aloud this verse from

Srimad-Bhagavatam: “On the other hand, that literature which is full of descriptions of the transcendental glories of the name, fame, forms, pastimes, etc., of the unlimited Supreme Lord is a different creation, full of transcendental words directed toward bringing about a revolution in the impious lives of this world’s misdirected civilization. Such transcendental literatures, even though imperfectly composed, are heard, sung, and accepted by purified men who are thoroughly honest.”

The room falls silent. Then Bhaktivedanta Swami speaks:

We have no information at the present time what is that spiritual world, but that is all stated in the Bhagavad-gita. Bhagavad-gita is the preliminary study, the A-B-C-D of spiritual life. Srimad-Bhagavatam is the graduate study. So everything is there. Unfortunately it was not distributed. So our Krishna consciousness movement has started to distribute this literature. We have already translated about twenty books. They are present before you. So our request to the publishers and booksellers is: Let this literature be distributed properly. People will be benefited. We have come to this bookseller’s office to request you... Of course, we have no means to advertise very much, but our advertisement is the sankirtan movement. We go from city to city, street to street, to invoke spiritual consciousness. If I beat on a small drum, maybe people can hear from a few yards. But books are big drums, they can be heard from country to country, from continent to continent. So our only appeal is that all booksellers, all publishers, may come forward and cooperate with us and distribute the transcendental literature in this unique form. They will find something sublime, and we’ll be benefited. Thank you very much. Hare Krishna.

After Prabhupada’s presentation reporters gathered around to interview His Divine Grace.

Reporter: And what will it profit us if we get to know about Krishna?

Prabhupada: The profit means you’ll be saved from the material existence. Material existence means repetition of birth, death, old age, and disease.

Reporter: Is Krishna consciousness genuine?

Prabhupada: Do you think we are simply wasting our time? We are so fools? Why do you ask this nonsense question, “genuine”? Unless it is genuine, why you are working so hard?

After the reporters left, Prabhupada chatted with Richard Francis Gombrich, an eminent Oxford professor of Sanskrit and Theravada Buddhism. A seven-course feast was dished out to everyone, something quite beyond the sherry and canapes usually served at a book launch.

Two nights later, on the eve of his departure for India, other devotees left his room and I was alone with Srila Prabhupada. I was swept by a mixture of feelings—sadness that our personal time was drawing to a close, but a little relieved, too, that I wouldn’t have to work so hard while he was away.... His presence was always so intense. I was also excited by recent auspicious beginnings but stressed by what the future might hold.

Prabhupada studied my soul and said, “And where is our Saraswati tonight?”

“She’s over at the cottage, chanting her rounds.”

“Oh, she is finishing now sixteen rounds?”

“Six. She chants six rounds a day... She is very happy to be with her mother again.”

“No, you are happy, \[laughter\] And your gems business, what is the future?”

I want to buy you a satellite, Srila Prabhupada, to circle the planet with your message. I want to line boulevards of major cities with billboards, buy a jet for you, my jagat-guru, build you marble temples on mountain peaks where lapis-lazuli Krishna and diamond-starred Radharani will greet a million pilgrims ... But I said, “Oh, Srila Prabhupada, I don’t know. I’m trying. I’ll go back to India soon and try to find the better quality of ruby from deeper in the mine. I just hope this all works out.”

“You just remember one thing—you just keep your temperature high. That is explained: tejiyasam na doshaya. That means if any way one comes in contact with Krishna he becomes purified, even though he’s sinful. Just like sun. Sun is never infected. Rather, the infected area becomes sterilized by the sunshine. This is the process. One who is very powerful, he has no fault. He can make mistake, but he can do nothing horrible.... So how your temperature is going on?”

“Everything is getting very nicely improved, Prabhupada, but, well, now you are leaving, the flames are burning a little low right now.”

“So this regulated life, holding class, chanting, that will keep us from falling down. It is essential to follow the regulative principles. You can do anything, but if you neglect that, then it is very risky. In the material world, if you have got money, so many bad associates will come and help you to squander your money and spoil your life. Therefore harishye tad dhanam shanaih. Sometimes Krishna takes away the money, makes him poor so that everyone will neglect him, and because he’s Krishna conscious, in the end he’ll take Krishna, ‘Sir, I have no other alternative. Please give me protection.’ That is Krishna’s special favor, that ‘This man wanted Me. Now he’s going astray. All right, I shall take everything away.’ That was my case also. My Guru Maharaja ordered me that ‘You do this,’ but I thought that ‘Let me become first of all rich man. Then I shall do.’”

“Maybe that is my case also!” \[laughing\]

“In the beginning I was thinking, ‘Why shall I beg? Let me earn money and then start Krishna consciousness.’ But that never happened. \[laughing\] Some astrologer told me that I should have been a man like a Birla. And I got all those chances in the chemical line to become rich man.”

“What happened? Why your business didn’t—?”

“Others became envious. They wanted to put some conditions.

But I did not accept. I wanted under my conditions. I was puffed up that ‘I am such a big—oh, everyone wants me.’ Something happened and everything was finished. I was obliged to take the dress of beggar and preach instead of earning money. Now something has happened wonderful. Now I am bigger than Birla, \[laughter\] That is my practical experience. And now I’m seeing that it is Krishna’s so much favor... You see?”

“I’m beginning to see.”

“Now our attempt to construct this temple will be recorded by Krishna. Yes. ‘Because he has spent his energy for My service.’ That will be our gain. Even if he has fallen down halfway, still there is no wrong. He has gained something. That much service which he has already given to Krishna, that is recorded. That is to his credit. Yatra abhadram abhud amusya kirn.”

\[long pause\]

“Thank you, Srila Prabhupada, for being so kind to me all these years, tolerating my stupidity ...” And tears pour from my eyes, I cannot speak. Finally, I raise my eyes to gaze across eons into the deep shining pools of his infinite mercy.

“Never mind, Shyamasundar, you have done something wonderful here. I will never forget you.”

**\* \* \***

Prabhupada leaves for India, the Swiss computer watch still dangling from his wrist. This past nine weeks has been the highest point, the perfection, of my years with Srila Prabhupada. Dancing with Prabhupada from Marble Arch to Trafalgar Square; relaxing with the lord of the Manor on his English estate; delighting in his conversations with George Harrison, Arnold Toynbee, and Rubeigh Minney; watching our Vedic king expound from that outrageous throne ... It just couldn’t have been any better.

But now it is time to knuckle down, take hold of loose ends and try to tie them back together.

My energies are divided. Jack of all trades and master of none, that’s me. Between spiritual practices like Deity worship and chanting and material stuff like taking care of my family and managing people, business, and money, I see I’m weak in all departments, stretched too thin. As the GBC I’m responsible for quickly expanding British ISKCON’s affairs. I’ve never really wanted to do that, be so responsible. I’m just not a good people manager. I’d rather do big projects on my own or with a few trusted friends. Reluctantly, I have carried on, and maybe this will turn out well, by Krishna’s grace. The number of UK devotees has increased, and they are now well organized in their activities. Inspired by Prabhupada’s visit, some have rushed off to plant Krishna’s flag in new lands. The Manor is in good hands with Mukunda and quickly maturing Bhaja Hari, Jai Hari, Janananda, Rohininandan, and others. The Deities are well looked after by Malati and the girls.

Malati: Prabhupada instructed me that “Krishna has come. Now you have to look after Him. There should be one bank account, and that money will only be used for Their maintenance and care.” Still, there was no money at the Manor, so we went to the pubs, at night. It was so raucous in those pubs that you couldn't really talk, so we'd hold out these little cans and yell, “Charity, for the Blue Boy at the Manor!”

With Malati and Mukunda here to supervise, the British devotees are strong enough to manage everything without me. I’m way over my head with the ruby business. Let me focus on that now, get out of the hole I’ve dug for myself and those around me.

What I’ve learned so far about my ruby business is that there’s just not enough market value in the near-surface rubies we’ve mined to date. Gem-crystal transparency seems to improve the deeper we go into the mine. So I make a plan: geologically, the rubies have erupted from a central subterranean chimney, so we’ll start mining there, go vertical, as deep as possible, where the crystals cooled slower and under more pressure when they formed out of plastic solutions millions of years ago. I write to the brothers in Hyderabad: “I’m coming down soon. The rainy season is over. Find a good water pump, get a crew ready, and we’ll start going vertical.” I’ll mine as much good stuff as possible—even if I have to grab a shovel and dig it out myself!—and then I’ll set up a cabochonpolishing factory someplace in India, maybe in Jaipur, using cheap, skilled labor. Once I have a big supply of finished goods I’ll go to New York City to sell it. This is the plan.

I have long discussions with Denis O’Brien before leaving. He’s still hopeful—“I will do it, trust me”—but it will take more time to find the proper market for our low-end rubies. He concurs, “Yes, if you have finished goods that will be better. But to set up a factory, pay the workers, where are you going to get the money for that?”

By the time I pay outstanding bills at the Manor, I’ll barely have £400 left in the bank—not enough for the big mining endeavor in India. But Krishna will help me, that much I’ve realized by now.

**\* \* \***

When I told Malati my plans and my lack of money to carry them out, she said, “Well, we have my diamond wedding ring. Perhaps you can sell it.”

You see? Melanie, Malati, still my down-home girl. All right, sentiment aside, we need the cash. It’s just a material bauble, yes, and it will pay for my ticket to India, Saraswati s return ticket to Dallas, and maybe finance mining operations for a while.

So I took the 2.23-carat yellow diamond ring down to Garrards, “Jewelers to the Crown since 1735,” at 24 Albemarle Street in Mayfair. The proprietor examined the stone with his high-power loupe and said it was an old Golconda miners-cut diamond that would have to be recut to suit modern tastes, thus losing about 30 percent of its weight. He offered me £600 for it. I held out for £1000 and we settled at £800.1 raced back to Letchmore Heath with the good news: I’ve got my grubstake!

George returned from Portugal and phoned me from Friars Park. “It was amazing,” he said. “I chanted Hare Krishna for two straight days, driving nonstop across France and Spain, getting lost, finding the way, high out of my mind on the mantra!” Despite increasing marital problems with Pattie, George was on a roll. Living in The Material World was still near the top of the charts, his Shankar Family album was in the can, recent meetings with Prabhupada have cleared his doubts, and he’s immersed himself deeply in the power and pleasure of the maha-mantra.

George has done so much for Krishna. The rubies are just sitting in the vault gathering dust, and who knows if or when they’ll sell. I’m soon off to India to get better stuff anyway, so I make a snap decision: let’s give this gem hoard to George. A small gift compared to the riches George has bestowed on us. We load the eighteen crates back into the temple van and I drive out to Henley-On-Thames.

“George, here is a ton of Krishna rubies from Krishna’s unlimited mine. After all you have given to Krishna, they’re yours now. Where do you want them?”

\[long laughing\] “Hmm, well, why don’t we toss them on the path like The Wizard ofOz and make a ruby-brick road?”

About a hundred yards southwest of the main house George had an Olympic-size outdoor swimming pool set in open lawns and encircled by a six-foot-wide crushed-chalk gravel walkway. I backed the van up to the pool area, and we spent a happy afternoon scattering bucket after bucket— thousands of ruby crystals—onto the gravel path.

For many years after this, George kept bowls of rubies on tables and shelves around the house and often entertained his guests with ruby-picking around the pool. Fashion photographer Nancy Lee Andrews described her 1979 visit to Friar Park with Ringo: “After that we settled in George’s study/music room and he handed me a bowl of rubies. Big ones and small ones, all cabochons. It was days before my birthday, and he said to pick whatever I want and have something made. While he and Ringo talked and played the guitar, I settled in front of the fireplace and designed a necklace with lots of hanging rubies. One of those nights I’ll never forget.”

**\* \* \***

Two weeks after Srila Prabhupada left the UK for India, on September 28, 1973 I paid Avis £339.46 for three months’ rental fees on the Capri and bought a one-way ticket on KLM to Bombay. I went to the Chartered Bank on Regent Street to withdraw the last of my funds in cash, prepared to hoist pick and shovel and, by sweat and toil and for as long as I could feed myself, dig deep into the soft sweet soil of India for perfect gems, come what may.

I asked the teller for my current balance. She said, “£10,325.” What? “Madame, there must be some mistake!” “No, sir. I see that yesterday you had a deposit of £10,000. So £10,325 is your correct balance.”

I dashed out of the bank, went to the royal-red phone booth in front of the bank and, on a premonition, phoned Denis O’Brien. “Denis, did you put ten thousand pounds in my account yesterday?” “No, I didn’t,” he said.

“But Denis, it’s there. I’m at the bank now. Then who ... ?” Long pause. Denis said, “Must have been your benefactor.” George.

I ran back into the bank, took out £325 for travel cash, and raced out of there, elated beyond words, to buy a toothbrush and some vitamin C, pack a bag, and catch a 747 to India the next day.

**\* \* \***

Frosty early morning on the day of my departure. I take one last japa walk through the village and acres around Bhaktivedanta Manor, following paths I’d strolled so many shining mornings with Srila Prabhupada. I am flooded by a nostalgic longing for my spiritual master.
