Stepping out through the Manor gates into the sleeping, early-autumn village—O Prabhupada!—I remember the empty after-party streets of Haight-Ashbury as we rushed to Frederick Street to hear your latest revelations. Passing our little lake in Hertfordshire, I pause to watch the ducks and am transported to Golden Gate Park and those misty mornings when it dawned on us that you are a special friend from heaven. The little path behind the rhododendron dell takes me to John’s place at Tittenhurst, where the idea took root to present your royal nature to this nation. Walking by harvested Helds cut to stubble for approaching winter, I see a glow in the east as we boarded our sunrise flight to Amsterdam, where— Hah!—your sober appearance on TopPop surprised millions. Orange light tickled the eastern sky as you led us to the holy Ganges for an ice-cold awakening—O Prabhupada!—those exotic dawns in old Bombay before traffic roared in the streets below, fresh breeze off the Arabian Sea connecting us to the ancient truths of India— we saw your mystery, your mastery of every possible topic. From there we flew to Moscow, where you changed the cold world in just five days! We chased rhinos on the bloody raw streets of Africa, we argued the meaning of being and time, and gambled for gold in the Alps. We sailed the skies together, you and I, to Australia, China, Japan, and across the world to Mexico, Los Angeles, and New York, around the globe again and again, increasing your temples from two to a hundred. You are like Columbus or Magellan, sole and complete commander to your historical voyage of discovery, leading us across uncharted seas and deep into cloud-shrouded lands like rockets in your slipstream.

I look across shadowed lawns as early October sun gilds gabled slate roofs and fluted chimneys and slides down the storybook outer walls of Bhaktivedanta Manor, lighting the mullioned windows to rooms upstairs where you so recently played with us. I remember the unusual beauty of your face and mouth, the flowing ease and subtle lines of cheeks and crinkles wrapping around and punctuating another perfect mood, your dancing eyes, now somber, unmoving in thought, now wide and full of mischief. I recall the soft golden flesh of your shoulder and the surprising hard tissues beneath it, the flow of blue veins under forearms, behind knees— how they throbbed with a cool, even pulse as you lounged in the sun and roared with power when you danced all day. Watching you shave always had me in stitches—the wide intent eyes and grimaces as you swiveled the mirror to see that your tilak was straight or to check on a possible loose tooth. I remember how you dressed yourself slowly, keenly observing each cloth before choosing, then tied and tucked your dhoti with care. Nor can I forget quick pink fingers at your buttonholes, and how sometimes you leaned forward for me to close the one at the neck.

Sun strikes the bright-painted rainbow windows enclosing Sri Sri Radha-Gokulananda, and I hear the ringing kartals and muted voices of devotees singing your praises: Jaya Prabhupada, Jaya Prabhupada, Jaya Prabhupada, Jaya Prabhupada... growing louder as I enter the temple room and fall flat, glancing to my right—wait, is he?—catching you for a moment, knowing you’re still here on your very cool throne.

I approach Radha and Krishna and see Them well-dressed, serene, and exhilarated with Their new home and family. I am weak with gratitude to Them for gifting me this raremost destiny. I’ll catch my spiritual master down in India now, hang out with him for a while, maybe chase some more rhinos, show him what I can do. How could life be any better than this?

**\* \* \***

After saying goodbye to everyone, I flew off to Bombay, tracing the footsteps of the perfect person, knowing all would be well in the shelter of my courageous, gorgeous, hilarious, buccaneer master, my father and dearmost friend, His Divine Grace Abhay Charan Bhaktivedanta Swami, my beloved Prabhupada.

Afterword

A galaxy is defined as a huge collection of stars and stellar remnants, like planets, bound together around a center of gravity. From the earth planet our unaided eyes can see only our own Milky Way galaxy and a small portion of the Andromeda galaxy. By December 2020 the Australian Square Kilometre Array Pathfinder, a radio telescope located in west Australia, had mapped three million galaxies. They expect to map “tens of millions of galaxies” in the future out of the estimated two trillion galaxies in our one observable universe.

A star is a self-luminous spheroid of plasma held together by gravity. Our sun is a star. The two trillion galaxies around us are estimated to contain 1024 — that’s 10 with 24 zeros — stars. One astronomer states that there are “10,000 stars for every grain of sand on earth.” This figure does not include the uncountable numbers of planets, asteroids, and other bodies in space.

How do we get our minds around this? A medium-sized grain of sand is 1/50 of an inch to 1/100 of an inch in size, so one cubic inch contains about 300,000 grains of sand, and one cubic meter contains about 18 billion grains. The volume of the earth is 1,097,509,500,000,000,000,000 cubic meters. “10,000 stars for every grain of sand on earth.” OK, enough ...

In our feeble attempt to understand space, here’s another fact: the farthest observable object in our universe—a galaxy called

GN-z11 — is 32.1 billion light-years away. Light travels at 670 million miles per hour, so one light-year is about six trillion miles. Multiply this by 32.1 billion ...

While we’re in space, consider small, inner space. So far, physics has identified neutrinos as pointlike entities with nearzero size. Neutrinos pass through matter unimpeded. 65 billion solar neutrinos per second pass through each square centimeter of the earth’s surface and out the other side at the speed of light. Basically, space is empty.

And time—what about time? Classical physics informs us that time is a river, an arrow, implying a beginning and an end. But quantum physics tells us, no, time travels in all directions at once. Particles of matter are “entangled,” so that what affects one particle simultaneously affects another particle, even if those particles are separated by a vast distance. Somehow, everything is simultaneously one and different. Albert Einstein called it “spooky action at a distance,” and he admitted that “the distinction between past, present, and future is a stubbornly persistent illusion.”

It’s all so vast! What is this endless cosmic manifestation of spacetime? Does it have a purpose? Does it mean anything? How do I fit into spacetime—me, this tiny being? Am I a blade of grass to bloom and wither in one season?

Perhaps Einstein says it best:

We are in the position of a little child, entering a huge library whose walls are covered to the ceiling with books in many different tongues. The child knows that someone must have written those books. It does not know who or how. It does not understand the languages in which they are written. The child notes a definite plan in the arrangement of the books, a mysterious order, which it does not comprehend, but only dimly suspects ...

We see a universe marvelously arranged, obeying certain laws, but we understand the laws only dimly. Our limited minds cannot grasp the mysterious force that sways the constellations.

Enter A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami (we call him Swamiji) in the particular solar cycle of earth named 1967. Enter the wise man from the ancient East, librarian for this huge collection of all knowledge he calls the Vedas. He reads us these books (“Just you try to follow”), teaches us the language to read them ourselves, introduces our inquiring minds to the Author Himself (“He is Krishna, the Supreme Personality”), and explains to our full satisfaction the “mysterious force that sways the constellations.”

We entered his library slowly at Erst. On the first shelf were three volumes of a book called Srimad-Bhagavatam. Their marvelous cover depicted a handsome bluish man playing a flute and dancing with a beautiful woman, both of them dressed in exotic, bright-colored clothes and draped with jewels and flower garlands, a palace in the background, and trees, cows, and other dancing figures—all surrounded by a whorl of pink blossoms which, as young hippies, we knew to be a lotus flower. Scattered about the cover, rays radiating from this central motif led to dozens of celestial globes, like planets, each inhabited by a fourarmed, named personality.

It’s wild! What’s going on here? On the inside flap the Swami explains:

There are millions and billions of suns in the millions and billions of universes all compact within the Maha Tattwa. The suns and moons are required within the universes because they are dark by nature. The Vedas instruct to go out of the dark universes and reach to the glowing effulgence of Brahmajyoti.... Srimad Bhagawatam helps us reaching the Supreme Planet Goloka Vrindaban and the door is open for everyone. The human life is meant for this particular aim of life called the highest perfection.

Next on his shelf was a tiny, eighty-page booklet Swamiji called Easy Journey to Other Planets, which he’d written in 1959. He had only one copy, so his New York disciples typed it onto stencils, then mimeographed and stapled the pages and mailed it out to us in San Francisco. The Swami starts the book by congratulating scientists for their recent discovery of antimatter and then, using precise logic, points out the holes and shortcomings in their understanding. He says stuff like, “\[In\] the highest planet of the material universe, Brahmaloka... the duration of life is said to be 4,300,000 1,000 2 30 12 100 solar years.” He says the soul, the nonmaterial particle, is smaller than “ten-thousandths of the tip of a hair.” What the Swami knows about space and time transcends anything the scientists have told us, and here’s why: “Full details of the antimaterial universe can be known only from the infallible source of liberated authorities who have thoroughly realized the constitution of the antimaterial principle.”

He calls these “liberated authorities” the parampara, the line of disciplic succession—and he can name hundreds of persons in this line—going back through time to the creation itself. The Swami says, yes, from these books you can know everything—space, time, matter, spirit, the beginning, the end, and the meaning and purpose of it all. And it’s an easy journey, he says.

And so gently he began his instruction.

But I’m especially concerned with me, tiny me. How do I fit? And here’s where Bhaktivedanta Swami bridges the gap, closes the circle between me and the vastness of space and time and the countless living entities, explaining to me why I matter.

The Swami tells us that Krishna, the energetic Source, has personally entered into every particle of His energy, that He is conscious of the movement of every atom as He moves it around with His inconceivable intelligence. “I am the generating seed of all existences. Nothing moving or nonmoving can exist without Me.” So it’s easy for Krishna, the Swami says, to reside in the heart of every living entity as witness and best friend. “Best friend” means that, as an individual spirit-soul made of antimatter, I— and all living entities—originally lived with Krishna in His place somewhere beyond the material spheres called Goloka Vrindaban, sporting with Him in eternal, blissful play; He, the Enjoyer, me the enjoyed and a tiny particle of Him, possessing His qualities in minute quantity, like a sunray carries a minute bit of the sun. One of these qualities is independence, the desire to enjoy, so at some point in “time,” I desired to enjoy on my own, away from Krishna, and Krishna, with all-loving tenderness created these material universes so I could play out my misplaced desire. Yet through all these lifetimes and vast cycles of me trying to enjoy the material worlds, Krishna has remained in my heart, slowly guiding me back to Him and, in this particular finite cycle called 1967, has sent me to Bhaktivedanta Swami for my final lesson. “It is just like the father,” the Swami says. “He loves all his children equally, and he loves you without condition, however much rascal you are.”

“Here, you just come here, sit next to me, just here,” the Swami says. New, this scent of mustard oil and musk; new and sublime, this touch of soft gold and saffron-colored person brushing my left elbow, electricity coursing through my shoulders, thinking, “At last I’ve found the Magic Man!”

Oh, we were a restless bunch, Swamiji, my generation, remember? We wouldn’t hear philosophy. So many philosophies were there—Yogi this and Tibetan that—just so many words in our ears. We wanted magic—music and magic. The doors of perception had recently parted, and we had glimpsed for moments some higher, more wonderful place. Seekers were we, then, for the Magic Man, someone with extraordinary powers, the Master, someone to lift the veil and lead us into that higher realm of being—and let the door slam shut behind us!

Your bedroom-slippered feet glide by, inches from my head bowed to the floor, trailing mustard and sandalwood and roses, and I rise to see your lustrous face, laughing and victorious or dark with purpose, eyes grave and hooded—your eyes slit tight in some deep thought or blooming wide like a child seeing its first elephant. This is what we had of you at the first: a face so beautiful, so full of every kind of strength and compassion and confidence and softness. So like a giant you were in our midst, then tiny and surrendered as a child in our arms, a form so ever new in its expressions we couldn’t pull our eyes away.

Of course, as you once said, yes, a face or body may be very beautiful, but as soon as the person they belong to opens their mouth we see they may be a fool. But when you open your mouth the universe sings, all secrets are revealed, all harmony and grace of sound perfect in your words and singing. You take a moment to sit down on your dais, put on those glasses that from my angle expand your eyes to impossible size, mutter krssh-na deep in your throat, tap the mic a time or two, and tell us, "Acintya-bheda-abheda-tattva—simult^neous oneness and difference. The heat and light of the sun is not different from the sunshine rays, but still the sun is not there in the rays. Similarly, although Krishna is not there, but everything is there in Krishna, and everywhere you can find Krishna also at the same time, because everything is Krishna’s energy.”

You read to us from Srimad-Bhagavatam that Krishna expanded Himself to live individually with each of His sixteen thousand wives in separate palaces. “So you think Krishna cannot be personally present there with you?”

Now, this is philosophy, I think, this is science, the sum total of everything: although Krishna is present everywhere by the cosmic manifestations of His energy, He simultaneously maintains His distinct personal identity, with His own name, form, qualities, abode, pastimes, and entourage. And I maintain my individual identity, too. Krishna and the living beings are simultaneously different (bheda) and nondifferent (abheda). And this truth (tattva) is held to be inconceivable (acintya). OK, this pretty well says it all, and I think, yes, you are the quantum swami. When we see you and hear you, all space and time are stopped and we are each connected in a unique kinship with Krishna in His unlimited magic world where everything is possible.

You tell of heroes, adventure, daring, and risk—of perfect comfort, of the place where this exists and the easy way to get there: that bliss is now and the place is here—just chant Hare Krishna, and see! And we see you, the effulgent example, so strange to us but somehow so familiar, the effortless, laughing, perfect man, free from doubt and flaw and error, the consummate actor, pirate chief, the great trickster, the grandfather and child of our concern—the mystic sage, next-door neighbor, the seer into our deepest thoughts—you, the Swami, Swamiji, weaving us deeper, deeper, deeper into your magic spell. “Just understand a little this Krishna consciousness life, take it up, it is so nice, you will feel—then you go out and spread.”

You weave our single weak threads into the unbroken cord of the parampara, bind us in the fabric of the eternal occupation you call sanatana-dharma, then bid us go forth. Krishna consciousness, you explain, has throughout time been an evangelistic movement and should be spread vigorously. “Our purpose is to spread Krishna all over the planet,” you say repeatedly. Spread, spread, spread, you say, so spread becomes our mission: move, preach, risk, dare. “When Krishna sees His devotee risk a little suffering for Him, He must immediately Himself come. Krishna will help you; you will see.” And we took off like rockets.

**\* \* \***

Decades later I ponder in awe the unlikeliness, the chance beyond reckoning, that Bhaktivedanta Swami has traveled uncountable universes, across trillions of galaxies, over time without beginning or end, to find us at this perfect historical moment—we insignificant blades of grass—to share great secrets and fill us with unimaginable joy as we bound off after him to chase the rhino.

In these volumes of Chasing Rhinos With The Swami I’ve recounted the stories, how the Swami coddled us, then threw us from the nest and led his untried children, haltingly at first, then swiftly around the globe—him now called Prabhupada and guiding, managing, and protecting us at every step. Nearly seven years I was at Prabhupada’s feet or by his side—never more than an airmail letter away—rocketing with him from the wild streets of Haight-Ashbury to the British Isles, to Europe, India, Russia, Africa, Australia, China—three times around the world until Krishna’s name was planted in almost every ear in every nation on planet earth, witness to these events.

People ask me, “How do you remember so much detail from those days?”

Hah! Left on its own, my feeble brain can’t even shop for groceries without first making a list. But Srila Prabhupada made it easy to remember him. He must have known everything he said and did would be important for posterity because from his earliest days among us he insisted that every letter he wrote be typed in triplicate, using carbon paper, and the records carefully kept. We didn’t have many books then, not even the Bhagavad-gita, and the ideas and words he presented in daily lectures were big and complex, even though he repeated themes over and over. We took notes at first, but lazy Americans, we soon bought bulky versions of newly invented reel-to-reel tape recorders and began to record the Swami’s lectures. As these machines got smaller and more portable, we recorded his room conversations, then morning walks—to the point that by the early ’70s in almost every photo of Prabhupada you’ll see someone’s hand and a tiny mic reaching for his face.

Photos. In the mid-’60s cameras were expensive and hard to use. Only Gurudas and a couple of other devotees had cameras. As cameras became cheaper and easier, photos of Prabhupada multiplied exponentially—first in black-and-white and then in color—as it dawned on us that the Swami’s every fleeting expression must be captured for all eternity. Then came films. Gurudas and Durlabh got movie cameras, and then Yadubara and Visakha came along and brought professionalism to filming Prabhupada.

Given the frantic, disorganized, explosive energy that fueled the days described in these books, it’s a wonder that these letters and tapes and photos and films survived. But survive they have, and due to the loving care and attention of the devotees at the Bhaktivedanta Archives in North Carolina, nearly all of Srila Prabhupada’s words and activities—year by year, day by day, hour by hour—have been preserved for a thousand generations to come. These saints have gathered this collection of Prabhupada’s words and deeds in a remarkable computer archive called the VedaBase.

In the VedaBase you’ll find nearly four thousand recordings and transcriptions of Srila Prabhupada’s lectures, conversations, philosophy book debates, morning walks, snippets from moving cars—stretching from his first days in 1966 in New York until the day he left the planet. Four thousand! And 6,586 letters. What’s most amazing to me, there are over forty thousand photos covering this eleven-year period.

So “detail,” you ask? Easy. All I had to do was set my clock for January 17, 1967, and let it run. Almost every letter, every lecture, every photo is there in the VedaBase. August 4, 1971? Let’s see. Here’s the letter Srila Prabhupada wrote to Karandhar on August 5. Oh, yeah, he mentions “Yesterday I talked with the high commissioner from India for the United Kingdom.” Now I remember what happened that day! The rest of the story unfolds in my brain, and I find a photo of Prabhupada in the high commissioner’s office that captures that moment in time.

And the computer, what a tool we have now, huh! The search engine. I can google “weather london august 4 1971” and tell you that 32 mm of rain fell in London on that day. Yes, and Prabhupada was feeling the damp cold. I can load one of Yadubara’s videos into my computer, freeze a frame, open “snip tool,” click “save,” and there you have it: fifty years ago, Prabhupada walking in Regent’s Park with me holding an umbrella over his head, and him saying, “What do you think, Shyamasundar, shall we go to Los Angeles tomorrow?”

The VedaBase documents are trail markers, the bones I used to build my stories, and I have only to sketch in the living flesh.

And don’t forget—most importantly—the transcendental factor! Krishna says that He is the source of memory and forgetfulness. Prabhupada says too: “Since a devotee writes in service to the Lord, the Lord from within gives him so much intelligence that he sits down near the Lord and goes on writing books.”

And have I not basked in pleasure as Krishna guided my hand? Over and again I have giggled and tingled in automatic overdrive as Prabhupada pops another word into my brain. Often I’ve looked up from a day’s writing and seen, Oh, it’s getting dark outside, then looked down and noticed I’m still in last night’s pajamas. Many were the times I got stuck with a thought—let’s see, what happened that day in Bombay fifty years ago? I’d wrack my brain all day, walk around my Los Angeles neighborhood, and try to remember. Then that night I’d have a dream and next morning fly to my computer and peck out the story in living detail. Or what about the time I spent a couple of weeks trying to track down a 1969 BBC interview with George Harrison. I knew it was a key interview, very important for the book, but I just couldn’t find it. I sent off for several books from Amazon.com, pored through them looking and looking, but couldn’t find it. Progress ground to a halt. Then early one morning an earthquake shook me awake—it’s L.A., after all—and I heard a few books hit the floor from the bookcase in the other room, then went back to sleep. Later, I padded toward the kitchen for breakfast and one of those fallen books caught my eye. It was open to the very page of George’s missing interview. Thank you, Krishna, You’ve done it again.

But how can our feeble efforts, by any measure, compare with Srila Prabhupada’s massive written compendium? In 1967 when we entered the Swami’s library, there were only a couple of books. By late 1968 we had his Bhagavad-gita at last, then, gradually, Teachings of Lord Chaitanya, then, in 1970, Krsna book, and slowly more chapters, then whole cantos, of Srimad-Bhagavatam.

In little more than ten years Srila Prabhupada left us thirty-eight works in over a hundred volumes; Caitanya-caritamrita alone is in seventeen volumes, Srimad-Bhagavatam is in eighteen volumes. The King James Version of the Bible is 783,137 words. The total word count for William Shakespeare over forty-three works is 884,421. How many millions of words are in Prabhupada’s books, his spoken lectures, his conversations—the sum total of his written legacy I leave for someone else to calculate.

**\* \* \***

From our earliest days with the Swami, there was a competition to catch his attention, make him happy with something you did. This is probably natural in large families—the desire to serve and please the father you love, to stand out and be noticed among your siblings by pleasing him the most. It’s that energy that fuels the stories you read in these pages. If Brahmananda has found a way to broadcast the Swami’s message by printing his Bhagavad-gita, then I had to find a bigger way to spread the message by catching the Beatles’ eyes. If Karandhar got a big temple together for Srila Prabhupada, then I must get a bigger one, because Prabhupada would travel to the biggest place and stay longer, where the action was.

But wherever we went, whatever we did, each devotee had a special, personal relationship with Srila Prabhupada. He told us that everyone has to serve someone; our constitutional position as living entities is to serve. OK, well, I might as well serve God. Prabhupada told us there are five basic relationships with Krishna: seeing Krishna in an awe-struck, passive state; serving Krishna in activity; or, higher yet, as His friend, or His parent, or even His lover. Looking back, I see myself filled at first with wonder, but shy to come forward until he beckoned me. Swamiji, you enlisted me in your motley crew, as you, the pirate chief, pointed over the horizon and said, “Set sail!” Gradually over the next few years, especially after you made me your personal secretary in 1971, we became friends, didn’t we, Srila Prabhupada, fellow conspirators, laughing as often as not, cooking up some far-out scheme to feed our mutual thirst for adventure. In one of your books you wrote that in friendship “the devotee is fully convinced that Lord Krishna cannot at all be dissatisfied if the devotee lives with Him on an equal level.” Equal! Hah! Oh, please forgive me, Prabhupada, if sometimes I sailed into your room unbowed or jokingly said your tilak’s a mess or laughed when you tried to be serious. If sometimes my deep-seated veneration was overshadowed by a burst of impulsive candor when you asked, “What do you think, Shyamasundar?” I always tried to remember that familiarity breeds contempt and that I am nothing compared to your magnificence, but you loved laughter and adventure, same as me, and you had a mission, you loved to risk, and we fell into step, side by side.

**\* \* \***

I don’t know. Looking back, maybe I took this risk thing a little too far. Starting with that ruby mine in India. Back in 1972 when I’d first stumbled across those rubies, you said, “You take this opportunity Krishna has given. Now you do it and let us see what happens.” You got me chasing rhinos, Prabhupada, and I’ve never stopped. At first I saw the ruby mine as a quick source of income for the expansion of your mission in the UK. I had gone straight from college to serving you, so at thirty years old I’d never done any real business in my life. Mining. Just to break into the earth and sort ounces of one mineral from tons of rock was effort beyond my imagining. Then trying to sell these hard-won gems into one of the most corrupt and competitive marketplaces on earth, one founded on blood and mirrors and the elusive price of vanity. The struggle carried me away. I wound up in New York City dealing with demons on an everyday basis, exhausted and out of control. Whatever money I made I poured back into the mine, to go deeper, to find the purest, most valuable rubies hidden at greater depths. I was desperate to regain your approval. Years went by, but find them I did, and at last I packed a hundred kilos of world-class rubies into crates—I was trying to calculate how many zeroes there are in a billion—and dropped them for export to California at Bombay’s airport, where they were promptly confiscated by Indira Gandhi’s officials under the 1975 Emergency Act. Overnight, everything lost. OK, Krishna, I get it: don’t be attached to the fruits of my labors and all that. And I remembered your long-ago comment, Srila Prabhupada, that I still have a few material fantasies, and by selling these jewels I may suffer, but the fantasies will go away.

Saraswati had come to live with me by then, and there I was with a new wife and a second family in Los Angeles, broke, and caught up in paying bills and taxes, with houses and cars and banks and lawyers, a life dependent on profit and loss. But one thing I’d won from the ruby-mining years was knowledge; my Indian partners had taught me an ancient science of how to find precious things in the earth by using the five senses and intelligence. How to recognize “host” rocks by their appearance, the sound they made when you dinged them with a hammer, the smell emitted by their ingredients, a sweet or bitter taste on the tongue. Using this knowledge, within a week of the bad news from Bombay I discovered a precious lapis-lazuli gem deposit in the San Gabriel Mountains above L.A., tracked it, nose to the ground, by the sulfur that gives lapis-lazuli its intense blue color, and so the show went on. Talk about risk. I hung from ropes on cliffs with drills and dynamite and for two years packed the lapis rocks down each night on my back, two miles to the road. My family was growing: Saraswati, a stepson, Jason, and a new baby daughter so beautiful we named her Crystal. And just when the money started rolling in, the government shut me down to include the mine in a new wilderness area. OK, Krishna, you’ve done it again—more fantasies gone, no regrets—but the bills still have to be paid.

Srila Prabhupada writes: “An expert geologist can understand where there is gold and by various processes can extract it from the gold ore. Similarly, a spiritually advanced person can understand how the spiritual particle exists within the body, and thus by cultivating spiritual knowledge he can attain perfection in spiritual life. However, as one who is not expert cannot understand where there is gold, a foolish person who has not cultivated spiritual knowledge cannot understand how the spirit exists within the body.” (Srimad-Bhagavatam 7.7.21, purport)

That’s me, stuck between two worlds. In 1980 gold shot to $800 an ounce, so I went for the gold. I found and developed a commercial-scale gold mine near Barstow in the Mojave Desert. In the Mother Lode country in central California I mined gold from buried ancient river channels in Eldorado and Calaveras counties. I learned the hard way, by trial and error, and sent money home so the kids could stay in school. Each time I’d start to break into big bucks, Krishna conspired to take the mine away—bad partners, insufficient investment—and in the boom-and-bust cycle special to bonanza mining throughout history, off I’d go to find a new one.

And didn’t you teach me to go for the rhino, Srila Prabhupada? These American digs are slim now, worked for a century, so where’s that virgin field where the gold lies thick on bedrock or winks in the sun in foot-thick veins, untouched by the hand of man? In 1982, a friend of a friend invited me to prospect her gold concession in Bolivar State in southern Venezuela. She said that very rich strikes were being made every day, but cautioned me that it was a roadless area the size of Colorado and filled with snakes and unimaginable dangers and that I’d be on my own for months at a time. South America, yes, the Amazon!

Six years I wandered those immense jungles and a thousand nameless streams in the Orinoco and Amazon basins. I saw and experienced in my bones the most intense natural displays of life and struggle and death and beauty on the planet—and Krishna saved my hide time after time.

If you’ve read the prologue to volume one of Chasing Rhinos With The Swami, this is where you came in. November 24, 1985, headwaters of the Rio Cuyuni 150 river miles from the nearest village, and me pinned back to back with Jose, my Yanomami guide, on a fallen log in a tiny jungle clearing by an angered jaguar circling us with blood-curdling roars as night descends and my flashlight fails. Hare Krishna! Hare Krishna!

Hours later, as sun burns off the mist and touches the tops of the canopy a hundred feet overhead, the jungle around us erupts with dunning bird calls, whoops, and whistles. A troop of howler monkeys passes nearby with ungodly screaming, and Jose indicates this is a good sign the jaguar has left. We begin to stir, and then—Que esi—a sound like far-off motors. Motors? Maybe airplanes? We hadn’t seen a plane cross these skies for months— louder now!—the cloudless sky darkens. Iridescent violet morpho butterflies with six-inch wingspans fall from the sky like huge purple snowflakes, clouds of them, millions, darkening the sky, falling on us and landing and flapping their wings, unlimited numbers of undulating amaranthine waves covering every surface! We sit entranced at this pulsing, noisy, mind-numbing miracle of migration. And just as suddenly, they lift and scatter and recompose in a cloud, moving west. Even the birds have grown quiet. One butterfly remains spread-winged on my knee, pausing in the sun. This perfect small creature, so important to me now in these few seconds in time. Our blue eyes meet and examine. Can this tiny being—well, not so tiny compared to an ant, to the millions of ants at home on this log—can such small beings conceive of me, a living entity so huge and complex, who creates machines of metal to fly across the earth? Or can I, this tiny human in a jungle so vast—can I conceive of a Krishna who has created the billions of beings just within my immediate vision and beyond that, trillions ofgalaxies filled with beings?

I get it! All of this just is, and I’m filled with pleasant wonder, an epiphany if you will, realizing the perfect fate of this butterfly, the ant crawling over my hand, how each small creature is so perfectly placed and equally important in the colossal, vibrant, evolving, revolving, eternal stream of conscious energy that can only be divine. And how rare this human form that I can understand this and, rarer yet, search out and meet the Supreme Person who has created all this, maybe hang out with Him in some sort of everlasting sport? O Prabhupada, how rare it is, how lucky I am, that you have come across the universes to introduce me to Krishna!

With a final flap of cognition the butterfly lifts off and insect and bird sounds rise to cacophony. Jose stands, stretches his limbs, sniffs the air in all directions. “Tigre no viene,” he says. The tiger has gone. KRISHNA! PRABHUPADA! I yell to the heavens. Saved again.

Still cautious, Jose leads us in a direction away from the jaguars kill, picking our way carefully over fallen debris—the tangle we had leaped through only hours before—and we slowly hack a path deeper into the heavy jungle gloom. He stops once, points high into a tree and says, “Avispa!” Wasp. Ah, I get it. African killer bees have infested these Venezuelan jungles; I'd heard the stories, how they descend on anyone who comes near their nest and sting grown men to death. Local Indians fear them more than any snake. We make a wide detour. We have no food, but we hack certain vines and drink pure water to replace sweat that pours from our bodies, or pause to pick ticks and leeches from arms and shoulders. It’s so dark in here you cant see the sun, and even my stout warrior friend seems to be zigging and zagging through this featureless labyrinth of giant trees and patches of tall grass and underbrush. Hours on hours. We halt, and Jose wont look at me as he mutters, “Perdido.” Lost.

If this lifelong denizen of these forests is lost, what hope do we have? Still high from this morning’s realizations, I say to him, “No se preocupe—Krishna!” and point to the sky. Jose’s stone face shows no expression as he squints at me through tribal scars and smears of red achiote, shakes his head once in agreement—and off we go again, swinging machetes and shouting KRISHNA! to the treetops. Hours more we wander this way and that, Jose trying to get his bearings in this shadowed jungle realm where everything looks the same. But I'm buoyed and elated by Krishna's magic, certain Prabhupada will save me again.

Suddenly, Jose stops and, with the tip of his machete, points to a knee-high branch and says, “Pica.” Trail. I bend close to see: Yes! This stub of branch has been cut by a blade; sap has flowed into the wound, but it's now black with age. How long ago had men passed through this savage wilderness? Circling around until he finds another cut, Jose gradually discerns some sort of invisible path as we straighten our helm and walk, then break into trot as he picks up the scent of his ancestors, and just before night we smell kerosene and there! the shining tents of our basecamp. As we approach the roaring campfire and bright faces of our comrades, Jose raises his arm and points to the sky, and it's the first time I've seen him smile, a full band of tiny sharp teeth, and his obsidian eyes sparkle as he says “Ha-re Krish-na,” and we both know Who has saved us.

Srila Prabhupada, can I tell you some stories now, give just a little overview of what’s happened to me over the years since I saw you last? You used to tell me your stories, how you worked so hard to build up businesses only to have them crash down on you, and how these reverses only made you stronger, convinced you of Krishna’s hand in your life, how He was guiding you to something more, to your higher calling.

After six years of near-death days in Venezuela, I’d had enough. I still wasn’t rich and I missed my distant family, so I returned to the States to try to find a good mine closer to home. Within months I discovered a huge deposit ofgem sapphires and gold in an ancient riverbed perched a hundred feet above the present-day Missouri River, near Helena, Montana. Six years I worked this mine, and with heavy equipment and innovative machines produced nearly seven million carats of sapphires in every color of the rainbow from twenty-seven acres of virgin Montana ground. It was the largest gem-mine in North American history to that time. I developed a laboratory process to heat-treat the sapphire crystals to enhance their color and value in the marketplace. But just when I started to break into profit, lawyers climbed out of the walls and dragged me through years of hostile litigation. In early 1993, I lost the lawsuit, declared bankruptcy, and my wife divorced me, taking the ranch and horses with her. Then my father died, and I found out I had Hepatitis C. More fantasies gone. In many ways all this lifted my burdens, and I felt strangely closer now to Krishna. Prabhupada, everything you said about the material world was true; I realized it myself, to the max. Suddenly bereft of all my anchors, I felt free again, and soon sniffed out a fabulous placer gold deposit on Ophir Creek in Powell County, Montana, worked it a few months, then sold it and, with that grubstake, headed for Africa!

I mined rubies a hundred miles from the nearest road in southern Tanzania, the only white face in a lawless rush of five thousand miners, and then headed for Madagascar. For another five years I prospected this amazing wilderness, an island roughly the size and shape of California with very few paved roads, from one end of the island to the other. I discovered and mined rubies, sapphires, gold, rare blue tourmalines and other gems. I’m good at finding these things, Prabhupada, but the gems and money somehow never stick to my fingers! In 1998 I went to North Vietnam to mine rubies near the China border; less than twenty years earlier, my countrymen had killed two million Vietnamese, but I borrowed a trick or two from you, Srila Prabhupada, and told the general who ran the district, “Sir, I went to jail rather than fight in this war,” and he said to his troops, “This man is a hero of the people. Give him whatever he wants!”

In the mid-’90s I began to travel from time to time from wherever I was in the world, joining Mukunda or Radhanath Swami for a week or two in Vrindaban, and my desire to be with devotees reawakened, grew, deepened. In 1999 I went to live in India.

From years of mining rubies in Andhra Pradesh I knew that

India had once been home to fabulous gem mines, and that over the centuries these mines had been lost and forgotten. I thought there must be some reason that the small town of Jaipur, deep in central India, had for centuries been the emerald-cutting and sales center of the world. Looking through ancient texts I found, yes, the early Rajput kings wore huge emeralds as garlands long before the Spanish discovered the Muso mines in Colombia, so there must have been a local source. Tracking telltale bands of biotite mica, I rediscovered seven ancient emerald mines stretching south of Jaipur in a nearly straight line for three hundred miles along the Aravalli Hills. The Indian government wouldn’t let me mine them.

I went to Orissa for three months and found ruby, sapphire, and cats-eye chrysoberyl deposits. Again, the government blocked my mining attempts. So I returned to the fantasy that had excited me since I’d started mining in Andhra Pradesh twenty-five years earlier: the legendary diamonds of Golconda. Since time immemorial, the greatest diamonds in the world have been mined near the banks of the Krishna River in south-central India—the Koh-i-noor, the Regent, the Pitt, the Blue Hope, the Dresden Green all came from that area. Over time, locations for these ancient mines had been lost, and they were assumed to have been exhausted. I thought, using hand tools and with a year-round water table in that region of about thirty feet below ground surface, how could ancient miners have exhausted diamond-bearing ores of deepmantle origin? They must only have scratched the surface.

Taking hints from centuries-old books and wandering the zone for nearly a year, I finally found the villages and old workings of the mythical Golconda diamond fields. Almost every test hole my crew and I dug across this landscape produced diamonds. Diving in shallow portions of the Krishna River, too, produced nice gems. It made sense that the primitive ancients could not have mined the river’s great depths, where loads of high specific-gravity diamonds must, by the laws of geology, be trapped in potholes and crevices. It was a bonanza beyond reckoning. I raised capital, built huge machines, dredges, and infrastructure, and spent years trying to get government permits to explore and mine this fabulous resource, but the time and effort involved gradually eroded my desire to continue such a herculean task, Srila Prabhupada. You always said, “A devotee should be aloof from material activities which require too much endeavor, beyond his capacity.” The fantasies are gone. So let it be. Now I just want to describe our days together, Prabhupada, to write these books so that hundreds of generations will know your glories. This is the real treasure.

**\* \* \***

“But, Shyamasundar,” people ask me, “all your adventures, fifty years of mining in godforsaken places on every continent—why don’t you write about those adventures?” Hah! The years I spent roaming the planet with Srila Prabhupada were the most exciting of my life. Nothing I’ve done before or since can come close to the suspense, thrills, mystery, and adventure I experienced in those years with my spiritual master. From Prabhupada I learned and realized courage, courage under the protection of Krishna and fueled by a higher yearning. Srila Prabhupada was totally fearless in every circumstance—on the tossing deck of a cargo ship across the rough Atlantic; wandering the mean streets of lower Manhattan with only pennies in his pocket; hanging out with the drug-addled crazies of 1960s America; rubbing shoulders with lords and ladies, bums, rock stars, philosophers, and kings; dropping behind the Iron Curtain under fire to plant real peace; invading India with twenty untested soldiers to change his nation’s destiny; roaring out Krishna’s name on every continent, staring down adversity. He was relaxed and laughing in the face of all danger, free from perplexity and hesitation, fixed in the moment no matter the gales and thunder and raised swords around him. After Prabhupada, the decades of my measured time are just a filling in, an enduring of the days until we meet again and, together, storm the walls that dare to hem us in.

Yes, I have seen much in my near-eighty years. I’ve won and I’ve lost. I’ve traveled every continent, met the famous and the infamous, and known many angels and the devil himself. I’ve seen beauty more than the eyes can absorb and forests aflame with total destruction. I’ve seen perfect babies being born and bodies with guts eaten out by jackals and crows. I’ve dodged bullets again and again. I’ve fallen overboard late at night from a yacht in the Bermuda Triangle, floating there as the mast light disappeared over the horizon and calling out loud for Krishna’s mercy. I’ve caressed a koala bear in my arms and been chased by a monster grizzly through aspen thickets. I’ve seen thousand-milewide rainbows rise over the Amazon and hunkered under a rock in Madagascar while a cyclone hurled my jeep and two barrels of diesel into the valley below. I’ve heard the midnight rhapsody of lions roaring across the veldt and kept two full-grown female African lions for a year in my Manhattan loft on West 20th Street. Snakes? Climbing to a gold prospect in the Sierra Madre, as my hat brim cleared the ledge above vne—wham!—a giant timber rattler exploded into my shoulder, missing my face by inches, and knocked me ten feet to the ledge below. I carried that unlucky snake’s skin from then on as a hatband around my Stetson. I’ve lain immobile in India, scarcely breathing as a king cobra dropped from the thatch roof onto my cot and slowly, very slowly, slithered over my bare legs and dropped to the floor. I sweated bullets while it coiled by the bed, raised and looked straight into my unblinking eyes, then made its way out the door. Gold, gems, property, fast cars, horses—and lawsuits, bankruptcy, perfidious betrayals. I’ve explored rivers few have seen, opened the wilderness with roads and machines, reached deep into the earth and pulled to sunlight great crystals from the hearts of continents— such chances to realize my fantasies Krishna and Prabhupada have given me.

Now, Srila Prabhupada, I have only one fantasy left: to be with you again.

**\* \* \***

Yes, adventures, Srila Prabhupada, and friendships. Fellow pioneers on your caravan of discovery, I made the best friends I’ll ever have: Mukunda, Yamuna, Malati, Gurudas, and Janaki. I say pioneers because we were all born of recent ancestors who had traveled from far foreign lands to carve out lives in the new America, where everything was possible. For us, travel, new ideas, experiments, let’s see what’s over the next hill—this was already a way of life for us. Prabhupada, you picked the perfect teammates for your fantastic enterprise.

Mukunda, now Mukunda Goswami. Michael Grant. The coolest of the cool, always the quiet, mysterious man who seems to know so much, musician extraordinaire, who never veers from his course once set, and who always sees the good in everyone. Over the years, Mukunda and I have met here and there—at my goldmine in the Mojave, or in Bangkok, in London, Moscow, New Zealand, India, and Sydney. In the spring of 1981 I owned a small cabin on the headwaters of Bear Creek in the San Gabriel Mountains near Los Angeles. I phoned Mukunda and said, “I’ll meet you up there. We’ll spend some time in the wilderness,” and gave him directions to the place. I saw his car in the parking lot, swung my pack on my back, and started up the half-mile trail to the cabin. About halfway up the trail I was startled by a six-foot rattlesnake on the path, freshly killed. When I got to the cabin Mukunda said, “Yeah, he tried to strike me, I had to kill him with a stick.” Mukunda’s still around, down there in Oz, hanging out with his flock of devotee-hundreds in the pleasant eucalyptus jungles of New South Wales, soaking up some rays and pitching peanuts to the kookaburras. I spent a couple of weeks with him there in 2016, and countless times we laughed together till tears flowed and we had to sit down.

Yamuna, gone from us now but forever in my heart and thoughts. Joanie Oslund. Molded by Montana winters in the mining town of

Butte, a tough, determined Taurus, like me, but sweet to the core. Growing up, we had our little private world where greatness was explored, poems and Bob Dylan and the Moody Blues and Tibetan mystics. Once we found the Swami, Yamuna surrendered so completely, more than I ever can, and lived her remaining years as a beacon of total devotion and service, personally guiding dozens of youngsters to Krishna. She turned down George Harrison’s offer to make her a rock star and instead struggled on the stubborn sands of Vrindaban to build Srila Prabhupada’s dream temple at Raman Reti. We met occasionally over the years, usually at her adobewalled outpost in British Columbia, where she kept bears from her potatoes with a huge black mastiff (who wouldn’t harm a flea.)

And Malati, dear Malati. Melanie Nagel. They threw away the mold. In the summer of 1963, when I first saw her roll up to our pad on Waller Street in Haight-Ashbury on her motorcycle, crash the kickstand with her booted heel and run up the front-porch steps toward me yelling “The fuzz are at it again!” I thought, wow, this chick’s for me. After we married and became the Swami’s disciples, her toughness and devotion kept me going. And she’s never stopped. She’s become the first female GBC zonal secretary, an ISKCON general now. She can often be found in West Virginia these days, running her show in the folksy hills of New Vrindaban. She has her own cabin there, I’m told. But you never know for sure where Malati is. She usually replies to emails within seconds or answers the phone on the first ring, but maybe she’s piloting a van full of women across Illinois (“If that bloody radiator holds, or the low tire—we haven’t got a spare!”), or maybe she’s in Kazakhstan. I never know.

Our daughter Saraswati, whom We dragged around the world with us during those frantic early years—your dearest little friend, Srila Prabhupada—is more than fifty years old now. Sara. She lives with her husband and two kids in a posh Chicago suburb. Sara’s a chip off her mother’s block, running all over the place, organizing everything. I recently spent a couple days with her power-shopping from morning to night to outfit her daughter at Santa Clara University, where Sabrina is pre-med. Sara’s son Spencer is in Florida doing his third year of pilot training. Last year I said, “Saraswati, would you fly in a plane with your son as pilot?” And she said, “No way!” But she did it anyway, and I guess the white-knuckle solo flight in a little Cessna went OK.

Gurudas, one-of-a-kind Gurudas. Roger Siegel. With his Einstein hair, red cheeks, and loopy grin as, decades later, he replays for me his Prem Sayal imitation. Shouting “Hare Krishnaaa” and throwing his right hand in the air, he whips his head back as if flipping long hair from his eyes—“O Krishna! I LAWV you!”— then falls backward in a mock swoon—and once again sending me to the floor with laughter. He’s still out in the Avenues in San Francisco, and we meet there now and then (“The key is under that vawss by the door”), or in London (“But Shyamasundar, you’ll have to find another place to stay. My wife is here”), or in Mumbai or Vrindaban, and remember you together, Srila Prabhupada. Our memory presentations are a hit with presentday devotees because we do a Laurel-and-Hardy act: “It’s a fine mess you’ve gotten us into here, Ollie!” Gurudas is stream-of-consciousness all over the place. Me, I try to stick to the narrative, the facts, the dates, and ground him while his on-the-road Ginsberg mind travels all over time, remembering with uncanny precision and capturing the voice and every nuance of some small joke the Swami made on a morning walk in 1968.

And Janaki, Joanie’s little sister. Jan Oslund. Miracle girl, I call her now. For years I’d heard that bad health had laid her low, but she’s popped out of that now, as vibrant and quick-minded as she ever was. She lives with her cat and Radha-Krishna Deities in a normal, quiet suburb of Portland, Oregon. Janaki’s so thankful and happy on the occasions I drive up to visit. We make veggie burgers, drink lemonade, and howl at those outrageous days when she was the Swami’s naughty daughter, tricking him to eat artichokes leaf by leaf.

I’m so proud to have known these special friends, and I have to think that Krishna’s central casting department worked overtime to put together this special cast of characters for the technicolor spectacular I like to call Chasing Rhinos With The Swami. And what about George, George Harrison?

**\* \* \***

“Hey, George! Ravi! Hare Krishna! Over here!” It’s February 24, 1974, the small airport in Jaipur, Rajasthan.

A few weeks earlier, I’d set up a ruby-polishing operation with seven workers in Nath Bazar in the old quarter of Jaipur, India’s ancient gem-cutting center. My rented loft was about ten minutes from Govind Devji temple, where I’d run three times a day when the siren rang out for puja. I was lonely in Jaipur, so one day I phoned Friar Park, and Pattie informed me that George was in Benares with Ravi Shankar—she didn’t know exactly where. I wrote out a message: “George, I’m in Jaipur. Would love to see you,” and dispatched a friend by train to Benares to try to find George. A few days later, George called and said that his flight to Bombay would stop at Jaipur for half an hour at noon the next day. He’d meet me at the gate.

“I couldn’t believe it,” George said. “I’m walking down this crowded street in Benares—no one even knows I’m in India—and this guy comes up, hands me a note, and says, ‘Haribol! Shyamasundar wants to see you!”’

Earlier that morning, walking down an alley to find a taxi to the airport, a man, a total stranger, had approached me and said, “Here, this should be yours,” and he handed me this ten-inch dazzling blue Deity of Krishna in threefold-bending form, playing a gold flute—my trained eye saw at once that this piece was carved from a single, giant, priceless aquamarine, a blue beryl crystal! When I looked up, the man was gone.

“Here, George, a little present for you, for your altar at home,”

I said, and handed him the precious Deity which, I’m told, still resides on his altar at Friar Park.

George went on to spread Krishna’s name and fame all over the world. I see a photo of George with President Ford in an Indian newspaper, and he’s wearing a Krishna button on his lapel. On New Year’s Eve, 1974 I’m sitting alone at midnight, hunched over my gem-polishing wheel in an ancient Moghul garden house near Hyderabad, working and listening to Radio Kuwait, and I hear George sing “Ring out the old, Ring in the new-w-w,” and I’m thinking, Yeah, George, Hare Krishna to you too. So glad you’re still out there, old friend.

He whose eyes have seen

What our lives have been

And who we really are

It is “He”

Jai Sri Krishna

He whose sweetness flows

To anyone of those

That care to look his way

See his smile

Jai Sri Radhe

(“It Is ‘He’ (Jai Sri Krishna)” from the Dark Horse album, December 1974)

Srila Prabhupada, do you mind if I tell a few more George Harrison stories? I think it’s important for posterity to better understand the life of one of your greatest servants, and how George kept Krishna in his life until his final days.

Now it’s high summer, 1977, and Oxfordshire is green and lush. On my way from L.A. to India on ruby business, I have a twelvehour layover in London, so I grab a cab at Heathrow and head over to Friar Park to see George. He shows me new improvements to the house, and then we hear her coming across the oak floors of the Great Hall behind us, clip, clip, clip—and turn to see this knockout, drop-dead vision of petite Spanish beauty, with high cheekbones, a shy intelligence, sparkling eyes and dimples. No wonder George is so—“Shyamsundar, meet Olivia!” Her radiant smile stops my heart. She tosses her raven hair, walks over and shakes my hand. We wind up in the day room drinking tea, Olivia pottering with the kettle and saucers. “Hey, for an L. A. gal you’re doing pretty well with this high-tea stuff, huh?”

“One lump or two?”

“‘Oh, those Spanish eyes’—you know that one, George?” “Cucumber sandwiches?”

“With or without crust? You gotta be kidding!”

Hi-jinks matinee, the tinkle of tea things, gazing out open French doors at what I remember as acres of weeds and brambles, now lawns and fountains and gardens of breathtaking dimension and beauty.

In the latter years of your time among us, Srila Prabhupada, George would come to see you whenever he could. The last time you met at Bhaktivedanta Manor, it was July 26, 1976. George suggested you should rest, and you replied, “I am taking rest now these twenty-four hours. Now I have no other business than to take rest, \[laughter\] Always resting at the lotus feet of Krishna, that is real rest.. Ante narayana-smritih: at the time of death if one remembers Krishna, immediately he goes to Krishna, then his life is successful. But at the time of death, we may not be able to speak or to think. I’ll ‘ahn, ahn,' but that’s all. So I may not be able to chant Hare Krishna. So better let us practice now in such a way that at the time of death we may remember. That is success.” George remembered these words, always.

In an interview with Mukunda Goswami in 1980, George said, “I’ve got to consciously leave this body at death. I don’t want to be reborn, don’t want to go through all this crap again... But if I expect, if I start thinking I’m going to consciously leave my body at death, a great maha-samadhi, and I get hit by a truck while I’m thinking about where I’m going, I’m not thinking about Krishna, it’ll all be a disappointment.”

**\* \* \***

In August 1993 the British devotees invite me, Mukunda, and Gurudas to Hertfordshire to celebrate the twentieth anniversary of Bhaktivedanta Manor. We borrow a car and drive out to Henley-on-Thames. Though George has seen Mukunda as recently as ten years ago, and me about fourteen years ago, this is the first time we four have met together in more than twenty years.

“The three Krishnateers!” laughs George, the clean-shaven country squire at home in his sweatshirt and leisure togs, embracing us one by one. Mukunda in shining saffron swami robes now, a bigtoothed smile, eyes half-closed with pleasure, as fresh-faced now as he was twenty years ago. We circle each other in the driveway at Friar Park, sizing up, examining. George chuckles at Gurudas’s huge size, scruffy beard and hair, and Gurudas, always the teddy bear, his emotions on his sleeve, tries to speak and “George! Twenty years!” is about all he can bluster through his tears. We move quickly past appearances and into goofy stuff. I pluck at a graying lock of George’s hair and say, “How are ya, Dad?” And we’re back to the way it was (is, and always will be), our little Krishna brotherhood, all for one and one for all.

George notices the front-door latch needs fixing as we careen into the vestibule, me and Gurudas fumbling with our laces to remove the running shoes everyone wears these days—hoots and hollers and shouts of “Olivia!” ringing in the high-vaulted entryway as we open the door to the Great Hall and go silent seeing this magnificent space, with its forty-foot-high beamed ceilings, stained glass windows, walls paneled in ancient dark oak, the dominant stone fireplace, and high above it a life-size face of Prabhupada staring down at us. Spread lovingly around us on tables, couches, and walls are the Harrison family bric-a-brac: ukeleles, songbooks, guitar picks, vases of fresh-cut garden flowers, tapestries and Turkish rugs, statuettes and souvenirs, flutes and photographs—and across the vast room, winking bright neon bubbles beneath the wide wrap-around staircase—the old Wurlitzer jukebox!

And we re right back into it, as if we’d just awakened from a long state of suspended animation. Freeze frame. OK, roll ’em. George showing us this and that, clowning with a nose-flute. Olivia comes in, and we’re all entranced. “Derek Taylor called. He’ll be here soon,” she announces. George’s teenaged son Dhani bursts into the room and George introduces us. Then Dhani says, “Gotta go practice!” and dashes off. George takes us outside for a tour of the grounds.

As we walk, one question is uppermost in our minds: How does George feel about Krishna now that Prabhupada’s gone?

“What’s it like now at the temple? Are there many devotees?” George asks me.

“Yeah, hundreds! They’ve come from all over Europe. They’re all so bright and shining. Everyone’s getting older, though. It’s definitely ‘the graying of the Hare Krishna movement.’ But it’s so good to see Mukunda and Gurudas again. And the temple is wonderful. Radha and Krishna are well looked after—to the same standards as before. But I guess I’m not a very good example anymore, George. I mean, I feel like an imposter compared to those saintly people. Everyone wants to know about Prabhupada and this and that, but my memory is shot. I can’t even remember how to tie my friggin’ dhoti!”

“Yeah,” George muses, and we walk some more.

Finally, Gurudas asks the biggie: “What about you, George, you still chanting and all that?”

We stop. George looks off at nothing and says, “I have my little”— he looks back at us, fakes a serious look with knitted brows and in comic deep voice says—“moments of doubt.” Then he laughs and says, “But yeah, I try to chant and meditate a little every day. It’s easier for me here, with the gardens and the peace.”

And we walk on in lockstep, muttering Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna...

Wowing at the Shiva fountain, getting some great photos of all of us from far out on the lawn, George’s castle in the background (om flag flying today). Some great gag shots in the topiary, one of Gurudas trying to get out of the cave, George’s arm pulling him back by the throat as he shouts, “Beware the hand of Maya!” We get lost in the maze, and in the herb garden George knows the names of every plant in Latin.

I know that George and Denis O’Brien have made several hit films, like Life of Brian and Time Bandits, over the past few years, so at one point I ask him, “How’s the movie business these days?” He grabs me by the throat and pretends to throttle me, and says, “That’s for introducing me to Denis O’Brien!”

“Wha-a-a, wait a minute. What’s that all about?” George sees I don’t know, so he tells me he and Denis have had a major falling out and have folded their company, Hand Made Films. “Sue-me, sue-you blues,” he laughs. Then he launches into another lawsuit story, telling us how the publishers of a song called “He’s So Fine” have sued him for plagiarizing their tune in “My Sweet Lord.” The court-case has dragged on for years.

“Yeah, I know what that’s all about, George,” I say, and glumly tell him about the fate of my sapphire mine in Montana, how after six years as America’s most successful gem mining operation, a lawsuit has not only cost me the mine but practically everything I own as well. I can see he doesn’t think this is in the same league as his problems until he asks how much I lost. “Two and a half million dollars,” I say. “Oh,” he says.

We walk out onto a little bridge that spans the stream below his lake, quiet now, leaning on the rails and regarding George’s jewel-like palace crowning the hill in the distance, silhouetted by the late sun. “What’s the matter, Shyam? You seem worried about something,” George asks. “Well... yeah, I guess it’s ... Well, I haven’t really thought much about it yet, but a couple of days ago, when my wife drove me to the Montana airport for my flight here, she told me she’s filing for divorce and showed me the papers.” Long pause. And then we all break into peals of laughter, knowing this material world is a vale of troubles, and we’re just glad we know the way to rise above and laugh at it.

George says, “Remember that one in Monty Python? One guy says, ‘I was so poor I slept on the floor.’ The other guy says, ‘You had a floor? We lived in a hole.’ And another guy says, ‘You’re lucky, we couldn’t afford a hole!”’

On the way back to the house we stroll over to look at the Olympic-size outdoor swimming pool. It’s got a four-foot-wide gravel walkway around it. George reaches down, scratches in the gravel and then hands me a little red stone. “Remember these?” The ton of rough rubies we’d poured on these paths twenty years ago—still here. “Yeah,” George says, “it’s great fun for the kids and people who come to swim. I let ’em keep whatever they find.”

Late afternoon now. Heading across the driveway to the back door we pass the four-car garage, and George can’t resist showing us his latest acquisition: his rocket car. All day he’s been talking about Formula One racing, how he goes to races around the world, his love affair with high-performance speed. But this car—we’ve never seen anything like it. It’s a fighter-plane fuselage mounted on four wheels. George puts on goggles, gets in the cockpit and we get some classic photos. Walking back to the house, George leads the gang in a rather disharmonious version of “Use my body like a car, taking me both near and far—oh, yes I met my friends all here in the material woor-rr-ld...”

In the house now, Derek Taylor has arrived—another blast from the past—and we eat and laugh and drink tea and remember old times. Gurudas and Olivia are locked into a discussion about yoga and spirituality. Dhani’s fifteen-year-old school chums show up. They’ve formed a rock band, and I run off to help them lug an amplifier to the studio where they’re setting up to practice. We regroup and reminisce about Prabhupada, the real miracle of what he did in such a short time only now coming into perspective. We talk about the difficulties ISKCON is going through and how hard it is to remain Krishna conscious now that Prabhupada is gone. George mentions the troubles he’s had, getting people to listen to his spiritual message, and how the preaching part of his life is behind him now. “I’d rather just sit in my garden and chant Hare Krishna,” he says.

Mukunda remembers something and dashes out to the car, returning with a huge book called Krishna Art. “Here, this is for you.” George’s eyes light up as he turns the pages, seeing glorious paintings of Krishna—as a baby, as a boy, as a glowing youth with all His friends, with Radha—performing all His wonderful pastimes. “This is a collection of the best paintings by ISKCON devotees all over the world.” George is blown away, and he just can’t keep his eyes off the brilliant pictures.

As always, a visit with George turns to music. We do a group version of “Govindam,” with George on mandolin. Then we chant Hare Krishna mantra for a while. George does a few of his new tunes. We shoot a group photo, with all of us sitting on the couch. Later, we gather around the jukebox. George has all his favorites on the menu, no coins required. We listen to the Radha Krishna Temple singles. Then George says, “Have you heard this song?” and we all go into deep meditation as Bob Dylan sings:

Don’t have the inclination to look back on any mistake, Like Cain, I now behold this chain of events that I must break. In the fury of the moment I can see the Master’s hand In every leaf that trembles, in every grain of sand.

Oh, the flowers of indulgence and the weeds of yesteryear,

Like criminals, they have choked the breath of conscience and good cheer.

The sun beat down upon the steps of time to light the way

To ease the pain of idleness and the memory of decay.

I gaze into the doorway of temptation’s angry flame And every time I pass that way I always hear my name. Then onward in my journey I come to understand That every hair is numbered like every grain of sand.

No one wants to break up the party and leave—and enough words have been said—so we just listen to record after record, sprawled on the stairs or stretched out on the floor, late into the night.

**\* \* \***

We’d been talking about it for the past three years, getting together with George in Vrindaban. It’s March 1996, and George is in India for a few weeks with Ravi, in Calcutta and Madras, to record their Chants of India album. Mukunda Goswami has made arrangements for George to join us for five days in Vrindaban. We’ll stay at Prithu’s place. Prithu das Adhikari is a German devotee who owns a beautiful, three-story walled villa about two blocks from the ISKCON temple at Raman Reti, where Srila Prabhupada’s sanctified remains are entombed. Mukunda flies to India from New Zealand. I was mining sapphires in Madagascar, about one day’s air journey away. On the evening of March 22, 1996, Mukunda, George, and I converge at the Taj Hotel on Mansingh Road in New Delhi.

After breakfast we head off to Vrindaban in a hired car. George sits in the front seat, but leans over the back for most of the three-hour trip, yakking and moving his arms, laughing at the driver’s wild attempts to get us there two seconds faster than the next guy. He talks about the traditional Vedic chants he’s recording with Ravi. We talk about Madagascar and New Zealand and our kids and the pollution and overcrowding of suburban Delhi, how the whole planet is getting trashed (“I’m thinking to write a song called ‘Your Planet Is Doomed,”’ says George), and we talk about the onset of hot season in the surrounding countryside as it jolts, jerks, and whizzes past us.

Our taxi squeezes up the narrow alley to Prithu’s gate; we unload, and meet Prithu, who shows us around the place. Later, we gather in George’s room. He looks fresh and excited. Dressed in white baggy pajamas and a cream kurta, he’s propped up on bright-colored pillows and lounging on the bed, playing with his new toy: a Sharp digital video camera, the first of its kind, with flexible lens mounted on a fiber-optic arm. He’s learning how to use it, showing us all the things it will do: freeze-frames a shot of my grinning face (God, I look haggard!), then a shot of Radha and Krishna from a painting on the wall (“Hey, now there’s a startling couple!”), then close-ups of flower blossoms in vases around the room, and a shot of the fierce Nrisimha medallion around Mukunda’s neck.

Someone suggests we go up to the roof. From three stories up we look out on a panorama of Vrindaban: flame trees blooming in giant clusters of orange, flocks of fluorescent green parrots screeching by overhead, the loud meowing of peacocks from surrounding mango groves, the unending din of drums and bells from nearby temples, their ornate towers and domes spread out as far as you can see, loudspeakers contending all over town with different tempos and tunes of the Hare Krishna mantra. George, Mukunda, and I group for a shot in the center of the roof and—while Prithu focuses in—George laughs, shakes the hair back from his eyes, raises his bead-bag with his right hand and says to the world: “Still Krishna after all these years!”

Gradually the others fade away, and George and I sit alone in his room. We talk about the last three years, how my Montana bubble burst and launched me out to Africa. “If it wasn’t for my dear friend Mukunda tracking me down somehow and luring me back to Vrindaban, who knows if I’d have ever found Krishna again. I’d probably be dead by now.”

George oohs and chuckles at my misadventures, and relates his own continuing saga of lawsuits and predators. But then he switches to gardening and the peace of family life, and we agree we’ve been so lucky on this narrow and twisty path to have found

Krishna, to be blessed with His supreme knowledge, and how we must each utilize these fading years to perfect our lives, practicing what we’ve realized to be facts. The tranquility of Vrindaban slowly settles into our limbs. We re tired. Talking on automatic pilot now. We bow down to one another, mutter our praises to Prabhupada, and surrender ourselves to our separate sleeps.

The next shots on our Vrindaban video show our little group sitting on marble benches on the balcony inside Prabhupada’s Samadhi, the huge, domed marble building that houses Prabhupada’s earthly remains. George, shoulder-length graying locks, short beard and mustache, looking bright in white pants and a kurta, Vaishnava beads tight at his neck, bead-bag strapped to his right hand—the room resounds with unseen pilgrims walking around, chanting and talking, and horns and noise from the street outside. We sit in a row and chant japa. George’s eyes are closed as he chants. Then suddenly they open and he says, softly, “To think, that Prabhupada is right here...” Then he stares off into space for a while, then kind of chuckles and whisks his cheek with his bead-bag and says, “Starting to get a little misty. Shall we go down to Starbuck’s for a cuppa coffee? Double cappuccino?”

In the next clip we’re standing around the large inner courtyard of the gurukula, ISKCON’s school for young boys, surrounded by about twenty five-to-ten-year-olds in bright yellow dhotis and shaved heads. George is standing in their midst (but they have no idea who he is) while they chant Sanskrit verses from the Bhagavadgita in child-voice unison: dum-ta-da-dum-ta-da-dum-dum-dum. “These guys know the Gita and a lot of other scriptures by heart,” someone says. The camera pans around the two tiers of balconies that ring the courtyard. They’re packed with a hundred curious young boys’ faces of every color and nationality. George’s grin is continuous. He loves this stuff.

Next, we’re clattering along Vrindaban’s main thoroughfare in a bicycle rickshaw— watch out for that cow!—Mukunda and I cracking up at something George says, the three of us heaped on a seat meant for two. Ever the clown when cameras are rolling, George makes wisecracks and glares at the cameraman, who’s bouncing along in the rickshaw in front of us—car horns, bicycle bells, people shouting—cut to:

Ka-blam! Ungh! The camera arcs down to a swirling montage of bare feet and a tile floor—then back up to Mukunda, Prithu, and I looking wide-eyed and shocked. “Are you OK?!” “Yeah,” says George, “raised up when I should’ve ducked. What a blessing!” He’s clobbered his head on the low opening to Prabhupada’s old rooms at the Radha-Damodar temple in the ancient quarter of Vrindaban, where we’ve gone to pay homage. A somber French devotee wanders onscreen, introduces himself (George says, “Smile, Prabhu, you’re on TV!”), and says that he’s the caretaker for this shrine, that Prabhupada stayed here for six years and blah-blah-blah—the guy probably hasn’t smiled in his life. We sit quietly and meditate on Prabhupada’s palpable presence, George chanting furiously on his beads. After several minutes Prithu pumps up Prabhupada’s harmonium and begins to lead us in kirtan, Mukunda on mridanga, George lazily keeping time, hitting a gong with a stick in his left hand, his right hand buried in his bead-bag. It’s the old standard Hare Krishna melody, and as the tempo builds we begin to realize who we are and where we are sitting, feeling Prabhupada’s sweet proximity. When it’s over, there’s a long silence, tears flowing down all our cheeks. It was only a moment, but Prabhupada was really here, and we felt his love for us and his pleasure at seeing us all together.

After, we take a little stroll around the Radha-Damodar temple grounds, George chuckling at the sign “Photo Copy Strictly Prohibited” and the papier-mache figures of cows and Ganeshes. Behind the temple we come across the tombs of great Vaishnava devotees and saints, and George is amazed: “Oh, these are the different swamis from hundreds of years ago? Chaitanya’s followers? Wow!”

Down at the Yamuna River now, we’re haggling for a rowboat and guide to drift us the mile or so along Vrindaban’s sacred waterfront, check out the views of temples, bathing ghats, and the ancient, crumbling palaces. As we push off from shore, George starts singing “Rollin’, rollin’ on the riv-v-ver.” Packs of red-rumped monkeys shadow our slow cruise, leaping and swinging from stone parapets and balconies along the shore. Mukunda points out some huge turtles lounging in the sun. We speculate on what they eat, and dead bodies is the consensus. George plays with his camera, shoots portraits of himself with wind-tossed hair in the reflective windshield. “Look, there’s the spot where Krishna killed the horse demon!” someone says, and we disembark at Keshi Ghat. “And here’s the tree where Krishna stole the bathing cowgirls’ clothes!” Just then some loud shouts, the camera jerks around and pans a crowd of people chasing a monkey with a woman’s purse in its mouth. The monkey, realizing that it’s seriously outnumbered, drops the purse, and we howl with laughter.

Now we see George’s artistic eye as he moves with the camera through the narrow streets and alleyways of old Vrindaban. He plays with the strobe effect, fixes on house ornaments, children’s faces, a guy repairing a broken bike. We get on separate rickshaws and he films while moving: signs (“Well-come” and “Krishna Photo Shop”), little boys pumping water from an old iron pump, architectural oddities and roofline arabesques, face shots, stills, ditches, doorways—“Look at the colors here!” he says, focusing on a lotus-sculpted doorway painted a rainbow of colors, framed by yellow and purple walls.

Next scene has us in a “God Shop,” which specializes in Deities and all types of worship paraphernalia. The fat Brijbasi owner, his mouth stained red with betel juice, squats behind his wares and bargains with Prithu and George for a three-foot set of marble Radha and Krishna Deities; 25,000 rupees is the last price we hear before the scene cuts off.

It’s about three o’clock in the hot afternoon. The Deities at the Krishna-Balaram temple are napping behind closed curtains, and George and the rest of us have gathered with half a dozen other devotees in the shaded portico before the altar to sing. At first there’s a kind of pensive look on George’s face (he told me later he had a headache), but as kirtan builds and begins to reverberate through the vast marble interior, George closes his eyes and his face is suffused with an inner radiance. When it’s his turn to lead, George’s voice swells with the Hare Krishna mantra with an intensity and beauty I’ve not heard from him before, and it goes on and on and on. The film ends.

We amble back to Prithu’s in a jubilant mood and hear a funny story. It seems some Italian paparazzo got wind of George’s whereabouts and with his long-lens camera, positioned himself on the corrugated iron roof of a building across from Prithu’s compound, covering his body with a camouflaged tarp. Prithu’s wife was walking in the garden when she heard a clicking noise up there; she picked up a brick and tossed it at the suspicious bundle, scoring a direct hit. Cursing and thrashing, the man jumped up to run, lost his footing, crashed to the ground, and limped away in a torrent of Italian obscenities.

Late afternoons and evenings we gathered in the main downstairs hall at Prithu’s and talked, had kirtan, and listened to tapes of Prabhupada singing and lecturing and of devotee music from around the world.
