George would pick up a guitar and strum a while and sing us snippets from songs he was working on, like “Any Road Will Get You There.” My favorite was his version of Dylan’s “In Every Grain of Sand.” At one point we’d been talking about some wild experience I’d had in Madagascar, and George hoisted his guitar, picked out a delta-blues riff, and started singing:

Oh, he’s looked his whole life

For that long-lost vein,

He’s had a little pleasure

But a lot of pain!”

We’re all laughing, George too, as he puts down the guitar and says, “I call that one ‘Shyamasundar’s Blues.’”

So pass five delightful days and nights. On the trip back to Delhi, George has gotten smart and turned the front suicide seat over to me. We get a late start, and it’s getting dark as we enter the huge city at the peak of rush hour. Sure enough, our driver gets lost, and in a crowded traffic circle—wham!—he rear-ends a motor scooter. Both drivers stop their vehicles in the middle of the honking jam, stand in the street and yell at each other, their threatening gestures illumined by a hundred headlights. Silence in the car. Then George reaches in his pants pocket and pulls out a $100 bill, hands it through the window to our driver, and says, “Just give him this and tell him to chant Hare Krishna. Let’s get outta here!” We shake with laughter, the tension breaks. We know we’re back in the chaos of the material world, but laughter, old friends and the Hare Krishna mantra have relieved us of all anxiety.

**\* \* \***

BHAKTIVEDANTA MANOR, JULY 7,1996

After ten years of litigation, politicking, and nationwide publicity, the Hertsmere Council, which governs affairs at Letchmore Heath, finally approved an access road and permission to continue public worship at Bhaktivedanta Manor. To celebrate, the devotees planned a victory dinner at the Manor, and invited George, Olivia, Mukunda, and me to attend.

A day before the dinner, I fly into London from Madagascar and phone George, who asks, “Will you be wearing civvies, you know, or a dhoti?” I say, “Dhoti.” Long pause. “What should I wear?” he asks. “Casual, George, just casual.”

He looks pretty good—Olivia, too—stepping out of his black Mercedes on this gorgeous Hertfordshire summer evening, the sun still high at 7 p.m. Of course, George always looks good, no matter what he’s wearing. There’s a sort of presence or celebrity glow that turns heads no matter how he’s dressed or how remote the locale—even in small villages in India they stare at him, knowing he’s someone special. Tonight he’s got long blackgray locks pulled straight back from his high forehead, the top layer gathered in a ponytail, the sides and back hanging thickly to his shoulders. Looks like he’s starting a new beard and mustache, gray and kind of scruffy. He’s wearing a plain blue cotton checked shirt with a white t-shirt showing at the throat, a single strand of dark neck-beads, jeans, and a big poplin windbreaker with lots of pockets. But it’s those dark dancing eyes that grab you from under his black bushy eyebrows (like his dad’s), and the big, white-toothed, devastating grin.

Olivia looks sharp, an L.A. lady in black jeans, simple white blouse beneath a trim white jacket, little flashes of earring beneath her raven hair. With her high Aztec cheekbones, there’s an air of Spanish mystery about her, but it’s her eyes that catch you—demure and sober, yet strong and direct, as if anger could come flashing fast. Sometimes she blinks real fast and tosses her hair (I swear sometimes she seems a petite female version of George).

In the foyer, on our way to the dining room, George’s attention is drawn to a big bronze plaque on the wall:

His Divine Grace A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada and the members of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness in Great Britain would like to express their eternal gratitude to Sriman George Harrison for his liberal donation of Bhaktivedanta Manor and grounds in the service of Lord Sri Krishna.

“Eternal, huh?” He pauses and thinks about that one for a while, then we move on into the dining room.

George, Olivia, Mukunda Goswami, and I, as guests of honor, are seated together by the windows; maybe a hundred others—devotees, local bigwigs, English politicians and bureaucrats, dignified Hindu leaders from around the UK—are seated around us. Lots of noise and laughter roar in the oak-paneled hall. We’re served a delicious multicourse meal, prasada. It’s the first time Olivia’s been to the temple, so she’s wowing at each of the dishes. We make jokes and small talk, then finger bowls are passed to us so we can wash our hands. At last, Akhandadhi das, the temple president, stands up near the big fireplace at the middle of the room, mic in hand, and gives thanks, one by one, to all the various men and women (clap, clap) who have made this victory celebration possible. Now it’s my turn. I’m nervous as hell—from the wilds of Madagascar to this!

“Bhaktivedanta Manor was completely George Harrison’s idea. One day he just said, ‘You guys are getting crowded down there at Bury Place. We need to look for a bigger place, out in the country.’ As a result of his quick decision, this glorious home for Radha and Krishna has come to pass. The Manor was Prabhupada’s favorite place. He used to gaze out the windows of his room upstairs and say, ‘George has given us this wonderful house. George has so much love for Krishna, and Krishna has so much love for him. He is more dear to me than my own son.’

“I feel a bit like Fats Domino or Little Richard standing up here after twenty-five years doing oldies but goodies—and some people say that the best things are accomplished in youth—but Srila Prabhupada has shown us by his example that age and experience are best. So for Bhaktivedanta Manor, and for all of Prabhupada’s children, I think the best is yet to come. As you said three months ago in Vrindaban, George: ‘Still Krishna after all these years!’ Thank you, George, for being in our lives” (clap, clap!).

Next, Mukunda gets up, makes a little speech, and calls for George and Olivia to come forward. Devotees hustle over and drape their necks with big garlands. George and Olivia stand there, shy and serious because they know they have to make a speech. Mukunda hands George a shoebox-sized package wrapped in green tissue and says that it comes with love from all the devotees.

George opens it and breaks into a delighted smile—it’s a small murti of Prabhupada sitting cross-legged, an eternal look on his face. He carefully sets it down, walks to the microphone, and says, “Well, it was a pleasure, a service, all rather spontaneous. I just did it because it had to be done ... somebody had to do it!”

Loud laughter, lots of clapping as he steps back, then suddenly turns, grabs the mic again and says, with an impish look, “You know, when I heard about the trouble you were having with the road, I sat kind of quietly in the background—but if you hadda failed I was ready to come offer you Henley-on-Thames!” Huge round of laughter and clapping.

Somebody leaked this statement to the newspapers, and the next day the Sunday Express exclaims:

MY SWEET LORD! EX-BEATLE INVITES

HARE KRISHNA SECT TO HENLEY

Former Beatle George Harrison has dismayed his neighbours in Henley-on-Thames by offering his £10 million mansion to the Hare Krishna sect. Residents on the edge of his 30-acre Friar Park estate in Oxfordshire fear their peace and tranquility will be shattered by the cymbol bashing and chanting of devotees. A NIMBY (not-in-my back-yard) campaign has already begun in the neighbourhood, whose residents include the Duke and Duchess of Kent, Philip Schofield and Chris Rea. “It’s hardly the Henley Royal Regatta, is it?” said resident John Davis. “I am religiously tolerant but I don’t want any Hare Krishnas here.” Harrison’s neighbour, Mrs Alethea Pitt, 82, was equally upset. She said: “Loads of people trooping here on a pilgrimage is just not acceptable. This is Henley!”

On the way back to his car George stops to admire the full moon shining on the lawns, lake, and gardens. “It looks just like Vrindaban,” he says. George carefully places his little Prabhupada on the back seat of the Mercedes, and as he’s buckling up he says he’ll come back and check the road construction progress at the end of August—“You don’t want to rush the road. You might have subsidence,” says the road-and-track veteran. “Haribol!” he shouts through his window as a hundred hands wave him off.

The next day Mukunda and I spent all day with George at Friar Park, swimming in the pool and catching up. Highlight of the day was our drive in George’s new McLaren Fl racing car. He paid like a million pounds for the car, and it was a beast. The speedometer topped out at 230 mph. George popped the trunk to show us the rear-mounted gold-plated motor, finely machined to within 1/8” of the car’s body. He’d had someone calligraph thousands of tiny Hare Krishna mantras as an undercoat to the Dark Purple Pearl paint, so from a distance the car appeared to shimmy and selfilluminate. The driver sat in the front center, with two bucket seats behind for passengers.

“Shall we take her for a spin?” George asks.

As George accelerated onto the narrow country road near Henley, the push against my body was so great I struggled to put the camera to my eye. I turn my head and get a shot of Mukunda, eyes closed, G-force pulling his cheeks to a grimace. George shifts gears, the engine howls, hedgerows flash past, and we hit 140 with a curve coming ahead—“Geor—there’s a curve!” He laughs, shifts down, taps the brake, and we cruise through the curve at 30. He eyeballs us in the rearview mirror, a wicked grin on his face, delighted to have scared the crap out of his Krishna friends.

As we drove back to his castle at a sedate speed, George started it, and we joined in the chorus, “Baby, you can drive my car... Yes, I’m gonna be a star...”

I spent three months in London in the summer of 1999, working with Bhakti Caru Goswami to script and shoot the UK episodes for his ambitious documentary of Prabhupada’s life, Abhay Charan. I went out to Friar Park often that summer, and George and I mostly just sat around the small wooden dacha George had imported from Russia, onion dome and all, tucked among the firs, cedars, and pines on a small rise overlooking the Thames valley. At the side of the dacha was a little garden divided into two parts—half planted in lilies and zinnias (“the flowers of my indulgence”) and the other half thistles and crabgrass (“the weeds of yesteryear”). George’d fire up the Russian stove, put on a kettle for tea, and open an ornate chest concealing a stereo, and we’d lie on the big bed and listen to music for hours. Chants of India, then Every Grain of Sand— over and over—Slim Whitman songs, a little cool jazz if it was raining, and George’s latest remixes for All Things Must Pass.

Walking me out to my car at the end of that glorious summer so I could catch my flight back to India, George said, “Ya know, I was at the Mayo clinic. They found a small spot on my lung and they wanted to take it out. And I thought, Why? Krishna has given me everything, and I’m so tired of this material world. I don’t really wanna stick around this place anymore. I just want to be with Krishna.”

Late November 2001, I was exploring for diamonds in Andhra Pradesh and got a phone call from Olivia. “George is not doing so well, and he really wants to see you and Mukunda.” I jumped on a flight from Hyderabad to Los Angeles, rented a car at LAX, and met Mukunda’s flight from New Zealand.

George had a little altar started at the foot of his bed, so Mukunda called Srivas at the L.A. temple and had him bring over an 18-inch set of gorgeous brass Radha-Krishna Deities dressed in bright clothes and jewels, and we sat Them front and center on the altar. George was so happy to see Radha and Krishna there, and he gazed at Them for long periods, smiling. Surrounded by his family, George laughed a lot as a few old friends stopped by—Ravi, Paul, Ringo. We didn’t speak much, just looked at each other with undying love, listening to tapes of Srila Prabhupada chanting, pondering the impossible. So George Harrison—“Hari’s son,” he liked to say—passed his days in that magnificent body. With Prabhupada chanting japa softly from speakers on the wall, my last words to him were, “Prabhupada’s here, and he’s taking good care of you, George.” His last words to me were, “Thank you.”

**\* \* \***

Let’s see, Prabhupada, what have I missed in these books, Chasing Rhinos? Which of your endless glories have I forgotten? What realizations have I had since starting this project so many years ago? Ahh, here’s one I’ve remembered most recently: you never make mistakes'.

I’m a little old now, prone to constant self-examination, and realize that from the moment I wake in the morning I make mistake after little mistake. I drop things, I forget where I put the sugar, screw the lid on crooked, aim for the wastebasket and miss. In all the years I have watched you up close, Prabhupada, you never make mistakes like this. Your every move, your every word is flawless, error-free. How can that be? I can only conclude that you are so fully Krishna conscious that you are in constant trance, aware that you are not the doer, so in harmony with Krishna’s desires that, really, Krishna is doing everything through you as He conducts your every graceful move and your every perfect word.

And I often add in lectures these days, “There are no perfect persons on this planet. But if once you find a perfect person, one who knows everything, one who makes no mistakes—why wouldn’t you follow him?”

Whenever I speak in India I emphasize the unique historical role you played to single-handedly revive the Vedic tradition in your country. Contemporary Indians don’t quite get this. When we first arrived there with you in 1970, Krishna was almost extinct in India—there were a few older Vaishnavas here and there, a few crumbling, unattended Krishna temples scattered around if you knew where to look for them; Vrindaban was a small, run-down village. Now! Now India is filled with Krishna. There are Krishna temples all over the place, in practically every town and village. Everywhere you look in India now you see “Krishna Bicycle Shop,” “Krishna Hotel,” Krishna-this, Krishna-that—Krishna everywhere. In March 2018 my car halted in a traffic jam two miles outside Vrindaban, and a policeman told us, “You won’t get in today. The entire town is filled up.” Your no-holds-barred lectures in 1971 New Delhi have matured into a nationwide political consciousness that considers the spiritual in acts of state. India’s economy, too, has boomed over these past five decades, and I can’t help but think that by your going to America and returning so successfully, you inspired millions of Indian citizens to emigrate and form the most successful new community in the US today: “See, our culture is welcome in States, there are Krishna temples, so let us go there!” Oh, I know, they have put your likeness on a five-rupee postage stamp, and Swami Bhaktivedanta Marg is a street name in most major Indian cities, but it may take some time before you are recognized by your countrymen for your true role as the greatest global Indian hero-saint in all of history.

You boldly dared to challenge modern science about space, time, evolution, and the history of humans on planet earth, using the Vedas as your authority and logic and common sense to verify your assertions. Srila Prabhupada, you are the ultimate scientist. You know everything, the cause and effect of everything. I chuckle today to see that each new discovery at the vanguard of contemporary science only confirms what you have already said. I read an article the other day that described a newly discovered burnt-out star in the constellation Centaurus; nicknamed Lucy, this dwarf star is a chunk of crystallized carbon—a diamond—equal to ten billion trillion trillion carats.

Another article I just read is a tenth-century commentary on the fourth verse of hymn 1.50 in the Rg Veda. This verse describes the sun. The Rg Veda is the oldest known Vedic text, passed down to us from time immemorial. The great sage Bhatta Bhaskara comments: “Thus it is remembered: \[O Sun\] you who traverse 2,202 yojanas in half a nimesha? Using the ancient Puranic equivalents of one yojana equaling nine miles, and one nimesha equaling 16/75 of a second, multiplying these figures out we get almost the exact figure for the speed of light that was only confirmed by modern science in 1975: 186,282 miles per second. Krishna says in the Bhagavad-gita, “I am also the Rg, the Sama and the Yajur Vedas.” Once again, Srila Prabhupada, your knowledge outshines them all.

Modern science has confirmed that time slows as an object accelerates through space, or you could say, time slows for a body in motion. You have certainly proved this fact, Prabhupada!

You rocketed around the world, never wasting a moment, and though every day with you seemed to last an eternity, what you accomplished in such a short time seems to defy the laws of possibility.

And you have trained your adepts to go forth and take up these arguments, a new generation of Vedic scientists who are gradually being heard and who may yet derail the myopic course of modern science and steer it to your absolute reality. Books by your disciples, such as Forbidden Archeology, by Michael Cremo (Drutakarma das) and Richard L. Thompson (Sadaputa das) and Cosmology on Trial, by Pierre St Clair (Vaiyasaki das) are bestsellers, expansions of your sharp evidence and logic, and they are making a big and growing impact on intellectual communities.

As a geologist, I’m constantly amazed and calmed by the knowledge you gave me as I roam the continents and examine how land and sea and time, even on this insignificant planet, stretch out and change and move, and how I, so miniscule and ephemeral, fit into this scripted landscape. As I write these words a thousand feet above sea level on the central coast of California, the rocks I sit on were once—not that long ago in geologic time—the seafloor somewhere near Antarctica, having drifted north ten thousand miles at the rate of a quarter-inch per year. I understand now, too, how perfect the climate must have been in India in Krishna’s time, five thousand years ago, when the Pleistocene ice sheet over the Himalayas was in final retreat, the rivers flowed full, the climate was sublime, and lush forest covered India; sea levels were much lower in those times, the royal city of Dwaraka stood at the coastline, and monks and armies marched to Indo-China across dry land on what is now the Bay of Bengal.

And yes, Prabhupada, you revealed to us how the Vedic culture is the mother of all cultures, how Sanskrit is the root of most languages on the planet, and that Vedic ideas and practices underlie all civilizations. I love to read books like Proof of Vedic Culture's Global Existence, by Stephen Knapp (Sri Nandanandana das). Everywhere you look through the history of human civilizations you will see the Vedic influence. But I have to laugh, too, when people try to take this concept a little too far, saying that everything comes from India. Back in about 2001, Mukunda turned me on to a hilarious British Indian TV comedy show called Goodness Gracious Me, where an Indian immigrant father in London is always telling his Westernized son, “Babu, everything comes from India.” Shakespeare, Leonardo da Vinci, the royal family (“they live in a joint family, work in the same business, have arranged mar-riages—India!”), Superman (“bad haircut, works two jobs, faster than an Indian train—India!”), Santa Claus (“big beard, huge belly, terrible suit—India!”), even Jesus (“worked for his father, managed to feed five thousand people with a small amount of food—India!”) Everyone in the Bible was Indian, except God, because He created the world in six days and rested on the seventh. “What kind of Indian doesn’t work on Sundays?”

After all these years in your Vedic library, Srila Prabhupada, we have learned everything!

Optimism is my primary emotion these days as I scan the planet and worldly affairs and weigh your accomplishments to date and picture how the future must unfold. I’m a little more reclusive than I used to be, but selling Chasing Rhinos forces me to get out and about, and as I check out ISKCON news websites every day and travel to temples around the world, I am so full of hope.

Prabhupada, you always said that you don’t want millions of stars; you want one moon. “The light from one moon outshines millions of stars.” Well, you’ll be happy to know you’ve created hundreds of thousands of moons out there in the world today. Your disciples and their disciples have expanded your pure teachings into every nook and cranny of contemporary geography and affairs. What strikes me is the purity they have maintained. It’s like you’re still present, Srila Prabhupada, in an amplified way, everywhere I go on this planet earth. When I meet Radhanath Swami or Malati or Indradyumna Swami or Bhakti-vijnana Maharaj or Yadubara or Visakha devi, Giriraj, Gopal Krishna, Gauranga in Mumbai, or Jai Nitai and Radha Mohan in London—the list is endless—so many moons! When I meet these great souls I know beyond question that your pure legacy is intact and exploding forth. I observe hundreds of your devotees at ISKCON leadership meetings in Mayapur from my little bookstall—most of whom I’ve never met or can’t remember their names (three generations on now, the simple names are all taken, and some are three or even four names long)—and I’m filled with hope. It’s only a matter of time. I won’t live to see it happen, but already the flowers of Krishna consciousness delight the planet, and someday hence your abundant fruits must certainly save the world.

You are the master gardener, Srila Prabhupada. You planted a potent acorn on fertile ground, and your Krishna consciousness society burst out and grew quickly in an open space. Then, no longer the exploding sapling, it struggled to overcome its competition, endure some nasty seasons. The trunk grew thicker, though, season by season, and the branches reached up—and at last your

ISKCON has emerged to the sun above all others, a mighty oak standing alone to nourish and guide the world. Prabhupada, you brought the dying spark of sanatana-dharma to this dried out husk of Kali-yuga, and created a wonderful, unstoppable forest fire in high winds. As the disenchantment and futility of material striving increase in this difficult age, the more intelligent in human society will certainly see Krishna consciousness as the only source of pure truth left. With so much momentum, your mission cannot fail.

**\* \* \***

The name Pradyumna das has appeared many times in these Chasing Rhino pages. Pradyumna probably spent more personal time with Srila Prabhupada than any other devotee, traveling with him for years to edit and transcribe his books. I ran into Pradyumna again a few years back in Mumbai. For decades now, Pradyumna has worked for Religions for Peace, a UN-accredited organization based in New York City. His job is to go all over the world and interview spiritual leaders from every faith and disposition, and to gather the best of them together for seminars and talks. Priests, rabbis, swamis, imams, lamas, witch doctors, sadhus, holy men of every stripe—you name it, he’s met them. I said, “Pradyumna, you’ve met the Dalai Lama, the popes and cardinals, yogis in Himalayan caves, every guru on the planet—how many Prabhupadas are out there?” Pradyumna is a sober person, an intellectual and professional skeptic. But this time he rolled his eyes back, got a silly grin on his face, and said, “No one even comes close.!”

**\* \* \***

Srila Prabhupada, how do I cope with a world without you physically present here? Sometimes I get so desperate to see you again! How do I maintain that special, tingling glow I always had when

I walked by your side or entered a room where you were sitting? Around you, the colors, shapes, sounds, other people—everything became more intense, heightened, magnified. In your presence I was thrilled with excitement, anticipation, a sense of comfort and safety, the certainty that whatever happens will be Krishna’s arrangement and perfect in every way. But what about now!

Yes, we have our sadhana, our daily practices, chanting the maha-mantra, reading your books, visiting you in photos or your murti in the temples, but I’ve come to realize that the glue that holds me so tightly to your personal presence all these years has been what I can only describe as mystical experience. Prabhupada, you have opened the doors to perception and made it possible for us to see Krishna everywhere at every moment of every day! You have ushered all of your devotees into the dimension of mystical experience. “Devotees automatically have all mystic power,” you said. The bird that flies past my window, the phone call I get out of the blue, the whispers in my head as I write these words—to sense the plan and hand of Krishna in all events has made it easy to remember you. Not only remember, but associate in real time with you as you reach over to pat my shoulder for a word well-placed or to tsk-tsk me for a thought ill-timed—as you shed your slippers and rustle across the rug to plop in that armchair over there and ask me, “So what’s the position?” or shoot me a note across the universe that all is well in Goloka and “Why not join me here?”

“The devotees of the Lord can see everything clearly... Devotees do not have to endeavor for any yoga-siddhi, or perfection in mystic powers. They are competent to understand everything by the grace of the Lord, who is sitting in everyone’s heart.” (Srimad-Bhagavatam 3.11.17, purport)

I need mystical experience in my life every day, Srila Prabhupada. I expect magic. This is what keeps me going, attached to you. I observe the flow, the coincidences, flashes of impossible beauty, the signs and indicators. I watch events and situations unfold around me turn out just the way you said they would and sense the magic everywhere, deepening more and more the personal, individual, mystical connection I have with you and Krishna.

I don’t often lecture in public, but when I do, devotees often ask, “How do you keep your spiritual strength?” And my answer these days is, “Prabhupada has given you the greatest gift: you have mystic powers. Now use them to see Krishna everywhere, play with Krishna, and expect magic!"

**\* \* \***

OK, Srila Prabhupada, my three volumes of Chasing Rhinos with you are at an end. If I haven’t remembered everything, or if I have omitted even one of your glorious activities, it’s because my heart is still covered with the corruption of material existence. My only solace in this regard is this verse you gave us from Srimad-Bhagavatam (10.14.7): “In time, learned philosophers or scientists might be able to count all the atoms of the earth, the particles of snow, or perhaps even the shining molecules radiating from the sun, the stars and other luminaries. But among these learned men, who could possibly count the unlimited transcendental qualities possessed by You, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, who have descended onto the surface of the earth for the benefit of all living entities?”

**\* \* \***

Srila Prabhupada, it is you who got me started on this writing business. As your secretary I learned from you the art of writing, and from that time I have lived in writing consciousness. Words—everything began to form around written words and the thinking process to transform things and experience into words on a page. Being with you meant living in a world of words. Now, writing these books, I’m again in a world of words. I can’t just watch a bird sing, I have to say what kind of bird it is, what color, describe its manner of flight. Where’s its mate? What’s it singing, and what does its song mean? In a way, it’s been a kind of pleasant torture to be trapped inside a cosmic network of words and language structures, weaving tiny chains of braided words trying to lasso the inconceivable. But now we’re at the end of these words, and I sort of know what Krsnadas Kaviraj Goswami must have felt in the final verses of his vast, three-volume Caitanya-caritamrta. After paying his obeisances to Lord Chaitanya and His associates, the great author writes:

The mercy of their lotus feet is my spiritual master, and my words are my disciples, whom I have made to dance in various ways.

Seeing the fatigue of the disciples, the spiritual master has stopped making them dance, and because that mercy no longer makes them dance, my words now sit silently.

My inexperienced words do not know how to dance by themselves. The mercy of the guru made them dance as much as possible, and now, after dancing, they have taken rest.

I decorate my head with the dust of the lotus feet of my audience. Now you have all drunk this nectar, and therefore my labor is successful.

**\* \* \***

O Prabhupada! Sometimes I’ve stayed this trail with you, glued to your lotus heels. Other times I wandered off, far off, and lost you in thick entanglements and concrete jungles. Sometimes we have camped and slept in adjoining beds. Some mornings I awake and find you gone, and I am desolate. But somehow here I am again, tucked into your slipstream and rocketing along at your usual lightning pace. Out here, Srila Prabhupada, out here now, I’m far past the point of no return—out of the pandemonium. How silent and sure it has all become out here, beside you, my sole anchor, guide, and friend.
